# Research report: Existing encyclopedias, wikis, databases and repositories of mechanical connections and mechanisms

Run 1 (prior art) of the Encyclopedia of Connections. 54 of 54 items researched. Generated by `generate_report.py` from `results/*.json`. Values the research agents marked `[uncertain]` are left out here but kept in the JSON files; in the contents, "(?)" marks a short value that is partly unverified. See `summary.md` for the conclusions.

## 1 DMG-Lib (Digital Mechanism and Gear Library)

*Encyclopedia / digital library.* TU Ilmenau-led digital library of mechanisms, gears, historic books and physical models, with animations and a mechanism catalogue (German/English)

### Basics

- **name**: DMG-Lib (Digital Mechanism and Gear Library / Digitale Mechanismen- und Getriebebibliothek)
- **url**: https://www.dmg-lib.org/ (redirects to the portal at https://www.dmg-lib.org/dmglib/main/portal.jsp; English interface via ?context_setLangCode=en)
- **resource_type**: digital library (with a mechanism catalogue, a searchable mechanism database, interactive animations, videos, CAx files, a multilingual thesaurus and full-text historic and modern literature)
- **cost**: Free
- **license**: All rights reserved. The Conditions of Use (section 5) say the content is protected by copyright, may be copied only for private, non-commercial use (German Copyright Act section 53), downloads get a one-time, non-transferable licence, and any commercial use is forbidden. Section 6 says the website may not be copied, republished or redistributed without DMG-Lib's consent. What this means for the encyclopedia: link to DMG-Lib pages freely, but do not copy its images, animations or text into the encyclopedia without permission
- **language**: Mixed. The interface is available in German, English, Spanish, French, Italian and Romanian. Content is mostly German (e.g. 'Malteserkreuzgetriebe', 'Schrittgetriebe'), with a large English set (the Artobolevskij mechanisms, which give English, French and Russian names) and some French-only entries
- **access_barrier**: none (no login needed to browse, search, view animations or play videos)
- **authority_level**: academic (a university research project led by TU Ilmenau with TU Dresden and RWTH Aachen, now run by the DMG-Lib e.V. association and linked to IFToMM Germany. Content comes from university model collections and published literature, but individual entries are not peer-reviewed)

### Coverage

- **covers**: Mechanisms and gears (mechanism and machine science) as a whole: linkages, cams, gear trains, friction drives, wedge, screw, belt and chain drives, hydraulic/pneumatic mechanisms and step (intermittent) mechanisms, plus the literature and people behind them. The home page counts are: 3,114 mechanism descriptions (functional models and machines), 36,078 documents (31,862 in full text), 2,818 interactive animations, 3,465 videos, 1,905 CAx files, 45,829 images, 5,823 people, 1,784 thesaurus concepts and 28 software tools. The mechanism descriptions include physical teaching models from TU Dresden (452), RWTH Aachen (328), TU Chemnitz (214), TU Ilmenau (174), Leibniz Universitat Hannover (52) and IFMA Clermont-Ferrand (386), plus 909 entries from Artobolevskij's collection of over 4,700 mechanisms (short entries grouped as 'Clutch and coupling mechanisms', 'Indexing mechanisms' and so on), and Reuleaux models
- **organisation**: Several routes: (1) browse the mechanism descriptions by 'fundamental mechanism' type (Reuleaux's groups), by application area or by the collection where the physical model is held; (2) an A-Z list of all 3,114 mechanisms; (3) a 'Mechanism search' form with facet filters (function, planar/spherical/spatial, number of links, input and output motion, degrees of freedom, relative position of input and output shafts, fundamental type, revolvability); (4) a free-text advanced search; (5) separate browsing of literature, animations, videos, CAx files, images, thesaurus and people; and (6) a timeline. For a beginner who doesn't know the terms it works poorly: titles are often German terms (e.g. 'Schrittgetriebe, stufenlos verstellbares') and the filters use engineering vocabulary ('transfer function', 'guidance mechanism'). The A-Z list mixes German, English and French titles, so the same type shows up under several names
- **scope_match**: high. It is a very large catalogue of mechanism TYPES rather than hardware, and includes many of the project's special behaviours (ratchets, overrunning clutches, safety friction clutches, ball-click detents, locking levers, toggle linkages, self-locking gears, Geneva drives, harmonic and cycloidal drives, differentials). It is aimed at engineers and historians, though, and has almost nothing on 3D printing, powered connectors or adhesives
- **taxonomy_used**: Reuleaux-based 'fundamental mechanisms' with 9 top-level groups (counts from the browse page): link-containing mechanism (linkages, 2,275), cam mechanism (202), gear train mechanism (364), friction-based mechanism (39), wedge mechanism (11), screw-containing mechanism (53), belt and chain drives (38), mechanisms containing a pressurising medium (46) and step mechanism (intermittent motion, 99). A mechanism can sit in more than one group. There are also 8 application areas (production, living/leisure, transportation, agriculture, medical technology, academic, several, other) and a function facet (function mechanism, guidance mechanism, summation mechanism, differential mechanism, adjustable mechanism, locking mechanism). The Artobolevskij entries also carry his own group names (e.g. 'Clutch and coupling mechanisms', 'Indexing mechanisms'). A separate page lists the Voigt catalogue of Reuleaux models (classes A-Z, e.g. N 'Sperr- und Schaltwerke' = ratchets and indexing, O planetary gear trains, P articulated couplings, X clock escapements, Z couplings)
- **example_types**: Safety friction clutch (torque-limiting clutch); Ball-type overrunning clutch (freewheel); Ratchet clutch with face engagement; Lever-type stop with a reversible pawl (reversible ratchet); Ball-click index / Spring-type indexing device (detent); Ratchet-type stop with a flexible link; Four-bar locking lever mechanism; Lever and spring latch; Lever-type eccentric-cam clamp (cam lock); Toggle linkage (over-centre); self-locking gear; Malteserkreuzgetriebe (Geneva drive, internal and external); Harmonic Drive / Wellgetriebe (strain-wave gear); Planetengetriebe, Cyclo- (cycloidal drive); Quaife limited slip differential; Differential bevel gears with limited automatic locking; Oldham coupling (Oldham-Kupplung); Double Hooke's joint (double universal joint); Scotch yoke mechanism; Torpedo-Freilaufnabe mit Rucktrittbremse (coaster-brake freewheel hub); Cone-wedge coupling; Worm gear; Solenoid
- **making_methods**: General mechanical engineering, machine design and the history of machines. There is nothing on 3D printing, CNC, woodwork or maker processes
- **item_count**: 3,114 mechanism descriptions; 2,818 interactive animations; 3,465 videos; 1,905 CAx files; 45,829 images; 36,078 documents (31,862 in full text); 1,784 thesaurus concepts (counts shown on the portal, September 2026)
- **special_behaviours_covered**: torque-limiting: yes (e.g. 'Safety friction clutch', 'Lever-type friction clutch'); self-locking: yes ('self-locking gear', wedge-type stops and clamps); hand-release only: partly (locking levers, spring latches, lever-type locks); one-way/ratchet: yes, many (ratchet clutches, overrunning clutches, pawl stops, freewheel hub); indexing/detent: yes (ball-click indexes, spring-type indexing devices, Geneva and step mechanisms); over-centre: yes ('Toggle linkage', toggle clamps); compliant: a few (ratchet stops with flexible or elastic links, spring links) but no compliant-mechanism section; print-in-place: no; gear trains/gearboxes: yes, strongly (364 gear-train entries including planetary, harmonic, cycloidal, worm and differentials); powered connectors: almost none (a 'Solenoid' and a 'Magnetic stirrer' entry, and no electromagnetic latches); adhesive types: no
- **entry_structure**: Name (often in 2-3 languages); a thumbnail image or drawing; a short function description; a structured data table (planar/spatial, number of links, drive and output motion, degrees of freedom, fundamental mechanism type, number of inputs and outputs, whether it can fully rotate, relative position of the shafts, transfer function such as 'dwell' or 'oscillating'); application area; the collection it belongs to; where the physical model is held, who made it, when, materials and size; links to videos, interactive animations and images; permanent links to DMG-Lib and Europeana. The Artobolevskij entries are shorter: a name, a line drawing, the collection group and a note of its number in Artobolevskij's book. There are no design rules and no how-to
- **audience_level**: undergraduate / professional / research (lecturers, students, design engineers and historians, as the project page says)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low. It is heavy on jargon and multilingual (many titles only in German), the filters use academic mechanism-theory terms, and there are no plain-English explanations. Its saving grace is the large number of videos and interactive animations, which show the motion without needing words
- **has_images**: yes. 45,829 images: photos of physical teaching models (acrylic display-case models, 19th-century metal and wood Reuleaux models), line drawings from Artobolevskij, and scanned book figures. Quality ranges from good model photos to small, low-resolution thumbnails and scans
- **has_animations**: yes. 2,818 interactive animations play in an in-browser HTML5 'AIS5' player (drag with the mouse to step through the motion), many of them made from figures in historic books. There are also 3,465 short videos of physical models (MP4/WebM, often very small, e.g. 240x344 pixels) and WebGL, VRML and Cinderella interactive models
- **gaps**: No plain-English explanations or 'what is this good for' guidance; many entries are German-only; no design rules (clearances, torque, strain) and no 3D-printing or maker advice; no downloadable STL/STEP files for making parts; almost nothing on powered connectors, snap fits, compliant or print-in-place joints, everyday quick-release hardware or adhesives; duplicate entries under different language names; a dated JSP-based interface with results pages that need a browser session; and a strict reuse licence
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes. Every mechanism, animation, video and image has a permanent link in the form handler?mcdsc=<id> (and manim=, video=, image=), which redirects to the viewer page, e.g. https://www.dmg-lib.org/dmglib/handler?mcdsc=3847025 (Safety friction clutch) or https://www.dmg-lib.org/dmglib/handler?mcdsc=1899025 (Wellgetriebe / Harmonic Drive). Linking by type works best for the English Artobolevskij entries
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Faceted 'mechanism search' by input motion -> output motion (rotation, straight-line, oscillating, screw), degrees of freedom and shaft arrangement (parallel, coaxial, crossing): a good model for the flow chart's 'what motion do you have / need' questions, reworded in plain English. (2) A function facet that includes 'locking mechanism' and 'adjustable mechanism', matching the project's special behaviours. (3) One permanent ID per type, with every media file (video, animation, CAD, images) cross-linked to it. (4) A multilingual thesaurus of terms with aliases, i.e. a proper-name-plus-aliases field. (5) Scrub-through animations (drag to move the mechanism) as a very beginner-friendly way to show how a type moves. (6) Grouping entries by Reuleaux's fundamental types (link, cam, gear, friction, wedge, screw, belt/chain, fluid, step) as a secondary 'family' axis
- **design_rules_given**: No. The mechanism entries are descriptive (structure and motion data only). Some of the hosted full-text literature (gear catalogues, textbooks) may contain design numbers, but they are not pulled out into the entries
- **generator_or_parametric**: No geometry generator. It lists 28 mechanism software tools (a catalogue of external special and universal mechanism software, CAD add-ons and so on) but does not generate custom parts
- **file_formats**: Images (JPG/PNG); videos (original AVI plus MP4 H.264 and WebM); interactive animations (AIS files played in an HTML5 player); CAx files including WebGL, VRML and Cinderella (interactive geometry) models; scanned books and papers (full-text page viewer). No STL or STEP files were seen
- **terminology_value**: high for German and multilingual terms: a 1,784-concept multilingual thesaurus of mechanism and machine science, and Artobolevskij entries giving English, French and Russian names side by side (e.g. 'Ball-click stop' = 'Verrouillage par bille'). Good for finding the proper German/English name and translations of a mechanism type, but not for plain-English beginner names
- **decision_support**: A faceted 'Mechanism search' form (not a flow chart): pick any combination of function (including locking and adjustable), dimension, number of links, input motion, output motion, degrees of freedom, relative position of input and output, fundamental type, number of inputs and outputs, and revolvability, and it returns matching mechanisms. There is also an 'interactive mechanism search'. There are no comparison tables and no selection advice
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Built from university teaching-model collections (Dresden, Aachen, Chemnitz, Ilmenau, Hannover, IFMA Clermont-Ferrand), Artobolevskij's 'Mechanisms in Modern Engineering Design' (over 4,700 mechanisms), Voigt's catalogue of Reuleaux models, and digitised books (e.g. Volmer's 'Getriebetechnik: Grundlagen'). It overlaps KMODDL (Cornell's Reuleaux models, which DMG-Lib links to on its 'Mechanisms worldwide' page) and 507/1800 Mechanical Movements-style collections. The thinkMOTION content is republished on Europeana. To cite an original, cite Artobolevskij or the model collection rather than DMG-Lib where possible

  - https://www.dmg-lib.org/dmglib/handler?video=6349053 (video page with MP4/WebM)
  - https://www.dmg-lib.org/dmglib/main/portal.jsp?mainNaviState=browsen.mecdesc&collId=1099&browDisplayMode=list&results=100&shortDesc=true&page=1 (A-Z list of all 3,114 mechanisms; pages 1-33 were read to find example types)
  - https://www.eleed.de/archive/8/3334 (Brix, Doring, Reessing 2012, 'DMG-Lib - The Digital Mechanism and Gear Library', eleed issue 8)
- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - image_license
  - maintained
  - reusable_data
  - year

## 2 KMODDL (Cornell Kinematic Models for Design Digital Library)

*Encyclopedia / digital library.* Photos, videos and simulations of Cornell's Reuleaux kinematic model collection, plus digitised historic texts

### Basics

- **name**: KMODDL (Kinematic Models for Design Digital Library), Cornell University Library
- **url**: https://engineering.library.cornell.edu/kmoddl/ (current landing page; the old address https://kmoddl.library.cornell.edu/ redirects here). It points to three places: the image and GIF collection on Cornell University Library Digital Collections (https://digital.library.cornell.edu/collections/kmoddl, which is bot-protected and could not be opened from here), the historic texts on eCommons (https://ecommons.cornell.edu/handle/1813/57656), and the original 2003 site archived on Archive-It
- **resource_type**: digital library (photos, animated GIFs, videos and simulations of Cornell's Reuleaux kinematic model collection, plus digitised historic books, now spread across library platforms)
- **cost**: Free
- **language**: English (the site and descriptions). Some digitised historic texts are in German, French or Latin (e.g. Voigt's 1907 'Kinematische Modelle nach Prof Reuleaux', Huygens' 'Horologium oscillatorium')
- **access_barrier**: none (open access; no login). Note: Cornell's Digital Collections site uses a browser bot-check, which normal browsers pass
- **authority_level**: academic (Cornell University Library with Cornell Mechanical and Aerospace Engineering and Mathematics faculty, e.g. Francis C. Moon and Hod Lipson; the collection is an ASME Mechanical Engineering Heritage Collection. Descriptions are curated rather than peer-reviewed)

### Coverage

- **covers**: Cornell's Reuleaux collection of 19th-century teaching models of machine elements (about 220 Voigt-catalogue Reuleaux models; ASME counts 230 Reuleaux models among about 300 artifacts), plus about a dozen Schroeder models and a Willis model. The models cover lower and higher pairs, crank and slider linkages, gear teeth, planetary gear trains, ratchets and indexing (step) mechanisms, clock escapements, universal joints and couplings, belt drives and belt shifters, friction wheels, reversing gears, rotary pumps (capsular mechanisms), straight-line and parallel-motion linkages, cams and curves of constant width (the Reuleaux triangle). eCommons holds 58 reference items: digitised historic machine books ('theatres of machines' such as Bockler, Ramelli and Leupold), Voigt's catalogue of Reuleaux models, Reuleaux biographies and papers about the project. The 2005 descriptions also list tutorials for middle-school to undergraduate level, mathematical simulations and STL files for 3D-printing working replicas
- **scope_match**: medium. It is entirely about mechanism TYPES, with good in-scope families (ratchets and pawls, Geneva/indexing, escapements, planetary trains, universal joints, couplings and clutches, belt drives). But it is limited to about 220 historic teaching models, and has nothing on snap fits, compliant or print-in-place joints, everyday quick-release hardware, powered connectors or adhesives
- **making_methods**: General engineering education and the history of machines. There is also an early 3D-printing (rapid prototyping) strand: Lipson and Moon printed working, pre-assembled replicas of Reuleaux models on a Stratasys FDM machine in ABS with soluble support, and the STL files were offered for download
- **special_behaviours_covered**: torque-limiting: only indirectly (friction clutches and couplings in the Y and Z classes, not safety slip clutches); self-locking: partly (screw mechanisms, clamping/friction ratchets); hand-release only: no explicit coverage; one-way/ratchet: yes (the N class: tooth ratchets, silent ratchets, clamping ratchets, centrifugal ratchets); indexing/detent: yes (Geneva and star-wheel indexing, escapements); over-centre: no explicit coverage; compliant: no; print-in-place: indirectly (the project printed pre-assembled working replicas in one piece using soluble support, an early form of print-in-place); gear trains/gearboxes: yes (compound, planetary and reversing gear trains, gear-tooth forms, worm); powered connectors: no; adhesive types: no
- **audience_level**: school (middle and high school tutorials) / undergraduate / research and history of technology; not aimed at makers

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium. The clear photos and moving GIFs of real brass-and-iron models are very approachable, and tutorials were written for school level. But titles use 19th-century engineering names, there is no plain-English 'what is it for' grouping, and the original tutorials and simulations only survive on a partly broken archived site
- **has_images**: yes. High-quality studio photographs of every catalogued model (some by professional photographer Jon Reis), shot from several angles, and scans of historic book pages
- **gaps**: Only about 220 historic teaching models, so many modern and maker-relevant types are missing (snap fits, living hinges, compliant and print-in-place joints, heat-set inserts, quick-release pins, cam buckles, torque limiters, electromagnetic latches, adhesives); no design rules or dimensions; no plain-English grouping; much of the original interactive content (tutorials, simulations, STL downloads) is now only on a partly broken archive; content is split across three platforms, and the Digital Collections portal blocks automated access
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Use the Reuleaux/Voigt class letters as an optional 'family' index next to plain-English names. (2) Pair each mechanism type with a short looping animated GIF: cheap, works everywhere, and very beginner-friendly. (3) Offer downloadable 3D-printable replicas (STL) of classic mechanisms, printed pre-assembled, so makers can hold and try each type. The Lipson/Moon paper shows ratchets, escapements, slider-cranks and straight-line linkages printed as working assemblies. (4) Link each type to the historic book page where it first appears, to give provenance. (5) Write tutorials at several levels (middle school, high school, undergraduate) for the same model
- **generator_or_parametric**: No generator. Fixed STL files of a few CAD-modelled Reuleaux models were made for download (not parametric)
- **terminology_value**: medium. It is authoritative for classic English and 19th-century German names of mechanism types (via Voigt/Reuleaux: e.g. 'Kreuzkupplung von Oldham' = Oldham coupling, 'Klemmsperrung' = clamping ratchet), and useful for historical aliases. It does not cover modern or maker vocabulary
- **decision_support**: None. There is no selector, flow chart or comparison. It offers only faceted search and browse in Digital Collections and a canned search for animated GIFs

### Other Info

- **sources**: 
  - https://engineering.library.cornell.edu/kmoddl/ (current landing page; redirect target of https://kmoddl.library.cornell.edu/)
  - https://engineering.library.cornell.edu/database/kmoddl-kinematic-models-for-digital-design-library/ (library database listing)
  - https://physicalsciences.library.cornell.edu/database/kmoddl-kinematic-models-for-design-digital-library/ (description: more than 200 Reuleaux models)
  - https://ecommons.cornell.edu/handle/1813/57656 (eCommons KMODDL References collection page)
  - https://ecommons.cornell.edu/server/api/pid/find?id=1813/57656 (DSpace API: collection description, 58 items)
  - https://ecommons.cornell.edu/server/api/pid/find?id=1813/58764 (Voigt 1907, 'Kinematische Modelle nach Prof Reuleaux', item metadata)
  - https://ecommons.cornell.edu/bitstreams/05908461-a661-4fec-83b6-fa577c68d88e/download (Lipson, Moon, Hai, Paventi, '3D-Printing the History of Mechanisms': 220 Voigt models, STL downloads, FDM replicas)
  - https://www.dlib.org/dlib/july05/07featured-collection.html (D-Lib Magazine July 2005 featured collection: contents, NSF/NSDL and IMLS grants, OAI-PMH, STL files)
  - https://www.asme.org/wwwasmeorg/media/resourcefiles/aboutasme/who%20we%20are/engineering%20history/landmarks/232-reuleaux-collection-of-kinematic-mechanisms-at-cornell-university.pdf (ASME landmark brochure: 230 Reuleaux models, about 300 artifacts, KMODDL contents)
  - https://ethw.org/ASME-Landmark:Reuleaux_Collection_of_Kinematic_Mechanisms_at_Cornell_University (ASME landmark summary)
  - https://www.dmg-lib.org/dmglib/main/portal.jsp?mainNaviState=search.reulmodls (Voigt catalogue class list A-Z used for taxonomy and example types)
  - https://www.dmg-lib.org/dmglib/main/portal.jsp?mainNaviState=browsen.mecworldwide (DMG-Lib's link and description of KMODDL)
  - https://digitalibra.omeka.net/items/show/42 (third-party catalogue record of KMODDL)

## 3 507 Mechanical Movements (Henry T. Brown) and 507movements.com

*Encyclopedia / digital library.* 1868 illustrated catalogue of 507 numbered movements (public domain) and the animated website built from it

### Basics

- **name**: Five Hundred and Seven Mechanical Movements (Henry T. Brown, 1868) and its animated online edition 507movements.com (Matt Keveney)
- **url**: https://507movements.com/
- **resource_type**: book (public-domain 1868 illustrated catalogue, with free scans on the Internet Archive) plus a digital library / interactive website (507movements.com) that re-presents every numbered movement with the original engraving and text, and adds animations for some of them
- **license**: Book: public domain. The Internet Archive marks its 1881 (Brown & Brown), 1886 (Brown & Hall) and 1908 (Brown & Seward) scans 'NOT_IN_COPYRIGHT', and its 1903 Library of Congress scan 'The Library of Congress is unaware of any copyright restrictions'. So the engravings and descriptions can be freely copied, linked and reused. Website: the about page says the static images and text come straight from the book, 'which is long out of copyright. You may freely use them', but asks people not to copy or mirror the whole site and to credit 507movements.com if they use an image. The owners claim copyright on the site layout and on the animations: the animations 'may not be reproduced without permission', including conversion to other formats, and permission will not be given to put them on other websites. In practice: link to the animation pages, and reuse images from the public-domain scans rather than from the site
- **language**: English (19th-century technical English)
- **year**: Book first published 1868. Many later editions; the Internet Archive holds 1881, 1886, 1903 and 1908 printings, and the website reproduces the 21st edition of 1908. Website launched around 2013 (Boing Boing wrote about it 'in 2013, when the site was new', and again on 27 Aug 2026). Server 'last-modified' date on the movement pages is 25 Nov 2025, and the site was online on 2026-09-25
- **access_barrier**: none (website and Internet Archive scans are open; the animations need a modern browser with HTML5 Canvas and JavaScript)
- **image_license**: Original engravings: public domain (scanned and retouched by the site owner, but the underlying images are out of copyright). Animations on 507movements.com: all rights reserved by the site owners, no reproduction or conversion without permission, and none granted for other websites. Internet Archive scans: public domain
- **authority_level**: Historic trade reference: Henry T. Brown's compilation for mechanics and inventors (not peer-reviewed). The website is a one-person enthusiast project (Matt Keveney, who also runs Animated Engines per Boing Boing) that faithfully reproduces the book; its animations are its own interpretation

### Coverage

- **covers**: 507 numbered movements (small mechanisms and machine elements) from 1860s engineering: belt and pulley drives, gearing of every kind (spur, bevel, crown, worm, elliptical, internal, mutilated/intermittent, epicyclic trains), cranks and substitutes for the crank, cams and eccentrics, ratchets and pawls, clutches, couplings and joints (universal, ball-and-socket, bayonet, union coupling), escapements and clock parts, governors, parallel motions and straight-line linkages, drawing instruments (ellipsograph, pantograph), levers and toggles, pumps, water wheels, steam-engine valve gear and some hydraulic/pneumatic devices. The website has 505 pages (some pages show two numbers, e.g. '40 and 41', '50 and 51'; pages 262 and 263 returned 404 on 2026-09-25), of which about 150 carry an animation
- **organisation**: Numbered 1 to 507 in the book's order, which loosely groups by type (pulleys and belts first, then gearing, then cranks/cams, ratchets, escapements, steam engines, hydraulics, epicyclic trains last) but has no headings. Access is by an alphabetical index of 19th-century names (e.g. 'Crank, substitutes for the', 'Gearing, mutilated', 'Stops for winding watches') that points to movement numbers, plus thumbnail pages to browse (colour thumbnails mark the animated ones) and prev/next links. There is no search and no grouping by what the movement does. For a beginner who doesn't know the old terms, it is hard to find a specific solution; it works best for browsing and inspiration
- **scope_match**: high: nearly every entry is a mechanism or connection TYPE (not a hardware catalogue), and many are the clever one-unit mechanisms the project wants (Geneva stop, bayonet joint, ratchets, clutches, toggle joint, epicyclic trains, differential screw). Minus points: old terminology, lots of steam/mill machinery, and no modern types (compliant, print-in-place, powered, adhesives)
- **taxonomy_used**: No formal taxonomy in the book: movements are simply numbered, in a rough order by motion conversion and machine element. The 1868 subtitle lists its fields: dynamics, hydraulics, hydrostatics, pneumatics, steam engines, mill and other gearing, presses, horology and miscellaneous machinery. The alphabetical index uses about 150 name headings (with sub-entries such as 'Gearing: bevel, crown, intermittent, mutilated, worm ...'). Descriptions often use a motion-conversion phrasing, e.g. 'Rotary converted into rotary motion', 'reciprocating rectilinear motion from uniform rotary motion'
- **example_types**: Bayonet joint (twist-lock, no. 245); Geneva stop for watch winding (no. 212, animated); stops for ratchet wheels and for spur gears (nos. 239, 240); ratchet and pawl, including double-pawl near-continuous ratchets (nos. 206, 225, 236, 271); stud/dog clutch 'clutch-box' (no. 52); friction cone clutch (no. 47); universal joints (nos. 50-51); ball-and-socket joint (no. 249); union coupling for pipes (no. 248); toggle joint for a punching press (over-centre toggle, no. 140, animated); lazy tongs (scissor linkage, no. 144, animated); double reversed screw / diamond-thread traverse (no. 108); differential screw (no. 266); two-speed differential pulley drive (no. 61); epicyclic (planetary) trains and the 'mechanical paradox' (nos. 502-507); worm gearing; mutilated (part-toothed) gears for intermittent motion; mangle wheel and mangle rack (nos. 192-199); centrifugal check-hooks, a fall-arrest safety catch for mine hoists (no. 253); Otis's safety stop for a hoist platform (no. 278); detachable/adjustable chain link (turnbuckle-style, no. 399); fusee (constant-torque cone for clocks, no. 46); centrifugal governor (no. 161)
- **making_methods**: General engineering of the 1860s (cast iron, forging, millwork, clock-making). Nothing specific to 3D printing, CNC or robotics, though many movements are easy to 3D print or laser-cut
- **item_count**: 507 numbered movements (505 web pages, about 150 animated as of 2026-09-25)
- **special_behaviours_covered**: one-way/ratchet: yes, many ratchet-and-pawl variants and ratchet stops. Indexing/detent: yes, Geneva stop, stops for spur gears and ratchet wheels, intermittent (mutilated) gearing. Over-centre: yes, toggle joint (no. 140) and toggle presses. Self-locking: partly, via safety catches (centrifugal check-hooks no. 253, Otis safety stop no. 278) and screw mechanisms. Torque-limiting: only indirectly (friction clutches and friction gearing that can slip; no explicit slip-clutch/torque limiter). Hand-release only: bayonet joint (twist to release). Gear trains/gearboxes: strong, including epicyclic trains, differential gearing, worm, bevel, elliptical and intermittent gears. Compliant: essentially none (springs appear only as parts). Print-in-place: none. Powered connectors (electromagnets, solenoids): none. Adhesive types: none
- **entry_structure**: Movement number (sometimes two numbers per page), the original engraving (a line drawing with letter labels), a short 1-5 sentence description from the book, and on about 150 pages an HTML5 Canvas animation shown next to the original. Occasional webmaster notes. No degrees of freedom, dimensions, design rules or CAD files
- **audience_level**: hobbyist to professional (written in 1868 for mechanics, inventors and patent draughtsmen); today mostly used by makers, students and designers for inspiration

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: the pictures and animations are very easy to follow and each description is short, but the text uses 19th-century terms ('mutilated gear', 'clutch-box', 'rectilinear motion'), there is no explanation of when to use each movement, and finding something needs the old-fashioned index
- **has_images**: yes: one detailed engraving per movement, individually scanned and retouched on the website (clean, high-contrast line art). The Internet Archive scans show the full book pages
- **has_animations**: yes, on the website only: about 150 of the 505 pages (checked on 2026-09-25 by looking for the animation script) have a smooth 2D animation drawn in the browser with a custom JavaScript/HTML5 Canvas library. The book itself has none
- **gaps**: Only about 30% of movements are animated; the rest are static engravings. Old names with no modern synonyms (e.g. no mention of 'dog clutch', 'Geneva drive' is 'Geneva-stop', no 'freewheel'). No grouping by what problem it solves, no search, no filters. No sizes, tolerances, materials or making advice, and nothing on 3D printing. Missing modern families: compliant mechanisms, snap fits, print-in-place joints, torque limiters, powered latches, magnets and adhesives. Much of the content is steam engines, pumps and mill machinery that is out of scope
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes: every movement has a stable, predictable page by number, e.g. https://507movements.com/mm_245.html (bayonet joint) or https://507movements.com/mm_212.html (Geneva stop, animated). Page titles take the form '507 Mechanical Movements, 245'. Note that combined pages (e.g. mm_050.html covers 50 and 51) and missing pages (262, 263) need checking before linking
- **reusable_data**: scrape-only for the website (no API or dataset). The list of 507 numbered movements, their book descriptions and the engravings are public domain and can be reused freely (best taken from the Internet Archive scans, which also offer OCR text). The site's animations and layout must not be copied or converted; its alphabetical index page (https://507movements.com/toc.html) is a convenient map from names to numbers
- **ideas_to_borrow**: Put the original static drawing side by side with a simple looping animation ('Animated' vs 'Original'). One short page per mechanism at a stable numbered URL with prev/next browsing. Colour thumbnails vs grey thumbnails as a quick visual marker of which entries have animation, a trick that could mark entries with a video, CAD file or print-tested model. Short, one-idea descriptions that say what motion goes in and what comes out ('rotary converted into reciprocating'), which suits an input/output step in the flow chart. An alphabetical index that lists several numbered variants under one family name (e.g. 'Ratchets and pawls: 49, 75, 76 ...')
- **design_rules_given**: no: no clearances, fits, strengths or torques; descriptions only explain how the motion works
- **generator_or_parametric**: no (the animation library is proprietary and not released; no CAD or parametric models)
- **file_formats**: Website: HTML pages, PNG images of the engravings, HTML5 Canvas animations (JavaScript, not downloadable). Internet Archive scans: PDF, EPUB, full-text OCR and page images
- **terminology_value**: medium: authoritative for the historic names of mechanisms (useful as aliases and for citing where a type first appears, e.g. 'Geneva-stop', 'mangle wheel', 'lazy tongs', 'toggle-joint', 'mutilated gear'), but not for modern standard terms, and there are no translations
- **decision_support**: none: no selector, flow chart, filters or comparison tables. The only aids are the numbered order, the alphabetical index of names and the thumbnail pages

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - cost
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - maintained

## 4 Nguyen Duc Thang animated mechanisms (thang010146)

*Encyclopedia / digital library.* YouTube channel and PDF books of 2,000+ CAD-animated mechanisms, indexed by DT Online's "2100 Animated Mechanical Mechanisms" page

### Basics

- **name**: Nguyen Duc Thang's animated mechanical mechanisms (YouTube channel 'thang010146' and the '4000 Animated Mechanical Mechanisms' PDF index), as indexed by DT Online's '2100 Animated Mechanical Mechanisms' page
- **url**: https://www.youtube.com/user/thang010146/about (channel, now at https://www.youtube.com/@thang010146); DT Online index page: https://wiki.dtonline.org/index.php/2100_Animated_Mechanical_Mechanisms; PDF list download: https://www.mediafire.com/file/7ffjovppxludixd/4000AMM.zip/file
- **resource_type**: video channel plus a free PDF book/index (4 PDF parts listing every video with a picture, short explanation and YouTube link); DT Online's page is a short wiki pointer to it
- **cost**: Free (YouTube videos; the zipped PDF index is a free 41.66 MB download from MediaFire)
- **language**: English (Vietnamese author; plain but slightly non-native English, short explanations)
- **year**: Videos published from 2010 to 2025 according to the PDF foreword (channel joined 6 Oct 2010). Coverage grew over time: about 2,100 animations in 2016 (Hackaday, DT Online), a '2700' PDF edition renewed 31 Oct 2017, and the latest '4000 Animated Mechanical Mechanisms' PDF edition dated July 2025 (uploaded to MediaFire 30 Jul 2025). The channel showed 4,093 videos on 2026-09-25, with a video uploaded about 5 days earlier
- **access_barrier**: none (YouTube is open; the PDF zip is downloaded from MediaFire, which shows ads)
- **authority_level**: educator / individual expert: a retired mechanical engineer (engineering degree 1969, Hanoi University of Technology; Doctor of Engineering 1984, Kosice University of Technology, Slovakia; designer at small mechanical enterprises in Hanoi until 2002). Not peer-reviewed; many entries are his own reconstructions of mechanisms from classic books (the PDFs cite, for example, Hiscox and Chironis) or of real products

### Coverage

- **covers**: Around 4,000 short CAD animations (the July 2025 PDF list is titled '4000 Animated Mechanical Mechanisms'; about 4,570 distinct YouTube links appear in the four PDFs because some entries link to extra versions or 'see a real mechanism' videos). Covers couplings, clutches (overrunning, sprag, roller, safety/slip clutches), every kind of gear and gearbox (planetary, worm, differential, variators/CVTs, gear shifting), friction, chain, belt and cable drives, linkages, cams, Geneva and ratchet drives, screw mechanisms, brakes, indexing, positioning and locking mechanisms, toggle linkages, snap-action mechanisms, straight-line and curve-drawing linkages, spherical and parallel mechanisms, hinges, doors, windows, gates, folding and scissor mechanisms, clamps and grippers, and machines for many industries (machine tools, conveyors, pumps and engines, vehicles, walking machines, furniture, toys, kinetic art)
- **organisation**: By function and motion conversion, in a numbered hierarchy of 39 chapters across 4 PDF parts, each with sub-sections and a table of contents with page numbers, e.g. 'Part 1 Transmission of continuous rotation: 1.1 Permanent couplings, 1.2 Clutches, 1.3 Gears ... 1.13 Variators'; 'Part 3 Mechanisms of specific purposes: 13 Brakes, 14 Safety clutches, 15 Mechanisms for indexing, positioning and locking, 16 Toggle linkages, 17 Mechanisms for snap motions ...'. Inside each section, entries are numbered variants ('Safety clutch 1', 'Safety clutch 2' ...). This works well for someone who knows what they want the mechanism to DO (e.g. 'locking', 'turn rotation into linear motion'), better than an alphabetical list. The YouTube channel itself is only searchable by title keywords, which DT Online warns 'is likely to reveal hundreds of variations'
- **scope_match**: high: almost every entry is a mechanism or connection TYPE, including many of the project's special behaviours (safety clutches, overrunning clutches, self-locking latches, snap mechanisms, indexing and locking, gearboxes). It goes well beyond scope in places (whole machines for industries, toys, vehicles), which would need filtering
- **taxonomy_used**: Functional / motion-conversion taxonomy with 4 top-level parts (1 Transmission of continuous rotation; 2 Other kinds of motion transmission; 3 Mechanisms of specific purposes; 4 Mechanisms for various industries) and 39 numbered chapters. Parts 1-2 sort by input and output motion (continuous rotation to interrupted rotation, to rotary oscillation, to linear motion, reciprocating to rotation, complicated motions) and then by element type (bars/linkages, gears, cams, belts and cables, chains, screws, friction drives, combined mechanisms). Part 3 sorts by purpose (brakes, safety clutches, indexing/positioning/locking, toggles, snap motions, vibration, drawing lines, copying, math operations, position control by degrees of freedom, folding, opening and closing, lifting). Part 4 sorts by industry. Chapter 11 'Study of mechanisms' also covers mechanical joints, planar and spatial mechanisms and springs
- **example_types**: Safety clutch / slip clutch (torque limiter) variants such as spring-loaded cone clutch and friction-disc clutch (Part 3, ch. 14; e.g. 'Safety clutch 3'); sprag overrunning clutch; roller overrunning clutch; ball overrunning clutch; ratchet one-way overrunning clutch; ratchet irreversible lock (back-driving stopper); self-locking gate latch; cam-guided latch; gravity-loaded latch; snap motion (snap-action) mechanisms; toggle linkages (over-centre); indexing mechanisms; Geneva mechanisms (57 entries); Oldham coupling; Schmidt coupling; universal joints; coil spring coupling; worm gear clutch; planetary reduction gear; chain harmonic drive; car differentials; variators (continuously variable transmissions); scissor mechanisms; tensegrity structures; quick release bearing; interlocking shafts; hinges (butt hinge, rising butt hinge and others)
- **making_methods**: General mechanical engineering and machine design (all modelled in Autodesk Inventor). Not aimed at 3D printing, CNC or woodwork specifically, although a few entries mention uses such as 3D printers (tripteron parallel mechanism)
- **item_count**: about 4,000 mechanisms (4,093 videos on the channel on 2026-09-25; 4,000 listed in the July 2025 PDF index; earlier editions listed about 2,100 in 2016 and 2,700 in 2017)
- **special_behaviours_covered**: Torque-limiting: yes, a whole chapter 'Safety clutches' (Part 3, ch. 14, about 20 'Safety clutch' entries: friction cone, friction disc, wedge and ball types). One-way/ratchet: yes, many overrunning clutches (sprag, roller, ball, ratchet, spring) and ratchet drives (Part 2, 2.3). Self-locking: yes, self-locking gate latches, ratchet irreversible lock, worm drives, locking mechanisms (Part 3, 15.3). Indexing/detent: yes, 'Mechanisms for indexing, positioning and locking' (angular and linear) and 57 Geneva entries; no entries titled 'detent' were found. Over-centre: yes, 'Toggle linkages' chapter and 'Mechanisms for snap motions'. Hand-release only: partly, through latches and locking mechanisms, but not labelled as such. Compliant: very little; the author states that mechanisms with 'flexible and fluid links' may be missing, although springs and a few flexible conveyors appear. Print-in-place: none. Gear trains/gearboxes: strong (Part 1 gears, planetary, worm, differentials, variators, gear shifting). Powered connectors: essentially none (one magnet-pad climbing robot; no solenoid or electromagnet latches). Adhesive types: none
- **entry_structure**: Title (often a numbered variant, e.g. 'Sprag overrunning clutch 1'), a still image from the animation, the YouTube link, and a short plain-text explanation (1-10 lines) that names the parts by colour ('the pink cam', 'the blue shaft'), says what is input and output and how it works, sometimes with a key design factor, a formula (e.g. taper set-over S = L·sinα), a source reference (e.g. Hiscox 1921 number) or a link to a video of the real device. The video itself is a short (often under a minute) coloured CAD animation, usually without narration
- **audience_level**: hobbyist to professional (the author writes it is for people who 'work with mechanical mechanisms on a daily basis or consider mechanical mechanisms as a hobby')

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: the colour-coded animations are very easy to understand without words, and explanations are short, but titles use engineering terms without definitions, variants are only numbered (not compared), and there is no guidance on which one to pick. Finding things on YouTube alone is hard; the PDF contents pages help a lot
- **has_images**: yes: every PDF entry has a clear coloured CAD still image; videos have thumbnails
- **has_animations**: yes: about 4,000 YouTube videos of colour-coded Autodesk Inventor animations (3D, rotating views, parts in distinct colours, often with a slider showing e.g. an overload condition). Some entries also link to videos of real mechanisms
- **gaps**: No CAD files to download, no sizes, tolerances, materials or 3D-printing advice. Variants are numbered but not compared, and there is no 'which one should I use' guidance. Almost nothing on compliant mechanisms, snap fits, print-in-place joints, magnets, powered latches or adhesives. Terminology is mostly English engineering names, with some awkward phrasing ('tooth-uncompleted gears' for intermittent/mutilated gears). The index lives in PDFs downloaded from a file-sharing site, not on a web page, so it cannot be searched online. Much of the content is whole machines or industrial equipment, outside the project's scope. The author wrote in July 2025 that he could not create more videos because of his health, so long-term availability depends on the YouTube channel
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes: each mechanism is its own YouTube video with a stable URL, e.g. https://youtu.be/b6uouA9Pqzo ('Safety clutch 3'), https://youtu.be/0gwzmLh03Bw ('Sprag overrunning clutch 1'), https://youtu.be/KAD9Xmh1tF4 ('Self-locking gate latch 1'). Video titles are generic and numbered, so the entry should quote the title. The PDFs themselves have no per-item web URLs
- **ideas_to_borrow**: Classify by what the mechanism DOES (input motion to output motion, or purpose such as 'locking', 'safety clutch', 'snap motion') rather than alphabetically, which maps well onto flow-chart questions. Group many numbered variants under one family so readers can compare solutions side by side. Colour-code parts in pictures and refer to them by colour in the text ('the pink cam locks the gate'), which is very beginner-friendly. State input and output explicitly for each entry. Show a failure or special state visually (e.g. a pink slider showing the overload position of a safety clutch). Link each idealised animation to a video of the real device. Split the collection into a small number of big parts (transmission, other motion, special purposes, industries)
- **design_rules_given**: Rarely: occasional formulas and key factors (e.g. taper turning set-over S = L·sinα; 'large gaps between the rings and midrib' as the key factor of a self-locking gate latch), but no systematic clearances, fits, strengths or torque values
- **generator_or_parametric**: no (Autodesk Inventor models are not shared; no generators or parametric files)
- **file_formats**: YouTube video (MP4 streaming); PDF (4 parts in a ZIP, 41.66 MB, 974 pages in total for the July 2025 edition). No CAD, STL or STEP downloads
- **terminology_value**: medium: useful English names and families for a very wide range of mechanisms (e.g. 'sprag overrunning clutch', 'Schmidt coupling', 'safety clutch', 'toggle linkage'), and many titles match classic textbook names, but some names are the author's own descriptive phrases and there are no aliases or translations
- **decision_support**: No selector or filters. The only decision aid is the hierarchical function-based table of contents in the PDFs (by motion conversion and by purpose), which lets a reader narrow down to a family and then compare numbered variants by watching them
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Original CAD animations by Nguyen Duc Thang, but many are re-creations of mechanisms from classic sources that the PDFs cite, including Hiscox's '1800 Mechanical Movements, Devices and Appliances' (1921), Chironis (and at least one '507 Mechanical Movements' reference), so there is heavy overlap with Brown's 507 Mechanical Movements, Hiscox, DMG-Lib and KMODDL. DT Online's '2100 Animated Mechanical Mechanisms' page is only a pointer to the channel and PDFs. Hackaday (29 Feb 2016) also featured the collection

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - image_license
  - license
  - maintained
  - reusable_data

## 5 BYU Compliant Mechanisms Research (CMR)

*Encyclopedia / digital library.* BYU research group site with a compliant mechanism library, flexure elements, lamina-emergent and origami-inspired mechanisms

### Basics

- **name**: BYU Compliant Mechanisms and Robotics Group (CMR), Brigham Young University
- **url**: https://www.compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/
- **resource_type**: Research group website (digital library of explainers, videos and free 3D-printable model files), backed by a book: the Handbook of Compliant Mechanisms (Wiley, 2013), whose Part Four is the 'Library of Compliant Mechanisms'
- **language**: English
- **year**: Group active since 1994 ('Bending the rules of kinematics since 1994'); Handbook of Compliant Mechanisms first published 2013; current website (c)2026, with 2026 publications and models listed
- **authority_level**: Academic peer-reviewed: the leading university research group on compliant mechanisms (Larry L. Howell, Spencer P. Magleby, Brian D. Jensen and others), with a long list of journal papers, theses and patents

### Coverage

- **organisation**: Website: menus (About, Research, People, Videos, Gallery, Resources). Maker Resources is split into tabs by family: Collaborations, Compliant Mechanisms, Origami, FlexLinks (compliant beams that fit popular building toys), Developable Mechanisms, OneCuts and Holiday. Each card has a one-sentence plain description and links to Thingiverse/Printables. There is no search, filter or decision aid on the model list. Handbook Library: by function, modelled on Artobolevsky's 'Mechanisms in Modern Engineering Design' (Chapter 10 Library Organization; Chapter 11 Elements of Mechanisms: 11.1 Flexible Elements, 11.2 Rigid-Link Joints; Chapter 12 Mechanisms: 12.1 Basic Mechanisms, 12.2 Kinematics, 12.3 Kinetics). The tabs work for beginners because each card says what the thing does. The Handbook's function-based scheme is more technical
- **scope_match**: High for the compliant / print-in-place family: it is the main authority on flexures, living hinges, bistable snap-through parts and one-piece clutches, which are exactly the 'clever types' in scope. Low for everything else (no adhesives, powered connectors, standard gearboxes or quick-release fasteners)
- **taxonomy_used**: Handbook Library of Compliant Mechanisms: classified by function, with a layout inspired by Artobolevsky. Two top-level groups: (1) Elements of Mechanisms, split into Flexible Elements and Rigid-Link Joints; (2) Mechanisms, split into Basic Mechanisms, Kinematic (motion) and Kinetic (force/energy) mechanisms. The website's maker list uses 7 informal tabs (Collaborations, Compliant Mechanisms, Origami, FlexLinks, Developable Mechanisms, OneCuts, Holiday). Research themes: compliant, lamina emergent, origami-inspired, developable
- **example_types**: Cross-axis flexural pivot (flexure hinge); Lamina Emergent Torsional joint (LET joint); living hinge; compliant overrunning clutch (freewheel, ratchet-and-pawl style); compliant centrifugal clutch (engages above a set speed); bistable compliant switch (snap-through, two stable positions); bistable-to-monostable magnetic hinge; MaLO / MaLDO magnetic hinges (fold 180 degrees, return open); magnetic gears; constant-force mechanism; ortho-planar spring; large-displacement linear-motion (straight-line) flexure; Flex-16 large-displacement monolithic hinge; compliant rolling-contact element (CORE) and CORE bearing; kinematic coupling (repeatable precise location); 2-DOF compliant pointing mechanism; fully compliant pliers; Wyrd mechanism (double-parallel compliant linkage); developable four-bar linkage; SORCE thick-origami vertex joint; compliant latch (in the St Patrick's Day box)
- **making_methods**: 3D printing first (FDM files on Printables/Thingiverse), plus laser cutting / sheet material (LEMs cut from flat sheet, e.g. polypropylene), paper folding for origami prototypes, and general engineering (injection moulding, machining, stamping, water-jet, EDM, MEMS microfabrication are named on the 'About Compliant Mechanisms' page)
- **entry_structure**: Website model card: name, photo, one-sentence plain-English description, family tag, links to Thingiverse/Printables/Instructables pages (STL files). Some models have a short YouTube demo. The explainer pages ('About Compliant Mechanisms', 'About Origami Mechanisms', 'Developable Mechanisms Explained') have definitions, photos, animations and advantages. Handbook library entries: graphic plus concise description of behaviour, with references (per the book's front matter)
- **audience_level**: Two levels: hobbyist/school (maker resources, videos, explainer pages written in plain language, classroom projects) and research/professional (publications, theses, Handbook, textbook)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: Medium-high for the maker pages and explainers: plain language ('a mechanism that gains at least some of its mobility from the deflection of flexible members'), everyday examples (ketchup-bottle lids, tape measures, mouse buttons), photos and videos. Low for the Handbook and papers (pseudo-rigid-body models, large-deflection beam theory)
- **has_images**: Yes: good-quality photos of physical prototypes on every model card, a photo gallery, and diagrams on the explainer pages
- **has_animations**: Yes: an embedded video on the home page, a Videos page with about 20 YouTube videos (e.g. 3D-printed compliant centrifugal clutch, ortho-planar spring, fully compliant switch, origami twists, offset panel technique) and animations on the origami explainer page
- **good_for_entry_links**: Yes for families: the explainer pages have stable URLs (e.g. https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/about-compliant-mechanisms, https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/developable-mechanisms-explained). Individual models are best linked on Thingiverse/Printables (e.g. https://www.thingiverse.com/thing:3007265, the compliant overrunning clutch), since the BYU site has no per-model page except some video pages (e.g. https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/3d-printed-compliant-centrifugal-clutch)
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Tabs by family, with a one-sentence 'what it does' on every card, work well for beginners. (2) Lead with everyday objects the reader already owns (ketchup-lid living hinge, tape measure, battery clip) before the theory. (3) The 'advantages' checklist (part count, cost, precision/no backlash, no lubrication, miniaturisation, weight, stored energy) can become flow-chart questions such as 'Must it work without lubrication?' or 'Do you want fewer parts?'. (4) Pair every entry with a printable test model and a short video. (5) The Handbook's split of 'elements' (joints) versus 'mechanisms' (assemblies), and of kinematic (motion) versus kinetic (force/energy) function, is a good way to separate single joints from one-unit mechanisms
- **generator_or_parametric**: No web generators or parametric CAD. Only fixed STL models, plus the beam-bending MATLAB scripts as calculators. The Pocket Kinematic Coupling model 'lets you design and experiment with your own set of contacts' (physical kit, not software)
- **file_formats**: STL and similar 3D-print files (via Thingiverse/Printables), Instructables how-tos, MATLAB .m scripts, Excel .xls, YouTube video, PDF papers
- **terminology_value**: High for compliant-mechanism terms: canonical names and definitions for compliant mechanism, lamina emergent mechanism (LEM), LET joint, pseudo-rigid-body model, developable mechanism, CORE, ortho-planar spring, cross-axis flexural pivot, bistable mechanism, thickness accommodation (offset panel, SORCE). English only
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Original source for most of its content (the group's own research). The Handbook library format is inspired by Artobolevsky's seven-volume 'Mechanisms in Modern Engineering Design'. Overlaps: Wikipedia's 'Compliant mechanism', 'Lamina emergent mechanism' and 'Developable mechanism' articles (all in Category:Mechanisms (engineering)); its models are mirrored on Thingiverse (user BYU_CMR), Printables (BYU CMR) and third-party sites such as Cults3D and Treatstock

### Other Info

- **sources**: 
  - https://www.compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/ (home page, opened via curl)
  - https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/maker-resources (model list, opened)
  - https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/about-compliant-mechanisms (opened)
  - https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/about-origami-mechanisms (opened)
  - https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/developable-mechanisms-explained (opened)
  - https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/software-analysis (opened)
  - https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/books (opened)
  - https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/videos (opened)
  - https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/publications (opened)
  - https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/who-we-are (opened)
  - https://compliantmechanisms.byu.edu/3d-printed-compliant-centrifugal-clutch (opened)
  - https://www.thingiverse.com/thing:3007265 (Compliant Overrunning Clutch, opened; page is client-rendered so licence not readable)

## 6 Wikipedia category trees for mechanisms and connections

*Encyclopedia / wiki.* Category:Mechanisms (engineering), Mechanical power transmission, Kinematic pairs, Couplings, Clutches, List of mechanical components; also German Wikipedia's Fügen tree

### Basics

- **name**: Wikipedia category trees for mechanisms and connections (English: Mechanisms (engineering), Mechanical power transmission, Clutches, Rotating shaft couplings, Linkages (mechanical), Gears, Fasteners, Joining, Adhesives; German: Kategorie:Fügen, Verbindungstechnik, Maschinenelement, Getriebe)
- **url**: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Mechanisms_(engineering)
- **resource_type**: Encyclopedia/wiki (category tree used as a free, machine-readable classification system)
- **cost**: Free
- **license**: Text is CC BY-SA 4.0 (stated in the page footer; German footer also says Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike). Linking is unrestricted. Reusing text needs attribution and the same licence. Category membership lists are facts plus CC BY-SA page content, so building a list from them is safe with attribution
- **language**: English and German (the same topics exist in many other language editions, linked via Wikidata)
- **access_barrier**: None
- **image_license**: Images come from Wikimedia Commons, each with its own licence (mostly CC BY-SA, CC BY or public domain; some fair-use files only on English Wikipedia). Many category pages link to a matching Commons category (e.g. Clutches, Gears), a good pool of openly licensed images and animations
- **authority_level**: Community or unvetted (volunteer-written, citation-based; some articles are flagged as needing more sources, e.g. 'Kinematic pair')

### Coverage

- **covers**: English Category:Mechanisms (engineering): 26 subcategories and 82 pages (e.g. Geneva drive, Torque limiter, Detent, Ratchet, Pawl, Compliant mechanism, Lamina emergent mechanism, Developable mechanism, Scissors mechanism, Universal joint). Subcategories include Clutches (21 pages), Gears (61 pages, 5 subcats incl. Epicyclical gearing), Linkages (mechanical) (59 pages, subcats Crankshafts and Hinges), Rotating shaft couplings (19), Couplers (18, rail couplers), Brakes, Actuators, Escapements, Locks (security device), Straight-line mechanisms, Switches, Simple machines, Valves, Pumps, Firearm actions. Category:Mechanical power transmission: 10 subcats (Belt drives, Chain drives, Gears, Shaft drives, Torque converters, Variators …) and 43 pages. Category:Joining: 9 subcats (Adhesives 103 pages, Fasteners 102 pages, Joinery, Knots, Welding, Brazing and soldering, Seals, Cement, Wafer bonding) and 22 pages (Clinching, Crimp, Staking, Suction cup, Swivel …). NOTE: 'Category:Kinematic pairs', 'Category:Couplings' and 'List of mechanical components' do NOT exist on English Wikipedia (404); the nearest pages are the article 'Kinematic pair' (in Category:Kinematics) and the article 'Machine element', which has a 'Types' list. German Kategorie:Fügen: 9 subcats (Fügendes Fertigungsverfahren, Kleben, Löten, Schweißen, Verbindungstechnik, Montagetechnik, Nähen, Fügen durch Umformen, Abfülltechnik). Kategorie:Verbindungstechnik: 5 subcats (Verbindungselement with 168 pages, Holzverbindungstechnik, Knotenkunde, Schraubverbindung, Verzahnung) and 34 pages (Formschluss, Kraftschluss, Stoffschluss, Selbsthemmung, Presspassung …). Kategorie:Getriebe: 79 pages. Kategorie:Maschinenelement: 11 subcats, 85 pages. In total, several hundred relevant articles across the trees
- **organisation**: A category tree (hierarchy of categories, each listing subcategories with counts and alphabetical pages). Categories are grouped by part family (clutches, gears, linkages, couplings) rather than by what the reader wants to do, so a beginner must already know the word 'sprag clutch' or 'Geneva drive'. The tree also mixes in off-topic items (firearm actions, garage door openers, brand names like Gorilla Glue in Adhesives, court cases in Fasteners). The German Fügen tree is more systematic because it follows the DIN 8580/8593 standard (joining processes) and the 'Verbindungstechnik' page sorts joints by how they hold: shape (Formschluss), friction (Kraftschluss) or material bond (Stoffschluss), and by whether they can be undone (lösbar, bedingt lösbar, unlösbar)
- **scope_match**: Medium: it names and illustrates most in-scope TYPES (torque limiter, freewheel, sprag clutch, Geneva drive, detent, strain wave gearing, cycloidal drive, bayonet mount, snap-fit, living hinge, compliant mechanism, electromagnetic clutch, adhesive chemistries), but these are buried among standard hardware, vehicle parts, brands and off-topic pages, with no maker-oriented guidance
- **taxonomy_used**: English: an informal, editor-built family hierarchy (Mechanisms (engineering) has 26 subcategories; Joining has 9; Mechanical power transmission has 10), not a formal scheme. The 'Kinematic pair' article gives Reuleaux's lower/higher pair classification. The 'Machine element' article splits machine elements into 3 types: structural, mechanisms that control movement, and control components. German: DIN 8580 main group 4 'Fügen', subdivided by DIN 8593 into 9 process groups (Zusammensetzen, Füllen, An- und Einpressen, Fügen durch Urformen, Fügen durch Umformen, Schweißen, Löten, Kleben, Textiles Fügen), plus a 3-way split by working principle (form-, force/friction-, material-locked) and a 3-way split by releasability
- **example_types**: Torque limiter; Slipper clutch; Freewheel; Sprag clutch; Centrifugal clutch; Dog clutch; Electromagnetic clutch; Magnetic particle clutch; Ratchet (device); Pawl; Detent; Geneva drive; Scotch yoke; Strain wave gearing; Cycloidal drive; Epicyclic gearing; Worm drive; Magnetic gear; Oldham coupler; Hirth joint; Magnetic coupling; Bayonet mount; Snap-fit; Dzus fastener (quarter-turn); Positive locking device; Electromagnetic door holder; Living hinge; Compliant mechanism; Lamina emergent mechanism; Kinematic coupling; Selbsthemmung (self-locking); Cyanoacrylate; Thread-locking compound; Impact glue (contact adhesive); Solvent bonding
- **making_methods**: General engineering (machine design, automotive, manufacturing processes, woodwork joinery, welding). Not aimed at 3D printing, though some pages (Living hinge, Snap-fit, Compliant mechanism) mention plastics
- **item_count**: Category:Mechanisms (engineering) 82 pages + 26 subcats (108 members per the MediaWiki API); Mechanical power transmission 43 + 10 subcats; Clutches 21; Rotating shaft couplings 19; Linkages (mechanical) 59; Gears 61; Fasteners 102 + 7 subcats; Adhesives 103 + 3 subcats; Joining 22 + 9 subcats; Hinges 7. German: Fügen 5 pages + 9 subcats; Verbindungstechnik 34 + 5 subcats (Verbindungselement 168); Getriebe 79; Maschinenelement 85 + 11 subcats; Kleben 25 + 3 subcats (Klebstoff 53). Counts as of September 2026
- **entry_structure**: Each article: lead definition, often an infobox or photo/diagram, history, types/variants, how it works, applications, 'See also', references, and links to other languages (useful for translations). Category pages themselves hold only titles, subcategory counts and parent categories
- **audience_level**: Mixed: general public to undergraduate; some articles (Kinematic pair, gear geometry) are professional/academic

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: Medium: each article starts with a plain one-line definition and often a picture or animation, but you must already know the name to find it; category trees are jargon-first and cluttered with off-topic pages
- **has_images**: Yes: most articles have photos or diagrams, quality varies from excellent SVG diagrams to amateur photos
- **gaps**: No 'what do you need it to do?' entry point, no design rules for makers (clearances, print orientation), no 3D-printing context, no CAD files. Categories mix types with brands, companies, standards bodies and vehicle systems. Several expected categories are missing (Kinematic pairs, Couplings, a list of mechanical components). Over-centre, hand-release-only and print-in-place joints are not grouped anywhere. Quality and sourcing vary between articles
- **reusable_data**: Structured and exportable: the MediaWiki API returns category members and counts as JSON (https://en.wikipedia.org/w/api.php, e.g. prop=categoryinfo gave 108 members for Category:Mechanisms (engineering)). Each article links to a Wikidata item and to other-language versions, enabling multilingual term lists. Lists of titles can be reused freely; article text under CC BY-SA 4.0 with attribution and share-alike. Note: the API rate-limits unauthenticated bursts (HTTP 429 seen), so crawl slowly with a descriptive User-Agent
- **maintained**: Yes, live and continuously edited (edits dated 2026 on several pages checked). Category contents change over time, so any snapshot should be dated
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Use Wikipedia article titles as the canonical 'proper term in brackets' and link to them from each entry. (2) Borrow the German classification: sort joints by how they hold (shape-locked / friction-locked / material-bonded) and by whether they can be undone (releasable / conditionally releasable / permanent); these make good flow-chart questions. (3) The DIN 8593 nine process groups are a ready-made top level for 'joining' methods including adhesives. (4) Reuleaux's lower vs higher pairs, via the 'Kinematic pair' article, for sorting moving joints. (5) Show subcategory counts to hint how big each family is. (6) Use interlanguage links to add German/other names as aliases
- **generator_or_parametric**: No
- **terminology_value**: Very high: the best free source for canonical names, redirects (aliases) and translations via interlanguage links and Wikidata. German pages add precise terms (Formschluss, Kraftschluss, Stoffschluss, Selbsthemmung, Freilauf, Sperrklinke, Malteserkreuzgetriebe)
- **decision_support**: None as a selector. Only indirect help: category hierarchy, 'See also' sections, comparison sections inside some articles (e.g. types of clutches), and the German releasability / working-principle classification in Fügen and Verbindungstechnik
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Secondary source that cites textbooks, standards (DIN 8580, DIN 8593) and papers such as Reuleaux (kinematic pairs) and BYU (compliant, lamina emergent, developable mechanisms). Images are shared with Wikimedia Commons. Overlaps heavily with every other item in this survey; mirrors of Wikipedia text elsewhere should not be counted separately

### Other Info
