## 7 Wikimedia Commons mechanism animation categories

*Encyclopedia / wiki.* Category:Animations of mechanisms and related image categories; openly licensed images and GIFs

### Basics

- **name**: Wikimedia Commons mechanism animation categories (Category:Animations of mechanisms (engineering) and related categories)
- **url**: https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Category:Animations_of_mechanisms_(engineering)
- **resource_type**: encyclopedia/wiki (openly licensed media repository organised as a category tree)
- **cost**: Free
- **license**: Each file carries its own free license, set by its uploader. Sampled animations: 'Ratchet Gear and Pawl.gif' is CC BY-SA 4.0; 'Geneva mechanism 6spoke animation.gif' and 'Cycloidal drive.gif' are public domain (PD-self); 'Harmonic drive animation.gif' is GFDL plus CC BY-SA 3.0/2.5/2.0/1.0. Linking to any file or category page is always fine. Reusing (embedding or copying) an image is allowed without asking, but CC BY/BY-SA files need a credit line (author, license and link), and BY-SA derivatives must stay BY-SA. Check the license box on each file page. Commons' own reuse guide says each file may have different crediting requirements.
- **language**: Multilingual. Category names are in English, but file names and descriptions are often in German, French, Dutch, Spanish, Italian, Czech, Russian or Arabic (e.g. 'Animiertes Prinzip Malteserkreuzgetriebe.gif', 'Anim engrenages droits.gif')
- **access_barrier**: none
- **authority_level**: community or unvetted (user-made animations; many drawn by engineering students, hobbyists and Wikipedia editors; not peer reviewed)

### Coverage

- **covers**: Animated GIFs (and some SVG, WebM/OGV video) of mechanisms and machine elements. The requested name 'Category:Animations of mechanisms' does not exist; the real category is 'Category:Animations of mechanisms (engineering)' (46 files, 1 subcategory: straight line mechanisms). It sits under 'Category:Mechanisms (engineering)' (14 subcategories incl. Cams, Compliant mechanisms, Gear train, Gears, Mechanical linkages, Straight line mechanisms, Torque limiters, Videos of mechanisms). Much larger sibling trees hold most of the useful animations: 'Animations of mechanical linkages' (208 files, 4 subcats: deployable structures, leg mechanisms, rotating shaft couplers, straight line mechanisms), 'Animations of machinery' (200 files, 22 subcats incl. gears and gearboxes, ratchets, cam discs, bearings, screws, chains, pistons, DC motors, leg mechanisms), 'Animations of gears and gearboxes' (82 files, 4 subcats), 'Animations of leg mechanisms' (66), 'Animations of cardan joints' (16), 'Animations of clocks' (21), 'Animations of cam discs' (7), 'Animations of screws' (7), 'Animations of springs (mechanical)' (4), 'Animations of bearings' (3), 'Animations of ratchets' (2). Per-type categories (e.g. 'Geneva drives', 46 files incl. several animations) mix animations with photos and diagrams.
- **organisation**: A category tree (folksonomy): each file can sit in several categories, and categories have several parents (e.g. 'Animations of straight line mechanisms' sits under linkages, linear movement animations, mechanisms and straight line mechanisms). Two parallel trees exist: 'Animations of X' categories (by media type) and plain type categories (e.g. 'Geneva drives', 'Harmonic drives', 'Torque limiters'). Inside a category files are listed alphabetically by file name, often in a foreign language, with no descriptions in the listing. Hard for a beginner who does not already know the proper term; easy to browse visually because thumbnails animate.
- **scope_match**: medium: a rich source of openly licensed animations for many in-scope mechanism types (ratchets, Geneva drives, harmonic and cycloidal drives, toggle clamps, CV joints, straight line linkages), but coverage is uneven, it is not a reference text, and some in-scope families (torque limiters, compliant mechanisms, adhesives, print-in-place) have few or no animations
- **taxonomy_used**: No formal taxonomy. Loose functional/topic categories under 'Animations of mechanical engineering' (7 subcats: automation, classical mechanics, machinery, manufacturing technology, mechanical parts, process engineering, propulsion) and 'Animations of kinematics' (7 subcats). 'Animations of machinery' has 22 subcategories, mostly by machine element (gears, cams, ratchets, bearings, screws, chains, pistons, motors) or by machine (engines, vehicles, weapons). 'Mechanisms (engineering)' has 14 subcategories.
- **example_types**: 
  - Ratchet and pawl (ratchet gear and pawl, ratchet rack and pawl)
  - Geneva drive (Maltese cross, 4-, 6- and 8-position, and interior Geneva drive)
  - Strain-wave gear (harmonic drive)
  - Cycloidal drive
  - Planetary / epicyclic gearing (sun and planet gears)
  - Worm gear
  - Rack and pinion
  - Elliptical gears
  - Constant-velocity joint (incl. Thompson coupling)
  - Universal joint (cardan joint, double cardan joint)
  - Toggle clamp (over-centre clamp: horizontal, vertical, push-pull, pneumatic)
  - Scotch yoke
  - Quick return mechanism
  - Dwell mechanisms (single and two dwell cam, dwell linkage)
  - Straight line linkages (Peaucellier, Watt, Roberts, Chebyshev, Hart, Sarrus)
  - Deployable scissor structures and Hoberman mechanism
  - Iris mechanism
  - Zipper (slide fastener)
  - Cable drag chain (energy chain)
  - Solenoid actuator
  - Manual gearbox (sliding-mesh and sequential)
  - Three-ring release (parachute quick release)
- **making_methods**: General engineering and kinematics teaching; not aimed at any making method (no 3D printing, CNC or woodwork guidance)
- **special_behaviours_covered**: one-way/ratchet: yes (ratchet gear and pawl, ratchet rack and pawl, 'Animations of ratchets'). indexing/detent: yes for intermittent indexing (Geneva drives, 'Schrittgetriebe 8 zu 1' stepping gear), detents not seen. over-centre: yes (four toggle-clamp animations). gear trains/gearboxes: strong (planetary, harmonic, cycloidal, worm, spur/helical, manual and sequential gearboxes, gear pump). powered connectors: partial (solenoid actuator, relay animation, pneumatic and hydraulic cylinders, DC motors), but no electromagnet or motorised latch animations found. torque-limiting: 'Torque limiters' category has only 4 static images (slip clutch SVG, Ringspann ball and spring torque limiter photos), no animation. compliant: 'Compliant mechanisms' has 7 static images (bistable switch, compliant clip, compliant iris, compliant pliers), no animation. self-locking, hand-release only, print-in-place: not found. adhesive types: not covered.
- **entry_structure**: Each file page has the image or animation, a short description (often in one language), date, source, author, license box, categories and file history; no degrees of freedom, design rules or how-to
- **audience_level**: Mixed: school to undergraduate (kinematics teaching animations), used as illustrations in Wikipedia

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: the animations themselves are very clear and show motion better than text, but the category tree, foreign-language file names and missing explanations make it hard to find the right one without already knowing the term
- **has_images**: yes: animations plus many static diagrams, photos and SVG drawings in the per-type categories; quality varies from simple 2D line drawings to rendered 3D (POV-Ray, Blender, OpenSCAD)
- **has_animations**: yes: mostly animated GIFs, some animated SVG and WebM/OGV videos (e.g. 'Watchmovement.webm', 'Filmmuseum Berlin - Oskar Messter Maltese Cross in function.webm')
- **gaps**: No explanations of when to use each type, no design rules, no making advice, no comparison between types. Coverage is uneven and follows what volunteers happened to animate. Some key in-scope types (torque limiters, compliant mechanisms, bayonet mounts, detents, cam cleats, adhesives, print-in-place joints) have no animations. Category names are inconsistent ('Animations of mechanisms (engineering)' vs 'Animations of machinery' vs 'Animations of mechanical linkages'), so useful files are scattered.
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes: every file and category has a stable URL, and files can be embedded with attribution. Per-type categories are good targets, e.g. https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Category:Geneva_drives and https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Ratchet_Gear_and_Pawl.gif
- **maintained**: yes: actively edited; files and categories keep being added and recategorised. Category pages and the API were live on 2026-09-25
- **ideas_to_borrow**: Show a looping animation at the top of every entry, because a moving picture explains a mechanism faster than words. Keep both 'by type' and 'animation' views of the same item. Use file-level license metadata so each borrowed image carries its credit automatically. Use the category tree idea of multiple parents (a toggle clamp is both a linkage and an over-centre lock) rather than a single strict hierarchy. Reuse existing Commons animations for entries instead of redrawing them.
- **design_rules_given**: No numbers or design rules
- **generator_or_parametric**: No generators; a few files include their source code or are made with parametric tools (e.g. 'Cycloidal drive.gif' rendered in POV-Ray with source code, 'Simple CV Joint animated with OpenSCAD.gif')
- **file_formats**: GIF (animated), SVG (including animated SVG), PNG, JPG, WebM/OGV video, PDF and DjVu scans, occasional XCF source files
- **terminology_value**: medium: category names follow common English engineering terms and link to Wikidata items (via Wikidata Infobox on some categories), and multilingual file descriptions give translations; but naming is inconsistent and not authoritative
- **decision_support**: None: no selector, filters (beyond category browsing and MediaSearch), flow chart or comparison tables

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - image_license
  - item_count
  - reusable_data
  - year

## 8 Hiscox 1800 Mechanical Movements, Devices and Appliances

*Encyclopedia / digital library.* Gardner D. Hiscox's 1899+ illustrated catalogue grouped by field; public domain scans on the Internet Archive

### Basics

- **name**: Hiscox, Mechanical Movements, Powers, Devices and Appliances (reprinted as '1800 Mechanical Movements, Devices and Appliances')
- **url**: https://archive.org/details/mechanicalmoveme00hiscrich
- **resource_type**: book (public domain scans in a digital library: Internet Archive, also mirrored on Wikimedia Commons and HathiTrust)
- **license**: Public domain in the US. The 1899 first edition scan is marked 'NOT_IN_COPYRIGHT' by the Internet Archive (University of California Libraries copy); the 1917 15th edition scan comes from the Library of Congress, which 'is unaware of any copyright restrictions'; Wikimedia Commons tags its copies 'PD US expired'. Pages and engravings from the pre-1929 editions can be freely linked, copied and reused. The 2007 Dover reprint is a separate product; its IA copy (1800mechanicalmo0000hisc) is lending-only and should not be used as the source. An 'opensource' IA upload of the Dover PDF carries a Public Domain Mark added by an uploader, which is doubtful
- **language**: English
- **year**: First edition 1899 (N. W. Henley & Co., New York, 1,649 engravings, 402 pages); many enlarged editions followed (1903, 1905, 1907, 1911, 1914); 15th enlarged edition 1917 (409 pages, 'more than eighteen hundred' illustrations, adds a chapter on straight line movements); 16th edition 1921; Dover reprint of the 16th edition 2007. A second volume, 'Mechanical Appliances, Mechanical Movements and Novelties of Construction' (1904, later editions to 1927), adds about 1,000 more engravings
- **access_barrier**: none (free scans, PDF and full text; no login needed for the pre-1929 editions)
- **image_license**: Public domain engravings (same as text). Scan images are free to reuse; crediting the Internet Archive / contributing library is courteous but not legally required
- **authority_level**: educator (a single author's illustrated reference book, compiled for inventors, students and mechanics; not peer reviewed; Hiscox was a mechanical engineer, M.E.)

### Coverage

- **covers**: About 1,650 (1899) to about 1,890 (1917 edition, per the publisher's advertisement) numbered engravings, each with a short caption-style description, covering mechanical powers (levers, screws, pulleys), power transmission (ropes, belts, friction gearing, spur, bevel and worm gears), gearing (racks, elliptical, worm, differential, stop-motion, epicyclic), motion control (ratchets and pawls, cams, cranks, intermittent motions, toggles, universal joints, springs), clock escapements, couplings and clutches, plus many whole machines (steam engines, boilers, pumps, turbines, windmills, electrical machines, ships, vehicles, mining machinery, firearms, bridges and trusses)
- **organisation**: By field of application: 18 numbered sections, then 'Additions' for each section and (in the 1917 edition) Section XIX of straight line movements. Figures are numbered continuously and the book ends with an alphabetical index. Each section's contents list is a long run of item names separated by dashes. Fine for browsing pictures, but a beginner has to guess which field (e.g. 'Mill and Factory Appliances' for clutches) holds the connection they want; the index helps only if they know the term
- **scope_match**: medium: sections XI (Gearing), XII (Motion and devices controlling motion), XV (couplings and clutches) and XVIII contain many in-scope types (ratchets, intermittent gears, Geneva stop, toggle joints, universal joints, friction clutches, bayonet joint, differential gears), but most of the book is whole machines (engines, boilers, pumps, locomotives, firearms) and period equipment that is out of scope
- **taxonomy_used**: By field/power source (not by motion): I Mechanical Powers; II Transmission of Power; III Measurement of Power; IV Steam Power; V Steam Appliances; VI Motive Power (gas and gasoline engines); VII Hydraulic Power and Devices; VIII Air Power Appliances; IX Electric Power and Construction; X Navigation and Roads; XI Gearing; XII Motion and Devices Controlling Motion; XIII Horological; XIV Mining; XV Mill and Factory Appliances; XVI Construction and Devices; XVII Draughting Devices; XVIII Miscellaneous Devices; plus (1917) XIX Straight Line Movements. 18 top-level groups in the 1899 structure, 19 in the 1917 edition
- **example_types**: 
  - Ratchet and pawl (double-pawl ratchet, ball socket ratchet, friction hauling ratchet)
  - Centrifugal safety catch for hoisting drums
  - Geneva stop (watch stop)
  - Mangle wheel and mangle rack
  - Intermittent gears and stop-motion gears
  - Toggle joint (toggle-joint press, toggle clip pipe joint, double toggle-joint friction clutch)
  - Friction clutches (cone clutch, multiple plate friction clutch, spring friction clutch, pin clutch)
  - Oldham coupling
  - Universal joint (Hooke's joint, double link universal joint, ball socket universal joint)
  - Bayonet joint
  - Differential gear and differential screw
  - Epicyclic / planetary trains (Ferguson's paradox, planetary crank gear)
  - Worm gear (hunting tooth, globoid, anti-friction worm gear)
  - Elliptical gears and scroll gears
  - Heart cam and grooved cylinder cam
  - Scotch yoke / slotted yoke and trammel gear
  - Lazy tongs movement
  - Straight line motions (Watt, Peaucellier-type lever-and-link motions)
  - Electro-magnetic ratchet driver
  - A curious padlock (cork-screw key)
- **making_methods**: General engineering and 19th/early-20th-century machine design (cast and machined metal, wood); nothing on 3D printing or modern making
- **item_count**: 1,649 engravings (1899 first edition); about 1,890 engravings in the 1917 15th edition ('more than eighteen hundred' on the title page); the 2007 Dover reprint is marketed as 1,800+
- **entry_structure**: Figure number, a capitalised name, one engraving, and one to a few sentences explaining the parts (letters on the figure) and how it works. No dimensions, design rules, degrees of freedom or making instructions
- **audience_level**: hobbyist to professional of its time (inventors, students of mechanics, artisans, draughtsmen, patent agents)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: the pictures are clear and descriptions are short, but the language is 1899-era trade vocabulary, many terms are obsolete, and there is no explanation of why or when to choose one design
- **has_images**: yes: about 1,650-1,890 line engravings, generally clear, ranging from simple diagrams to detailed cross-sections; scan quality is good on the Internet Archive copies
- **has_animations**: no (static engravings only)
- **gaps**: No modern types (compliant mechanisms, snap fits, print-in-place, strain-wave gears, magnetic couplings, adhesives). Heavy on obsolete machines. No selection guidance, dimensions or materials. Organisation by industry rather than by function, so the same kind of mechanism is scattered across sections. Old names differ from modern terms
- **reusable_data**: scrape-only but easy: the Internet Archive offers free full text (OCR, e.g. mechanicalmoveme04hisc_djvu.txt), PDF and page images, and a metadata API; the figure list could be extracted from the OCR (numbered captions such as '1287. OLDHAM COUPLING'). The list and engravings are public domain, so they can be reused without restriction. OCR has errors that need cleaning
- **maintained**: Static historical book; the scans are stable and available on the Internet Archive, HathiTrust and Wikimedia Commons ('Category:Mechanical movements, powers and devices (Hiscox)', 12 files) as of 2026-09-25. Dover keeps the reprint in print
- **ideas_to_borrow**: Picture-first entries with a figure number and one short paragraph, labelled with letters on the drawing. Grouping many small variants of one idea (e.g. a dozen ratchet forms) side by side so readers can compare. A numbered figure system that the index and cross-references can point to. Its public domain engravings can be reused as historical illustrations for entries (e.g. bayonet joint, Oldham coupling, toggle joint)
- **design_rules_given**: Very few: occasional formulas in the Mechanical Powers section (lever, Prony brake rule) and some tables; no clearances, fits or torque values for connection types
- **generator_or_parametric**: none
- **file_formats**: Scanned book on the Internet Archive: text PDF, grayscale PDF, DjVu, plain-text OCR, hOCR and page images (JP2); PDF on Wikimedia Commons; print paperback (Dover)
- **terminology_value**: medium: a good source of historical names and aliases (e.g. 'mangle wheel', 'Hooke's angular shaft coupling', 'lazy tongs', 'wiper cam'), useful for alias lists, but many names are dated or brand-based and need modern equivalents
- **decision_support**: None: no selector, flow chart or comparison tables; only the contents lists per section and an alphabetical index

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - cost
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - good_for_entry_links
  - special_behaviours_covered

## 9 Mecapedia (Universitat Jaume I)

*Encyclopedia / wiki.* Spanish-language mechanical engineering encyclopedia started in 2006, with a Catalogos section; a direct counterpart to this project

### Basics

- **name**: Mecapedia: Enciclopedia de Ingenieria Mecanica (Universitat Jaume I)
- **url**: https://www.mecapedia.uji.es/ (redirects to https://www.mecapedia.uji.es/Index.html; Catalogues section: https://www.mecapedia.uji.es/ind/catalogos.html)
- **resource_type**: encyclopedia/wiki (university-authored online encyclopedia, not openly editable; readers report errors through a Google Form on the Contact page)
- **cost**: Free
- **language**: Spanish only (occasional English equivalents in brackets, e.g. 'backlash', 'torque limiter')
- **year**: Started 2006 by lecturers of the Mechanical Engineering Area, Universitat Jaume I (stated on the home page); page database shows the latest entry update on 6 March 2025 (friction-wheel drive catalogue page). Site rebuilt on Bootstrap 5 with a new JavaScript animation library; in 2024 it won the Federacion Iberoamericana de Ingenieria Mecanica 'Francisco Alba Juez' prize for best publication
- **access_barrier**: none
- **authority_level**: educator (university lecturers; each page names its author, e.g. Antonio Perez, Jose V. Garcia, Marta C. Mora; not peer-reviewed)

### Coverage

- **covers**: University-level mechanical engineering topics: machine elements (keys, pins, cams, springs, belts, screws), systems (couplings, bearings, gear trains, belt/chain/cable drives, ball screws, power screws), classic planar linkages (four-bar, six-bar Watt and Stephenson, straight-line linkages, quick-return, Scotch yoke, pantograph), plus concepts, phenomena (friction, fatigue, irreversibility/self-locking, backlash), laws, models, parameters and procedures. The page database (db.js) lists 304 pages: 63 systems, 62 concepts, 40 models, 37 elements, 27 phenomena, 24 catalogue pages, 21 procedures, 16 parameters, 9 laws, 3 standards pages, 2 lessons; 40 pages carry animations
- **organisation**: A side menu of content-type categories (Concepts, Elements, Phenomena, Lessons, Laws, Models, Parameters, Procedures, Systems), plus 'Everything' (A-Z list), 'Random page', 'Animations', 'Nomenclature' (symbol list for formulas), 'Catalogues', 'Standards', 'Calculators' and a search box. Each category page is an alphabetical list of titles. Grouping by content type (element vs system vs concept) is a textbook idea that does not help a beginner who is looking for 'something that does X'; you need to already know the Spanish term
- **scope_match**: medium: covers many in-scope mechanism and transmission TYPES (gear trains, planetary, worm, cams, Scotch yoke, straight-line linkages, dwell mechanisms, Oldham coupling, self-locking screws), but it is a general mechanical-engineering course encyclopedia in Spanish with nothing on snap fits, print-in-place, compliant joints, powered latches or adhesives, and its Catalogues section is a list of links to manufacturers' catalogues (the standard-hardware kind of resource the project leaves out)
- **taxonomy_used**: Two schemes. (1) Site-wide: 11 content-type categories (Conceptos, Elementos, Fenomenos, Lecciones, Leyes, Modelos, Parametros, Procedimientos, Sistemas, Catalogos, Estandares). (2) The Catalogues page source still holds an older, commented-out functional classification of machine components with 14 top-level groups: raw materials; actuators (manual, pneumatic, electric motors, hydraulic, vibrators); material feeding; shafts and axles; joining parts with no degrees of freedom (screws, washers, nuts, pins, rivets, keys, taper bushes, clamps); joining parts by simple kinematic pairs (rotary guides/bearings, linear guides, wheels, ball joints, couplings); transmission systems of several pairs (belt, chain, gear, ball screw, power screw, cable, linear actuators); elastic, damping and anti-vibration elements; brakes, clutches and torque limiters (freewheels, disc and drum brakes, fluid couplings, torque limiters); automation elements; complex transmission systems; machine safety; machines; maintenance (tools, seals, lubricants)
- **example_types**: Oldham coupling (acoplamiento de Oldham); sleeve coupling (acoplamiento de manguito); Scotch yoke (mecanismo de yugo escoces), in 4-link and 3-link pin-in-slot forms; dwell / intermittent-motion six-bar (mecanismo con movimiento intermitente); Whitworth quick-return mechanism; Chebyshev, Hoekens, Peaucellier, Roberts and Watt straight-line linkages; pantograph; planetary / epicyclic gear train (tren de engranajes planetarios); worm gear (engranaje de tornillo sinfin); rack and pinion (transmision por pinon-cremallera); friction-wheel drive (transmision por rueda de friccion); ball screw (husillo de bolas); power screw / lead screw (tornillo de potencia); Woodruff key (chaveta Woodruff); cam types: plate, wedge, ribbed cylindrical, grooved cylindrical and face cams; cable drive (transmision por cable); taper locking bush (buje conico de fijacion, catalogue only); torque limiter (limitador de par, catalogue only); fluid coupling (acoplamiento hidraulico, catalogue only)
- **making_methods**: General mechanical engineering / machine design (industrial machinery); nothing specific to 3D printing, CNC or hobby robotics
- **item_count**: 304 pages in the site database (about 280 encyclopedia entries plus 24 catalogue pages); 40 pages with animations
- **entry_structure**: Title, category badge, author, last-modified date, a short definition paragraph, photos or diagrams with captions, formulas (MathJax) in analysis pages, SVG/JavaScript animations on 40 pages, and an automatic 'see also' list of linked entries. Catalogue pages are a table: company, links to its web page and PDF catalogues, and a one-line description (e.g. 'friction torque limiter', 'ball torque limiter')
- **audience_level**: undergraduate (engineering students), also useful to professionals

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: short, clear definitions with photos and animations, but written in Spanish textbook language that assumes engineering vocabulary (kinematic pair, link, transmission angle), and there is no plain-language entry point
- **has_images**: yes: photos of real parts and simple diagrams on many element/system pages; quality is decent but small and uneven, some pages have no images
- **has_animations**: yes: 40 pages have interactive-looking SVG animations drawn in the browser by the site's own JavaScript library (e.g. four-bar, slider-crank, Scotch yoke, straight-line linkages, cam and follower, internal and external gears)
- **gaps**: Spanish only; no plain-English or beginner layer; no function-based or 'what do you need to do' navigation; nothing on 3D printing, snap fits, print-in-place, compliant mechanisms, clever everyday connectors, powered latches or adhesives; torque limiters and freewheels have no explanatory pages; no design rules for printed parts; no downloadable CAD
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes: each entry has a stable, readable per-page URL, e.g. https://www.mecapedia.uji.es/pages/mecanismo_de_yugo_escoces.html (Scotch yoke) or https://www.mecapedia.uji.es/pages/irreversibilidad.html (self-locking). Useful as a Spanish-language 'further reading' link
- **maintained**: yes: online and updated (latest page date 6 March 2025 in db.js; 2024 award noted on the home page). The legacy index URL http://www.mecapedia.uji.es/indices.htm now returns 403, so old deep links may be broken. Wayback Machine could not be checked from this environment
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) One small JSON 'database' file listing every entry with category, author, date and an 'animated' flag, which drives all index pages; a good lightweight model for the encyclopedia. (2) An 'Animations' index that lists only the entries with moving diagrams. (3) A 'Random page' button for browsing. (4) Keeping 'explanatory entry' and 'where to buy it (catalogues)' as separate linked pages, so the explanation stays brand-neutral. (5) The old functional catalogue tree (joints with no freedom / joints with one freedom / transmissions / elastic elements / brakes, clutches and torque limiters) is a usable top-level grouping idea. (6) An 'Irreversibility' concept page that ties several mechanisms together by behaviour
- **design_rules_given**: Some: Calculators page links to Google Sheets for spring pre-design, leaf springs, transverse pin strength, torsion bars, stress concentration and fatigue limits; Standards pages summarise standard dimensions for keys, Woodruff keys and pins. Nothing on 3D-print clearances, snap-fit strain or torque settings
- **generator_or_parametric**: No geometry generators; the Calculators are Google Sheets for sizing (springs, pins, shafts). The animations are parametric SVG code but not offered as a tool
- **file_formats**: HTML pages, SVG/JavaScript animations, JPG/PNG images, links to manufacturers' PDF catalogues, Google Sheets calculators
- **terminology_value**: high for Spanish: a good source for correct Spanish names of mechanisms and machine elements (e.g. yugo escoces, tornillo de potencia, rueda libre, limitador de par) when adding translations or aliases; English equivalents are only given occasionally
- **decision_support**: None: no selector, flow chart, filters or comparison tables; only category lists, search, random page and cross-links between entries

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - image_license
  - license
  - reusable_data
  - special_behaviours_covered

## 10 Mechanical Design 101 (J. Michael McCarthy)

*Encyclopedia / wiki.* UC Irvine professor's hub of linkage animations, lecture notes and links to other mechanism collections

### Basics

- **name**: Mechanical Design 101 (J. Michael McCarthy, UC Irvine)
- **url**: https://mechanicaldesign101.com/ (linkage animations category: https://mechanicaldesign101.com/category/linkage-animation/; Linkage Designs: https://mechanicaldesign101.com/linkage-designs/)
- **resource_type**: encyclopedia/wiki in the project's list, but in practice an educator's WordPress blog and resource hub (posts, videos, lecture notes, a shop for books and apps)
- **cost**: Free to read; the site also sells McCarthy's books and software (e.g. 21st Century Kinematics $159.00, Geometric Design of Linkages $44.99, Kinematic Synthesis of Mechanisms print $29.95, Introduction to Theoretical Kinematics $9.99)
- **license**: '(c) Copyright - Mechanical Design 101' in the footer; no open licence found, so all rights reserved: fine to link to posts, not to copy images or text
- **language**: English
- **access_barrier**: none (logging in only needed to comment)
- **authority_level**: educator (a UC Irvine Distinguished Professor, ASME Fellow and former editor of the ASME Journal of Mechanical Design and Journal of Mechanisms and Robotics); blog posts are not peer-reviewed but often summarise his group's published research

### Coverage

- **covers**: Mainly linkages (bar mechanisms): the 'Linkage Animations' category holds 101 posts of animated four-bar, six-bar, eight-bar, spherical and spatial linkages, many designed by his students for real tasks (car doors, bookcases, monitor arms, spoilers, ailerons, retractable handle, folding structures, flapping wings, walking-robot legs, curve-drawing linkages). Other categories: Kinematic Synthesis (50), Robotics Information (56), Lecture Notes (31), Engineering Data (26), Student Projects (39), Racecar Engineering (28), plus fiction ('Creative') and Arduino tutorials ('The Bored Robot'). Lecture notes cover DC motor torque and power, four-bar analysis, slider-crank, elementary robotics, cam and follower systems and GD&T
- **organisation**: Blog categories and date order (newest first), with top-menu sections (Machine Design, Linkage Designs, Energy and Environment, Design Innovation, Robotics, Lecture Notes, Creative) and a search box. There is no index by mechanism type or by what a mechanism does, so a beginner has to scroll through posts; post titles often name the application ('Retractable handle', 'Open a door') which helps a little
- **scope_match**: low: the content is almost all planar and spatial linkages designed with research-level synthesis methods, and many posts show whole machines (walking robots, racecars) that the project leaves out; nothing on joints, fasteners, snap fits, clutches, latches or adhesives. Useful only as inspiration for clever linkage motions
- **taxonomy_used**: No formal mechanism taxonomy. Posts are grouped by blog category (about 22 categories, e.g. Linkage Animations, Kinematic Synthesis, Kinematics, Lecture Notes, Engineering Data). Within linkages, posts use the standard research names: 4R four-bar, slider-crank, Watt I and Stephenson II/III six-bars, eight-bars, spherical and spatial (RSSR, RPR-2SS) chains, and task names from linkage synthesis (function generator, path generator, rigid-body guidance)
- **example_types**: Four-bar function generator (door opener); slider-crank; Watt I six-bar with a translating (rectilinear) output link; Stephenson II six-bar function generator; Stephenson III six-bar (ballistic function); rectilinear eight-bar linkage; eight-bar motion amplifier; spherical four-bar; spherical Watt I six-bar (car door); spatial RSSR car-door linkage; spatial RPR-2SS six-bar (valve); Jansen-type leg mechanism and wing mechanism; Kempe translational (straight-line platform) linkages; contra-parallelogram curve-drawing linkage (trifolium); Bezier curve-drawing linkages; six-bar folding / deploying linkage; eight-bar port closure tool; tensegrity linkage; skew pantograph; cam and follower (lecture notes)
- **making_methods**: Robotics and mechanism design with CAD (SolidWorks, GeoGebra); some student prototypes are 3D printed in PLA (e.g. the 2025 car-door linkage driven by a DC gear motor through a crank-rocker)
- **item_count**: 101 posts in the 'Linkage Animations' category (about 50 more under 'Kinematic Synthesis', many overlapping); plus 6 lecture-note PDFs linked from the Lecture Notes page
- **special_behaviours_covered**: Mostly none of the project's special behaviours. Straight-line / parallel (rectilinear) motion and folding / deploying motion are covered; gear trains appear only as the drive trains inside student walking robots (six-leg walkers driven by gear trains of up to 18 gears), not as explained types. No torque-limiting, self-locking, ratchet, detent, over-centre, compliant, print-in-place, powered-latch or adhesive content found
- **entry_structure**: A blog post: title, date, author, one or two paragraphs describing the linkage and who designed it, an embedded video or GIF-style animation (often GeoGebra, SolidWorks or CATIA renders), sometimes a photo of a prototype, tags and related posts. No degrees-of-freedom tables, design rules or CAD downloads
- **audience_level**: undergraduate / graduate and research (kinematic synthesis); a few Arduino and robotics posts aimed at hobbyists

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low: posts assume kinematics vocabulary (coupler curve, function generator, curvature theory, Watt I / Stephenson II) and explain little; the animations are the most accessible part
- **has_images**: yes: renders, screenshots and photos of student prototypes; quality varies from good to small low-resolution screenshots
- **has_animations**: yes: the main strength; about 101 posts with linkage animation videos (GeoGebra, SolidWorks Motion, CATIA) mostly embedded from YouTube, plus videos of working walking robots
- **gaps**: No index by type or function, no plain-English explanations, no design rules or tolerances, no CAD files to download, nothing on everyday joints, latches, clutches, snap fits or adhesives; heavy mixing of mechanism content with racing, fiction and news posts
- **good_for_entry_links**: moderately: posts have stable dated permalinks, e.g. https://mechanicaldesign101.com/2014/01/20/six-bar-linkage-with-rectilinear-moving-link/, good as 'see an animation' links for specific linkage entries (straight-line six-bar, car-door linkage, Jansen leg); but each post is about one design, not a general type
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Name linkages by the everyday job they do ('opens a door', 'lifts a car door up and over', 'retractable handle', 'folds a structure') next to the proper term; this is exactly the plain-English-first approach the encyclopedia needs. (2) Short looping animation per mechanism as the main content. (3) Point readers onward to bigger collections (it links DMG-Lib and AnimatedEngines rather than duplicating them). (4) Pair each concept with a free interactive tool (MotionGen app, GeoGebra books) so readers can play with it
- **generator_or_parametric**: Points to linkage-design software from his group and collaborators (MechGen / Mechanism Generator for SolidWorks sketches, MechGen Suspension iPad app, Synthetica and Sphinx for spatial and spherical linkages, the MotionGen four-bar synthesis app); GeoGebra books with adjustable four-bar models. Not a geometry generator for 3D printing
- **file_formats**: Web pages, embedded YouTube video, images (JPG/PNG), PDF lecture notes; software and apps sold or linked separately
- **terminology_value**: medium: authoritative for linkage synthesis terms (Watt I, Stephenson II/III, RSSR, function vs path generator, rigid-body guidance, rectilinear linkage) but not for general connection or mechanism names, and no aliases or translations
- **decision_support**: None: no selector, flow chart, filters or comparison tables

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - design_rules_given

## 11 Interactive mechanism demo sites (Wolfram Demonstrations, Colorado State video demos, MechSimulator)

*Encyclopedia / digital library.* Interactive and video demonstrations of linkages, cams, gear trains and Geneva drives (Wolfram demos are CC BY-NC-SA)

### Basics

- **name**: Interactive mechanism demo sites: Wolfram Demonstrations Project (Machines / Mechanical Engineering topics), Colorado State University 'Engineering and Physics Video Demonstrations' (Mechanisms section), and MechSimulator (Mechanisms & Machines simulators)
- **url**: Wolfram: https://demonstrations.wolfram.com/topic/machines (JavaScript app; its data comes from https://www.wolframcloud.com/obj/resourcesystem/published/DemonstrationRepository/json/topic/machines.json); CSU: https://video-demos.colostate.edu/mechanics/mechanisms/ (plus /cams/ and /linkages/ sub-pages); MechSimulator: https://mechsimulator.com/mechanisms/
- **resource_type**: interactive tool / video channel (three separate sites: a large peer-reviewed library of interactive Wolfram Language 'Manipulate' models; a university lecturer's index page of short demo videos, photos and GIFs; and a single-author set of browser-based mechanism simulators)
- **cost**: Free for all three. Wolfram: free to view and use in the browser; editing or authoring needs Mathematica/Wolfram Language (paid) or a free Wolfram Cloud account. CSU: free. MechSimulator: free, no signup, funded by Google AdSense ads
- **language**: English (all three)
- **access_barrier**: Wolfram: none to view in the browser (JavaScript + Wolfram Cloud; Cloudflare-protected), optional free Wolfram Player desktop app or Mathematica to run the downloadable notebooks offline. CSU: none (direct MP4 links and YouTube). MechSimulator: none (runs client-side in the browser, no account)
- **authority_level**: Wolfram: community-contributed but editorially reviewed before publication, each demo carries named authors and a permanent citation (strong 'educator/community, reviewed'). CSU: educator (Dr David Alciatore, Colorado State University mechanical engineering). MechSimulator: educator (single mechanical engineering instructor, M.Tech Machine Design; formulas from Shigley, Norton, Hibbeler), not peer-reviewed

### Coverage

- **covers**: Wolfram: 13,216 Demonstrations overall; the Machines topic lists 325 and Mechanical Engineering 290 (483 unique across both). Mechanism content includes linkages (four-bar, coupler-curve atlas, Theo Jansen, Klann, Chebyshev walking, Peaucellier/Hart inversors, Sarrus, Bricard 6R, pantograph, quick-return, slider-crank, Scotch yoke), cams (radial cam design, cylindrical cam, idealized cam-follower), gears (involute, cycloidal, elliptic, hyperboloidal, planetary gear train, noncircular planetary, continuously variable planetary transmission, gearless cycloidal speed reducer, 5-speed manual transmission), intermittent motion (Geneva drive, Graham escapement), couplings and clutches (Oldham coupling, universal joint, positive-contact claw clutch, gyroscopic joint), and small clever devices (quick-release linkage, fastening mechanism using a rocker, spring return button, wave spring, cylinder-lock key cutting). CSU: about 70 links on the General mechanisms page plus 16 on Cams and 11 on Linkages: real-hardware videos (bowling pinsetter mechanisms, automobile differential, planetary gear drive, worm drive, lead-screw overhaul, ratcheted CVT, spherical scissor linkage, Thompson coupling vs CV joint vs U-joint, washing-machine museum) and cam/linkage photos. MechSimulator: 17 tools on the Mechanisms & Machines page in 4 topics (linkages/cams/intermittent motion 5, power transmission 3, dynamics 5, bearings & machine elements 4), including four-bar linkage, slider-crank, Scotch yoke/double-slider (Oldham coupling, elliptical trammel), cam & follower, Geneva mechanism (3-8 slots), gear trains (simple, compound, worm), manual synchromesh gearbox, belt & chain drive, power screw (self-locking); the whole site has 89 simulators per its About page (the sitemap lists 144 tool URLs)
- **organisation**: Wolfram: topic tree (Engineering & Technology > Machines, Mechanical Engineering, Robotics ...) plus A-Z index, search, 'related Demonstrations' and 'more by author'; each topic page is a flat list of thumbnails. Works for browsing pictures, but finding things needs the proper term, and mechanism demos are mixed with maths curiosities (e.g. hinged dissections, clocks). CSU: one hand-written alphabetical-ish bullet list per page (General / Cams / Linkages), organised by example machine rather than mechanism type; easy to skim but not searchable by function. MechSimulator: by course topic in textbook order (kinematics > transmission > dynamics > machine elements), with a helpful 'What each mechanism converts' table (input motion > output motion > typical use); the best of the three for a beginner who thinks in terms of motion
- **scope_match**: medium: together they animate many in-scope mechanism TYPES (Geneva drive, cams, Scotch yoke, planetary/worm/cycloidal gear trains, Oldham and universal couplings, claw clutch, quick-release linkage, ratcheted CVT, self-locking power screw), but they are teaching demos for motion and kinematics, not a catalogue of connections; almost nothing on snap fits, print-in-place, compliant joints, powered latches or adhesives
- **taxonomy_used**: Wolfram: subject topic tree with 14 top-level topics (Mathematics, Computation, Physical Sciences, Life Sciences, Business & Social Systems, Systems Models & Methods, Engineering & Technology, Our World, Creative Arts, Kids & Fun, Programming Functionality ...), with Engineering & Technology split into 12 subtopics including Machines, Mechanical Engineering and Robotics; no mechanism taxonomy. CSU: by engineering course area (Controls, Mechanics > Dynamics / Mechanisms (General, Cams, Linkages) / Solid Mechanics / Vibration, Energy, Mathematics ...). MechSimulator: 9 site categories; Mechanisms page uses 4 groups following a 'theory of machines' course (Linkages, Cams & Intermittent Motion / Power Transmission / Dynamics of Machines / Bearings & Machine Elements) plus a motion-conversion table
- **example_types**: Geneva drive (Maltese cross) [Wolfram, MechSimulator]; Scotch yoke [Wolfram, MechSimulator, CSU]; Oldham coupling [Wolfram, MechSimulator]; universal joint (Hooke's joint) [Wolfram]; Thompson constant-velocity coupling [CSU]; positive-contact claw clutch (dog clutch) [Wolfram]; quick-release linkage [Wolfram]; fastening mechanism using a rocker [Wolfram]; spring return button [Wolfram]; wave spring / wave spring washer [Wolfram]; planetary (epicyclic) gear train [Wolfram, CSU]; gearless cycloidal speed reducer [Wolfram]; continuously variable planetary transmission [Wolfram]; ratcheted continuously variable transmission [CSU]; worm drive [CSU, MechSimulator]; automobile differential [CSU]; desmodromic (closed-form) cams [CSU]; cylindrical (barrel) cam [Wolfram]; Graham clock escapement [Wolfram]; Peaucellier straight-line linkage [Wolfram, CSU]; Sarrus linkage [Wolfram]; spherical (geodesic) scissor linkage [CSU]; self-locking power screw [MechSimulator]; swash plate drive [Wolfram]
- **making_methods**: General engineering / theory of machines teaching; nothing aimed at 3D printing, CNC or hobby robotics (CSU also has robotics and mechatronics sections)
- **item_count**: Wolfram: 13,216 Demonstrations in total; 483 in the Machines and Mechanical Engineering topics, of which roughly 100 are about linkages, cams, gears, couplings and similar mechanisms. CSU: about 100 mechanism links (about 70 general, 16 cams, 11 linkages). MechSimulator: 17 mechanism tools (89 simulators site-wide per the About page)
- **entry_structure**: Wolfram: title, author(s), live interactive model with sliders ('Manipulate'), snapshots, short caption/details, categories, related demos, permanent citation, downloadable source notebook, embed code. CSU: one-line link text to an MP4, GIF, photo or YouTube video; no written explanation. MechSimulator: animated canvas with sliders, live graphs, presets, four modes (Simulate, Explore, Practice, Quiz), SI/imperial units, and a written user guide explaining the mechanism and its phases
- **audience_level**: Wolfram: high school to research (mostly undergraduate). CSU: undergraduate engineering. MechSimulator: diploma, apprenticeship and polytechnic students (early undergraduate)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: moving pictures make mechanisms easy to grasp and MechSimulator explains each one in plain language with a motion-conversion table, but Wolfram captions are terse and assume maths vocabulary, and CSU gives only link titles with no explanation
- **has_images**: yes: Wolfram snapshots are clean computer renderings (MIT-licensed); CSU has real photos of engines, cam followers, bike linkages (variable quality, some old); MechSimulator draws clear 2D diagrams on canvas
- **has_animations**: yes, the main strength: Wolfram interactive 3D/2D models with sliders running in the browser via Wolfram Cloud; CSU real-hardware MP4 videos, GIF animations and high-speed video; MechSimulator animated, adjustable browser simulations with live velocity/acceleration graphs
- **reusable_data**: Wolfram: structured and exportable: public JSON files (all.json index of every demo, topics-tree.json, per-topic and per-demo JSON with authors, dates, categories, snapshots and notebook links) used by the site itself; the list and content are CC BY-NC-SA 3.0 (non-commercial, share-alike), snapshot images MIT. CSU: scrape-only, no licence. MechSimulator: not reusable (terms forbid scraping and mirroring); link only
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) MechSimulator's 'What each mechanism converts' table (input motion > output motion > typical use) is a ready-made flow-chart layer for motion converters. (2) MechSimulator's presets (e.g. '6-slot indexing', 'film projector') show a type in real uses. (3) Wolfram's per-entry 'related' and 'more by author' links and permanent citations. (4) Embedding or linking a Wolfram live model or MIT-licensed snapshot next to an entry gives a free interactive picture. (5) CSU's approach of pairing a mechanism with a real everyday machine (bowling pinsetter, washing machine, car differential) helps beginners recognise where they have seen it. (6) Phase labels on animations (dwell, engage, drive, exit, lock) explain how a mechanism works step by step
- **design_rules_given**: Some, only in MechSimulator: Geneva geometry (R = a/tan(pi/n), dwell %, index angle), Grashof condition for four-bars, belt tension ratio, power-screw raising/lowering torque and self-locking condition, gear ratio/torque, bearing life (ISO 281), AGMA gear strength; Wolfram demos compute geometry but rarely state rules; CSU none. Nothing on printed clearances
- **generator_or_parametric**: Yes, in a limited way: every Wolfram demo is a parametric model whose source notebook can be downloaded and edited in the Wolfram Language (not CAD); MechSimulator lets you change dimensions and read off values but gives no geometry export
- **file_formats**: Wolfram: web interactive, PNG snapshots, .nb notebook source (CDF/Wolfram Player). CSU: MP4 video, GIF, JPG/PNG, one PPS PowerPoint, YouTube links. MechSimulator: HTML/JavaScript canvas only (values can be copied)
- **terminology_value**: medium: Wolfram titles and CSU captions use proper engineering names (Oldham coupling, Graham escapement, Peaucellier inversor, desmodromic cam), and MechSimulator explains terms like inversion, dwell and self-locking in plain words; none gives aliases or translations
- **decision_support**: Only MechSimulator's motion-conversion comparison table and a bearing-selection tool (choose among eight bearing types by life, load and lubrication); no selector or flow chart in Wolfram or CSU

## 12 RoyMech

*Encyclopedia / wiki.* UK engineering reference site covering couplings, clutches, brakes, gears, springs, keys and locking devices

### Basics

- **name**: RoyMech (Roy Beardmore's mechanical engineering reference site)
- **resource_type**: encyclopedia/wiki (single-author engineering reference website of notes, tables and formulae; not openly editable)
- **cost**: Free
- **license**: All rights reserved: roymech.org's Notes page reads 'Copyright (c) 2000 - Roy Beardmore, All Rights Reserved' and the site footer reads '(c) 2020 Roymech.org', with a disclaimer that the information is for general purposes only. No open licence, so link to pages but do not copy text or diagrams
- **language**: English (British engineering usage and BS/ISO/DIN standards)
- **authority_level**: educator (a practising UK mechanical engineer's personal reference notes, drawing on British Standards and textbooks such as Uicker, Pennock and Shigley's Theory of Machines and Mechanisms; not peer-reviewed; well known and recommended on engineering forums)

### Coverage

- **covers**: Broad mechanical-engineering reference: Drive Components (38 pages: rigid, muff, beam, pin, rubber-disc, spider (jaw), Bibby, chain, gear, diaphragm (MetaFlex), fluid and universal couplings; plate, cone, eddy-current, dog, fluid and sprag clutches; band, block, disc and drum brakes and friction materials; spur, helical, bevel, worm, epicyclic and harmonic gears, gearboxes and differentials; belts, timing belts, V-belts, roller chain; bearings, shafts, seals, drive motors), Machine Elements (cams, escapements, flywheels, indexing mechanisms, ratchets, reciprocating and rocking mechanisms, power screws, shock absorbers, plus a 'Mechanisms' page), Springs (helical compression/extension/torsion, disc, leaf, spiral), Keyways and splines, Circlips and pins, Screws and fastenings (including a 'Screw Locking and Retaining Methods' page), Rivets, Welds, Adhesives (natural, rubber/contact, thermoplastic incl. PVA and cyanoacrylate, thermoset incl. epoxy, anaerobic, toughened), plus materials, tolerances and fits, tribology, fatigue, lifting, seals, pumps, valves and more. About 580 pages in the roymech.org sitemap
- **organisation**: Hierarchical menus by engineering subject (Background, Engineering, Design, Components 1 and 2), each leading to an index page and an alphabetical list of pages. It assumes you know the component's name (e.g. 'Drive Couplings', 'Sprag'), so it is poor for someone who only knows what they need it to do. The 'Mechanisms' page is the exception: it lists mechanisms by the job they do
- **scope_match**: medium: many in-scope TYPES are explained (flexible couplings, sprag/overrunning clutches, dog clutches, eddy-current clutches, ratchets and pawls, Geneva and other indexing mechanisms, escapements, harmonic, epicyclic and worm gears, differentials, screw-locking methods, snap-action/toggle mechanisms, and adhesive families by chemistry), but much of the site is standard-hardware dimension tables (screws, keys, circlips, pins, flanges, steel sections), which the project leaves out, and it has nothing on 3D printing, snap fits, compliant or print-in-place parts
- **taxonomy_used**: Site-wide: by engineering subject area (about 36 resource sections on the Resources index, e.g. Adhesives, Circlips & Pins, Drive Components, Keyways, Machine Elements, Screws & Fastenings, Springs). The Mechanisms page uses a functional classification 'based on the type of motion required', taken from Uicker, Pennock and Shigley, with 17 groups: snap-action mechanisms, linear actuators, fine adjustment mechanisms, clamping mechanisms, location devices, ratchets, escapements, indexing mechanisms, swinging or rocking mechanisms, reciprocating mechanisms, reversing mechanisms, couplings and connectors, sliding connectors, stop/pause/hesitation mechanisms, curve generators, straight-line generators, tracing mechanisms. Locking devices are split into friction locking vs positive locking; clutches by how torque passes (friction, positive, magnetic, fluid); adhesives into natural, elastomer, thermoplastic, thermoset and toughened
- **example_types**: sprag clutch (one-way / overrunning clutch); dog clutch (positive clutch); cone clutch; eddy-current clutch (magnetic clutch); fluid coupling (soft-start coupling); Bibby coupling (grid-spring coupling); spider / jaw coupling; beam coupling (helical-slot flexible coupling); diaphragm (disc) coupling; universal coupling (Hooke's / Cardan joint) and pin 'uni-joint'; pawl ratchet; spring-blade ratchet; magnetic (solenoid) ratchet; friction ratchet; Geneva mechanism (external, internal, spherical); mutilated gears (intermittent gearing); cycloidal intermittent gearing; harmonic drive (strain-wave gear); epicyclic (planetary) gears; differential gearbox; escapements; snap-action (toggle / bi-stable) mechanisms; Nyloc nut, castle nut with split pin, tab washer, wire locking, threadlocking (anaerobic) adhesive; adhesive families: PVA, cyanoacrylate (superglue), contact (rubber) adhesive, epoxy, anaerobic, acrylic solvent cement
- **making_methods**: General mechanical engineering and machine design (industrial machinery, lifting equipment); nothing for 3D printing, laser cutting or hobby robotics
- **item_count**: About 580 pages (roymech.org sitemap); 38 pages in Drive Components, 12 in Machine Elements, 10 in Springs, 18 in Screws, 4 main adhesive-type pages
- **entry_structure**: Page title, short definition, a table of types with one-paragraph descriptions and small GIF line drawings, sometimes formulae and dimension tables from British Standards, then a list of links to suppliers and further reading
- **audience_level**: professional (design engineers and draughtsmen), also used by engineering students

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: descriptions are short and practical, and type-comparison tables (couplings, clutches, adhesives) are easy to scan, but it uses engineering jargon without explanation, has typos, small dated diagrams and no animations
- **has_images**: yes: small, simple GIF line drawings on most component pages (couplings, indexing mechanisms, ratchets, locking devices); functional but low resolution and dated; some pages say pictures are at suppliers' sites instead
- **has_animations**: no animations, videos or interactive models on the pages opened
- **reusable_data**: scrape-only: plain static HTML with an XML sitemap (https://roymech.org/sitemap.xml, 580 URLs); all rights reserved, so the list and text cannot be reused beyond linking and citing facts
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) One comparison table per family (type, how it works, notes, link), as on the Couplings and Clutches pages; ideal as the 'compare options' view at the end of a flow-chart branch. (2) The functional mechanism list (snap-action, clamping, location, ratchets, indexing, reversing, stop/pause ...) as flow-chart questions. (3) The friction-locking vs positive-locking split for anything that must not come loose. (4) The adhesive rule 'if two different materials are to be bonded, pick the adhesive that lists both' as a simple selector. (5) Stating when locking is really needed (guards, lifting, vibration, safety parts) before listing options
- **design_rules_given**: Yes, mostly for standard parts: adhesive shear strength (MPa), peel strength (N/mm) and temperature ranges by type; anaerobic retainers work with about 0.05 mm diametral clearance and an engagement length about 2x diameter; harmonic drives 30:1 to 350:1 per stage and usually below 6 kW; tapped-hole depth 1.5x diameter; key and spline strength, bolt preload and torque, spring design, ISO fits. Nothing for printed parts
- **generator_or_parametric**: No generators; formulae and tables only
- **file_formats**: HTML pages with GIF/JPG diagrams; links to external PDFs
- **terminology_value**: high for British/European engineering names and aliases (Bibby coupling, muff coupling, sprag clutch, mutilated gear, Nyloc/Simmonds nut, circlip, split cotter pin, tab washer) and for the matching BS/ISO/DIN standard numbers; no translations
- **decision_support**: Comparison tables for couplings, clutches, brakes and adhesives, plus the adhesive substrate-matching rule and the friction vs positive locking guidance; no interactive selector or flow chart

## 13 Free and public-domain machine design textbooks (Slocum FUNdaMENTALs of Design, UArk open textbook, US Navy Basic Machines)

*Encyclopedia / digital library.* MIT's free FUNdaMENTALs of Design, University of Arkansas' CC BY-NC open textbook, and the public-domain US Navy NAVPERS 10624 Basic Machines

### Basics

- **name**: Free and public-domain machine design textbooks: FUNdaMENTALs of Design (Alexander Slocum, MIT), Introduction to Mechanical Design and Manufacturing (David Jensen, University of Arkansas open textbook), and Basic Machines (US Navy NAVPERS 10624 / 10624-A)
- **resource_type**: book (three free textbooks; Slocum and Navy as PDF, UArk as an online Pressbooks book with PDF/eBook downloads); listed in the project as a digital library
- **cost**: Free (all three can be read or downloaded without payment); the Navy book is also sold in print as a Dover reprint (ISBN 0486217094)
- **language**: English
- **year**: Slocum: book copyright 2007, chapters dated 1 January 2008, web page updated 1 February 2008 (zip last modified April 2008); not updated since. UArk: copyright year 2024. Navy: first edition 1946 (NAVPERS 10624), revised 1965 (NAVPERS 10624-A, 15 chapters), Dover reprint 1971; the 1946 scan was uploaded to the Internet Archive in 2014
- **access_barrier**: none (direct PDF downloads for Slocum and the Navy book; UArk is open on Pressbooks, but its host was blocked from this research environment)
- **authority_level**: educator (Slocum is an MIT professor, book written for MIT course 2.007; Jensen is a University of Arkansas professor, open textbook funded by the university libraries) and official government training manual (US Bureau of Naval Personnel); none is peer-reviewed research

### Coverage

- **organisation**: All three are textbooks organised in chapters by component family (linkages, power transmission, bearings, joints) or, for the Navy book, by the classical simple machines (lever, pulley, wheel and axle, inclined plane, screw, gears) building up to complex machines. None is organised by what the reader wants to do. Slocum's two-page spreads (text page plus a picture collage page) are designed for browsing by picture, which helps someone who does not know the terms; the Navy book uses everyday shipboard examples (hatch dogs, capstans, anchor winches) and plain language. Finding a specific connection type means using each chapter's table of contents
- **taxonomy_used**: Slocum: 10 topics by design area (process, ideas, principles, linkages, power transmission I and II, power systems, structures, structural connections and interfaces, bearings); within linkages, joints are classed by degrees of freedom (single-DOF, multiple-DOF, higher pairs). Navy: the classical six simple machines (lever with 1st/2nd/3rd class, block and tackle, wheel and axle, inclined plane, screw, gears) plus physics chapters and 'complex machines' as combinations; the 1965 edition adds 'machine elements and basic mechanisms'. UArk: 16 parts following the design process and then manufacturing processes (casting and molding, forming and shaping, machining, joining, plastics)
- **example_types**: Toggle (over-centre) clamping linkage; scissor (extending) linkage; compliant flexure mechanism, folded-beam flexure and bistable flexure; flexural pawl and ratchet (cable clamp); slip clutch with adjustable spring torque; one-way spring clutch (spring pressed over shaft ends); capstan with one-way clutch; interference (press) fit; low-cost pinned shaft coupling; differential leadscrew; non-backdriveable (self-locking) leadscrew; planetary (epicyclic) gear train; open differential; worm drive and non-reversing worm; rack and pinion; kinematic coupling (three-groove) and quasi-kinematic coupling; elastic averaging; adhesive joint geometries (lap, scarf, double strap); Oldham coupling; spiral jaw (ratchet-type) clutch and overrunning (sprag) clutch; ratchet-and-pawl platen drive and escapement; detent roller and spring; pelican hook (quick release); interrupted-screw breech plug (twist lock); hatch dog (lever plus wedge)
- **making_methods**: General engineering and robotics (Slocum is written for the MIT 2.007 robot design contest and mentions FIRST robot teams; covers waterjet cutting and prototyping gears); UArk covers design for manufacturing and assembly with machining, casting, molding, forming, joining and plastic processes; Navy covers shipboard and workshop machinery. None is specific to 3D printing
- **audience_level**: Slocum: advanced high school to university (the author suggests using it for FIRST robot teams); UArk: undergraduate; Navy: school level / enlisted sailors with no engineering background

### Usefulness

- **gaps**: No index by function or behaviour, no plain-English lookup by what you want to achieve, nothing specific to 3D printing, snap fits or print-in-place, no CAD files; Slocum is dated (2008) and all rights reserved; the Navy book is 1940s-1960s technology (typewriters, ship machinery) and not organised around connections at all; the UArk book could not be opened from this environment
- **reusable_data**: Navy: fully reusable (public domain), with OCR text and PDFs on the Internet Archive. UArk: reusable non-commercially with attribution (CC BY-NC). Slocum: not reusable (all rights reserved), link only; the Excel spreadsheets are downloadable but no licence is given
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Slocum's 'picture page first' idea: let readers browse a collage of photos, then read the detail for whatever catches their eye, which suits people who do not know the terms. (2) Slocum's advice to 'turn over the whole box of parts' before choosing, i.e. show all the options in a family side by side. (3) The Navy book's method of explaining a complex device as a chain of simple machines ('start where the energy goes in and follow it step by step'), which could be a tip on each entry. (4) Use everyday examples (hatch dogs, capstans, jacks) to explain a principle before naming it. (5) Pair each type with a small calculator, as Slocum does with spreadsheets. (6) Reuse the public-domain Navy line drawings for gear, clutch, coupling, ratchet and detent entries
- **generator_or_parametric**: Slocum provides downloadable Excel design spreadsheets per chapter (linkages, power transmission, screws and gears, motors/springs/pistons, structures, interfaces, bearings, precision engineering); not geometry generators. Others: none
- **file_formats**: PDF (Slocum chapters and a whole-book zip; Navy scans with OCR text, EPUB for the 1971 edition; UArk PDF, eBook and XML per the Open Textbook Library), Excel XLS spreadsheets (Slocum), web pages (UArk)
- **terminology_value**: medium: good for proper names of classic machine elements (Oldham coupling, sprag clutch, kinematic coupling, quasi-kinematic coupling, elastic averaging, toggle, detent, escapement, pelican hook), and the Navy book gives plain-English equivalents ('blocks - pulleys to a landlubber'); no translations
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Original works. Slocum draws on his Precision Machine Design and MIT 2.007; the Navy Basic Machines overlaps with other Navy training manuals and the Dover reprint; all three overlap with standard machine design textbooks (Shigley, Norton) and with Wikipedia articles on the same elements. The UArk book is listed in the Open Textbook Library
