## 14 Robotics competition mechanism wikis (Game Manual 0, FRCDesign.org, Unofficial FRC Mechanism Encyclopedia)

*Encyclopedia / wiki.* Maker-level FTC/FRC guides to drivetrains, linear slides, intakes, gearboxes and mechanism examples, organised by function

### Basics

- **name**: Robotics competition mechanism wikis: Game Manual 0 (gm0, FTC), FRCDesign.org (FRC), and the Unofficial FRC Mechanism Encyclopedia (Project Bucephalus, FRC team 5985)
- **url**: gm0: https://gm0.org/en/latest/ (source repo https://github.com/gamemanual0/gm0). FRCDesign.org: https://frcdesign.org/ (mechanism examples https://frcdesign.org/mechanism-examples/, design handbook https://frcdesign.org/design-handbook/; source repo https://github.com/frcdesign/FRCDesign.org). Mechanism Encyclopedia: https://www.projectb.net.au/resources/robot-mechanisms/ (announced at https://www.chiefdelphi.com/t/the-unofficial-frc-mechanism-encyclopedia/398617)
- **resource_type**: encyclopedia/wiki (gm0 and FRCDesign.org are open-source documentation websites built from GitHub repos; the Mechanism Encyclopedia is a single web page of YouTube playlists)
- **cost**: Free
- **language**: English (the Mechanism Encyclopedia also offers Portuguese, Turkish, Spanish, Hebrew and French versions)
- **year**: gm0: version 0.2.0 released 14 September 2020 (earlier versions existed) and still updated, with its robot gallery including 2023 to 2025 seasons. FRCDesign.org: publicly launched 31 August 2024, with a 2025-2026 season update thread (August 2025, posts to May 2026). Mechanism Encyclopedia: posted 3 January 2022, updated since (mentions 2022-23 games; footer 2026)
- **access_barrier**: none for reading; FRCDesign's learning course exercises need a free Onshape account, and its FRCDesignLib part library needs a free access form
- **authority_level**: community (written by experienced FTC/FRC students and mentors, including championship-level teams; not official FIRST documents and not peer reviewed)

### Coverage

- **covers**: gm0 'Common Mechanisms' (about 35 pages of a roughly 130-page site that also covers team management, electronics and software): design tradeoffs; drivetrains (tank/skid-steer, holonomic, sample drivetrains); power transmission (motion mounting, shaft retention, direct drive, gears, roller chain, timing belt, glossary); linear motion (drawer slides, extrusion slides, linear bearing slides, custom slides, lead screws, rack and pinion, rigging); arms; linkages (linkage slides, four-bar, virtual four-bar); passive intake/claw; active intake (roller, wheeled, compliant wheel); transfers; dead wheels (odometry); full and mini turrets; plus motor gearbox internals (spur vs planetary), servo guide, fastener guide and 3D printing/machining/laser cutting guides. FRCDesign.org (169 pages, 114 of them in the Onshape learning course): design handbook on structure, materials, fasteners, 3D printing and motion components (flanged, standard, needle and X-contact bearings, bushings, hex and round axles); course projects on belts, chain, gears, shafts and bearings, motors, backlash, chain tensioning, compression, 'zombie axles', cable clamps, chain attachment, elevator bearing blocks and rigging; mechanism examples in 5 families (swerve drivebases, pivoting and linkage intakes, shooters, continuous and cascade elevators, pivots) with 3 in-depth write-ups. Mechanism Encyclopedia: about 44 YouTube playlists in 8 sections (game piece examples, 8 drivetrain types, 10 intake playlists by game piece, 5 indexers, 4 lift types, 7 shooter types, 5 climber types, 4 miscellaneous such as turrets and suction)
- **organisation**: By robot function (subsystem): drivetrain, intake, lift/linear motion, arm, shooter, climber, turret, power transmission. The Mechanism Encyclopedia adds a second key: the kind of object to be handled (small balls, large balls, cubes, rings, cones, poles), which lets a beginner look up by 'what do I need to pick up' without knowing any terms. gm0 and FRCDesign use sidebar trees and search. This works well for robot builders but not for someone looking for a joint or connection type
- **scope_match**: low to medium: mostly whole robot subsystems (drivetrains, shooters, intakes, climbers), which the project treats as whole machines, plus standard hardware; but they do cover in-scope transmission and motion types (planetary vs spur gearboxes, chain, belt, rack and pinion, lead screws, cascade vs continuous rigging, four-bar and virtual four-bar, over-centre linkages, shaft retention methods, bearings and bushings) at a practical maker level
- **taxonomy_used**: By subsystem/function. gm0 'Common Mechanisms' has 11 groups (design tradeoffs, drivetrains, power transmission, linear motion, arms, linkages, passive intake/claw, active intake, transfers, dead wheels, turrets). FRCDesign mechanism examples have 5 groups (drivebases, intakes, shooters, elevators, pivots). The Mechanism Encyclopedia has 7 mechanism sections (drivetrains, intakes, indexers, lifts, shooters, climbers, others), sub-sorted by game piece or by type
- **example_types**: Virtual four-bar (belt- or chain-linked arm that keeps the end level); four-bar linkage; linkage-driven slides; over-centre linkage to resist back-driving; cascade vs continuous elevator rigging; drawer-slide and extrusion-slide linear extensions; lead screw; rack and pinion; planetary vs spur gearbox; bevel gears; roller chain and timing belt (GT2/HTD) drives with idlers and turnbuckle tensioners; dead axle vs live axle vs 'zombie axle'; hex-shaft interfaces; set-screw vs clamping shaft collars and E-clip retaining rings; X-contact (four-point) bearing; needle bearing press-fitted into a roller end cap; crush blocks in box tube; compliant intake wheels; suction/vacuum grippers; turrets; mecanum, swerve, H-drive and kiwi drives
- **making_methods**: Competition robotics (FTC kit systems such as goBILDA and REV; FRC aluminium box tube, COTS parts), with 3D printing (PLA, PETG, TPU and more), CNC machining, laser cutting and Onshape CAD
- **item_count**: gm0: about 130 pages in total, about 35 of them on mechanisms. FRCDesign.org: 169 pages (114 learning course, 7 design handbook, about 12 mechanism example pages). Mechanism Encyclopedia: about 44 YouTube playlists, each holding many videos
- **entry_structure**: gm0: page per mechanism with plain description, highlighted 'Term' definitions, advantages and disadvantages, tips and warnings, photos of real team robots, and sometimes an interactive Onshape CAD example. FRCDesign.org: handbook pages with tables and photos; mechanism examples with links (CAD document, Chief Delphi build thread) and a 'Behind the Design' write-up of the reasoning (torque analysis, gearbox packaging); course pages with exercises. Mechanism Encyclopedia: table rows of type, short notes and a playlist link
- **audience_level**: competition robotics (high-school FTC/FRC students and mentors; hobbyist-friendly)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: high: written for rookie teams in plain language, with glossaries (gm0 'Know Your Lingo' and per-section glossaries; FRCDesign glossary) and lots of photos; some FTC/FRC slang and vendor names (goBILDA, REV, WCP, SDS) need decoding
- **has_images**: yes: many photos of real competition robots and CAD renders (e.g. 19 images on gm0's rigging page, 20 on FRCDesign's motion components page), generally good quality
- **has_animations**: yes, video: the Mechanism Encyclopedia is entirely YouTube playlists of robots in action; gm0 embeds some YouTube videos (e.g. on over-centre linkages) and links interactive Onshape CAD models you can drag to see motion; FRCDesign links public Onshape CAD documents
- **gaps**: Aimed at complete robot subsystems in a competition context, not at connection types; little on joints, latches, clutches, snap fits, print-in-place, adhesives or powered latches; heavy reliance on vendor-specific kit parts (goBILDA, REV, WCP); no index by behaviour (locks, slips, one-way); the Encyclopedia gives no explanations beyond one-paragraph notes
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes for gm0 and FRCDesign: stable per-topic URLs good for 'see a real example' links, e.g. https://gm0.org/en/latest/docs/common-mechanisms/linkages.html (four-bar, virtual four-bar, over-centre), https://gm0.org/en/latest/docs/common-mechanisms/power-transmission/shaft-retention.html, https://gm0.org/en/latest/docs/common-mechanisms/linear-motion-guide/rigging.html, https://frcdesign.org/design-handbook/power-transmission/motion-components/. gm0 uses a 'latest' version path, which is stable but may shift if pages are renamed. The Encyclopedia has only one page with anchor sections
- **maintained**: yes: gm0 is live on Read the Docs with a CI and link-check workflow, gallery updated to 2025; FRCDesign.org is actively developed (Stage 1A rework, Stage 2 rework, FRCDesignLib and FRCDesignApp in 2025-2026); the Mechanism Encyclopedia page is online with a 2026 footer, and the author promised continuous playlist updates
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Look up by the object or job, not by the term: the Encyclopedia's 'what game piece do you need to grab' key maps well to a flow-chart question such as 'what are you holding or moving?'. (2) gm0's Design Tradeoffs table (problem vs solution with short pros and cons) as a compact comparison format. (3) Highlighted 'Term' boxes that define the proper name inline, matching the project's plain-English-first style. (4) FRCDesign's 'Behind the Design' write-ups that explain why a real design chose a mechanism. (5) Link an interactive CAD model you can drag, or a playlist of real examples, from each entry. (6) Advantages/disadvantages lists and 'Danger/Tip' call-outs per type
- **design_rules_given**: some: gm0 gives gear centre-distance and pitch-diameter formulas, gear mesh advice, filament temperatures, and reach targets (more than 18 in extension, 24 in preferred); FRCDesign gives 3D-printing clearance of about 0.1 to 0.5 mm (0.004 to 0.020 in) depending on fit, teardrop holes (100 degrees), bolt sizes with tap and close-fit drill sizes, and keep gear reductions to 3 stages or fewer to limit backlash; the Encyclopedia gives none
- **generator_or_parametric**: FRCDesign's FRCDesignLib (a shared Onshape library of configurable parts such as spacers, crush blocks and HTD pulleys), FeatureScripts and KrayonCAD for quick robot layouts; gm0 links example Onshape documents; the Encyclopedia has none
- **file_formats**: Web pages; Onshape CAD documents (viewable in a browser, exportable to STEP/STL through Onshape); YouTube video; gm0 can be built to PDF from its source (make latexpdf)
- **terminology_value**: medium: good for maker/competition names and slang (virtual four-bar, cascade vs continuous rigging, dead/live/zombie axle, crush block, over the bumper intake, C2C centre-to-centre) with definitions, but these are community terms, not formal engineering names, and there are no translations except the Encyclopedia's page languages
- **decision_support**: gm0 Design Tradeoffs table comparing common choices (2 vs 4 drive motors, claw vs intake, spur vs planetary gearbox, arm vs linear extension) with short reasons and recommendations; FRCDesign 'intake golden rules' and backlash source/solution tables; the Encyclopedia's two-key table (subsystem by game piece) works as a simple lookup. No interactive selector or flow chart
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: gm0 is the FTC counterpart of FRC resources and links FTC Docs, REV documentation and Spectrum 3847's reading list; FRCDesign.org builds on Onshape learning courses and Chief Delphi / Open Alliance build threads (e.g. 6328, 1678, 2910 robots) and overlaps gm0 on power transmission and linear motion; the Mechanism Encyclopedia curates team reveal videos from YouTube (FRC, FTC, VEX) and was inspired by team 1678's 'steal from the best, invent the rest'

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - image_license
  - license
  - reusable_data
  - special_behaviours_covered

## 15 School and hobby mechanism education (DT Online wiki, NotesandSketches, Rob Ives paper automata)

*Encyclopedia / wiki.* UK Design & Technology mechanism teaching material and paper automata mechanism packs aimed at beginners

### Basics

- **name**: School and hobby mechanism education: DT Online wiki (UK Design & Technology), NotesandSketches Mechanisms Module (Laszlo Lipot) and Rob Ives paper automata mechanisms (Essential Mechanisms packs and Mechanism explainer pages)
- **url**: DT Online Mechanisms category: https://wiki.dtonline.org/index.php/Category:Mechanisms ; NotesandSketches Mechanisms Module: https://www.notesandsketches.co.uk/Mechanisms_module.html ; Rob Ives mechanism explainers: https://www.robives.com/mechanism/ and Essential Mechanism shop: https://www.robives.com/essentialmech/
- **resource_type**: Mixed: encyclopedia/wiki (DT Online, a MediaWiki site), teaching notes with animations and tests (NotesandSketches, HTML + PDF pages), and paper model library plus short explainer pages (Rob Ives, a shop of downloadable card-model templates with a membership)
- **cost**: DT Online: free (funded by Amazon affiliate links). NotesandSketches: HTML theory pages and PDFs free online; the terms page says Laszlo Lipot stopped trading in September 2016 and, from 1 January 2017, grants free educational use of resources marked 'free of charge' (older pages still show the old prices: GBP 30 single user, GBP 60 school site licence). Rob Ives: explainer pages free; individual Essential Mechanism templates GBP 5 each (GBP 2.50 each on his Substack page), Essential Mechanisms Pack One (12 models) GBP 15, or membership GBP 39 per year (GBP 35 recurring) that unlocks all downloads; a free account tier exists
- **language**: English (UK)
- **access_barrier**: DT Online: none. NotesandSketches: none for the HTML/PDF notes, but the animations are Flash (.swf) files that modern browsers cannot play, so only screenshots show. Rob Ives: none for the explainer pages; paywall (per-item purchase or membership) for the printable templates
- **authority_level**: educator (DT Online is written by D&T teachers and educationalists; NotesandSketches by a former head of D&T and Brunel University part-time lecturer; Rob Ives is a former teacher and professional paper engineer and author). None is peer-reviewed

### Coverage

- **covers**: School-level simple machines and mechanisms. DT Online: a Mechanisms category with 5 subcategories (Cams 7 pages, Gears 8, Levers 5, Linkages 5, Pulleys 10) and 19 direct pages (e.g. Ratchet, Crank and Slider, Whitworth Quick Return, Types of Motion, Velocity Ratio, Mechanical Advantage, links to 507 Mechanical Movements and 2100 Animated Mechanical Mechanisms), plus related categories: Fixings and Fastenings (10 pages incl. Hinges and Stays; Locks, Bolts and Catches; Knock Down Fittings; Pins and Clips; Keys, Keyways and Splines), Wood Joints (14 joints), Adhesives (4 pages) and Pneumatics. About 55 mechanism pages plus about 30 joining pages. NotesandSketches: about 30 topic pages (cams and followers, chain and sprocket, couplings incl. flanged, split, flexible and universal joint, crank and slider, bearings of 7 kinds, brakes, clamps, knurling, gears incl. worm and bevel, levers, linkages, pulleys and belts, ratchet and pawl, 5 spring types, screws, ball and roller screws) with 2 x 100-question tests and product-analysis exercises. Rob Ives: 15 short mechanism explainer pages (cam, cams, crank, crank slider, bell crank, piston, levers, linkages, gears, bevel gear, worm gear, rack and pinion, ratchet, Geneva, quick return) and about 55 buildable paper Essential Mechanism models (cams, cranks, gears, Geneva, Scotch yoke, springs, compound mechanisms)
- **organisation**: DT Online: MediaWiki category tree by D&T curriculum topic (Mechanisms > Cams / Gears / Levers / Linkages / Pulleys), with alphabetical page lists, search, a glossary category and 'Buyers Guides'. Easy for beginners because the topics follow the school syllabus and use everyday examples (scissors, bicycles, door handles). NotesandSketches: a single module index page grouped by topic (motion, levers, linkages, cams, gears, pulleys, friction, bearings, springs, screws), then exercises. Rob Ives: a paged list of mechanism explainers and a shop category of models; the Essential Mechanisms pack groups 12 models into 4 families (Cams, Cranks, Gears, Compound). All three start from 'what kind of motion do you want' (rotary, linear, reciprocating, oscillating), which suits beginners
- **scope_match**: low to medium: they explain the classic school mechanism types (ratchet, cams, crank-slider, Geneva, quick return, worm and bevel gears, linkages) in very plain language, and DT Online also covers adhesive types, knock-down fittings, catches, magnetic gears and solenoids, but the depth is shallow and there is nothing on torque limiters, compliant or print-in-place joints, snap fits or powered latches beyond a basic solenoid page
- **taxonomy_used**: UK D&T curriculum scheme: four types of motion (linear, rotary, reciprocating, oscillating) plus mechanism families. DT Online: 5 mechanism subcategories (Cams, Gears, Levers, Linkages, Pulleys) under Mechanisms, with separate Fixings and Fastenings, Wood Joints, Adhesives and Pneumatics categories. NotesandSketches: about 13 topic groups (mechanical systems, cams, chain and sprocket, couplings, cranks, friction and bearings, gears, levers, linkages, moments, motion, pulleys and belts, ratchet, springs, screws/motion control). Rob Ives: 4 families in the Essential Mechanisms pack (Cams, Cranks, Gears, Compound Mechanisms)
- **example_types**: ratchet and pawl (one-way mechanism), linear ratchet (as in cable ties and quick-release bar clamps), Geneva drive / Geneva stop (intermittent motion), double Geneva drive, Whitworth quick-return mechanism, crank and slider, Scotch yoke, bell crank, reverse-motion linkage, parallel-motion linkage, push-pull linkage, four-bar chain, snail cam (drop cam), swashplate, cylindrical (barrel) cam, eccentric cam, worm gear, bevel gear, rack and pinion, idler gear, magnetic gears (slip when overloaded), co-axial counter-rotating drive, fusee, cone pulley, block and tackle, windlass, knock-down (KD) fittings, keys, keyways and splines, flexible and universal-joint couplings, ball screw and roller screw, paper compression spring
- **making_methods**: School workshop making: card and paper modelling (Rob Ives; DT Online Primary pages), stripwood, MDF and plastic sheet, woodwork joints, simple electronics and pneumatics kits. General design and technology rather than 3D printing, CNC or robotics
- **item_count**: DT Online: about 55 mechanism pages (19 in Mechanisms plus 35 in its 5 subcategories) and 22 files in the category; whole wiki 576 articles and 2,074 images. NotesandSketches: about 30 mechanism topic pages, 200 test questions and about 15 exercises. Rob Ives: 15 mechanism explainer pages and 56 Essential Mechanism products (40 on page 1 including the pack, 16 on page 2)
- **entry_structure**: DT Online: image, Description, Features and Applications, embedded YouTube animations (often Nguyen Duc Thang clips) with one-line captions, Wikipedia links, buyers-guide template, category links. NotesandSketches: a screenshot linking to a Flash animation, a few paragraphs of plain explanation with bold key words, and a printable PDF version. Rob Ives explainers: one small image and 1-2 paragraphs. Rob Ives models: photo, description of the motion ('turn the handle and ...'), video, downloadable PDF template with build instructions
- **audience_level**: school (UK Key Stages 2-4, ages about 7-16) and hobbyist (paper automata makers, all ages)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: high: all three use short, everyday-language explanations with product examples (winches, fishing reels, bicycles, door handles, cable ties) and introduce the proper term in bold; Rob Ives describes each mechanism by what happens when you turn the handle, and his paper models let a beginner build and hold the mechanism
- **has_images**: yes: DT Online has simple labelled diagrams and photos (small, often low-resolution, some GIFs); NotesandSketches has clear textbook-style colour illustrations and animation screenshots; Rob Ives has good product photos of finished paper models and small diagrams on explainer pages
- **has_animations**: yes, with caveats: DT Online embeds YouTube animations (mostly from Nguyen Duc Thang's 2100 Animated Mechanical Mechanisms) and a few GIFs (animated radial cam, snail cam, Whitworth quick return, magnetic gears); NotesandSketches animations are Adobe Flash (.swf), which no longer play in current browsers; Rob Ives has YouTube videos of models working
- **gaps**: Shallow, school-syllabus coverage: no torque limiters, slip clutches, over-centre latches, cam cleats, bayonet mounts, snap fits, living hinges, compliant or print-in-place joints, harmonic or cycloidal drives; no design numbers for 3D printing; NotesandSketches animations are dead Flash; Rob Ives' buildable content is paywalled and paper-only; DT Online pages are small and some are stale (most edits 2016-2017) with broken-file-link pages
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes for DT Online and NotesandSketches, which have stable readable per-topic URLs, e.g. https://wiki.dtonline.org/index.php/Category:Mechanisms and https://www.notesandsketches.co.uk/Ratchet.html (with a PDF at https://www.notesandsketches.co.uk/PDF/Ratchet_and_pawl.pdf); Rob Ives explainer pages are stable too (e.g. https://www.robives.com/mechanism/ratchet/) and product pages suit a 'build a paper model of it' link (e.g. https://www.robives.com/project/essential-mechanisms-pack-one/)
- **reusable_data**: DT Online: structured and exportable through the MediaWiki API (https://wiki.dtonline.org/api.php returns category members, page wikitext and revision dates as JSON); reuse of the list and text is allowed under CC BY-NC-SA 3.0 (non-commercial, share-alike, attribution). NotesandSketches: scrape-only, copyright, educational use only. Rob Ives: scrape-only shop listings, all rights reserved
- **maintained**: DT Online: online, MediaWiki updated to 1.43.9, but mechanism content is largely unchanged since 2016-2017 and only one active user (latest recent change seen 24 February 2025). NotesandSketches: online but frozen since the 2016 trading stop; Flash animations no longer usable. Rob Ives: actively maintained (new models and 2025 book page; footer 2025). Wayback Machine could not be checked from this environment
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Start the flow chart with the four types of motion (linear, rotary, reciprocating, oscillating) using arrow icons, as DT Online and NotesandSketches do. (2) Describe every mechanism by 'turn this and that happens' (Rob Ives) before naming it. (3) Tie each type to everyday products (cable tie = linear ratchet, fishing reel = ratchet and pawl, film projector = Geneva) and to product-analysis exercises. (4) Offer a printable PDF version of each entry (NotesandSketches). (5) Link 'build a cheap cardboard model' options for hands-on learning (Rob Ives Essential Mechanisms). (6) Short self-tests or quizzes per family. (7) DT Online's Adhesive Types page is a compact model for a material-to-glue chooser
- **design_rules_given**: Few: school maths only (velocity ratio and gear ratio calculations, mechanical advantage, moments, lever classes); no clearances, fits, strain limits or torque values. DT Online adhesive pages give qualitative advice (contact adhesive left 10 minutes before bonding; acetone softens cyanoacrylate)
- **file_formats**: HTML, PDF (NotesandSketches notes and exercises; Rob Ives templates), GIF/PNG/JPG images, Flash SWF (NotesandSketches, obsolete), embedded YouTube video; DT Online also JSON via the MediaWiki API
- **terminology_value**: medium: good source for UK school terms and plain aliases (snail cam, bell crank, push-pull linkage, reverse-motion linkage, windlass, knock-down fittings, impact adhesive = contact adhesive, super glue = cyanoacrylate), but not authoritative for engineering naming
- **decision_support**: None as a selector; the only guidance is the motion-type grouping, DT Online buyers guides (e.g. Adhesives Buyers Guide) and NotesandSketches problem-solving exercises (choose a mechanism for a pedal bin, door handle, food mixer, waving display). No filters or comparison tables

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - generator_or_parametric
  - image_license
  - license
  - special_behaviours_covered
  - year

## 16 Chinese mechanism collections (HUANGYAN920/Mechanical, XAUAT typical mechanisms 3D library, Zhihu GIF compilations)

*Encyclopedia / digital library.* Chinese-language mechanism animation libraries and an MIT-licensed HTML showcase of 257 mechanisms in 11 categories

### Basics

- **name**: Chinese mechanism collections: HUANGYAN920/Mechanical ('MECH ENGINE' mechanism example library), Xi'an University of Architecture and Technology (XAUAT) 'Common typical mechanisms 3D library' (常见典型机构3D库), and Zhihu/CSDN mechanism GIF compilations (机械原理动图)
- **resource_type**: Mixed: GitHub repo with an interactive tool (HUANGYAN920, a single-file HTML page of parametric kinematic simulations); digital library of 3D CAD models from a university course site (XAUAT, SolidWorks eDrawings files); informal GIF compilations on a Q&A/blog platform (Zhihu, CSDN)
- **cost**: Free
- **language**: Chinese (Simplified); HUANGYAN920 also gives an English code name for each mechanism (e.g. SLIDER-CRANK, GENEVA, HARMONIC-DRIVE)
- **authority_level**: HUANGYAN920: community or unvetted (one GitHub author; not peer-reviewed; descriptions are short and unchecked). XAUAT: educator (an excerpt from an undergraduate research paper supervised by Associate Professor Tong Zhixue (同志学), compiled by Zhang Xizhong (张西仲), citing standard Chinese textbooks such as Sun Huan's Theory of Machines 7th ed.). Zhihu: community or unvetted

### Coverage

- **scope_match**: medium-high for HUANGYAN920: many in-scope mechanism and transmission TYPES, including unusual ones (harmonic drive, cycloidal drive, ball planetary reducer, magnetic gear, sprag and overrunning clutches, one-way bearing, ball detent, safety coupling, toggle clamp, differential screw, roller screw, Klann and Jansen linkages), but it is aimed at industrial machine design, not at connections, and has no snap fits, print-in-place, adhesives or powered latches except an electromagnetic chuck. Low for XAUAT (about 20 classic textbook examples, largely whole machines) and for the Zhihu compilations (unsourced GIFs)
- **taxonomy_used**: HUANGYAN920: 11 top-level categories, a Chinese Theory of Machines scheme extended with applications: linkage mechanisms (连杆机构), cam mechanisms (凸轮机构), gear mechanisms (齿轮机构), intermittent-motion mechanisms (间歇机构), screw mechanisms (螺旋机构), clamping mechanisms (夹紧机构), hydraulic mechanisms (液压机构), universal/coupling transmissions (万向传动), flexible (belt/chain/rope) transmissions (挠性传动), robot mechanisms (机器人机构), bio-inspired mechanisms (仿生机构). XAUAT: textbook chapters (linkage, cam, gear/gear train, other common mechanisms incl. intermittent, combined mechanisms, mechanisms in machines), with cam sub-classification by cam shape, follower shape, follower motion and closure method (force-closed vs form-closed), and gear trains split into fixed-axis, epicyclic and compound
- **example_types**: harmonic drive / strain-wave gear (谐波减速器), cycloidal pin-wheel reducer (摆线针轮减速), ball planetary reducer (钢球行星减速), few-tooth-difference planetary reducer (少齿差行星减速), magnetic gear (磁齿轮, slips when overloaded), non-circular (elliptical) gears, differential (差速器), overrunning clutch (超越离合器), sprag clutch / friction one-way clutch (摩擦式单向离合器), one-way bearing (单向轴承), reversible ratchet (双向棘轮), Geneva drive incl. star and twin-station versions (槽轮机构), escapement (擒纵机构), ball detent indexing (钢球定位), globoid indexing cam (弧面分度凸轮), safety coupling with shear pin or ball release (安全联轴器), Oldham coupling (十字滑块联轴器), toggle clamp / toggle press (肘杆), wedge clamp and wedge force booster (斜楔增力), eccentric cam clamp (偏心夹紧), expanding mandrel (涨瓣心轴), collet (弹性筒夹), differential screw (差动螺旋), planetary roller screw (行星滚柱丝杠), constant-breadth and constant-diameter cams, Jansen and Klann walking linkages, Peaucellier and Hart straight-line linkages
- **making_methods**: General mechanical engineering and machine design (industrial machinery, automation fixtures, vehicles); nothing specific to 3D printing, though the parametric simulations are useful for designing printed linkages and gears. XAUAT is SolidWorks CAD modelling and motion simulation
- **entry_structure**: HUANGYAN920: card with Chinese name, English code name, category and index number; detail pop-up with a large live SVG simulation, a one-sentence description (principle, features, typical use), a parameter quick-reference table (e.g. principle, function, reset method, typical application), sliders for geometry (crank radius, link length, tooth count, pivot positions), speed and zoom, a pausable timeline, presets for some mechanisms, export to SVG/PNG/parameter JSON and a shareable link. XAUAT: working principle paragraph, a link to the 3D eDrawings model with motion, and a description of the simulated velocity/displacement curves. Zhihu: GIF plus a caption
- **audience_level**: HUANGYAN920: undergraduate and professional machine designers (useful to hobbyists who can read the English code names). XAUAT: undergraduate. Zhihu: hobbyist and general public

### Usefulness

- **gaps**: All in Chinese; HUANGYAN920 has no plain-language explanation, no design rules, no real-world photos, no sources, and some simulations are schematic stand-ins (e.g. the safety coupling reuses a two-disc sketch); nothing on joints for 3D printing, snap fits, compliant hinges, adhesives or powered latches. XAUAT is small, dated 2010 and effectively unviewable. Zhihu GIFs are unsourced reposts, often watermarked, and not stable to link
- **reusable_data**: HUANGYAN920: highly reusable. The whole library is one MECHANISMS JavaScript object in index.html, with a key, Chinese name, English code, category, index number, description, parameter table, slider definitions and a build() drawing function per mechanism; MIT licence allows reuse of the list, text and code with attribution. XAUAT: scrape-only list, no licence. Zhihu: not reusable
- **maintained**: HUANGYAN920: active in 2026 (README says 257 mechanisms, all parametric; demo online and identical to index.html). XAUAT: online but frozen since July 2010, and its parent course directory returns 403/404. Zhihu: posts online but not maintained collections; could not be opened from this environment
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Live, parameter-driven simulations instead of GIFs: let the reader drag link lengths or tooth counts and see the motion change. (2) A pausable timeline to freeze and explain a moment. (3) One data object per mechanism (name, English name, category, one-line description, key facts table, sliders) driving the whole site, as a lightweight catalogue format. (4) A 'key facts' table per entry with rows such as principle, function, reset method (e.g. 'replace part' for a shear-pin coupling) and typical application; the reset row maps well onto the project's reuse and removability filters. (5) Share links that encode the mechanism and settings. (6) Export an SVG/PNG of the current pose for use in entries (MIT licensed). (7) XAUAT's habit of teaching each type through a real application (landing gear = dead-point lock, projector = Geneva) helps beginners
- **design_rules_given**: Few: HUANGYAN920 gives qualitative parameter tables (e.g. repeatability 'good', feel 'clear' for the ball detent) and simulation ratios, not numeric design rules. XAUAT gives simulated speeds and ratio checks for its examples and the conditions for constant-velocity double universal joints. No clearances, fits or torque values
- **generator_or_parametric**: Yes (HUANGYAN920): every one of the 257 mechanisms is parametric in the browser (sliders for geometry, speed and zoom, presets such as offset quick-return or double crank) and can export SVG, PNG and parameter JSON. It is a 2D kinematic drawing tool, not a CAD/STL generator. XAUAT and Zhihu: no
- **terminology_value**: high for Chinese names: HUANGYAN920 pairs standard Chinese mechanism names with English code names for 257 types (e.g. 槽轮机构 = Geneva, 超越离合器 = overrunning clutch, 谐波减速器 = harmonic drive, 钢球定位 = ball detent, 肘杆 = toggle), useful for adding Chinese aliases; check the English codes against a proper glossary since some are abbreviations
- **decision_support**: None: HUANGYAN920 has category filters, text search and hit counts but no selector, flow chart or comparison tables; XAUAT and Zhihu have none

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - access_barrier
  - beginner_friendliness
  - covers
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - file_formats
  - good_for_entry_links
  - has_animations

## 17 BOSL2 (Belfry OpenSCAD Library v2)

*CAD library.* OpenSCAD library with hinges, snap pins, dovetails, joiners, gears, threading, rabbit clips and more

### Basics

- **name**: BOSL2 (Belfry OpenSCAD Library v2)
- **url**: https://github.com/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2 (code and README); documentation wiki at https://github.com/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/wiki (read here through its raw wiki source, because github.com pages are blocked in this environment)
- **resource_type**: CAD library (an OpenSCAD code library with a large auto-generated reference wiki)
- **cost**: Free
- **license**: BSD 2-Clause (the LICENSE file says 'Copyright (c) 2017-2019, Revar Desmera'; openscad.org also lists it as BSD-2-Clause). This is a permissive licence: the code can be reused, changed and shipped (even commercially) as long as the copyright notice is kept. Linking to the wiki pages is always fine
- **language**: English
- **access_barrier**: none to read the wiki in a browser; to generate parts you need a desktop install of OpenSCAD (2021.01 or later) plus the library, and you have to write a little OpenSCAD code
- **authority_level**: community or unvetted (a large, well-known open-source community project started by Revar Desmera, now under the BelfrySCAD organisation with several maintainers; listed on the official openscad.org Libraries page, but not a standard or a peer-reviewed source)

### Coverage

- **covers**: A very large general OpenSCAD toolkit (51 library files, 959 entries in the function/module index), of which a smaller 'parts library' matters for connections: joiners.scad (half joiners and full joiners that screw together, dovetails, snap pins and sockets, rabbit clips, Hirth face splines); hinges.scad (knuckle hinges with a filament, screw or print-in-place cone pin, living hinges, folding hinges with snap locks); partitions.scad (cutting a big model into two interlocking pieces with jigsaw, dovetail, hammerhead, finger, comb, sawtooth or sine-wave seams); gears.scad (spur, helical, herringbone, internal ring, rack, bevel incl. spiral, crown/face, worm, double-enveloping worm, plus a planetary-gear ratio calculator); threading.scad (ISO/UTS, trapezoidal, ACME, square, buttress, NPT and BSPP pipe, garden hose, ball-screw threads); bottlecaps.scad (PCO-1810, PCO-1881, SPI and mason-jar necks and caps and adapters); screws.scad and screw_drive.scad (screws, nuts, nut traps, drive recesses); cubetruss.scad (snap-together truss clips and joiners); sliders.scad (V-groove rail and slider); modular_hose.scad (ball-and-socket modular hose segments); tripod_mounts.scad (Manfrotto RC2 quick-release plate); ball and linear bearings; NEMA stepper mounts; and hooks.scad. The 'Parts' topic lists 64 entries; about 30-40 modules are directly about joints, fasteners or motion parts
- **organisation**: By code file (a Table of Contents listing each .scad file and its sections), plus an A-Z index of every function and module, a topic index (158 topics such as Joiners, Hinges, Gears, Threading, Bottles, Screws, Modular Hose, Parts), a cheat sheet and a tutorial list. It is organised like a programming reference, not by what a maker wants to do. A beginner who already knows the word 'dovetail' or 'hinge' can find it quickly through the Topics page; someone who only knows 'I want two parts to click together' has no route in
- **scope_match**: medium. It generates several clever connection TYPES that fit the scope well (rabbit clips with an optional one-way lock, snap pins, print-in-place hinges, living hinges with snap locks, Hirth couplings, interlocking partition seams, dovetails, a full range of gear types, ball-and-socket hose), but it is a code library, not a catalogue: much of it is general geometry tools, and parts like screws, bearings and bottle threads are standard hardware
- **taxonomy_used**: No mechanism taxonomy. Grouping is by library file (51 files: constants, transforms, attachments, shapes, masks, ..., threading, screws, screw_drive, bottlecaps, ball_bearings, cubetruss, gears, hinges, joiners, linear_bearings, modular_hose, nema_steppers, polyhedra, sliders, tripod_mounts, walls, wiring, hooks), then sections inside each file. Within joiners.scad the sections are Half Joiners, Full Joiners, Dovetails, Tension Clips and Splines; hinges.scad has Hinges and Snap Locks; gears.scad groups gears (spur, ring, rack, crown, bevel, worm), gear assemblies and dimension calculations
- **example_types**: Rabbit clip (rabbit-eared snap clip, with optional locking flanges); Snap pin and snap-pin socket (split snap pin, fixed or free-turning); Half joiner / joiner (screw-together interlocking tab joint); Dovetail joint (straight, tapered, rounded 'puzzle piece' style); Hirth joint (Hirth face spline coupling); Knuckle hinge with filament or screw pin; Print-in-place knuckle hinge with interlocking cones; Living hinge (flexure hinge) mask; Folding hinge with snap locks (snap_lock / snap_socket); Jigsaw, dovetail, hammerhead, finger, comb, sawtooth and sine-wave interlocking partition seams; Spur, helical and herringbone gears; Internal ring gear; Rack and pinion; Straight, zerol and spiral bevel gears; Crown gear (face gear); Worm and worm gear, double-enveloping worm; Planetary gear set (ratio calculator); Buttress, ACME, trapezoidal and square threads; Ball screw rod; Bottle neck/cap adapters (PCO-1881, mason jar); Garden hose couplers; Modular ball-and-socket hose; Cube truss snap clip; V-groove rail and slider; Manfrotto RC2 quick-release plate
- **making_methods**: 3D printing first (FDM: clearances, '$slop' printer calibration, teardrop holes, print-in-place hinges, print orientation notes), and parametric CAD in OpenSCAD generally. Nothing on CNC, woodwork or laser cutting as such, although dovetail slopes follow woodworking practice
- **item_count**: 959 indexed functions, modules and constants across 51 library files; 64 entries under the 'Parts' topic; about 30-40 connection/mechanism modules (joiners.scad 11, hinges.scad 6, gears.scad about 11 geometry modules plus about 17 calculation functions, partitions.scad 4, threading.scad 20)
- **special_behaviours_covered**: torque-limiting: no; self-locking: partly (worm drives are included, but self-locking is not discussed; the rabbit clip's lock=true makes a clip that 'may be irreversible'); hand-release only: yes, sort of (the rabbit clip docs say that leaving an access point in the socket wall gives a clip that locks but can be released by pressing its ears); one-way/ratchet: partly (locking rabbit clips go in easily but block removal; no ratchet or pawl module); indexing/detent: partly (Hirth face spline gives fixed angular positions; no detent module); over-centre: no; compliant: yes (living hinges, snap pins, rabbit clips and snap locks all rely on the part flexing); print-in-place: yes (knuckle_hinge in_place=true with interlocking cones); gear trains/gearboxes: yes, strongly (spur, helical, herringbone, ring, rack, bevel, crown, worm, planetary ratio solver, backlash and profile shift); powered connectors: no (only NEMA stepper motor models and mount masks); adhesive types: no
- **entry_structure**: Each module has: name and one-line synopsis, topics, 'see also' links, usage signatures, a written description (often several paragraphs of practical advice), an arguments table with defaults, and numbered examples each with the OpenSCAD code and a rendered image; some add labelled dimension figures (e.g. the rabbit clip). Degrees of freedom and strength data are not given
- **audience_level**: hobbyist to professional (makers who can write OpenSCAD code; the gear section is fairly technical)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low to medium. The explanations of individual parts are clear and practical (e.g. how to tighten a snap pin, why hinges need clearance, what backlash is), and every example has a picture. But you must write OpenSCAD code, the pages are long programming references, and there is no plain-English way to discover which joint you need
- **has_images**: yes. Every example has a clean rendered image of the generated geometry (joiners.scad has about 46 images, hinges.scad about 29, gears.scad about 111), plus some dimension diagrams. They are computer renders, not photos of printed parts
- **gaps**: No selector or flow chart; organised by code file, not by need; needs coding skills and a desktop install; no photos of real printed results or strength test data; no torque-limiting, ratchet/pawl, detent, over-centre or cam-lock modules; no powered connectors or adhesives; still beta, with API changes; module anchors on the wiki can change when code is reorganised
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes, reasonably. Each file has its own wiki page and each section and module has a stable-looking anchor, e.g. https://github.com/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/wiki/joiners.scad#section-tension-clips (rabbit clips and snap pins), https://github.com/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/wiki/hinges.scad and https://github.com/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/wiki/joiners.scad#section-dovetails. Anchors follow the pattern <file>.scad#module-<name>. Good as a 'make it yourself' link from an entry
- **reusable_data**: structured and reusable: all code and the wiki are in Git repositories (BelfrySCAD/BOSL2 and its wiki repo, readable as Markdown), and the docs are generated from comments in the source, so a list of modules, topics and synopses could be scraped from the .scad files or the wiki Markdown. The code is BSD-2-Clause, so the list and even the geometry generators can be reused with attribution
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Each connection explained with its 'knobs' in plain terms: e.g. the snap pin's tightness is set by 'preload' and 'clearance', the rabbit clip by 'snap' depth and 'compression' - the encyclopedia could list the 2-3 settings that matter for each type. (2) A single printer calibration value ('$slop') with a printable calibration test, which the encyclopedia could recommend before printing any fitted joint. (3) Showing variants side by side (locking vs non-locking clip, filament pin vs print-in-place hinge, dovetail vs jigsaw seam). (4) 'See also' links between related joints. (5) Offering a 'make it yourself' link to a parametric generator on each entry. (6) The partition seam patterns (jigsaw, dovetail, hammerhead, finger, comb) as a neat visual list of ways to split a big print into pieces
- **design_rules_given**: yes, for several types: snap pin default clearance 0.2 mm and preload 0.2 mm; rabbit clip default compression 0.1 mm and clearance 0.1 mm, socket about 0.4 mm deeper than the clip, with sizes tested in PLA on a Prusa MK3; knuckle hinge default gap 0.2 mm between segments, clearance 0.15 mm ('a layer thickness or two'), default pin hole for 1.75 mm filament, print-in-place cone angle 45 degrees; living hinge leaves 2 layer heights of material; dovetail slopes 1:4, 1:6 or 1:8 (woodworking standard), $slop added on each side of the socket; modular hose clearance 0.05-0.1 mm in PETG; $slop calibration procedure (typical value e.g. 0.15 mm); gear backlash and profile-shift guidance
- **generator_or_parametric**: yes, this is its whole purpose: parametric OpenSCAD modules that generate custom geometry for every part (clips, pins, hinges, dovetails, gears, threads and so on)
- **file_formats**: SCAD source; OpenSCAD can then export STL, 3MF, OFF, AMF, SVG and DXF (not STEP). The docs are Markdown wiki pages with PNG renders
- **terminology_value**: medium: uses correct engineering names (Hirth face spline, crown/face gear, zerol bevel gear, double-enveloping worm, buttress thread, profile shifting, backlash) and explains them briefly, and the gear section cites tec-science references. It also coins its own names ('rabbit clip', 'half joiner', 'cubetruss') that are not standard terms
- **decision_support**: none: no selector, flow chart or comparison table. The nearest thing is prose advice inside each module (e.g. rabbit clip vs snap pin: 'unlike the snap pin, this clip is rectangular and can be made at any height') and 'see also' cross-links
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Successor to BOSL v1 (revarbat/BOSL, 2017, not compatible). Overlaps MCAD (gears, nuts and bolts), NopSCADlib (hinges, gears, vitamins) and threads.scad, all listed on openscad.org/libraries.html. Its gear maths follows the tec-science.com references it cites. Cite BOSL2 itself for its clip and joiner designs, which are original to the library

### Other Info

- **sources**: 
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/master/README.md (README: features, parts library, beta note, installation)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/master/LICENSE (BSD 2-Clause, 2017-2019 Revar Desmera)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/master/version.scad (BOSL_VERSION = [2,0,759])
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/Home.md (wiki home, documentation indices)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/TOC.md (list of 51 files and all sections and modules)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/AlphaIndex.md (A-Z index, 959 entries)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/Topics.md (158 topics; Parts topic with 64 entries)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/Tutorials.md (tutorial list)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/joiners.scad.md (joiners, dovetails, snap pins, rabbit clips, Hirth spline, default clearances)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/hinges.scad.md (knuckle, print-in-place, living hinges, snap locks)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/partitions.scad.md (interlocking partition cut patterns)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/gears.scad.md (gear types, backlash, references)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/threading.scad.md (thread types)

  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/modular_hose.scad.md (modular hose clearances)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/revarbat/BOSL/master/README.md (BOSL v1 predecessor)
  - https://openscad.org/libraries.html (official list: BOSL2 (beta), BSD-2-Clause)
  - https://hackaday.com/2025/02/18/belfry-openscad-library-bosl2-brings-useful-parts-and-tools-aplenty/ (Hackaday article, 18 February 2025)
- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - has_animations
  - image_license
  - maintained
