## 18 OpenSCAD libraries list (MCAD, NopSCADlib and others)

*CAD library.* The official openscad.org list of libraries and the notable mechanism/joint libraries in it

### Basics

- **name**: OpenSCAD libraries list (openscad.org Libraries page, with MCAD, NopSCADlib and the other notable mechanism/joint libraries)
- **url**: https://openscad.org/libraries.html (official list). Related lists: https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/OpenSCAD_User_Manual/Libraries (OpenSCAD User Manual page with the MCAD section and an older 'Other libraries' list) and https://github.com/openscad/openscad/wiki/Libraries (older GitHub wiki list, read via its raw source)
- **resource_type**: CAD library (a curated directory of OpenSCAD code libraries; each listed item is itself a CAD library)
- **cost**: Free (the list and every library on it)
- **language**: English (a few libraries have non-English names inside the code, e.g. UB.scad uses German module names such as Gewinde (thread), Glied (hinge) and CyclGetriebe (cycloidal gear))
- **access_barrier**: none to read the list and the library docs; a desktop install of OpenSCAD is needed to generate parts (MCAD is bundled with OpenSCAD, the others must be downloaded)
- **authority_level**: community or unvetted (the list is kept by the OpenSCAD project, which gives it some official standing, but the libraries are community projects of mixed quality; the Wikibooks page itself says MCAD bundles 'a lot of stuff, of varying quality')

### Coverage

- **covers**: The official page lists 29 libraries in two groups. General (11): BOSL, BOSL2 (beta), dotSCAD, NopSCADlib, UB.scad, Functional OpenSCAD, Constructive, StoneAgeLib, BOLTS, Asset Collection, Board game toolkit. Single Topic (18): Round Anything, Mark's Enclosure Helper, funcutils, threads.scad, Smooth Primitives, Function Plotting, ClosePoints, Tray, YAPP project box generator, STEMFIE Parts Library, Catch'n'Hole, Pathbuilder, SCON, Altair's 2D Library, Doll house, Dimensions, LEGO.scad, Technic.scad. MCAD is NOT on the openscad.org page; it is described on the Wikibooks page as the library bundled with OpenSCAD. The libraries most relevant to connections and mechanisms are: BOSL2 (clips, snap pins, dovetails, hinges, Hirth joints, gears, threads - see its own entry); MCAD (involute and bevel gears, nuts and bolts, bearings, screws and augers, stepper and servo mounts, teardrop and polyholes, trochoids, gridbeam, LEGO compatibility); NopSCADlib (148 sections of parts: printed flat hinge, door hinge and door latch, press-fit connectors, drag chain, strap handle, knobs, printed pulleys, involute gears, plus 'vitamins' such as magnets, an OpenGrab electro-permanent magnet, circlips, springs, heat-set inserts, shaft couplings, rod ends, belts, pulleys, leadnuts, rails); UB.scad (thread, ball and roller bearing, hinge, double hinge, snap/clip-in pin, cycloidal gear, spring-loaded axle clamp); Mark's Enclosure Helper (hinged boxes with snap-fit catch clasps, magnet or screw closures, dovetails for joining boxes); STEMFIE (a printable construction toy of interlocking connections and fasteners); threads.scad; Catch'n'Hole (nut catches, screw holes); LEGO.scad and Technic.scad; Board game toolkit (boxes with catches). The older Wikibooks and GitHub-wiki lists add a U-Joint (universal joint) library, a roller-chain sprocket module, a '2D connection library' for joining laser-cut sheets, aluminium-extrusion connectors and a pinball parts library
- **organisation**: A short flat page with two groups, 'General' and 'Single Topic', and a jump menu at the top. Each entry has a one-paragraph description, a Library link, a Documentation link and a licence. There is no search, no tags and no grouping by function, so you have to read the blurbs to know that, say, Mark's Enclosure Helper has snap-fit catches. It is easy to read but gives no route from 'I need two parts to click together' to a library
- **scope_match**: low to medium. The list itself is a directory of code libraries, not a catalogue of connection types, and most entries are general geometry tools. A handful of the listed libraries (BOSL2, NopSCADlib, UB.scad, MCAD, Mark's Enclosure Helper, STEMFIE) do generate in-scope connection and mechanism types, and NopSCADlib's vitamins are largely standard hardware, which is out of scope
- **taxonomy_used**: Two top-level groups only: 'General' (broad libraries) and 'Single Topic' (focused libraries). Inside the libraries: MCAD groups files as 'Currently provided tools', 'Tools (alpha and beta quality)', 'Very generally useful functions and constants', 'Other' and external utils; NopSCADlib groups its 148 sections into six columns: Vitamins A-H, Vitamins I-Q, Vitamins R-Z (bought-in parts), Printed (printed parts), Utilities and Core Utilities
- **example_types**: Snap pin / clip-in pin (UB.scad Pin, BOSL2 snap_pin); Rabbit clip (BOSL2); Dovetail joint (BOSL2, Mark's Enclosure Helper); Print-in-place hinge (BOSL2); Living hinge (BOSL2); Flat hinge with filament pin (NopSCADlib flat_hinge); Double hinge (UB.scad DGlied); Snap-fit catch clasp (Mark's Enclosure Helper); Magnetic catch (Mark's Enclosure Helper, NopSCADlib magnets); Electro-permanent magnet (NopSCADlib OpenGrab EPM, magnetised and de-magnetised electronically); Press-fit connector (NopSCADlib press_fit, optionally glued or solvent-welded); Door latch (NopSCADlib); Retracting strap handle printed in flexible filament (NopSCADlib); Cable drag chain with 45 degree max bend per link (NopSCADlib); Cycloidal gear (UB.scad CyclGetriebe); Involute spur and bevel gears (MCAD involute_gears, NopSCADlib gears); Universal joint (U-Joint library, older list); Roller chain sprocket (older list); Hirth joint (BOSL2); Heat-set threaded insert (NopSCADlib inserts); Circlip / retaining ring (NopSCADlib); Rod end / spherical bearing (NopSCADlib); Shaft coupling (NopSCADlib); Swiss picture clip (NopSCADlib); LEGO Technic-compatible pins and beams (Technic.scad); Laser-cut sheet connections (2D connection library, older list)
- **making_methods**: Mainly 3D printing (FDM) and parametric CAD; NopSCADlib also targets CNC-routed sheet parts (DXF) and 3D printer/electronics enclosure building; the older list includes laser-cut sheet joints and CNC drilling templates (pinball library)
- **item_count**: 29 libraries on the openscad.org page (11 General, 18 Single Topic); about 25 more on the older Wikibooks 'Other libraries' list and about 20 on the old GitHub wiki list (with overlaps). Inside them: MCAD about 40 .scad files; NopSCADlib 148 documented sections; BOSL2 959 indexed functions and modules
- **special_behaviours_covered**: torque-limiting: no library found; self-locking: not as a type (worm gears only in BOSL2); hand-release only: partly (BOSL2 locking rabbit clip with a release access point; snap-fit catch clasps in Mark's Enclosure Helper); one-way/ratchet: no dedicated ratchet module found; indexing/detent: partly (BOSL2 Hirth spline); over-centre: no; compliant: yes (BOSL2 living hinges and snap pins, UB.scad snap-in pin, NopSCADlib strap handle in flexible filament, snap-fit catches); print-in-place: yes (BOSL2 print-in-place hinge); gear trains/gearboxes: yes (MCAD involute and bevel gears, NopSCADlib involute gears and geared motors, UB.scad cycloidal gear, BOSL2 full gear set and planetary calculator); powered connectors: partly (NopSCADlib OpenGrab electro-permanent magnet, magnets, steppers, servos, geared motors, microswitches; no solenoid latch or motorised lock found); adhesive types: no (NopSCADlib press_fit only says 'add solvent or glue to make a permanent fixture')
- **entry_structure**: On the list: name, one-paragraph description, Library link, Documentation link, licence. Inside NopSCADlib: part name, short description, links to the definition, implementation and test code, tables of properties, functions and modules, a rendered image, and a bill-of-materials style list of vitamins. Inside MCAD: only a bullet list of file names with a few words each
- **audience_level**: hobbyist to professional (OpenSCAD users; NopSCADlib is aimed at people building 3D printers and electronics enclosures)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low. The list is short and readable, but it only tells you which code library to download; to get any connection you must install OpenSCAD and write code, and the libraries' docs are programming references. MCAD's docs are especially thin
- **has_images**: The official list: essentially none. The libraries: BOSL2 and NopSCADlib have many rendered images of every part (NopSCADlib has a full rendered catalogue in its readme and a gallery); MCAD has none in its README
- **has_animations**: no (none on the list or the MCAD/NopSCADlib readmes)
- **gaps**: No grouping by what the connection does; no search, tags or filters; no pictures on the list; MCAD (the bundled library) is missing from the official page and has minimal documentation; no torque-limiting, ratchet, detent, over-centre or cam-lock generators found in the listed libraries; no adhesive information; powered connectors only as off-the-shelf part models; mixed licences (GPL and LGPL libraries need care if code is reused); older lists contain dead links (Google Code, Google+)
- **good_for_entry_links**: partly. The list page has per-library anchors (e.g. https://openscad.org/libraries.html#NopSCADlib, #BOSL2, #STEMFIE), which are fine for a 'find more generators' link but not for single connection types. Individual parts are better linked in the libraries' own docs (e.g. BOSL2 wiki module anchors, or the #flat_hinge style anchors in NopSCADlib's readme on GitHub)
- **reusable_data**: The list is scrape-only (a small static HTML page, no API; no licence stated for the page), but the Wikibooks copy is CC BY-SA 4.0. The libraries themselves are in public Git repositories and are reusable under their own licences (BSD-2, MIT and CC0 ones freely; GPL/LGPL ones with conditions)
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) For each encyclopedia entry, a short 'Make it yourself' line naming a generator library and its licence, as the official list does (Library / Documentation / License). (2) NopSCADlib's split into 'vitamins' (things you buy) and 'printed' parts is a clear, beginner-friendly way to show whether a connection is printed, bought or both. (3) NopSCADlib's single-page catalogue with a rendered picture per part, and its rules written as plain notes (e.g. drag chain '45 degree maximum bend per link', flat hinge 'can bend back to 270 degrees'). (4) Grouping 'General' vs 'Single topic' is too coarse; the encyclopedia should group by function instead, which is the gap this list leaves
- **design_rules_given**: Not on the list. Inside libraries: NopSCADlib gives some (drag chain 45 degree max bend per link; flat hinge margins to open 270 degrees; gears profile-shifted below 19 teeth at 20 degree pressure angle, 7 teeth practical minimum; heat-set inserts pushed in with a soldering iron at 200 C); BOSL2 gives clearances for clips, pins and hinges (see its own entry); MCAD gives almost none (polyholes and teardrops are print-accuracy helpers)
- **generator_or_parametric**: yes, every listed library is a parametric OpenSCAD generator; the list itself is not
- **file_formats**: SCAD source for all libraries; OpenSCAD exports STL, 3MF, SVG and DXF. NopSCADlib's Python scripts also produce STL files for all printed parts, DXF files for CNC-routed parts, bills of materials and assembly manuals
- **terminology_value**: low for the list itself. Inside the libraries it is mixed: NopSCADlib uses standard supplier names for vitamins (circlips 'aka tapered retaining rings', rod ends 'sometimes called a spherical bearing or rod-end eye'), which helps with aliases; UB.scad gives German terms (Gewinde, Glied, Getriebe) useful for translations
- **decision_support**: none: no selector, filters or comparison tables, just two groups and short descriptions
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: The openscad.org page overlaps the older Wikibooks 'Libraries' page and the openscad GitHub wiki 'Libraries' page (both older and partly stale). BOSL2 has its own separate entry in this research, so do not double count it here. MCAD includes code taken from Thingiverse (involute_gears from thing:3575, teardrop from thing:3457); NopSCADlib's printed pulleys remix droftarts' Parametric Pulleys (Thingiverse thing:16627, CC BY-SA) and its gear maths follows KHK and tec-science references. BOLTS overlaps standard-parts catalogues (out of scope)

### Other Info

- **sources**: 
  - https://openscad.org/libraries.html (official list: 29 libraries with descriptions and licences)
  - https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/OpenSCAD_User_Manual/Libraries (MCAD section and older 'Other libraries' list; last edited 26 March 2026; CC BY-SA 4.0)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wiki/openscad/openscad/Libraries.md (older OpenSCAD GitHub wiki library list, incl. U-Joint library and extrusion connectors)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/openscad/MCAD/master/README.markdown (MCAD README: file list, LGPL 2.1, maintenance notes)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/openscad/MCAD/master/involute_gears.scad (header: 2010 GregFrost, LGPL 2.1)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nophead/NopSCADlib/master/readme.md (NopSCADlib catalogue of 148 sections: hinges, latch, press fit, drag chain, gears, magnets, OpenGrab EPM, inserts)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nophead/NopSCADlib/master/CHANGELOG.md (release v21.43.1, 2026-09-24)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nophead/NopSCADlib/master/docs/usage.md (requirements and scripts)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/UBaer21/UB.scad/main/README.md (UB.scad parts: thread, bearing, hinge, snap-in pin, cycloidal gear)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sbambach/MarksEnclosureHelper/master/REFERENCE.md (hinged box with snap-fit catch clasps, magnets, dovetails)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/BelfrySCAD/BOSL2/master/README.md (BOSL2, covered in its own entry)
- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - image_license
  - license
  - maintained
  - year

## 19 GitHub awesome lists (awesome-openscad, awesome-3d-printing, awesome-mechanical-engineering, awesome-robotics)

*GitHub repo or dataset.* Curated link lists that may point to mechanism and joint libraries

### Basics

- **name**: GitHub awesome lists: awesome-openscad (dahoo), awesome-3d-printing (ad-si), awesome-mechanical-engineering (m2n037/awesome-mecheng and brandonhimpfen), awesome-robotics (kiloreux)
- **url**: https://github.com/m2n037/awesome-mecheng (most useful of the group). Others: https://github.com/dahoo/awesome-openscad, https://github.com/ad-si/awesome-3d-printing, https://github.com/brandonhimpfen/awesome-mechanical-engineering, https://github.com/kiloreux/awesome-robotics (all READMEs read via raw.githubusercontent.com)
- **resource_type**: GitHub repo or dataset (curated link lists, 'awesome lists', each a single Markdown README)
- **cost**: Free
- **language**: English
- **year**: awesome-robotics (kiloreux) created 2015, last commit Sept 2024; awesome-3d-printing (ad-si) created Jan 2016, last commit Aug 2026; awesome-openscad (dahoo) created Jan 2016, last commit May 2022; awesome-mecheng (m2n037) created June 2016, last commit Sept 2024; brandonhimpfen/awesome-mechanical-engineering created Jan 2025, last commit Sept 2026
- **access_barrier**: None (public GitHub READMEs)
- **image_license**: Almost no images of their own (badges, one banner image in awesome-mecheng). Images belong to the linked sites
- **authority_level**: Community or unvetted: volunteer-curated link lists accepting pull requests, no editorial review of technical content

### Coverage

- **covers**: None of the four catalogues connection or mechanism types themselves; they are pointers. awesome-mecheng (m2n037, about 386 links) is the only one that points straight at mechanism libraries: 507 Mechanical Movements, thang010146's 2,100+ animated mechanisms, DMG-Lib, KMODDL, Animated Engines, CMU 'Introduction to Mechanisms', the Chironis/Sclater 'Mechanisms and Mechanical Devices Sourcebook', the Linkage simulator, MechaniCalc bolted-joint and lug analysis, and plastic design guides (BASF snap-fit manual, Bayer snap-fit joints, MIT living hinge guide, DuPont design principles). awesome-openscad (dahoo) has only 8 library links, a few relevant: a 2D connection library (YouMagine), BOLTS, MCAD, a threads library and a universal-joint (U-joint) library on Thingiverse. awesome-3d-printing (ad-si, about 190 links) covers printers, CAD tools (incl. build123d, OpenSCAD, Onshape), slicers, firmware, model repositories and online tools; the only connection-related items are the Free Universal Construction Kit (adapters between toy construction systems) and a Gridfinity layout tool. awesome-robotics (kiloreux, about 120 links) is about courses, books, software (ROS, planners, simulators), papers and competitions, with nothing on joints, gearboxes or hardware connections. brandonhimpfen's list (about 45 links) is generic software (CAD, FEA, CFD) and societies
- **organisation**: Each is one README split into headed sections by resource kind (courses, books, software, CAD tools, slicers, etc.), not by what the thing does. awesome-mecheng has the deepest tree (Mechanics > Theory of Machines > Mechanism Design; Manufacturing > Plastic). For a beginner looking for a connection, this ordering is unhelpful: you must already know that 'mechanism design' or 'plastic snap-fit manual' is what you want
- **scope_match**: low: they are lists of tools and courses, not catalogues of connection types. awesome-mecheng is medium as a discovery aid, because its 'Useful Websites', 'Mechanism Design' and 'Plastic' sections point to the key mechanism libraries and snap-fit guides already in this project's prior-art list
- **taxonomy_used**: No mechanism taxonomy. Grouping is by resource type/discipline: awesome-mecheng about 10 top-level groups (General, Mathematics, Programming, Mechanics, Theory of Machines, Thermal Engineering, Manufacturing, FEA, Software Packages, SubReddits, plus a GATE exam section); awesome-3d-printing 16 groups; awesome-robotics 10 groups; brandonhimpfen 10 groups; awesome-openscad 1 group (Libraries)
- **example_types**: Only reachable through links, not described in the lists: snap-fit joints (cantilever snap hooks) via the BASF and Bayer manuals; living hinges via the MIT guide; linkages (four-bar etc.) via the Linkage simulator and CMU Introduction to Mechanisms; universal joint (Cardan/U-joint) via the OpenSCAD U-Joint library; screw threads via the threads library; bolted joints and lugs via MechaniCalc; the 507 movements and thang010146's 2,100+ animated mechanisms (Geneva drives, cams, ratchets, gear trains) via links; universal construction-kit adapters (Free Universal Construction Kit)
- **making_methods**: Mixed: 3D printing (awesome-3d-printing, awesome-openscad), general mechanical engineering (awesome-mecheng, brandonhimpfen), robotics software (awesome-robotics)
- **item_count**: About 750 links in total: awesome-mecheng about 386, awesome-3d-printing about 190, awesome-robotics about 124, brandonhimpfen about 45, awesome-openscad 8 (counted from the READMEs on 2026-09-25)
- **special_behaviours_covered**: None directly. Indirectly, through linked resources: snap-fit and compliant/living hinges (plastic design guides), gear trains, ratchets and indexing mechanisms (507 Movements, thang010146, DMG-Lib, KMODDL). No links for torque limiters, powered connectors, print-in-place or adhesives
- **entry_structure**: One line per entry: linked name plus a short description (sometimes a host tag such as `MIT.edu`, `youtube`, an ISBN or a :dollar: paid flag)
- **audience_level**: Undergraduate (awesome-mecheng, awesome-robotics), hobbyist (awesome-3d-printing, awesome-openscad), professional (brandonhimpfen)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low: one-line descriptions, many links to textbooks and professional software; nothing explains a connection type
- **has_images**: no (text lists only)
- **has_animations**: no (but they link to animation collections such as 507movements.com, Animated Engines and thang010146's YouTube channel)
- **good_for_entry_links**: no: nothing to link from a single entry. The repo pages themselves (e.g. https://github.com/m2n037/awesome-mecheng) are stable and suit a single 'further reading' page, not individual entries
- **reusable_data**: Structured enough to scrape (Markdown bullet lists with links), not a dataset. Reuse: CC0 for awesome-openscad and awesome-3d-printing (free for any use); GPL-3.0 for awesome-mecheng (copies must stay GPL); WTFPL-style for kiloreux; unclear for brandonhimpfen
- **maintained**: awesome-3d-printing and brandonhimpfen's list are active (commits in Aug-Sept 2026). awesome-mecheng and kiloreux/awesome-robotics last committed Sept 2024 (open, not archived, 22 and 40 open issues). awesome-openscad (dahoo) last committed May 2022 and is effectively dormant
- **ideas_to_borrow**: Use awesome-mecheng's 'Useful Websites' and 'Plastic' sections as a checklist of upstream sources to cite (507 Movements, thang010146, DMG-Lib, KMODDL, snap-fit and living-hinge manuals). Copy the awesome-list habit of tagging each link with host and whether it costs money (`:dollar:`), which would help beginners. A small 'Further reading' page in awesome-list form (by making method) could sit at the end of the encyclopedia. The openscad.org Libraries page model (one card per library with licence and docs link) is a better template than the lists themselves
- **design_rules_given**: No (the lists give no numbers; the linked snap-fit manuals and MechaniCalc pages do)
- **generator_or_parametric**: No, but they point to generators: OpenSCAD libraries (MCAD, threads, U-joint, 2D connection library), build123d, the Linkage simulator, a 3D box generator and the Gridfinity layout tool
- **file_formats**: Markdown README only (awesome-3d-printing includes a comparison table of 3D file formats: 3MF, AMF, STEP, STL, OBJ, etc.)
- **terminology_value**: low: names only, no definitions or aliases
- **decision_support**: None (no filters, flow chart or comparison tables for connections; awesome-3d-printing's only table compares file formats)
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: All follow the sindresorhus/awesome format. awesome-mecheng overlaps heavily with this project's other prior-art items (507 Movements, thang010146, DMG-Lib, KMODDL, BASF/Bayer snap-fit guides); awesome-openscad overlaps with the official openscad.org Libraries page, which is the fuller upstream list; kiloreux/awesome-robotics links to jslee02/awesome-robotics-libraries and ahundt/awesome-robotics (software/ML focused)

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - gaps
  - license

## 20 Other parametric CAD libraries (cq_warehouse / bd_warehouse, Onshape FeatureScript libraries)

*CAD library.* CadQuery/build123d part libraries and Onshape custom features that generate snap hooks, lip joints, gears and chains

### Basics

- **name**: Other parametric CAD libraries: cq_warehouse (CadQuery), bd_warehouse (build123d), cq_gears, and Onshape FeatureScript custom-feature libraries (Onshape Custom Features spotlight, dcowden 'Awesome FeatureScripts', FRCDesign FeatureScript list)
- **url**: https://bd-warehouse.readthedocs.io/en/latest/index.html (bd_warehouse docs); https://cq-warehouse.readthedocs.io/en/latest/index.html (cq_warehouse docs); https://github.com/gumyr/bd_warehouse; https://github.com/gumyr/cq_warehouse; https://github.com/meadiode/cq_gears; https://www.onshape.com/en/features/custom-features; https://github.com/dcowden/featurescript; https://frcdesign.org/resources/featurescripts/
- **resource_type**: CAD library (Python code-CAD part generators for CadQuery/build123d, and Onshape custom features written in FeatureScript), plus two community link lists of FeatureScripts
- **language**: English
- **year**: cq_warehouse: repo created July 2021, docs v0.8.0 (c) 2022, last commit Jan 2024. bd_warehouse: repo created June 2023, first PyPI release 0.1.1 in Nov 2025, latest 0.3.0 on 2026-07-12. cq_gears: created July 2021 (v0.51). Onshape FeatureScript: launched 2016 (Snap Hook and other staff examples date from 2016); dcowden list created Aug 2016, last commit April 2021; FRCDesign list current in 2026
- **access_barrier**: Python libraries: desktop install (Python plus CadQuery or build123d via pip) and some coding. Onshape features: free login to Onshape (browser-based), then 'Add custom features' from the toolbar; no coding needed to use them
- **authority_level**: Community or unvetted, with some manufacturer backing: cq_warehouse/bd_warehouse are by one lead developer (gumyr, also the build123d author) following ISO/DIN tables; Onshape's Snap Hook, Spur Gear, Belt, Lap Joint and Wave Spring features were written by Onshape staff (manufacturer); most other FeatureScripts are by community members (many from FIRST Robotics Competition teams)

### Coverage

- **covers**: Generators for standard mechanical parts and a handful of joint types, not a catalogue of types. bd_warehouse: bearings with press-fit holes, eccentric hex-flanged bushings, threaded fasteners and holes, flanges, involute gears (spur, helical, rack, worm and worm wheel), OpenBuilds parts, O-rings with gland grooves, pipes, retaining rings with grooves, DIN 6885 parallel keys and keyways, sprockets, threads (ISO, Whitworth, BSPP, Acme, trapezoidal, plastic bottle). cq_warehouse: bearings, roller chain wrapped around any set of sprockets, fasteners, sprockets, threads, finger-jointed boxes. cq_gears: spur, helical, herringbone, ring, planetary gearsets, straight and helical bevel gears, rack. Onshape: the official spotlight shows about 16 features (incl. Snap Hook, Spur Gear, Belt around pulleys, Thread Creator, Straight Spline, Mounting Boss); the dcowden list has 95 FeatureScripts in groups including Joinery (snap hook, box/dovetail finger joint, mortise and tenon, lap, rabbet, T-slot, butt joint with pin, break-off tabs) and Gears/Drives (spur gear, planetary gears, drive-train generator, belt, sync rotator, wave spring, metric keys, straight spline); FRCDesign lists about 40 robotics FeatureScripts (belt and chain generator, sprockets, GT2 timing pulleys, polycord belts, robot shafts and spacers, thrifty insert). No lip-and-groove joint generator was found in any of these libraries
- **organisation**: Python libraries: one documentation page per module named after the part (gear, sprocket, chain, bearing...), then per class. Onshape: flat 'spotlight' grid on onshape.com; dcowden groups by function (Joinery; Surfaces; Manufacturing and 3D printing; Primitives; Structures; Fasteners, Threads and Hardware; Gears, Drives, Shafts and Pulleys; Patterning; Sheet Metal; Analysis; Misc); FRCDesign groups by robot sub-task (Recommended, Structure, Power Transmissions, Pocketing, Manufacturing). Works only if you already know the part's name
- **scope_match**: medium: gear trains (planetary, worm, helical, rack, bevel), chain and belt drives, snap hooks, keyed and splined shaft joints, eccentric bushings and joinery are in scope as types, and the generators let makers produce them; but most content is standard hardware (fasteners, flanges, pipes, bearings) that the project leaves out, and nothing explains when to choose which
- **taxonomy_used**: No formal taxonomy. Python libraries: by part family (about 12 modules in bd_warehouse, 7 in cq_warehouse). dcowden: 11 functional groups. FRCDesign: 5 groups by robot sub-task. build123d's own assembly joints use a joint-type scheme: rigid (fixed), revolute (hinge), linear (slider or pin-in-slot), cylindrical (screw), ball (gimbal)
- **example_types**: involute spur gear; helical gear; herringbone gear; rack and pinion; worm and worm wheel (worm gear); bevel gear; planetary (epicyclic) gearset; ring gear; roller chain and sprocket drive; timing belt (GT2) and pulley drive; round polycord belt drive; cantilever snap hook; finger (box) joint and dovetail; mortise and tenon; T-slot bolted joint; break-off tabs; wave spring; parallel key and keyway (DIN 6885); straight spline shaft; hex-flanged eccentric bushing (for adjusting centre distance / belt tension); retaining ring (circlip) in a groove; press-fit bearing hole
- **making_methods**: 3D printing (FeatureScripts for 3D-printed mass, thrifty inserts, snap hooks), CNC/laser cutting and sheet metal (kerf compensation, dogbones, finger joints, auto layout), competition robotics (FRC power transmission), general engineering (ISO/DIN parts)
- **special_behaviours_covered**: gear trains/gearboxes: yes, strongly (spur, helical, herringbone, bevel, worm, planetary, rack, plus Onshape Drive Train Generator that picks gears for a target reduction ratio, and chain/belt drives). compliant: partly (cantilever Snap Hook, break-off tabs, wave spring). self-locking: only implicitly (worm gears can be non-backdrivable, not explained). adjustable: eccentric bushing. torque-limiting, hand-release-only, one-way/ratchet, indexing/detent, over-centre, print-in-place: no. powered connectors: no (only cable/wire routing features). adhesives: no
- **entry_structure**: Python docs: short plain description, code example, parameter list, rendered picture, calculated properties (pitch radius etc.), some design notes. Onshape features: a public document with the feature, short description, sometimes a video; the dcowden and FRCDesign lists give one-line descriptions and sometimes video examples
- **audience_level**: Hobbyist-to-professional coders (Python libraries); competition robotics and hobbyist (Onshape FeatureScripts via FRCDesign); professional (Onshape spotlight)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium for Onshape features (point-and-click, no coding, but jargon like 'module', 'pressure angle', 'keyway' is unexplained); low for the Python libraries (needs Python and CAD kernel install, and knowledge of the part's standard)
- **has_images**: yes: rendered CAD images in the Python docs (gears, chains, bearings, pillow block example) and document thumbnails on Onshape pages; clear but schematic
- **gaps**: No explanation of what each connection does or when to use it; no comparisons. Snap Hook FeatureScript makes only the hook, not the matching groove, catch or clearance gaps (an April 2025 Onshape forum thread asks exactly this). No lip-and-groove, bayonet, living hinge, print-in-place, detent, ratchet or torque-limiter generators found in these libraries. Few print tolerances. Python route needs coding; Onshape route needs an account and public documents on the free plan
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes for gear and drive entries: stable per-module doc pages, e.g. https://bd-warehouse.readthedocs.io/en/latest/gear.html (gears) and https://cq-warehouse.readthedocs.io/en/latest/chain.html (chain around sprockets). Onshape documents have stable public URLs (e.g. the Snap Hook document linked from the dcowden list), but opening them needs JavaScript and may prompt for login
- **maintained**: bd_warehouse: active (release 0.3.0, July 2026; 37 open issues). cq_warehouse: effectively superseded by bd_warehouse (last commit Jan 2024, not archived). cq_gears: 'work in progress', not archived. dcowden FeatureScript list: dormant since April 2021 (95 entries, some may be outdated). Onshape custom features page and FRCDesign list: current in 2026
- **ideas_to_borrow**: Link each gear/drive entry to a free generator so a maker can go from 'I need a worm drive' to printable geometry. Borrow bd_warehouse's plain-English opening lines (e.g. why involute gears mesh smoothly, telescope-mount example) and its meshing rule. Borrow FRCDesign's grouping by the job you are doing (structure, power transmission, pocketing) as a flow-chart branch. Borrow build123d's joint pairs (rigid/fixed, revolute/hinge, linear/slider, cylindrical/screw, ball/gimbal) as a simple degrees-of-freedom vocabulary for the finder. Note in snap-fit entries that generators often make only one half of the joint
- **design_rules_given**: Some: gear meshing rule (same module and pressure angle; centre distance = module x (n1 + n2) / 2 mm) in bd_warehouse; DIN 6885 key widths for shafts 6-50 mm with a note to check key length for shear (DIN 6892); ISO/DIN dimensions for threads, O-ring glands and retaining ring grooves; bearing press-fit holes. No snap-fit strain limits or 3D-print clearances found
- **generator_or_parametric**: yes: this is their main purpose. Python classes generate parametric solids (CadQuery/build123d); Onshape custom features generate geometry inside a Part Studio from typed parameters
- **terminology_value**: medium for gears and drives (correct ISO/DIN terms: module, pressure angle, transverse vs normal module, worm flank, addendum/dedendum, tooth tip shape); low for other connection types
- **decision_support**: Almost none. Only calculators: Onshape Drive Train Generator picks gear sizes for a target reduction ratio; FRCDesign's Origin Cube functions compute gear diameters and belt/chain centre distances; 'doesinlinechaintensionerfit' checks a tensioner fits. No selector between connection types
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: bd_warehouse is the build123d successor of cq_warehouse (same author, same fastener/thread/sprocket/bearing modules); cq_gears overlaps both for gears; build123d's 'External Tools and Libraries' page also lists py_gearworks (gear and drive generator) and bd_vslot. Onshape's spotlight features (Snap Hook, Spur Gear, Belt) also appear in the dcowden list; FRCDesign bundles features from several FRC authors (Julia's, Alex's, 2471, TLamp's, CADSHARP's). Overlaps with OpenSCAD libraries (BOSL2 gears, NopSCADlib) listed on openscad.org

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - cost
  - file_formats
  - has_animations
  - image_license
  - item_count
  - license
  - reusable_data

## 21 Boxes.py

*CAD library.* Open-source laser-cut generator with finger and dovetail joints, flex cuts, living hinges, hinges, gears and pulleys

### Basics

- **name**: Boxes.py (Florian Festi's open-source laser-cut box and parts generator)
- **url**: https://boxes.hackerspace-bamberg.de/ (online generator, hosted by Hackerspace Bamberg; also mirrored at https://www.festi.info/boxes.py/). Source: https://github.com/florianfesti/boxes (read via raw.githubusercontent.com). Docs: https://florianfesti.github.io/boxes/html/index.html
- **resource_type**: CAD library (Python library plus a web generator and Inkscape plug-in); also usable as an interactive tool
- **cost**: Free
- **license**: Software is GPL v3+ (README and LICENSE.txt). The FAQ says the drawings you generate are NOT covered by the GPL, so you may cut and sell the objects; the author asks people not to resell unmodified generated files. Linking is fine; reusing its code means keeping it GPL
- **language**: English (docs and code); the web generator interface is also translated into German, Spanish, French, Brazilian Portuguese and Simplified Chinese
- **year**: Started around 2013 (edges.py header reads 'Copyright (C) 2013-2016 Florian Festi'); Hackaday.io project page created 30 March 2016; Hackaday Prize entry in 2017 (Hackaday article, 10 March 2021). Latest docs build 'Last updated on Sep 23, 2026'
- **access_barrier**: none for the web generator (runs in the browser, no login); desktop install (Python 3, plus pstoedit for DXF/G-code/PDF) only if you want to run it locally or write your own generators
- **authority_level**: community or unvetted (a long-running open-source maker project led by one developer, Florian Festi, with many contributors; not peer-reviewed, but widely used and tested by makers)

### Coverage

- **covers**: Laser-cut sheet joints and the parts built from them. The core 'edge' types (about 40 edge classes in edges.py) are: finger joints (box joints) for 90-degree corners, with four finger styles (rectangular, springs, barbs, snap); finger-hole edges for T-joints and inner walls; flat dovetail joints for joining pieces in the same plane (JointPanel makes panels larger than the laser bed); flex cuts (kerf bending) for rounded corners and living hinges; pin hinges and hinge pins; chest hinges; cabinet hinges (knuckle hinges with separate eyes); slide-on lids with locking springs; click-on lids (bent-hook snap connectors); stackable edges with feet; grooved edges; gripping edges; mounting edges with keyhole (pear-shaped) screw holes; handle edges; rack (gear rack) edges; bed bolts (captive nut and bolt slots) in finger joints. On top of these sit 182 ready-made generators in 8 groups, including mechanical parts: spur gears, a multi-stage gearbox, planetary gears (one generator marked unstable), timing belt pulleys, a linkage test model, a servo robot arm, NEMA stepper motor mounts, serrated knobs, a bayonet (twist-lock) box, latched service hatches, rolling shutter doors made of flex, sliding lids and drawers, a laser-cutter clamp and holdfast, and test pieces for kerf (burn) and flex settings
- **organisation**: The web generator groups its 182 generators by what you are making: Boxes, Boxes with flex, Trays and drawer inserts, Shelves, Wall-mounted, Hole patterns, Parts and samples, Misc, and Unstable, with a photo gallery. Joint types are not browsable by name in the web UI; they appear as folded 'edge settings' inside each generator and on the AllEdges demo generator. The API docs list edges by a one-letter code (f/F finger joint, d/D dovetail, X flex, i/j/k hinge, c/C click, R rack...). Easy to use if you know the object you want (a box with a hinged lid), less so if you are looking for a joint type
- **scope_match**: medium: it is a working catalogue of clever flat-pack joints and moving parts for laser cutting (snap and barbed finger joints, living hinges, printed-in-sheet pin and cabinet hinges, bayonet twist-lock, click-on lids, slide-on lids with spring catches, gear trains, planetary gears, belt pulleys, rack and pinion), but laser cutting is a later, lower-priority making method for the project, and most generators are whole boxes and shelves rather than connection types
- **taxonomy_used**: Two levels: (1) by object, 8 top-level generator groups plus 'Unstable' (Boxes, Boxes with flex, Trays and drawer inserts, Shelves, Wall-mounted, Hole patterns, Parts and samples, Misc); (2) by edge type, a flat list of about 40 edge classes in the API (straight, finger joint, finger holes, dovetail, flex, hinge, chest hinge, cabinet hinge, slide-on lid, click connector, stackable, grooved, gripping, mounting, handle, rack, rounded triangle), each with a Settings class
- **example_types**: finger joint (box joint), including 'springs', 'barbs' and 'snap' finger styles; finger-hole T-joint (mortise-and-tab); flat dovetail joint (jigsaw-style panel joint); flex cut living hinge (kerf-bent lattice hinge); laser-cut pin hinge (knuckle hinge with separate pin); chest hinge; cabinet hinge (multi-eye knuckle hinge with a bored pin); slide-on lid with locking springs (sliding lid with detent catch); click-on lid (cantilever snap hook); bayonet lid (twist-lock box of stacked rings); spring-latched service hatch (press-and-slide latch); rolling shutter (tambour door made from flex cuts); stackable edges with feet (nesting/stacking interlock); keyhole mounting slots (pear-shaped screw holes); captive-nut bed bolts in finger joints (T-slot nut and bolt); spur gears; multi-stage gearbox; planetary (epicyclic) gear set; timing belt pulley; rack and pinion edge; linkage test kit (four-bar linkage)
- **making_methods**: Laser cutting of sheet material (plywood, MDF, acrylic, cardboard, paper); the generated SVG/DXF also works for CNC routing and knife/vinyl cutters. Not aimed at 3D printing
- **item_count**: 182 generators in the docs list (September 2026); about 40 edge classes in boxes/edges.py; about 172 sample photos in the gallery
- **special_behaviours_covered**: Compliant: yes, flex cuts (living hinges, rolling shutters, FlexTest samples) and spring/barb/snap finger styles. Indexing/detent and snap-in: partly, via slide-on lid 'locking springs' and click-on lid hooks. Self-locking / twist-lock: yes, the BayonetBox (lugs that lock when twisted). Hand-release only: yes, the SideDoorHousing service hatches use laser-cut spring latches that lock by themselves when closed and open only when pressed in and slid aside (generator source notes); SideHingeBox also has a latch. Gear trains/gearboxes: yes, spur gears, a multi-stage GearBox, Planetary gear sets (Planetary2 'balanced force difference planetary gear' marked not yet working), rack and pinion, timing belt pulleys. Powered connectors: only mounts for motors (NEMA stepper mounts, servo robot arm segments); no electromagnets or solenoid latches. Torque-limiting, one-way/ratchet, over-centre: none found. Print-in-place: not applicable (laser-cut). Adhesives: none, apart from the manual's note that brittle acrylic should be cut undersized to leave a gap for glue while plywood can be press-fitted without glue
- **entry_structure**: Each generator page has: name, one-line description, a parameter form (sizes in mm, material thickness, burn/kerf correction, edge settings for every joint type used, output format), a live preview of the cut sheet, a gallery photo, and Generate/Download/Save-to-URL/QR-code buttons. The API docs give each edge type's class, settings with default values (mostly as multiples of material thickness) and a short explanation
- **audience_level**: hobbyist (makerspace laser-cutter users); the Python API is aimed at programmers who want to write their own generators

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: the web form lets a beginner get a working box by entering size, material thickness and kerf, and the FAQ tells beginners to leave edge settings folded. But joint settings are named in code jargon ('burn', 'surroundingspaces', 'edge_width', one-letter edge codes), there is no explanation of when to choose which joint, and the manual admits the burn setting is 'counter intuitive'
- **has_images**: yes: a photo gallery of about 172 real, cut-and-assembled sample objects (good quality, maker photos), plus an SVG preview of the cutting plan for every generator; no labelled diagrams explaining how each joint works
- **has_animations**: no animations or videos on the pages opened; the SVG preview updates when you change parameters (interactive, but static 2D)
- **gaps**: No per-joint explanations or diagrams (joints are only visible inside finished boxes); no guidance on strength, which joint to choose, or glue vs press fit beyond the kerf notes; nothing for 3D printing; only laser-cut sheet geometry; no special behaviours such as ratchets, torque limiting or over-centre latches; gears and planetary parts are basic and one planetary generator is marked unstable; English-only documentation
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes: every generator has a stable per-item URL on the web generator, e.g. https://boxes.hackerspace-bamberg.de/BayonetBox?language=en (twist-lock box) and https://boxes.hackerspace-bamberg.de/AllEdges?language=en (all edge types, including finger styles). Parameters can be saved into the URL, so an entry can link to a pre-set example
- **reusable_data**: structured: the whole library is a GPL v3 Python repo (boxes/edges.py for joint geometry, boxes/generators/ for 182 generators, boxes/gears.py and boxes/pulley.py), with Sphinx API docs listing every edge and its default settings. The list of generators and edge types can be reused (facts are not copyrightable); copying code or images means following GPL v3
- **maintained**: Yes, actively maintained: the docs were rebuilt on 23 September 2026 (Sphinx 9.1.0) and the online generator was live on 25 September 2026. The Hackaday.io project page was 'last updated a year ago', so news goes to GitHub rather than Hackaday
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Express every design rule as a multiple of material thickness (finger width 2t, hinge axle 2t, dovetail depth 1.5t) so one rule scales to any sheet; the same trick could scale 3D-printed joint rules to wall thickness or nozzle size. (2) A kerf/clearance test piece (BurnTest, FlexTest) linked from each joint entry, so makers can tune fit before printing the real part. (3) An 'all edges' demo page that shows every joint side by side. (4) 'Save to URL' links that open a generator with the exact settings used in an entry's example. (5) Colour conventions in output files (black outer cut, blue inner cut, red help lines, green engraving). (6) Grouping generators by the object the maker is building as a second way into the catalogue
- **design_rules_given**: Yes, for laser-cut joints: finger joint defaults are finger 2t, space 2t, play 0 (t = material thickness); dovetail angle 50 degrees, size 3t, depth 1.5t; flex cut connection 1t, distance 0.5t, width 5t, stretch 1.05; hinge axle 2t, hinge strength 1t; cabinet hinge bore 3.2 mm, eye radius 1.5t, play 0.05t; click-on hooks bend out 5 degrees, depth 3t; gear rack module 3 mm, 20 degree pressure angle. The manual gives kerf tuning in 0.01-0.005 mm steps and says tabs of 0.2-0.3 mm in plywood still let parts be pushed out by hand; snap fingers are only drawn for fingers 1.9t wide or more
- **generator_or_parametric**: Yes, fully parametric: 182 web generators and a Python API (turtle-graphics style edge drawing) to write new ones; also runs as an Inkscape extension and from the command line
- **file_formats**: SVG and PostScript natively; with pstoedit also DXF, G-code, PDF, PLT (HPGL); LightBurn (lbrn2) and an SVG variant for Ponoko
- **terminology_value**: low to medium: uses common maker names (finger joint, dovetail, living hinge, flex, chest hinge, cabinet hinge, bayonet, burn = kerf) but coins its own class names and one-letter codes, and does not give aliases or translations of joint names
- **decision_support**: No selector, flow chart or comparison table. Choice is by browsing object groups and the photo gallery, then tuning parameters in a form; the only 'decision' help is the FAQ on fit (thickness, burn) and test-piece generators

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - image_license

## 22 HCI fabrication research on joints (Joinery by Zheng, SpringFit, kyub, Metamaterial Mechanisms)

*GitHub repo or dataset.* Research tools and catalogues of laser-cut joints and cell-based metamaterial mechanisms (e.g. a one-piece door latch)

### Basics

- **name**: HCI fabrication research on joints: Joinery (Zheng, Do and Budd), SpringFit, kyub and Metamaterial Mechanisms (Hasso Plattner Institute HCI lab, Baudisch et al.), plus related Kerf-Canceling Mechanisms and Flaticulation
- **url**: No single home page; a cluster of project pages: Joinery web tool https://clementzheng.github.io/joinery/ (code https://github.com/clementzheng/joinery); SpringFit https://hpi.de/baudisch/projects/springfit.html; kyub https://hpi.de/baudisch/projects/kyub-a-3d-editor-for-modeling-sturdy-laser-cut-objects.html (app at https://kyub.com/); Metamaterial Mechanisms https://hpi.de/baudisch/projects/metamaterial-mechanisms.html (editor https://jfrohnhofen.github.io/metamaterial-mechanisms/, code https://github.com/jfrohnhofen/metamaterial-mechanisms)
- **resource_type**: GitHub repo or dataset / interactive tool (research prototypes with academic papers); not a catalogue in the encyclopedia sense
- **language**: English
- **year**: Metamaterial Mechanisms UIST 2016 (follow-up 'Understanding Metamaterial Mechanisms', CHI 2019); Joinery web tool (c) 2016, Instructable published 6 Feb 2017, paper at Creativity & Cognition 2017; kyub CHI 2019; SpringFit UIST 2019; Kerf-Canceling Mechanisms UIST 2020; Flaticulation UIST 2022. Pages still online in September 2026 (HPI pages 'Copyright 2026'; CMU Interactive Structures page 'Last update: 21 Sep 2026')
- **authority_level**: academic peer-reviewed (ACM UIST, CHI and Creativity & Cognition papers, mostly from the Hasso Plattner Institute HCI lab (Patrick Baudisch), plus Joinery by Clement Zheng, Ellen Yi-Luen Do and Jim Budd, and Flaticulation by Chiao Fang, Vivian Hsinyueh Chan and Lung-Pan Cheng; Metamaterial Mechanisms was nominated for the UIST 2016 best paper award / honourable mention)

### Coverage

- **organisation**: Each project is a separate paper with its own figures, grouped by research problem (fit across machines, strength, parametric generation, compliant mechanisms) rather than by the job the joint does. There is no shared index; you need to know the project names. The papers explain each joint with numbered photo figures, which is clear once you find them
- **scope_match**: high: these are exactly unusual, clever connection and mechanism TYPES that act as one unit (cantilever-spring press-fit replacements, kerf-independent bearings, one-piece compliant door latch and pliers, detent hinges, interlocking and loop-insert sheet joints), with tested rules. Limits: small number of types, mostly laser cutting (a lower priority for the project) and research-prototype tools
- **taxonomy_used**: No shared taxonomy. Joinery classifies by joint profile (9 in code: finger, angled finger, loop insert x3, hem, tab insert, interlocking, flap). SpringFit classifies by what the press fit holds: mount (round or non-round), finger joint, cross/notch joint, T-joint (4 classes). Metamaterial Mechanisms builds up from cell types (rigid cell, shear cell) to members and hinges, basic mechanisms (hinge, four-bar), compound mechanisms and simple machines (4 levels). Kerf-Canceling Mechanisms classifies by mechanism: bearing, slider, gear pair
- **example_types**: cantilever-spring mount (SpringFit spring-loaded press-fit replacement); cantilever-spring finger joint; cantilever-spring cross (notch) joint; cantilever-spring T-joint; constant-stress cantilever spring; loop insert joint (Joinery hook-and-loop sheet joint); hem joint (folded seam); tab insert joint (tab and slot with flap); interlocking joint (flexible interlocking flaps); flap joint; angled finger joint; boxel (finger-jointed closed box module, kyub); supported lengthening joint with butterfly tabs (kyub); boxel dowel joint (poseable push-fit joint); shear cell hinge array (metamaterial living hinge); metamaterial door latch (one-piece rotary-to-linear compliant latch); metamaterial pliers; metamaterial four-bar and pantograph; kerf-canceling bearing (self-similar wedge insert that jams on its axle); kerf-canceling slider and gear pair; Flaticulation articulated-angle T-pattern joint and detent hinge
- **making_methods**: Laser cutting (Joinery, SpringFit, kyub, Kerf-Canceling Mechanisms, Flaticulation) in plywood, acrylic, card and thin sheet; FDM 3D printing in flexible TPU (NinjaFlex, SemiFlex) and laser-cut rubber foam for Metamaterial Mechanisms
- **item_count**: About 25 joint and mechanism types across 6 projects: Joinery 9 joint profiles (code); SpringFit 4 converted types; kyub 3 joint kinds (finger-jointed boxel, dowel, lengthening joint); Metamaterial Mechanisms about 6 demo mechanisms (hinge, four-bar, door latch, pliers, pantograph, Jansen walker); Kerf-Canceling 3 (bearing, slider, gear pair); Flaticulation 2 (articulated joint, detent hinge)
- **special_behaviours_covered**: Compliant: yes, strongly (metamaterial shear-cell hinge arrays; SpringFit cantilever springs; Joinery interlocking and loop-insert flexible joints). Print-in-place / no assembly: yes, metamaterial mechanisms are single printed parts. Self-locking / hand-release: partly, the metamaterial door latch is a spring-returned latch opened by turning the handle; SpringFit mounts hold parts by spring force and let them be removed. Indexing/detent: yes, Flaticulation detent hinges and set-angle clutching joints. Torque-limiting, one-way/ratchet, over-centre: none found. Gear trains: only kerf-canceling gear pairs (and the kyub/Jansen walker needs a separate axle); no gearboxes. Powered connectors: none (SpringFit only mounts motors and LEDs). Adhesives: none; kyub and SpringFit are designed to hold without glue (kyub test objects were press fit, not glued)
- **entry_structure**: Per project: project page with summary, captioned figures, publication details, PDF, demo video and (for some) code and a live tool. Per joint inside a paper: name, photo figure, how it works, design parameters (length, thickness, deflection, angle), evaluation. The Joinery tool shows each joint profile's parameters (material thickness, finger width, hook count, grip, tolerance) as editable fields
- **audience_level**: research (HCI and digital fabrication researchers), with tools aimed at novice designers and hobbyist makers

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: the project pages explain the problem in plain language with good photos (e.g. SpringFit's 'a cantilever spring is simply a long thin piece of material that pushes against the object to be held'), and Joinery has a step-by-step Instructable; but the papers are academic, the tools are research prototypes with limited support, and nothing is organised for someone looking for 'a joint that does X'
- **has_images**: yes: high-quality photos and step-by-step figures of each joint and mechanism in the papers and project pages (e.g. SpringFit spring finger, cross and T-joints; the metamaterial door latch with its cell grid)
- **has_animations**: yes: demo videos on YouTube and HPI for SpringFit, kyub, Metamaterial Mechanisms (75.6 MB video), Kerf-Canceling Mechanisms and Flaticulation; interactive 3D editors (metamaterial editor with stiffness and force simulation; kyub 3D editor; Joinery 2D joint editor)
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes for project-level links: stable HPI project URLs (e.g. https://hpi.de/baudisch/projects/springfit.html for cantilever-spring joints, https://hpi.de/baudisch/projects/metamaterial-mechanisms.html for the one-piece door latch, https://hpi.de/baudisch/projects/kerf-canceling-mechanisms.html for kerf-proof bearings) and DOIs (e.g. https://doi.org/10.1145/3332165.3347930). No per-joint pages
- **reusable_data**: Code is reusable: Joinery (MIT, joint profiles and default parameters in js/joints.js), Metamaterial Mechanisms editor (MIT, JavaScript voxel editor plus Grasshopper simulation, STL export via OpenJSCAD). The list of joint types is facts and can be reused with citation; paper text and figures are ACM/HPI copyright. No structured dataset
- **maintained**: Archived research outputs: the tools are no longer developed (Joinery 2016-2017, metamaterial editor 2016, marked 'not a product, but a research prototype' with limited support), but project pages were online in September 2026 and kyub.com still serves its web app. The Thingiverse collection for metamaterial models returned 404
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Explain why press fits fail (kerf, material stiffness, printer tolerance) and offer a spring-based alternative; the same idea maps directly to 3D-printed snap fits and clearances. (2) A 'robust to machine differences' flag for each entry (does it still work if your printer or cutter is a bit off?). (3) Show a joint as a problem, a failure photo and a fixed version (SpringFit's a/b/c figures). (4) Build mechanisms up from cells: rigid part, hinge, basic mechanism, compound mechanism, as a simple ladder for explaining compliant mechanisms to beginners. (5) The one-piece door latch as a flagship example of a compliant latch. (6) Joinery's idea of letting the user pick joints per edge and exporting a ready cut file could inspire a 'try this joint' generator link
- **design_rules_given**: Yes, some: SpringFit halving spring length or doubling its thickness makes it 8x stiffer; a longer, thicker spring of equal stiffness took 7.5x more variation (up to 1.5 mm); typical kerf variation 0.1 mm at 10 N; the two test cutters differed in kerf by 0.12 vs 0.20 mm; springs use a constant-stress cantilever model. kyub: a single finger-jointed boxel in 4 mm 3-layer plywood withstood over 500 kg; plates up to A2 (60 x 40 cm). Metamaterial Mechanisms: best in flexible TPU (NinjaFlex, SemiFlex), better than PLA; laser-cut versions best in rubber foam; the printed Jansen walker survived about 5,000 cycles at about 120 rpm without visible fatigue. Joinery defaults: finger width 8 mm for 3 mm board, interlocking tolerance 0.1 mm
- **generator_or_parametric**: Yes: Joinery (web, parametric joints applied to your SVG edges), SpringFit (converts existing SVG cutting plans), kyub (3D web editor that unfolds to SVG plates with kerf correction), Metamaterial Mechanisms editor (voxel/cell editor exporting STL), Kerf-Canceler (finds bearings, sliders and gear pairs in cutting plans and swaps in kerf-canceling versions) and Flaticulation (Rhino/Grasshopper plug-in that unfolds 3D models and adds articulated joints or detent hinges)
- **file_formats**: SVG (Joinery, SpringFit, kyub export); STL (metamaterial editor); Grasshopper .gh files (Flaticulation, metamaterial simulation); PDF papers; MP4 and YouTube videos
- **terminology_value**: medium: good source for research names of newer types (cantilever-based mounts and joints, metamaterial mechanism, shear cell, hinge array, boxel, kerf-canceling bearing, articulated-angle joint, detent hinge) and for aliases such as notch joint = cross joint = slotted joint = edge lap joint (kyub paper); not a general authority for traditional names
- **decision_support**: None as a selector. Joinery offers a joint-profile menu with editable parameters; SpringFit automatically classifies each press fit (mount, finger, cross, T) and picks spring sizes; kyub's paper compares construction methods (notch joints vs closed boxes) by strength
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Builds on earlier HCI fabrication work (FlatFitFab, SketchChair, Platener, CutCAD). SpringFit cites kyub and Joinery as related tools. Metamaterial Mechanisms overlaps with BYU compliant mechanism research (living hinges, four-bars) and with Ion et al.'s 'Understanding Metamaterial Mechanisms' (CHI 2019, automatic design from motion paths). Joinery and kyub overlap with Boxes.py finger joints and living hinges (count finger joints once)

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - access_barrier
  - cost
  - covers
  - gaps
  - image_license
  - license

## 23 CAD assembly joint datasets (Fusion 360 Gallery Assembly / JoinABLe, AutoMate, Assemble Them All)

*GitHub repo or dataset.* Research datasets of joints and mates between CAD parts, with joint-type labels

### Basics

- **name**: CAD assembly joint datasets: Fusion 360 Gallery Assembly Dataset (with its JoinABLe joint subset, Autodesk Research), AutoMate (University of Washington with Adobe/Onshape data), and Assemble Them All (MIT CSAIL with Autodesk)
- **url**: https://github.com/AutodeskAILab/Fusion360GalleryDataset (Fusion 360 Gallery, including the Assembly and Assembly-Joint data); https://github.com/AutodeskAILab/JoinABLe (JoinABLe code); https://zenodo.org/records/7776208 (AutoMate dataset; code at https://github.com/deGravity/automate); https://github.com/yunshengtian/Assemble-Them-All (Assemble Them All code and dataset links). The github.com pages were read through their raw README files
- **resource_type**: GitHub repo or dataset (three related machine-learning research datasets of real CAD assemblies, where parts are connected by labelled joints, also called mates, plus code and papers)
- **cost**: Free (large downloads: Fusion 360 Assembly-Joint data 2.8 GB; AutoMate about 35 GB in total, most of it Parasolid and STEP geometry; Assemble Them All 5.4 MB to 778 MB per subset)
- **language**: English
- **year**: Fusion 360 Gallery Reconstruction dataset paper 2020 (arXiv 2010.02392); Assembly Dataset and JoinABLe first released with arXiv 2111.12772 in November 2021 (v2 April 2022; CVPR 2022); Fusion 360 licence updated 11/2021. AutoMate: arXiv 2105.12238 May 2021 (v2 October 2021), ACM Transactions on Graphics December 2021 (SIGGRAPH Asia 2021); dataset deposited on Zenodo and Dryad 27 March 2023. Assemble Them All: arXiv 2211.03977 November 2022, ACM TOG December 2022 (SIGGRAPH Asia 2022); README notes a dataset update in March 2024 (dataset_2403)
- **access_barrier**: none for the download links (no login). But using the data needs technical skills: 7z/zip archives of several GB, B-Rep files (.smt needs Autodesk Shape Manager/Fusion 360; Parasolid .x_t needs the commercial Parasolid kernel), and Python/conda code. STEP and OBJ versions can be opened in free tools such as FreeCAD
- **authority_level**: academic peer-reviewed (CVPR 2022, ACM Transactions on Graphics / SIGGRAPH Asia 2021 and 2022), from Autodesk Research, MIT CSAIL, University of Washington and collaborators. The joint labels themselves are set by the original CAD designers (community), not by expert annotators

### Coverage

- **covers**: Real designer-made CAD assemblies and the joints (mates) between their parts. Fusion 360 Gallery Assembly: 8,251 assemblies with 154,468 parts, each joint labelled with one of Fusion 360's 7 joint types (rigid, revolute, slider, cylindrical, pin-slot, planar, ball), plus motion limits and rest pose, contact surfaces and 24 recognised hole types (counterbore, countersunk, blind, through, tapered and so on). Its Assembly-Joint subset (used by JoinABLe): 19,156 joint sets with 32,148 joints between 23,029 parts. AutoMate: 255,211 assemblies, 451,967 unique parts and 1,292,016 unique mates scraped from public Onshape documents, labelled with Onshape's 8 mate types (frequencies among the paper's 541,635 unique mates: fastened 62.1%, revolute 12.7%, planar 11.8%, slider 5.3%, cylindrical 5.1%, parallel 1.8%, ball 0.6%, pin slot 0.5%), plus 'mate relations' that couple two mates (Onshape offers gear, rack-and-pinion, screw and linear relations). Assemble Them All: 12,970 physically valid assemblies (8,776 two-part, the rest 3 to 235 parts) with a small 24-assembly rotational subset (screws and nuts, puzzles, other twisting motions); it labels assembly paths and sequences, not joint types
- **organisation**: Organised as data files by numeric or hashed ID, not for browsing: one JSON per assembly or joint set, with parts as B-Rep/STEP/OBJ files. Joint types are enum values (e.g. 'RevoluteJointType', 'FASTENED'). There are no human-readable pages, categories or search. For someone who does not know the terms it does not work at all as a reference; its value is the compact, standard list of joint types and the statistics of how often each is used
- **scope_match**: low. It is about generic CAD joint TYPES by degrees of freedom (rigid, hinge, slider and so on), which are the building-block motions every connection has, not the unusual or clever connection and mechanism types the encyclopedia wants. It does not name snap fits, ratchets, torque limiters, latches or adhesives. Useful mainly as a sanity check for a 'how does it move' filter and for real-world frequency data
- **making_methods**: General engineering and product design in CAD (Fusion 360 and Onshape users: robotics, machine design, automotive, toys, product design, according to the Fusion 360 category tags). Aimed at machine learning and robotic assembly research, not at a making method
- **item_count**: Fusion 360 Gallery Assembly: 8,251 assemblies / 154,468 parts; Assembly-Joint subset: 19,156 joint sets / 32,148 joints / 23,029 parts. AutoMate: 255,211 assemblies / 451,967 unique parts / 1,292,016 unique mates (the paper's cleaned subset: 92,529 top-level assemblies and 541,635 unique mates). Assemble Them All: 12,970 assemblies (8,776 two-part, 2,620 with 3-9 parts, 1,468 with 10-49, 106 with 50-235) plus 24 rotational assemblies. Joint-type vocabulary: 7 (Fusion 360) and 8 (Onshape) types
- **entry_structure**: Per joint: the two parts, the joint type, the designer-selected faces or edges on each part that define the joint, joint axis (origin and direction), offset, angle, flip flag, motion limits (minimum, maximum, rest value) and the transform to the assembled pose. Per assembly: the part hierarchy (tree), occurrences, bodies with material and physical properties, contact faces, labelled holes, user tags (category, industry, products) and a PNG thumbnail. AutoMate: parts, occurrences with 4x4 transforms, mates with type and mate connector frames, mate relations with ratio or length, mate groups and sub-assemblies. No descriptions, design rules or how-to
- **audience_level**: research (machine learning, computer graphics, robotic assembly planning)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low. The datasets are large machine-learning archives with JSON and CAD kernel formats and no plain-English explanations. The only beginner-friendly parts are the README diagrams: a figure of the 7 Fusion 360 joint types with their degrees of freedom, and animated GIFs of parts being assembled
- **has_images**: yes. Every assembly and joint set has a PNG thumbnail; the READMEs and papers have mosaics of sample assemblies, a diagram of the 7 joint motion types, contact and hole illustrations, and the AutoMate paper has a figure of its 8 mate types with arrows showing the allowed motions. Good quality but small and technical
- **has_animations**: yes, limited. Animated GIFs in the JoinABLe README (task, representation and architecture) and Assemble Them All README (parts being disassembled and reassembled along planned paths, including screw-out motions); a GIF showing how a joint is set up in Fusion 360. Assemble Them All includes a simulation viewer that animates assemblies. No interactive web viewer
- **gaps**: No clever or special connection types at all: no snap fits, latches, ratchets, detents, torque limiters, compliant joints, print-in-place joints, powered connectors or adhesives. No names in everyday words, no descriptions, no design rules, no guidance on when to use which joint. The type list is only the dozen or so generic CAD joints. The data needs specialist tools, and the Fusion 360 part of it cannot be redistributed or used commercially
- **reusable_data**: structured. All three are downloadable datasets with documented JSON schemas (AutoMate also has Parquet metadata tables for parts, assemblies and mates), so joint-type counts or example assemblies could be extracted by script. Licence for reuse: AutoMate is CC0 1.0 (free reuse, including the type list and statistics); Fusion 360 Gallery and Assemble Them All data are non-commercial research only and cannot be redistributed in full. The joint-type names themselves are generic CAD vocabulary and can be reused freely
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Use a short, fixed 'how does it move' vocabulary, based on degrees of freedom, as one flow-chart filter: rigid/fixed, hinge (turns), slider (slides), turn-and-slide (cylindrical), ball (swivels), pin-in-slot, flat-sliding (planar); CAD users already know these words. (2) Show each motion type with the small arrows diagram used in the Fusion 360 and AutoMate figures (translation arrows plus rotation arrows per axis). (3) Record motion limits and a rest position for each entry (e.g. a hinge that opens 0 to 110 degrees). (4) Real-world frequency data: among about 540,000 unique Onshape mates in the AutoMate paper, 62% are rigid (fastened) and only about 1% are ball or pin-slot, which shows that most real joints are fixed and moving joints are the minority. (5) Treat 'coupled motions' (gear, rack-and-pinion, screw relations) as their own group, as Onshape does. (6) Separate 'how it moves' from 'how it is held' (e.g. hole type: counterbore versus countersunk)
- **design_rules_given**: No. Only data: contact is defined within a 0.1 mm tolerance, and holes have measured diameters and lengths, but there are no clearances, fits, torques or strength figures
- **generator_or_parametric**: No geometry generator for makers. The data come from parametric CAD (Fusion 360 and Onshape B-Reps), and the research code can predict or plan joints (JoinABLe predicts joint axes and poses; AutoMate had an Onshape browser extension that suggests mates; Assemble Them All plans assembly paths), but none of it produces custom connection geometry
- **file_formats**: JSON (assembly, joint set and graph data in NetworkX format), SMT (Autodesk Shape Manager B-Rep), STEP, OBJ meshes, PNG thumbnails, Parasolid .x_t (AutoMate), Parquet tables (AutoMate), pickle preprocessed files (JoinABLe); archives are 7z and zip
- **terminology_value**: medium. A good authority for the standard CAD names of joint types (revolute, slider, cylindrical, pin-slot, planar, ball, rigid/fastened, parallel) as used in Fusion 360 and Onshape, and for how the two programs name the same thing differently (rigid versus fastened). Useless for names of clever or specialised connections
- **decision_support**: None. There is no selector, flow chart or comparison. The only decision-relevant content is the degrees-of-freedom figure for each joint type and the frequency table of mate types
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Fusion 360 Gallery comes from about 20,000 user designs on the Autodesk Online Gallery; its joint types follow the Fusion 360 API. AutoMate is scraped from public Onshape documents and follows Onshape's mate types. Assemble Them All builds mostly on the Fusion 360 Gallery assemblies (Willis et al.), plus assemblies from Ebinger et al. 2018 and puzzle assemblies from Zhang et al. 2020; it shares authors with JoinABLe. The JoinABLe paper compares itself with AutoMate as a concurrent, similar dataset. Overlaps with articulated-object datasets such as PartNet-Mobility/SAPIEN and Shape2Motion, which label hinge, slider and screw joints on everyday objects (cited in both papers); the ABC dataset has Onshape CAD parts but no joints

### Other Info

- **sources**: 
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AutodeskAILab/Fusion360GalleryDataset/master/README.md (Fusion 360 Gallery README: dataset list, counts, download sizes, papers)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AutodeskAILab/Fusion360GalleryDataset/master/docs/assembly.md (Assembly Dataset docs: JSON schema, 7 joint types, contacts, 24 hole types, tags)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AutodeskAILab/Fusion360GalleryDataset/master/docs/assembly_joint.md (Assembly-Joint data: 19,156 joint sets, 32,148 joints, 23,029 parts; joint motion and geometry)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AutodeskAILab/Fusion360GalleryDataset/master/LICENSE.md (Fusion 360 Gallery Dataset licence, non-commercial research, updated 11/2021)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AutodeskAILab/JoinABLe/main/README.md (JoinABLe README: task, download links, CVPR 2022)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AutodeskAILab/JoinABLe/main/LICENSE (JoinABLe code MIT licence)

  - https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.03977 and https://arxiv.org/pdf/2211.03977 (Assemble Them All paper: Table 1 dataset statistics, rotational dataset of screws, puzzles and others)
  - https://cad.onshape.com/help/Content/relations.htm (Onshape help: gear, rack and pinion, screw and linear mate relations)

## 24 PartNet-Mobility / SAPIEN

*GitHub repo or dataset.* 2,346 articulated objects with hinge, slider and screw joints annotated; on Hugging Face

### Basics

- **name**: PartNet-Mobility dataset / SAPIEN (SimulAted Part-based Interactive ENvironment), UC San Diego, Stanford University and Simon Fraser University
- **resource_type**: GitHub repo or dataset (a research dataset of 3D articulated everyday objects with labelled moving joints, plus an open-source physics simulator that loads them)
- **cost**: Free (dataset access requires an approved request; the simulator is free to install with pip)
- **license**: Dataset: custom research licence ('Terms of Use') from UCSD, Stanford and SFU: non-commercial research and educational use only, no warranty, the user indemnifies the universities, access can be terminated, and users must also follow the ShapeNet terms of use because the models build on ShapeNet (originally from 3D Warehouse). Hugging Face tags it 'license: other'. Simulator code: Apache License 2.0 (copyright UCSD SU Lab 2020-2024 and Hillbot Inc. 2025). For the encyclopedia: link to it, but do not copy its models or renders onto a public site
- **language**: English
- **access_barrier**: free login plus manual approval. On Hugging Face you must log in, give your full name, affiliation and purpose, and be verified through your account's primary email; university addresses are usually approved automatically, while generic addresses (Gmail, Outlook) may be rejected. Using the data then needs 3D and simulation software (Python, a GPU for the simulator)
- **authority_level**: academic peer-reviewed (CVPR 2020), from the Hao Su lab at UC San Diego with Stanford (Leonidas Guibas) and Simon Fraser University (Angel X. Chang). Joint annotations were made by trained annotators with a purpose-built web tool

### Coverage

- **covers**: 2,346 3D models of everyday indoor objects in 46 categories, with 14,068 movable parts, each connected by a labelled joint. Only 3 motion types are annotated: hinge (turning around an axis, e.g. doors, lids, laptop screens), slider (moving along an axis, e.g. drawers, buttons) and screw (turning and sliding on the same axis, e.g. bottle caps, swivel chair height), plus fixed parts and whole objects marked as 'fixed base' or 'free'. Hinges and sliders have motion limits (angles, lengths); screws record limits and whether the turn and slide are coupled. Categories include bottle, box, bucket, cabinet (storage furniture), camera, cart, chair, folding chair, clock, coffee machine, dishwasher, dispenser, door, eyeglasses, fan, faucet, fridge, globe, kettle, keyboard, knife, lamp, laptop, lighter, microwave, monitor, mouse, oven, pen, phone, pliers, pot, printer, remote, safe, scissors, stapler, suitcase, switch, table, toaster, toilet, trash can, USB stick, washing machine and window
- **scope_match**: low. It labels only three generic joint motions (hinge, slider, screw) on household objects, not connection or mechanism types. It has no latches, snap fits, ratchets, detents, torque limiters, gears, powered connectors or adhesives. It is mainly useful as a large set of real-world examples of where hinges, slides and screw caps are used, and for 'how does it move' vocabulary
- **making_methods**: Not aimed at making. It is for computer vision, robotics simulation and robot learning (e.g. training robots to open drawers and doors). Its objects are household products whose joints are made by injection moulding, sheet metal and furniture hardware, but the dataset does not describe how they are made
- **item_count**: 2,346 objects (paper), 46 categories, 14,068 movable parts; the Hugging Face repository lists 2,347 object zip files. Largest categories: storage furniture/cabinet (345 objects, 1,174 parts), table (101), faucet (84), chair (80); keyboards alone have 3,593 movable parts (keys)
- **special_behaviours_covered**: torque-limiting: no; self-locking: no; hand-release only: no; one-way/ratchet: no; indexing/detent: no (motion limits only; no clicks or detents); over-centre: no; compliant: no (all parts are rigid links; the simulator adds friction and damping values); print-in-place: no; gear trains/gearboxes: no; powered connectors: no; adhesive types: no. The only in-scope-adjacent content is the screw joint (threaded caps) and joint motion limits
- **audience_level**: research (computer vision, robotics and robot learning)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low. Access needs an approved research request, the data are URDF and mesh files meant for a simulator, and there are no plain-English explanations. The object categories (kettle, stapler, laptop) are familiar, and the three motion names (hinge, slider, screw) are plain words, which helps
- **gaps**: Only three motion types, with no information on HOW a joint is built (what kind of hinge, how a drawer runner or snap works), no holding or locking behaviours (latches, detents, catches, ratchets), no fasteners, gears, springs, powered connectors or adhesives, no design rules, and non-commercial, approval-only access. Everyday products only, so no maker-specific joints such as print-in-place or compliant hinges
- **reusable_data**: structured but restricted. The dataset is downloadable per object (zip with URDF and JSON annotations) and a metadata.json file exists on Hugging Face (gated, not opened), so joint-type counts per category could be computed by script. Licence: non-commercial research and education only, and ShapeNet terms also apply, so the models and derived images cannot be republished on a public site. The category list and the three motion names are generic and can be reused freely as vocabulary
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Use three or four plain motion words as the first 'how does it move' question in the flow chart: turns (hinge), slides (slider), turns and slides together (screw), does not move (fixed). (2) Give each moving joint a motion range (e.g. 'opens 0 to 110 degrees', 'slides 30 mm'), as PartNet-Mobility does with motion limits. (3) Illustrate each joint type with familiar household examples (door hinge, drawer, bottle cap, swivel chair), which beginners recognise immediately. (4) The annotation tool's question-and-answer approach ('Does this part move relative to that one? Does it turn or slide? Does the slide also turn?') is itself a good model for a beginner decision tree. (5) Record whether two motions are coupled (screw: turning forces sliding)
- **design_rules_given**: No. Only motion limits (angles, lengths) and simulator physics values (friction, damping, density set to plausible ranges); no clearances, fits, strength or torque figures
- **generator_or_parametric**: No geometry generator. The simulator can build articulated objects in code (articulation builder with revolute, prismatic and fixed joints) and load URDFs, but it does not generate connection geometry for making
- **terminology_value**: low to medium. Useful for the plain synonyms used in robotics: hinge = revolute joint, slider = prismatic joint, screw = helical joint, and for everyday examples of each. It has no names for specific connection or mechanism types
- **decision_support**: None for makers. There is no selector or comparison. The mobility annotation tool (not public) used a question-and-answer flow to identify fixed, hinged and sliding parts, which is a useful pattern but not a feature of the dataset

### Other Info

- **sources**:

  - https://huggingface.co/api/datasets/sapien-sim/PartNetMobility (repository metadata: created 2026-07-18, last modified 2026-07-30, 2,347 object zip files, metadata.json, license: other)

  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/haosulab/sapien/master/readme.md (SAPIEN simulator README: Hugging Face is the primary way to access PartNet-Mobility; version 3.0 changes; PhysX 5 GPU; ManiSkill)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/haosulab/sapien/master/LICENSE (Apache 2.0; copyright UCSD SU Lab 2020-2024, Hillbot Inc. 2025)
