## 25 Linkage and mechanism datasets (LINKS, 3M planar linkage dataset, MechBench, MechVerse)

*GitHub repo or dataset.* Large datasets of planar linkages and mechanism assemblies from 2022-2026 research papers

### Basics

- **name**: Linkage and mechanism datasets from research papers (LINKS, the 3M planar linkage dataset, MechBench, MechVerse)
- **url**: LINKS: https://decode.mit.edu/projects/links/ (project page), code at https://github.com/ahnobari/LINKS, data at https://huggingface.co/datasets/ahn1376/LINKS-10M. 3M dataset: https://par.nsf.gov/biblio/10635249 (NSF public-access record of the ASME paper). MechBench: https://dynamo-web.pages.dev/ and https://github.com/Mayank3108/DYNAMO. MechVerse: https://mechverse.pages.dev/ and https://huggingface.co/datasets/Mayank-p3108/MechVerse
- **resource_type**: GitHub repo or dataset (four separate machine-learning research datasets, each with a paper; not a reference work)
- **language**: English
- **year**: LINKS: 2022 (arXiv 30 Aug 2022; ASME IDETC 2022); the LINKS-10M Hugging Face copy was uploaded August 2025; follow-up LInK paper May 2024 (TMLR 2024). 3M dataset: ASME Journal of Mechanical Design vol. 147 no. 4, published April 2025. MechBench: arXiv 15 Sep 2025 (DYNAMO paper). MechVerse: arXiv 14 May 2026 (under review); Hugging Face dataset last updated 19 June 2026
- **access_barrier**: none to read the project pages and arXiv papers; to use the data you need programming (Python notebooks, NumPy/Parquet/NPZ files) and a lot of disk space (LINKS-10M is about 24 GB to download; MechVerse is a large Video.zip). The 3M dataset paper sits behind the ASME paywall (NSF public-access record only), and a public download link was not found

### Coverage

- **covers**: Mostly randomly generated or synthetic machine-learning training data, not named mechanism types. LINKS: 100 million one-degree-of-freedom planar linkages (flat bar-and-pin mechanisms, 1-DOF planar linkages) with up to 20 joints, plus 1.1 billion traced paths (coupler curves); the Hugging Face copy LINKS-10M holds 10 million mechanisms plus 20,000 test items. 3M dataset: nearly 3 million single-DOF four-bar, six-bar and eight-bar linkages with open and closed coupler curves, including sliding-joint (prismatic) variants; a follow-up paper uses a curated 33-type subset of about 1.2 million. MechBench: 693 synthetic gear assemblies (spur, helical, double-helical/herringbone, rack and pinion, internal ring gears, bevel, worm and screw gears, planetary), 2 to 7 parts each, with meshes and motion paths. MechVerse: 21,156 short video clips of 1,357 moving assemblies in 141 categories; 904 are everyday hinged objects from PartNet-Mobility (doors, laptops, drawers) and 453 are CAD mechanisms (cam and follower, elliptical trammel, engine piston, four-bar linkage, Hobson joint, universal joint, and about 114 one-off 'unique mechanisms')
- **organisation**: By dataset file and number, not by name or purpose. LINKS is organised by graph structure (topology) and simulated path, with a 'curated' subset that removes most circles and arcs. The 3M dataset is grouped by linkage family (4-, 6-, 8-bar) and mechanism type. MechBench is one flat set of numbered assemblies (Assembly####.npz) with gear-type illustrations. MechVerse is grouped into three difficulty tiers (Easy: 1-2 independent parts; Medium: 3-8 coupled parts; Hard: 10-50 parts) and by category name. None of these can be browsed by someone who does not know the terms; they are meant for loading into code
- **scope_match**: low to medium. They are about linkages, gear trains and cam mechanisms (in scope), but they are machine-learning training data with numbered or random examples rather than a catalogue of named clever connection types; no latches, snap fits, clutches, ratchets or adhesives. MechVerse's CAD category list and MechBench's gear-type list are the most usable parts
- **making_methods**: General engineering and robotics design automation (computer-aided mechanism design and machine learning). Not aimed at 3D printing, CNC or woodwork, although the MechBench meshes/STEP files and MechVerse OBJ files could be printed
- **item_count**: LINKS: 100,000,000 mechanisms and 1.1 billion coupler curves (10,000,000 plus 20,000 test items in the LINKS-10M copy). 3M dataset: about 3,000,000 mechanisms. MechBench: 693 gear assemblies. MechVerse: 21,156 clips of 1,357 assemblies in 141 categories (453 CAD mechanism assemblies)
- **audience_level**: research

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low. The pages are written for machine-learning researchers (terms like coupler curve, chamfer distance, SE(3) trajectories, contrastive learning). There are no plain-English explanations of what each mechanism does. The exceptions are the LINKS web demo (a simple browser tool to build and animate linkages and try curve-matching challenges) and the MechVerse and MechBench project pages, which show short animations
- **has_images**: yes, on the project pages and papers: rendered gear-type pictures (MechBench), rendered assemblies and dataset charts (MechVerse), and path/linkage diagrams (LINKS). They are research figures, not labelled teaching diagrams
- **has_animations**: yes. MechVerse is itself 21,156 animation clips (Unity renders of mechanisms moving) with an interactive viewer on its project page; MechBench/DYNAMO project page shows predicted versus true gear motion; LINKS has an interactive browser 'Linkage Designer' (https://decode.mit.edu/links/) for drawing and simulating linkages, and LInK has a Hugging Face alphabet-drawing demo (it returned HTTP 503 when checked)
- **gaps**: No names or plain descriptions for most items (LINKS mechanisms are random graphs); nothing on joints, fasteners, snap fits, latches, clutches, ratchets, compliant or print-in-place parts, powered connectors or adhesives; no design rules, dimensions, materials or tolerances; very large downloads needing code; licences unclear for LINKS, MechBench and the 3M set; 3M dataset download location not found
- **good_for_entry_links**: poor for single entries: there are no stable per-mechanism web pages. Best used as 'further reading' links from a family overview, e.g. the LINKS web demo https://decode.mit.edu/links/ from a 'linkages' page, the MechBench project page https://dynamo-web.pages.dev/ from a 'gear types' page, or https://mechverse.pages.dev/ for mechanism animations
- **maintained**: LINKS: static since 2022, with a new Hugging Face copy in August 2025; project page and demo online in 2026. 3M dataset: published 2025, still cited by 2026 follow-up papers. MechBench: released September 2025. MechVerse: new (May 2026), Hugging Face data updated June 2026. All pages opened successfully on 2026-09-25 except the LInK Hugging Face demo (HTTP 503)
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) MechVerse's three-tier difficulty scale (independent parts / a few coupled parts / many coupled parts) is a neat way to label mechanism complexity in the encyclopedia. (2) MechVerse's controlled motion vocabulary (rotation, translation, rotation+translation, planar; clockwise, opening, extending, sliding, tilting) could seed the flow chart's 'what motion do you need' question. (3) MechVerse's structured description of each mechanism (fixed parts, moving parts, what drives what, direction, range) is a good template for an entry's 'how it moves' box. (4) MechBench's side-by-side gear-type illustration is a good model for a gear family overview. (5) The LINKS 'draw a path, find a linkage that traces it' demo is a fun interactive idea to link to for linkages
- **design_rules_given**: no. No clearances, tolerances, strengths or torques. The only numbers are geometric (joint coordinates, gear counts, motion ranges such as 180, 360 and 720 degrees for the speed variants)
- **generator_or_parametric**: partly: LINKS code (Python) generates and simulates random planar linkages, and the LINKS web demo lets you build and simulate a linkage in the browser; LInK synthesises a linkage to trace a drawn curve. MechBench and MechVerse are fixed collections, not generators
- **terminology_value**: low to medium: correct academic names for linkage and gear types (four-bar, six-bar, coupler curve, rack and pinion, internal involute gear, worm, screw gear, Hobson joint, elliptical trammel), but no definitions, aliases or translations
- **decision_support**: none for choosing a mechanism. The research tools do 'inverse design' (given a path you want, find a linkage that traces it), which is a kind of automated selector for linkages only, but it needs code or the demo, not a flow chart
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: MechVerse's easy tier is built from PartNet-Mobility (SAPIEN) everyday articulated objects; its CAD set and MechBench come from the same Purdue group (Karthik Ramani's lab) and share authors. LInK (2024) builds on LINKS. The 3M dataset follows earlier Stony Brook four-bar datasets (Deshpande and Purwar 2019-2020) and is used by later papers (e.g. a 2026 autoregressive-transformer paper). Related but separate: 'A Dataset for Mechanical Mechanisms' (about 9,000 sketch images with descriptions, arXiv 2409.03763) and Lipson's image-based planar linkage dataset (arXiv 2510.03308). Cite the original papers for each

### Other Info

- **sources**: 
  - https://arxiv.org/abs/2208.14567 (LINKS paper abstract: 100 million 1-DOF planar linkages, 1.1 billion coupler curves, submitted 30 Aug 2022)
  - https://decode.mit.edu/projects/links/ (LINKS project page: up to 20 joints, curated subset, demo, ASME IDETC 2022 citation)
  - https://decode.mit.edu/links/ (LINKS 'Modern Linkage Designer' browser demo with curve-matching challenges)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ahnobari/LINKS/main/README.md (LINKS README: four data files, Hugging Face link, code; read in place of https://github.com/ahnobari/LINKS; LICENSE file returned 404)
  - https://huggingface.co/api/datasets/ahn1376/LINKS-10M and https://huggingface.co/datasets/ahn1376/LINKS-10M/raw/main/README.md (LINKS-10M: 10,000,000 train + 20,000 test, Parquet, about 24 GB, created Aug 2025, no licence tag)
  - https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.20592 (LInK paper: contrastive retrieval over 10 million mechanisms, LINK ABC benchmark, TMLR 2024)
  - https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ahnobari/LInK/main/README.md and https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ahnobari/LInK/main/LICENSE (LInK code/data release, CC BY-NC-SA 4.0)
  - https://par.nsf.gov/biblio/10635249 (NSF public-access record of 'A Dataset of 3M Single-DOF Planar 4-, 6-, and 8-Bar Linkage Mechanisms...', Nurizada, Dhaipule, Lyu, Purwar, J. Mech. Des. 147(4), 2025-04-01)
  - https://arxiv.org/html/2606.17409 (2026 follow-up paper describing the 3M dataset: prismatic variants, curated 33-type subset of about 1.2M, data availability statement)
  - https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12430 (DYNAMO paper introducing MechBench: 693 synthetic gear assemblies, Purdue, 15 Sep 2025)

## 26 Model repository collections (Printables, Thingiverse, MakerWorld, GrabCAD)

*Model library.* Tags and collections of print-in-place, compliant, snap-fit and mechanism models and test prints

### Basics

- **name**: Model repository collections (Printables, Thingiverse, MakerWorld, GrabCAD): tags, searches and collections of print-in-place, compliant, snap-fit and mechanism models and test prints
- **resource_type**: model library (four large user-upload 3D model sharing sites; not curated encyclopedias)
- **image_license**: Photos and renders are uploaded by each designer and follow that model's licence (or none is stated); there is no site-wide open licence for images. Treat images as not reusable unless the individual model is CC-licensed, and then credit the designer
- **authority_level**: community or unvetted (user uploads; Printables and MakerWorld run contests and staff picks, and GrabCAD is used by engineers, but there is no technical review)

### Coverage

- **covers**: Everything people 3D print, including a large long tail of working examples of in-scope connection and mechanism types. Printables keyword search counts (api, 2026-09-25; relevance matches, so approximate): print in place about 4,730; hinge about 4,980; ratchet about 1,400; dovetail about 1,140; gearbox about 870; snap fit about 730; bistable about 550; compliant about 480 (compliant mechanism 87); ball joint about 430; planetary gear about 390; heat set insert about 390; bayonet about 300; tolerance test about 260; differential about 210; detent about 180; magnetic latch about 180; solenoid about 135; flexure about 110; cycloidal about 90; worm gear about 76; clearance test 62; Geneva 61; universal joint 60; electromagnet 35; living hinge 33; harmonic drive 24; slip clutch 14; cam cleat 13; strain wave 11; torque limiter 2. Thingiverse has tag pages for the same ideas (e.g. print_in_place, compliant_mechanism). MakerWorld is similar and adds OpenSCAD/Fusion customisable models. GrabCAD holds engineering CAD (native and STEP files) of gearboxes, linkages, couplings, latches and so on, often not made for printing
- **scope_match**: medium. Not a catalogue of types, but it is the biggest source of real, printable examples of almost every in-scope type (print-in-place joints, compliant and bistable mechanisms, snap fits, bayonet locks, cam cleats, ratchets, Geneva drives, cycloidal and strain-wave gearboxes, slip clutches, tolerance test prints), which is exactly what the 3D-printing-first audience needs to link to. Quality and naming are uneven
- **example_types**: Print-in-place hinge; Print-in-place cam bag clip; Print-in-place planetary gear set; Compliant mechanism (flexure) launcher; Bistable compliant mechanism (snap-through switch or pen); Compliant robot gripper; Living hinge box; Snap-fit hinge and snap-fit case; Bayonet lock (twist-lock) container; Cam cleat; Ratchet mechanism (ratchet wrench, ratchet tube squeezer); Slip clutch (spool holder with slip clutch); Torque-limiting driver; Geneva drive (Geneva mechanism fidget and clock); Cycloidal drive (cycloidal gearbox and compound cycloidal reducer); Strain-wave gear (harmonic drive); Herringbone planetary gear; Worm gear; Differential; Universal joint; Detent mechanism; Magnetic latch; Solenoid and electromagnet mounts; Tolerance and clearance test prints
- **making_methods**: 3D printing first (FDM and resin; Printables, Thingiverse and MakerWorld); Thingiverse also hosts laser-cut and CNC files; GrabCAD is general engineering CAD (machining, sheet metal, robotics) with 3D printing as one use
- **audience_level**: hobbyist (Printables, Thingiverse, MakerWorld); professional and student engineers (GrabCAD)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium. Very visual (photos of real printed parts, videos on many pages, 'makes' from other users) and in everyday language, so beginners can see what a mechanism looks like and just print it. But there are no explanations of how or why a type works, names are inconsistent, and you have to know what to search for; quality varies a lot
- **gaps**: No type-based organisation or behaviour filters; no explanation of principles or design rules beyond what each designer writes; inconsistent naming and tag spelling (compliantmechanim, printinplaces); many untested or poor-quality models and a growing share of AI-generated models (a reason given for the 2026 Thingiverse sale); licences often non-commercial or personal-only, so images cannot be reused; pages can disappear when designers delete them; sites block scripts; almost nothing on adhesives
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes, per model: each model has its own permanent-looking page with an ID (Printables model pages are numbered IDs plus a slug, Thingiverse uses thing:<id>), and tag pages give a live list, e.g. https://www.thingiverse.com/tag/print_in_place or https://www.thingiverse.com/tag/compliant_mechanism. Best used as 'print one to try' links from an entry (e.g. a Geneva drive fidget, a cycloidal gearbox, a clearance tolerance test). Links can break when uploaders remove models, so prefer popular, long-lived models or tag/search pages
- **maintained**: yes, all four are active in 2026. Thingiverse was bought by MyMiniFactory on 12 Feb 2026 and stays a separate site; Printables and MakerWorld are actively developed by printer makers (Prusa Research, Bambu Lab); GrabCAD is run by Stratasys (help page last updated October 2020 but the site footer says 2026). Individual models come and go
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Link each encyclopedia entry to a curated 'try it' model, preferably a print-in-place or fidget demo, plus a clearance/tolerance test print, so beginners can hold the mechanism. (2) Show 'makes' (other people's photos of real results), which builds trust. (3) Tag vocabulary shows the words makers actually use (printinplace, snapfit, fidget, bistable, tolerancetest), useful as aliases and search terms in the flow chart. (4) Customiser buttons (Thingiverse Customizer, MakerWorld Parametric Model Maker using OpenSCAD or Fusion parameters) are a good pattern for a 'make your own size' link. (5) Printables' licence picker is a good example of labelling reuse rights clearly on each item
- **design_rules_given**: only informally: some designers state clearances, layer heights or tested materials in their descriptions, and there are hundreds of tolerance/clearance test prints (Printables: about 260 'tolerance test' and 62 'clearance test' results) which let users measure their own printer's fit. No systematic numbers
- **terminology_value**: low. Tags and titles are user-chosen, often misspelled or informal ('fidget', 'clicky'), and occasionally correct engineering names (strain wave gear, cycloidal reducer, Geneva drive). Useful to learn the words beginners use, not as an authority
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Many models are cross-posted on two or more of these sites (and Cults, MyMiniFactory, Thangs); aggregators like Yeggi, STLFinder and Thangs index them all. Thingiverse Customizer's format is the OpenSCAD Customizer standard also used by MakerWorld's Parametric Model Maker. Remixes chain back to originals, so cite the original designer's page. Designs often implement types described in BYU CMR, BOSL2 and 507 Mechanical Movements

### Other Info

- **sources**:

## 27 Hackaday articles and collections on mechanisms

*Encyclopedia / wiki.* Hackaday blog and hackaday.io coverage of compliant, print-in-place and clever mechanisms

### Basics

- **name**: Hackaday articles and collections on mechanisms (hackaday.com blog tags and series, plus hackaday.io projects, Hack Chats and contests)
- **url**: https://hackaday.com/tag/compliant-mechanism/
- **resource_type**: forum / blog (a daily news blog, hackaday.com, whose posts are grouped by tags and occasional series, plus hackaday.io, its community project-hosting site with project pages, live 'Hack Chat' events and contests). It is not an encyclopedia: it's a large, loosely tagged archive of short news posts that point to makers' projects and videos
- **cost**: Free (ad-supported). Reading hackaday.com needs no account; hackaday.io project pages can be read without an account, but its search and tag filters need a free login (checked 2026-09-25)
- **language**: English
- **access_barrier**: none for hackaday.com. hackaday.io: none to read a project page, free login to search or filter projects by tag (logged-out tag URLs such as hackaday.io/projects?tag=Print-in-place just show recent projects)
- **image_license**: All rights reserved or unknown. Most images in posts are screenshots or photos taken from the featured maker's project, video or website (e.g. 'Source: Lovejoy Inc', 'Source: Dominique Toussaint (Wikipedia)'), plus embedded YouTube videos that belong to their creators. Do not copy images; link to the post or, better, to the original project
- **authority_level**: community or unvetted, with light editorial curation: staff writers (e.g. Dan Maloney, Dave Rowntree, Danie Conradie) pick and summarise reader-tipped projects and videos. Posts are journalism, not tested design guidance. The 2018 'Mechanisms' series and the Hack Chats with named experts (e.g. mechanical engineer Amy Qian on compliant mechanisms, Jan 2022) are more educational

### Coverage

- **covers**: Thousands of hacks and builds, of which a few hundred posts are about mechanism types. Relevant tag counts on 2026-09-25 (from the tag pages / REST API): compliant mechanism 21, compliant mechanisms 3, flexure 17, living hinge 8, print in place 21, mechanism 29, mechanisms 23, snap fit 2, ratchet 12, clutch 10, latch 15 (mostly electronic latches), hinge 16, gearbox 50, cycloidal drive 7, harmonic drive 11, strain wave gear 7, 3D printed gears 17, gear 74, gears 77, differential 20, worm gear 4, geneva drive 5, linkage 17, kinematics 22, dovetail 6, joinery 15, threaded insert 9, solenoid 146, electromagnet 68, nitinol 10, shape memory alloy 4, adhesive 18, glue 30, epoxy 83, cyanoacrylate 9. Topics span compliant and flexure mechanisms (flexure joysticks, six-axis flexure positioners, compliant toggles and blasters), print-in-place joints and hinges, 3D-printed gearboxes (cycloidal, strain-wave, planetary), one-way clutches and ratchets, snap fits, living hinges, electro-permanent magnets, shape-memory-alloy (nitinol) actuators and adhesives (super glue history, stretch-release adhesive, electrically released adhesive, glue test rigs). The whole blog holds about 58,446 posts (REST API total, 2026-09-25)
- **organisation**: Reverse-date order inside free-form tags (e.g. /tag/compliant-mechanism/, /tag/print-in-place/), categories and a site search. Tags are inconsistent (both 'compliant-mechanism' with 21 posts and 'compliant-mechanisms' with 3; 'gear' and 'gears'; 'latch' mixes door latches with electronic latch circuits), so one topic is spread over several tags. There is no grouping by function or motion. The 2018 'Mechanisms' series gives one explainer per family. For a beginner, it works for browsing and inspiration, but it is hard to find 'the mechanism that does X' without already knowing its name
- **scope_match**: medium: many posts are exactly the clever one-unit types the project wants (print-in-place hinges, compliant toggles, flexures, one-way clutches, 3D-printed ratchets, strain-wave and cycloidal gearboxes, electro-permanent magnets, a 3D-printed break-over torque wrench), and it is strongest where classic books are weakest (3D printing, compliant, powered). But it is news about individual builds, not a catalogue of types, and much tagged content is off-topic (whole robots, electronics)
- **taxonomy_used**: No formal taxonomy: flat user-chosen tags (hundreds of them) and a few loose categories. The 2018 'Mechanisms' series by Dan Maloney effectively uses machine-element families as its groups: zipper, screw thread, bearings, mechanical seals, gears, cable ties, couplings, springs, hook-and-loop fasteners, levers, solenoids, abrasives, and lead screws and ball screws (13 articles)
- **example_types**: Print-in-place geared hinge (herringbone gears, 2022); print-in-place butt hinge with indexing lock (Maker's Muse, 2024); living hinges and printed flexure springs (Slant 3D video, 2025); linear flexure stage (parallel leaf-spring flexure, 2021); spherical flexure joint joystick (2021); compliant toggle cord lock with printed spiral spring (2026); compliant mechanism that shrinks instead of stretching (auxetic-like, 2025); one-way clutch screwdriver (overrunning roller clutch, 2022); 3D-printed ratchet wrench (2022); two-way to one-way geared coupler from automatic watches (2022); 3D-printed break-over torque wrench that snaps past 0.6 Nm (torque-limiting, 2026); cantilever and annular snap-fit joints (2022); strain-wave (harmonic) gearbox (2022); cycloidal drive (2018-2026); harmonic pin-ring gearing (2026); capstan drive and Simpson rolling-joint drive for robot joints (2020); Geneva drive (2012-2024); rigid chain (zip-chain) actuator (2020); electro-permanent magnet (2026); nitinol shape-memory wire actuators (2018-2023); stretch-release and electrically released adhesives (2020, 2024); super glue (cyanoacrylate) history (2023); print-in-place connector for wires (2021)
- **making_methods**: Mostly FDM 3D printing, with some SLS printing, laser cutting, CNC, wire EDM (flexure positioners), woodwork and electronics/robotics. Strong on hobby 3D printing and robotics
- **special_behaviours_covered**: compliant: yes, strongest area (compliant-mechanism, flexure and living-hinge tags; Compliant Mechanisms Hack Chat). Print-in-place: yes (print-in-place tag, 21 posts; geared hinge, PCB grippers, pump). One-way/ratchet: yes (ratchet tag; overrunning-clutch screwdriver; watch two-way-to-one-way coupler). Indexing/detent: some (indexing print-in-place hinge; detents appear in knob and tool posts). Torque-limiting: rare but present (3D-printed break-over torque wrench, 2026; the REST search found no post using the phrase 'torque limiter'). Self-locking: a little (compliant cord-lock toggle). Over-centre: barely (two posts mention 'over-center'). Hand-release only: not as a theme. Gear trains/gearboxes: yes, many (gearbox 50, cycloidal, strain-wave, planetary, differential, worm). Powered connectors: yes (electro-permanent magnet explainer, solenoid series article, nitinol/shape-memory actuators, magnet and electromagnet tags). Adhesive types: some (super glue history, stretch-release adhesive, electrically released adhesive, glue test stand, epoxy tag), but no side-by-side guide of adhesive chemistries by material
- **entry_structure**: A typical post: headline, author and date, 3-6 paragraphs summarising a maker's project, with photos/GIFs, an embedded YouTube video, links to the original project (Printables, Thingiverse, Instructables, hackaday.io, YouTube) and to related older Hackaday posts, then tags and a long comment thread. The 2018 'Mechanisms' series posts are longer explainers with history, how it works, and variants. hackaday.io project pages have description, details, build logs, files, components and instructions. No degrees of freedom, no standard entry template
- **audience_level**: hobbyist to professional (electronics and maker enthusiasts; comments often add expert engineering detail)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: posts are short, chatty and plain-English with pictures and videos, but they assume you already know what you're looking at, use names without defining them, and give little context on when to choose one mechanism over another
- **has_images**: yes: photos, screenshots and animated GIFs in most posts (quality varies, many are video stills from the maker)
- **has_animations**: yes: most mechanism posts embed a YouTube video of the maker's build or demonstration, and some include animated GIFs (e.g. a zipper animation, a one-way ratchet gear GIF). No interactive models
- **gaps**: No catalogue structure: no list of types, no consistent names or tags, and no way to search by what you need it to do. Posts describe one build, not the general type. Almost no design rules, clearances or strength data (and those come from the linked videos). Coverage is patchy: few posts on torque limiters, over-centre latches, bayonet mounts, cam cleats, reel closures or detents. Old links to maker projects rot. hackaday.io search needs a login. Adhesives are covered as news stories, not as a comparison of glue types
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes, as 'see it built' links: each post has a stable dated URL, e.g. https://hackaday.com/2022/01/24/one-piece-geared-hinge-can-take-the-weight/ (print-in-place geared hinge) or https://hackaday.com/2022/08/25/printable-one-way-driver-skips-ratchet-for-a-clutch/ (overrunning clutch). Tag pages (e.g. https://hackaday.com/tag/print-in-place/) are good 'more examples' links. For the design itself, link to the original project the post cites
- **maintained**: Very active: new posts daily (latest checked 25 Sep 2026); mechanism-tagged posts continue into 2026 (e.g. compliant toggle, April 2026; electro-permanent magnet, April 2026; micrometer displacement sensor, June 2026; 3D-printed cycloidal gearbox test, Aug 2026). hackaday.io is online; some project pages are years old and not updated
- **ideas_to_borrow**: Pair each type with a real 'see it built' example and a short video, which is what makes Hackaday posts engaging. The 2018 'Mechanisms' series format (one family per article: history, how it works, main variants, where you meet it in daily life) is a good template for family overview pages. 'Cool Mechanism Day' and 'Tech in Plain Sight' framing (explain a clever mechanism found in everyday objects, e.g. the watch two-way-to-one-way coupler, string trimmer heads, super glue) suits a beginner audience. Link 'related posts' at the end of each entry. Hack Chats show the value of a named expert Q&A on one family (compliant mechanisms)
- **design_rules_given**: rarely, and only second-hand from the featured project or video: e.g. 0.4 mm thick printed leaf springs for a linear flexure (2021 post); elongation at break of about 8% PLA, 10% ABS, 24% PETG, 100% nylon, with the advice to design snap fits to only 10-15% of that (2022 'Oh Snap!' post); PLA makes a poor printed spring and PETG works better (2026 compliant toggle post); a break-over torque wrench set at 0.6 Nm (2026). No systematic tables
- **generator_or_parametric**: no generator of its own; some featured projects link to parametric models or OpenSCAD/CAD files on Printables, Thingiverse, Cults3D or GitHub
- **file_formats**: HTML posts with JPG/PNG/GIF images and embedded YouTube video; RSS and WordPress REST JSON. CAD/STL files only via the linked projects (hackaday.io projects can host files)
- **terminology_value**: low to medium: useful for the names makers actually use (print-in-place, living hinge, flexure, strain wave, cycloidal drive, electro-permanent magnet) and for spotting new trends, but names are informal and inconsistent, and there are no translations
- **decision_support**: none: no selector, flow chart, filters or comparison tables. Only tags, site search and 'related posts' links. A few posts compare two options (e.g. 'Harmonic Vs Cycloidal Show Down', 2022; 'Do You Need A Cycloidal Drive?', 2021)
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Hackaday mostly re-reports other people's work: Printables/Thingiverse/Cults3D models, YouTube channels (Slant 3D, Maker's Muse, Amy Makes Stuff, Mechanistic, CNC Kitchen), Instructables and university research. It has pointed readers to other items in this prior-art list: KMODDL (2012 post), DMG-Lib (2012 post) and Nguyen Duc Thang's '2100 Animated Mechanical Mechanisms' (2016 post), and it cites the MIT Precision Compliant Systems Lab flexure index. Cite the original project, not the Hackaday summary, where possible

## 28 Mechanisms and Mechanical Devices Sourcebook (Sclater and Chironis)

*Book.* Illustrated sourcebook of about 2,000 mechanisms and devices, grouped by function, in several editions

### Basics

- **name**: Mechanisms and Mechanical Devices Sourcebook (Nicholas P. Chironis and Neil Sclater, McGraw-Hill, 5 editions 1991-2011)
- **url**: https://www.mheducation.com/highered/mhp/product/mechanisms-mechanical-devices-sourcebook-5th-edition.html
- **resource_type**: book (illustrated engineering reference, also sold as an eBook and included in McGraw-Hill's AccessEngineering online library)
- **license**: All rights reserved (commercial McGraw-Hill copyright, 2011 for the 5th edition per the publisher page). Fine to cite and link; drawings and text must not be copied or reproduced without permission. Note: full-text PDFs posted on sites such as rexresearch1.com, academia.edu or flipbook sites appear to be unauthorised copies and should not be linked
- **language**: English
- **year**: 1st edition 1991 (Nicholas P. Chironis, editor; xi, 447 p.); 2nd edition 1996 (Chironis and Sclater; xiii, 463 p.); 3rd edition 2001 (495-500 p.); 4th edition Dec 2006 / 2007 (xv, 505 p.); 5th edition 2011 (Neil Sclater; xiii, 546 p., published 15 July 2011 per McGraw-Hill). The 5th edition is the latest found; no 6th edition was found on the publisher page, Open Library or Google Books
- **access_barrier**: paywall (buy the book or eBook) or academic licence (AccessEngineering, which also blocked access from this environment with HTTP 403). Free alternatives need a free login: Internet Archive lending of the 1991 and 2011 editions, or a library copy

### Coverage

- **covers**: Thousands of classic industrial mechanisms and machine elements shown as annotated line drawings with short descriptions, grouped by function: linkages; gears and gear drives (planetary, differential, worm, variable-speed); cam, Geneva and ratchet drives; clutches and brakes (including overrunning clutches); latching, fastening and clamping devices; chains, belts, springs and screws; shaft couplings; torque, speed, tension and limit controls; pneumatic, hydraulic, electric and electronic controls; parts-handling, packaging and safety mechanisms. Later editions add tutorial chapters (basics of mechanisms, motion control), robots, MEMS and nanotechnology, rapid prototyping, 3D CAD and renewable-energy mechanisms. Claimed size: 'over 1,200 drawings' (2nd ed.), '2,501 mechanisms and mechanical devices' (3rd ed.), 'over 2000 drawings' (4th ed.), 'thousands of drawings' (5th ed.)
- **scope_match**: high: it is one of the richest printed collections of clever one-unit mechanism TYPES (latches, clamps, detents, overrunning clutches, torque limiters, toggles, Geneva drives, couplings), grouped by the job they do, which matches the project's 'find a connection by what it must do' goal. Minus points: all-rights-reserved and paywalled, aimed at industrial metal machinery, and nothing on 3D printing, print-in-place or adhesives
- **taxonomy_used**: By function and machine element. 1st edition (1991), 11 chapters: parts-handling mechanisms; reciprocating and general-purpose mechanisms; special-purpose mechanisms; spring, bellow, flexure, screw and ball devices; cam, toggle, chain and belt mechanisms; geared systems and variable-speed mechanisms; coupling, clutching and braking devices; torque-limiting, tensioning and governing devices; nonmechanical methods of machine and mechanism control; fastening, latching, clamping and chucking devices; key equations and charts for designing mechanisms. 5th edition (2011), 19 chapters: basics of mechanisms; motion control systems; stationary and mobile robots; mechanisms for renewable power generation; linkages; gears; cam, Geneva and ratchet drives; clutches and brakes; latching, fastening and clamping devices; chain and belt devices; spring and screw devices; shaft couplings and connections; motion-specific devices; packaging, conveying, handling and safety mechanisms; torque, speed, tension and limit control systems; instruments and controls (pneumatic, hydraulic, electric, electronic); 3D digital prototypes and simulation; rapid prototyping; new directions in mechanical engineering
- **making_methods**: General engineering and industrial machine design (metal parts, machining, stamping, castings); later editions add robotics, rapid prototyping (3D printing processes) and CAD as background chapters, not as a way of making the mechanisms
- **item_count**: About 2,000-2,500 illustrated mechanisms and devices, depending on edition ('2,501' claimed for the 3rd edition, 'over 2000 drawings' for the 4th); about 1,200 drawings in the 2nd edition
- **entry_structure**: Short illustrated articles: a title, one or more annotated line drawings (letter-labelled parts), and a few sentences to a few paragraphs per device explaining how it works and where it is used. Tutorial chapters add formulas and glossaries. No CAD files, no degrees-of-freedom notation per entry, few dimensions
- **audience_level**: professional (machine designers, engineers, inventors) and undergraduate; also popular with advanced hobbyists for ideas

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: the drawings are clear and each idea is short, and grouping by function helps, but text uses industrial terms without much explanation, there are no photos or animations, and it gives no guidance on choosing between options or making them at home
- **has_images**: yes: thousands of clean black-and-white technical line drawings, the core of the book
- **gaps**: No 3D-printing, print-in-place or modern compliant-mechanism design; no adhesives; no snap-fit design rules. Old industrial context (packaging machines, instruments). No photos, animations, CAD files or sizes. Paywalled and all-rights-reserved, so images can't be reused and many readers can't follow a link to the content. No selector or flow chart
- **good_for_entry_links**: no for per-item links: there are no public per-mechanism pages. Entries can be cited by edition, chapter and page (e.g. '5th ed., ch. 9, Latching, fastening, and clamping devices'). AccessEngineering has chapter-level URLs but they are subscription-only and were not opened. The best public links are the Internet Archive lending record (https://archive.org/details/mechanismsmechan0000scla) and Open Library
- **reusable_data**: not reusable: all rights reserved, no dataset or API. Only bibliographic metadata is reusable (Open Library JSON, Internet Archive metadata including the 5th-edition chapter list). The chapter headings can inspire our own function-based grouping, but not be copied wholesale with their contents
- **maintained**: Not updated since the 5th edition (2011); still in print/eBook from McGraw-Hill in 2026. The 5th edition is on AccessEngineering, and the Internet Archive keeps lendable scans of the 1991 and 2011 editions (added to its collections; the 2011 scan's metadata was last touched Feb 2024)
- **ideas_to_borrow**: Group entries by the job they do (latch/fasten/clamp; limit torque; couple shafts; stop and index; one-way drives) rather than by shape, which maps well onto flow-chart questions. Clean, labelled line drawings that show only the working parts. 'N ways to do X' collections that show several alternatives side by side for one job. A separate tutorial chapter on the basics plus a glossary, so entries can stay short. A 'recycle proven designs' framing: each drawing is presented as an idea to adapt
- **design_rules_given**: few: tutorial chapters give kinematic formulas and key equations and charts (e.g. for gears, cams, linkages; 1991 'Key equations and charts for designing mechanisms' chapter), but most entries give no clearances, fits or torque values
- **generator_or_parametric**: no
- **terminology_value**: high for standard English industrial names of mechanisms and machine elements (overrunning clutch, toggle, Geneva drive, detent, chuck, flexure), with glossaries of cam, gear, mechanics, motion-control and robotics terms in later editions; no translations
- **decision_support**: none: no selector or comparison tables, only function-based chapters, a comprehensive index and glossaries

## 29 Mechanisms in Modern Engineering Design (Artobolevsky)

*Book / classification system.* 5-volume (7-book) Soviet catalogue of about 4,000 mechanisms with a formal classification

### Basics

- **name**: Mechanisms in Modern Engineering Design: A Handbook for Engineers, Designers and Inventors (Ivan I. Artobolevsky)
- **url**: No official publisher page (the Soviet publisher Mir no longer exists). Best bibliographic records: DMG-Lib series record https://www.dmg-lib.org/dmglib/handler?docum=3977009 (full text not available there) and DMG-Lib author page https://www.dmg-lib.org/dmglib/main/biogrViewer_content.jsp?id=183004&skipSearchBar=1 (lists the English, Russian and French editions). Scans of the English edition are on the Internet Archive as community uploads, e.g. https://archive.org/details/ArtobolevskyMechanismsInModernEngineeringDesignVol1, with no rights statement (see license)
- **resource_type**: book (multi-volume illustrated handbook) / classification system
- **language**: Russian original; English translation (Mir, 5 volumes in 7 books); French translation 'Les mecanismes dans la technique moderne' (Mir, 1975-1978)
- **year**: Predecessor: 'Mechanisms' (Russian, 4 volumes, USSR Academy of Sciences, 1947-1952, about 4,000 mechanisms). Russian 'Mekhanizmy v sovremennoi tekhnike' 1st edition 1970-1976 (5 volumes), 2nd revised edition in 7 volumes 1979-1981 (Nauka, 3,248 pages). English translation 1975-1980 (Vol. 1 1975, Vol. 2 1976, Vols. 3-4 1977, Vol. 5 1979/1980), reprinted 1979. No later edition
- **access_barrier**: Paywall / out of print for legitimate copies (buy second-hand or borrow from a library). DMG-Lib holds catalogue records only (full text not available)
- **image_license**: All rights reserved, same as the text. The line drawings are copyrighted and cannot be reused. A 2024 research dataset (Ghezelbash, arXiv 2409.03763) scraped 4,142 of its 2D sketches; that does not make them free to reuse
- **authority_level**: academic / professional reference: compiled by Academician I. I. Artobolevsky (USSR Academy of Sciences, a founder and the first President of IFToMM, the International Federation for the Promotion of Mechanism and Machine Science). Not peer-reviewed in the modern sense, but the standard large mechanism catalogue of the 20th century

### Coverage

- **covers**: Thousands of numbered mechanisms, each drawn and briefly described, across the whole of mechanical engineering. Vol. 1 and Vol. 2 (parts 1 and 2): lever mechanisms (linkages built from lower pairs), 2,288 mechanisms, starting with sections on the kinematic pairs themselves (Nos 1-54) and movable joints (Nos 55-119), then simple lever mechanisms (clamping, braking, stop/detent/locking, indexing, clutch, governor, safety, adjustment ...) and linkwork (four-bar to multi-link, parallel cranks, straight-line guides, dwell mechanisms, pantographs). Vol. 3: gear mechanisms (numbering continues from No. 2289). Vol. 4: cam, friction and flexible-link (belt, chain, cable) mechanisms. Vol. 5 part 1: hydraulic and pneumatic mechanisms; Vol. 5 part 2: electric mechanisms (sections 33-36: simple, lever-type, toothed and complex electric mechanisms). Many entries are whole devices from old machines, instruments and patents
- **organisation**: Two-level system. First by construction (what the mechanism is made of: lever, gear, cam, friction, flexible-link, hydraulic, pneumatic, electric), which decides the volume and section; then, inside each section, by function (what it does: e.g. 'Gripping, Clamping and Expanding', 'Stop, Detent and Locking', 'Indexing', 'Clutch and Coupling', 'Dwell', 'Safety'). Mechanisms are numbered continuously through all volumes. Two tables at the front of Vol. 1 (Table 1: classification by structural features; Table 2: classification by functional features) cross-link the two schemes. For a beginner the functional sub-groups are the useful handle ('I need something that locks'), but you must already know which construction family to look in, the same function is spread over many volumes, and readers complain that the order inside a group is hard to follow
- **scope_match**: high for mechanism TYPES: it is almost entirely clever mechanisms, with whole functional groups for locking, detents, indexing, clutches, safety devices, clamping and adjustment, plus full volumes on gear trains and on electric (electromagnet, solenoid, relay) mechanisms. Lower for makers: it is 1970s Soviet industrial practice with no 3D printing, snap fits, compliant joints or adhesives, and much of it is whole devices rather than single connectors
- **making_methods**: General mechanical engineering: machine tools, instruments, engines, vehicles, automation (industrial practice of the 1940s-1970s). Not aimed at 3D printing or hobby making
- **entry_structure**: Mechanism number, group heading, short name, a simple line drawing (schematic or isometric, with lettered links), and a plain-text description of how it moves; some entries add formulas (relationships between link lengths, displacements and velocities). Inventor names are given only when well known. No sources are cited per entry, no dimensions for building, no photos
- **audience_level**: professional (engineers, designers, technicians) and inventors; the author says he avoided specialist jargon so that people without engineering training can follow it

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low to medium. The author deliberately used simple drawings and plain descriptions, and the functional groups are intuitive. But the drawings are dense 1970s schematics, the descriptions are terse and full of reference letters, there are thousands of entries with little explanation of why you would choose one, and forum readers find the order 'no obvious order' and the explanations hard to follow
- **has_images**: yes. Every mechanism has a line drawing (schematic or isometric). Quality is mixed: clear but small and old-fashioned, and scans online vary in quality
- **has_animations**: no (print book). Some of its mechanisms have been animated by others (e.g. Nguyen Duc Thang's videos), but not as a set
- **gaps**: No maker methods (3D printing, laser cutting), no snap fits, living hinges, compliant or print-in-place joints, no adhesives; no dimensions, tolerances or materials; no selection guidance or comparison between alternatives; out of print, copyrighted and hard to get legally; old-fashioned examples; the functional index is split across volumes; no per-entry URLs
- **good_for_entry_links**: low. There are no stable legitimate per-mechanism web pages. The best you can do is cite 'Artobolevsky, Vol. X, No. NNNN' in text, and link to a catalogue record such as https://www.dmg-lib.org/dmglib/handler?docum=3977009. Linking to the unlicensed scans on the Internet Archive is not recommended
- **reusable_data**: Not reusable as a dataset from the publisher. The classification scheme itself (the list of structural families and functional group names) is an idea, not protected expression, and can be adapted with attribution. A 2024 research dataset ('A Dataset for Mechanical Mechanisms', about 9,000 images, 4,142 of them 2D sketches taken from Artobolevsky Vols 1-4) is on GitHub (farghea/database_for_mechanical_mechanism) with images on Google Drive. The arXiv paper is CC0, but the scraped images keep their original copyright
- **maintained**: No. Closed historical work (last Russian edition 1979-1981; English 1975-1980). The author died in 1977 and Mir Publishers no longer exists. It lives on through used copies, libraries and unofficial scans
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) The two-axis index: classify each entry both by what it is made of (construction family) and by what it does (function), and link the two in a cross-reference table. This maps well onto a flow chart ('what do you need it to do?' then 'which kind of parts can you make?'). (2) Functional group names written as plain needs: 'Stop, Detent and Locking', 'Gripping, Clamping and Expanding', 'Switching, Engaging and Disengaging', 'Link-Length Adjustment', 'Safety'. (3) Start the catalogue with the basic joints (kinematic pairs and movable joints) drawn pictorially rather than as symbols. (4) Continuous numbering so every entry has a short, citable ID. (5) One simple drawing per entry plus a short 'how it moves' paragraph
- **generator_or_parametric**: No
- **file_formats**: Printed book. Unofficial scans exist online as PDF/DjVu; no CAD, STL or data files
- **terminology_value**: medium. Good for systematic English names of mechanism families and functional groups (via the translator's standard terms), and for Russian-English equivalents through the parallel editions. Some terms are Soviet-translation style (e.g. 'linkwork', 'flexible-link mechanisms', 'inversors') and differ from modern Western or maker usage
- **decision_support**: Partial. The functional classification (Table 2) lets a designer look up mechanisms by the job they must do, and Table 1 by structure. There is no flow chart, filter or comparison of pros and cons

### Other Info

- **sources**: 
  - https://mirtitles.org/2018/10/13/mechanisms-in-modern-engineering-design-artobolevsky/ (Mir Books blog: preface extract, 7 books in 5 volumes, volume titles, translator Nicholas Weinstein, first published by Mir 1976, reprinted 1979)
  - https://www.engineersedge.com/mechanics_machines/mechanisms_in_modern_engineering_design_14885.htm (full preface and Vol. 1 table of contents with mechanism number ranges; 2,288 lever mechanisms in Vols 1-2; Table 1 and Table 2 classifications)

## 30 Reuleaux, The Kinematics of Machinery

*Classification system.* 1875 book that founded the classification of kinematic pairs and chains; the basis of the KMODDL models

### Basics

- **name**: The Kinematics of Machinery: Outlines of a Theory of Machines (Franz Reuleaux; German original 'Theoretische Kinematik', 1875)
- **url**: https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The_Kinematics_of_Machinery (full public-domain transcription of the 1876 English edition, with the scanned pages). Scans: https://archive.org/details/kinematicsofmach00reulrich (English, 1876), https://archive.org/details/theoretischekin00reulgoog (German, 1875)
- **resource_type**: book / classification system
- **license**: Public domain. The 1876 Macmillan English translation (Alexander B. W. Kennedy) and the 1875 German original are both out of copyright; Wikisource marks the original PD-old worldwide and the translation public domain in the US (published before 1931; translator died 1928). The Wikisource transcription text is offered under CC BY-SA, but the underlying work is public domain. What this means for the encyclopedia: text and figures can be quoted, reproduced and adapted freely
- **language**: German original (1875); English translation (1876); French translation 'Cinematique: principes fondamentaux d'une theorie generale des machines' (1877)
- **year**: 1875 (German, F. Vieweg und Sohn, Braunschweig, with a separate atlas of plates); 1876 (English, Macmillan, London); 1877 (French). Second volume 'Die praktischen Beziehungen der Kinematik zu Geometrie und Mechanik' (Lehrbuch der Kinematik, Vol. 2) in 1900. Latest edition: Dover unabridged and corrected reprint of the 1876 English edition, 1963
- **access_barrier**: none (free scans on the Internet Archive and Cornell eCommons, free transcription on Wikisource). The Dover 1963 reprint on the Internet Archive is lending-library only
- **image_license**: Public domain (19th-century woodcut figures and plates). 59 figures are already extracted on Wikimedia Commons in 'Category:Images from The Kinematics of Machinery (1876)', and the whole scan is there as a DjVu file
- **authority_level**: academic: the founding text of the theory of machines by Franz Reuleaux ('father of kinematics'), director of the Berlin Gewerbe-Akademie; a classic, historically authoritative, not peer-reviewed in the modern sense

### Coverage

- **organisation**: By theory, chapter by chapter: general outlines, phoronomics (relative motion, centroids), pairs of elements, incomplete pairs, incomplete chains, history, kinematic notation, kinematic analysis, chamber-crank trains, chamber-wheel trains, constructive elements, complete machines, kinematic synthesis. It is a treatise, not a look-up catalogue: there is a detailed contents list and an alphabetical index, but no grouping by 'what do you need it to do'. Hard going for someone who doesn't know the terms, and much of the vocabulary is Victorian (e.g. 'click-gear', 'centroid', 'chamber-wheel train')
- **scope_match**: medium. It founded the classification of connection types by how two parts touch and move (the kinematic pair), which is the backbone of any joint taxonomy, and it covers several in-scope families (pawls and ratchets, locks, brakes, clutches and couplings, planet-wheel trains, cams, belt and chain drives, springs). But it is theory-heavy, has no maker content, and treats most items briefly as examples of the theory
- **taxonomy_used**: Reuleaux's kinematic classification. (1) Pairs of elements: lower pairs (surface contact; the closed pairs are the screw, the turning pair and the sliding pair) versus higher pairs (line or point contact, e.g. gear teeth, cams, curves of constant breadth), plus force-closed and flexible (tension/pressure organ) pairs. (2) Kinematic chains: simple and compound, and their inversions (fixing different links). (3) Twelve class symbols for kinematic elements: S screw, R solid of revolution, P prism, C cylinder, K cone, H hyperboloid, G sphere (globe), A sector or arc, Z tooth or projection, V vessel or chamber, T tension organ, Q pressure organ, combined into formulas for chains and mechanisms. (4) A practical classification of constructive elements in three groups: I rigid elements (fastenings such as rivets, keys, strained joints and screwed joints, then pins, bearings, shafts, fixed couplings, levers, cranks, connecting rods, crossheads, friction wheels, toothed wheels, flywheels); II flectional elements (tension organs, pressure organs and their partners such as pipes, pistons, cylinders, stuffing boxes and valves, and springs); III trains (click-gear, brakes, moveable couplings and clutches). With the notation he showed that the four-bar linkage gives 54 mechanisms in 12 classes. The later Voigt model catalogue grouped Reuleaux's models into about 25 lettered classes (A-Z)
- **example_types**: screw pair; turning (revolute) pair; sliding (prismatic) pair; higher pairs such as the Reuleaux triangle in a square (curve of constant breadth); force-closed pairs held by springs or gravity; slider-crank and its inversions (swinging block, turning block); double slider-crank (elliptic trammel, Oldham coupling); crossed slider-crank; parallel and anti-parallel cranks; conic (spherical) crank chains (Hooke's universal joint); Root's blower and other rotary pumps (chamber-wheel trains); click-gear, i.e. pawl and ratchet holding (push click and pull click); ratchet trains (intermittent one-way drive with retaining gear); locks as ratchet trains (Chubb, Bramah, Hobbs, Yale); brakes and brake gear; engaging and disengaging gear (clutches); escapements and 'curb-trains'; springs as constructive elements (tension, pressure, bending and twisting springs); Watt's planet-wheel (sun-and-planet) train; epicyclic chamber-wheel gear; cam pairs; screwed and keyed joints (fastenings as closed chains)
- **making_methods**: General 19th-century mechanical engineering (machines, engines, pumps, locks). Not aimed at any modern making method
- **entry_structure**: Not entry-based. Each numbered section gives prose explanation, woodcut figures with lettered links, and the mechanism's symbolic formula (e.g. (C''3 P-) for a slider-crank chain), with historical notes and references to inventors and patents
- **audience_level**: undergraduate / professional / research (19th-century engineering students and engineers); today mainly history of technology and kinematics researchers

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low. Dense Victorian prose, symbolic formulas and specialist terms. It is valuable for its concepts (joint = pair, mechanism = chain) but not readable as a beginner's guide
- **has_images**: yes. Hundreds of clear 19th-century woodcut line figures plus plates (the German edition has a separate atlas of 8 folded plates, per the Internet Archive record). Public domain
- **has_animations**: no (book). Cornell's KMODDL and DMG-Lib show photos, GIFs and videos of the physical Reuleaux models that were made to accompany it
- **gaps**: Not a catalogue: no plain-English 'what is it for' grouping, no selection advice, no dimensions or design rules, no modern elements (snap fits, compliant or printed joints, quick-release hardware, powered latches, adhesives), old terminology, and the symbolic notation fell out of use after Reuleaux's death
- **good_for_entry_links**: medium to high. Wikisource has stable per-page URLs for every scanned page, e.g. https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Page:The_Kinematics_of_Machinery.djvu/478 (click-gear, push and pull clicks) and https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Page:The_Kinematics_of_Machinery.djvu/489 (locks as ratchet trains; brakes). Good for 'historic source' links on single entries such as ratchets, locks, Reuleaux triangle or slider-crank
- **reusable_data**: Fully reusable: the work and its figures are public domain. Structured access: Wikisource page-by-page wikitext (MediaWiki API), Wikidata item Q19091519, a DjVu scan and 59 extracted figures on Wikimedia Commons, and Internet Archive full-text OCR. The Cornell eCommons DSpace API exposes the German edition (Lehrbuch der Kinematik V.1 and V.2) and related KMODDL references
- **maintained**: Closed historical work (1875/1876, Vol. 2 1900). The online copies are well maintained: Wikisource page last edited January 2026 (most pages are still marked 'not proofread'); several Internet Archive scans; Cornell eCommons copies added 2018
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Describe every connection as a 'pair': what the two contacting surfaces are and how they let the parts move (surface contact = lower pair, line/point contact = higher pair). This is a simple top-level question for the flow chart ('do the parts slide, turn, screw, roll or grip?'). (2) The idea of 'closure': a pair can be held together by its shape (form-closed), by a force such as a spring or gravity (force-closed), or by the rest of the chain. This maps directly onto snap fits, magnets and spring latches. (3) Treat fastenings as pairs that have been locked so they no longer move, putting fixed joints and moving joints on one scale. (4) His grouping of 'click-gear, brakes, couplings and clutches' as 'trains' that stop or start motion is close to the project's special behaviours (one-way, self-locking, hand-release). (5) Inversion: show how fixing a different part turns one chain into several mechanisms, a nice way to show relatives between entries
- **design_rules_given**: No numerical design rules (no clearances, fits, strengths or torques). It gives geometric methods (centroids, tooth profile construction, Willis's method) and kinematic analysis only
- **generator_or_parametric**: No
- **file_formats**: Book. Online: Wikisource HTML, EPUB, MOBI and PDF exports; Internet Archive PDF, DjVu, EPUB and OCR text; Commons DjVu and individual figure images
- **terminology_value**: high for the origin and history of core terms: kinematic pair, lower and higher pair, kinematic chain, inversion, closure, centroid (later 'centrode'), Reuleaux triangle, and for German-English equivalents (Kennedy's translation notes, e.g. his split of 'click-gear' for holding pawls vs 'ratchet-gear' for driving pawls). Some of its words are obsolete, so pair them with modern names
- **decision_support**: None. There is no selector or comparison. The notation and the synthesis chapter were meant as a systematic way to generate mechanism options, but they are theoretical, not a look-up tool

## 31 Ingenious Mechanisms for Designers and Inventors (Jones)

*Book.* 4-volume collection (1930-1967) of mechanisms submitted by practising engineers, grouped by type

### Basics

- **name**: Ingenious Mechanisms for Designers and Inventors (4 volumes; Vol. I and II edited by Franklin D. Jones, Vol. III by Holbrook L. Horton, Vol. IV by John A. Newell and Holbrook L. Horton), Industrial Press / Machinery
- **url**: https://books.industrialpress.com/9780831110840/ingenious-mechanisms/
- **resource_type**: book (4-volume printed reference set, now publisher eBooks; Vol. I and II also scanned in the Internet Archive via the Digital Library of India)
- **language**: English
- **year**: Vol. I 1930 (it replaced F. D. Jones's 1918 'Mechanism and Mechanical Movements'; reprinted many times, e.g. 11th printing 1957); Vol. II 1936; Vol. III 1951; Vol. IV 1967. The set was later issued as a four-volume paperback set (Industrial Press lists 'January 1967'); still sold as eBooks by the publisher in 2026
- **access_barrier**: paywall for the publisher eBooks and print; none for the Internet Archive scans of Vol. I and II (free full view, PDF and OCR text, no login). Vol. III and IV have no free copy found; one IA copy of a 1936 printing is lending-only (free login, borrow)
- **authority_level**: educator / trade press (edited by the Machinery magazine editors who also produced Machinery's Handbook; mechanisms were contributed by practising engineers to Machinery's columns and chosen because they 'have stood the test of actual practice'; not peer reviewed)

### Coverage

- **organisation**: By function: each volume is split into about 17-21 chapters named after what the mechanism does (e.g. 'Overload Relief Mechanisms and Automatic Safeguards', 'Intermittent Motions from Ratchet Gearing'), and later volumes reuse the same chapter headings so the four books work as one reference library, with an index in each. Inside a chapter, each mechanism is a short titled section with a numbered figure. Grouping by job ('I need something that stops when overloaded') suits a beginner better than grouping by geometry, but the chapter and section names use 1930s machine-shop jargon (tripping, back-gears, dwell, feed motion) and there is no plain-English lookup
- **scope_match**: high for mechanism types (overload releases, slip and breakable-pin safety devices, ratchets and silent ratchets, over-running clutches, detent and indexing locks, Geneva drives, interlocks, toggle mechanisms, differential and epicyclic gear trains, electromagnetic trips); low on making method, since every example is from cast-iron industrial machinery, not 3D printing or hobby robotics
- **taxonomy_used**: Functional classification by the job the mechanism does in an automatic machine (not Reuleaux's kinematic pairs). Vol. I has 20 chapters, Vol. II 17, Vol. III 18, and Vol. IV 20 (adding 'Varying Continuously Rotating Output' and 'Clutch and Disconnecting Devices'). Recurring top-level groups: cams; intermittent motion (ratchet, gear/cam, Geneva); tripping/stop and overload; locking, clamping and locating; interlocking; reversing; reciprocating (crank, cam, variable-stroke, quick-return); oscillating; combined rotary-linear; speed changing and regulating; differential motions; straight-line motions; feeding, transfer, hoppers and ejecting; miscellaneous
- **example_types**: Breakable (shear) pins to prevent overload; cam-and-spring overload release; spring-and-toggle relief mechanism; beveled spring-plunger relief (detent-type slip coupling); overload relief for worm drive; silent ratchet with double-ended pawl; ratchets with lifting pawls to prevent noise; friction ratchets of cam, toggle and worm-pawl type (silent one-way clutches); one-revolution coiled-spring clutch (wrap-spring clutch); clutches engaged by a wedging action; over-running clutches; Geneva wheel for intermittent motion (including a version designed for slight over-travel); combined indexing and locking mechanism; lock to prevent reversal of rotation; interlocking segments on levers; toggle joint; sun-and-planet motion and compound epicyclic gear trains; differential gearing for large speed reduction and the Chinese windlass (differential hoist); magnetic clutch with automatic band brake; electromagnetic trip controller; centrifugal speed-limiting device; straight-line motions
- **making_methods**: General engineering and automatic machine design (machine tools, presses, feeding machinery); nothing specific to 3D printing, laser cutting or hobby robotics
- **entry_structure**: Short titled section (a few paragraphs) with one or more numbered line drawings labelled with letters, explaining what the parts are, how it works and what makes it special. No dimensions or design rules in most entries, no degrees of freedom, no CAD, no how-to-build
- **audience_level**: professional (machine designers, toolmakers and inventors of the 1930s-1960s); readable by a keen hobbyist with patience

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low to medium: descriptions are plain and short and every mechanism has a drawing, but the vocabulary is old machine-shop jargon, the drawings are dense lettered diagrams of industrial machines, and there are no photos or animations
- **has_images**: yes: many black-and-white line drawings (working diagrams), usually clear but with letter labels explained in the text; scans on the Internet Archive are of mixed quality
- **has_animations**: no
- **gaps**: No 3D printing, plastics or small-scale making; no compliant or print-in-place designs; no adhesives; few numbers or sizing rules; old jargon without plain-English aliases; examples are embedded in big machines, so the reader must extract the underlying connection type; only Vol. I and II are freely readable, and there are no stable per-mechanism links
- **maintained**: Static book, out of print in paper; the publisher still sells the eBooks (product pages live in September 2026). The Internet Archive scans are stable
- **ideas_to_borrow**: Chapters named after the job the mechanism does ('Overload relief and automatic safeguards', 'Locking, clamping and locating devices', 'Interlocking devices', 'Tripping or stop mechanisms'), which map well onto flow-chart questions like 'should it let go when overloaded?'; keeping the same headings across volumes so a growing collection stays consistent; putting several variants of one idea side by side so readers can compare 'similar in purpose but different in design'; one short 'what it is, how it works, why it is clever' description per drawing
- **design_rules_given**: Few: mostly qualitative. Some sections give general theory (cam motion types, coefficients of friction, power transmitted by friction gears, epicyclic train analysis), but there are no clearances, fits or strain limits for making the parts
- **generator_or_parametric**: no
- **terminology_value**: medium: a good source for classic names of machine mechanisms (Geneva wheel, trip dog, toggle relief, silent ratchet, over-running clutch, differential windlass), but the terms are old-fashioned and not always today's standard names; no translations or alias lists
- **decision_support**: none beyond the functional chapter grouping and the index; no selector logic, flow chart or comparison tables
