## 32 Handbook of Compliant Mechanisms (Howell et al.)

*Book.* Book with a library of compliant mechanism types and elements, from the BYU group

### Basics

- **name**: Handbook of Compliant Mechanisms (edited by Larry L. Howell, Spencer P. Magleby and Brian M. Olsen; Wiley, 2013)
- **url**: https://www.wiley.com/en-us/Handbook+of+Compliant+Mechanisms-p-9781119953456
- **resource_type**: book (edited reference handbook with an illustrated library of compliant mechanisms; also on Wiley Online Library as an online book, DOI 10.1002/9781118516485)
- **license**: All rights reserved, copyright 2013 John Wiley & Sons, Ltd. Linking to the Wiley pages is fine; figures cannot be reused without permission from Wiley (and in some cases from the original paper or patent authors)
- **language**: English
- **year**: 2013 (e-book January 2013, hardcover April 2013); first edition only, no later edition found
- **access_barrier**: paywall (buy the book or e-book, or use a university library with a Wiley Online Library subscription)
- **image_license**: All rights reserved (Wiley). Many library drawings are redrawn from papers and patents by the BYU group and others. The BYU CMR group's own website and maker models are a separate, freer route to images
- **authority_level**: academic (edited by leading compliant-mechanism researchers at Brigham Young University and Los Alamos National Laboratory, with chapter contributors from other universities; published by Wiley)

### Coverage

- **organisation**: The library (Chapters 10-13) is sorted by function in two groups: 'Elements of Mechanisms' (flexible elements and rigid-link joints, sorted by joint type: revolute, translate, universal) and 'Mechanisms' (basic mechanisms, then Kinematics, meaning the motion it makes, then Kinetics, meaning what it does with force and energy), each item with a reference number. A final chapter shows real products sorted the same way. Sorting by what the part does (e.g. 'Latch', 'Ratchet', 'Stability', 'Constant force') works well for someone who does not know the names, although some category names are technical ('Metamorphic', 'Mode', 'Kinetics')
- **scope_match**: high: compliant mechanisms are a named in-scope family, and the library includes many clever one-piece connectors and behaviours (snap-through bistable latches, compliant ratchets and overrunning clutches, living hinges, lamina emergent joints, constant-force and detent mechanisms) that suit 3D printing
- **taxonomy_used**: Two-level functional classification modelled on Artobolevsky-style rigid mechanism handbooks: (1) Elements of Mechanisms: flexible elements (beams, revolute, translate, universal) and rigid-link joints (revolute, prismatic, universal, others); (2) Mechanisms: basic mechanisms (four-bar, six-bar), Kinematics (translational, rotational, translation-rotation, parallel motion, straight line, unique motion path, stroke amplification, spatial positioning, metamorphic, ratchet, latch, others) and Kinetics (energy storage, stability, constant force, force amplification, dampening, mode, others). So 2 top-level categories and about 25 subcategories
- **example_types**: Living hinge; cross-axis flexural pivot; cartwheel flexure; lamina emergent torsional (LET) joint, inside and outside; compliant rolling-contact element (CORE); split-tube flexure; bistable hinge; bistable latch; bistable button and snap-action switch; overrunning ratchet clutch; X-bob ratchet; triggering ratchet; free-wheeling clutch; pin clutch; centrifugal clutch; detent mechanism; blind snap-fit fastener; battery cover clip; constant-force crank slider; ortho-planar spring; dampening ortho-planar spring; compliant force amplifier (crimper, pliers); fluid-level-indicator locking mechanism; rotational joint with fixed rotational centre and locking system; wire-gate carabiner; Walker liner locks; torque wrench
- **making_methods**: General product design and precision engineering: moulded plastics, MEMS (microscopic machines), precision flexure stages and sheet (lamina emergent) designs. Not written for 3D printing, but most planar designs translate directly to FDM printing and laser cutting
- **entry_structure**: Library entries are short: name, reference number and category, a drawing or figure (often showing the moved and unmoved positions) and a brief description with a literature or patent reference. The modelling chapters give equations and worked examples. No CAD files or build instructions
- **audience_level**: undergraduate to professional (the introduction and library are aimed at product designers and inventors; the modelling and synthesis chapters are graduate/research level)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: Wiley says the handbook 'avoids technical jargon' and Chapter 1 is written in very plain language with everyday examples, and the library is mostly pictures; but the middle chapters are heavy engineering maths and some library names are research acronyms (COPMM, HCCM, RaPR, CHEQR)
- **has_images**: yes: 'fully illustrated', with line drawings and photos for every library entry (images are marked in bold in the index)
- **has_animations**: no (print book); the BYU CMR website and YouTube channel have videos of many of the same mechanisms
- **gaps**: Expensive and paywalled; no 3D-printing guidance (layer direction, material choice for PLA/PETG/TPU, print tolerances); no CAD or STL files; few step-by-step sizing rules outside the maths chapters; many entries are MEMS or precision-stage designs rather than maker hardware; only compliant mechanisms, so no rigid gears, powered connectors or adhesives; entries are brief and assume the reader will look up the cited papers
- **good_for_entry_links**: low: no per-mechanism web pages. The book and its chapters have DOIs on Wiley Online Library (book DOI 10.1002/9781118516485), but chapters sit behind a paywall and the Wiley Online Library pages could not be opened from here (bot check), so cite by book, section and page instead; the Wiley product page https://www.wiley.com/en-us/Handbook+of+Compliant+Mechanisms-p-9781119953456 is the stable link
- **reusable_data**: not reusable as data: the classification scheme (category and subcategory names) can be borrowed as an idea with citation, but the entry list, text and figures are all rights reserved and there is no dataset or API
- **maintained**: Static book (first edition 2013, no second edition found); still in print and listed on Wiley and on the BYU CMR 'Books' page in 2026
- **ideas_to_borrow**: Split the catalogue into 'building blocks' (joints and elements) versus 'whole mechanisms'; within mechanisms, sort by the motion made (kinematics) and by what happens to force and energy (kinetics: stores energy, snaps between stable positions, gives constant force, amplifies force, damps); give each entry a reference number and show everyday product examples next to the abstract mechanism; Chapter 2's 'how to use this handbook to find an idea' walkthrough and Chapter 13's worked example are good models for the flow chart; Chapter 1's plain-language explanation that stiffness and strength are not the same thing
- **design_rules_given**: Some: Chapter 1 has a table of yield-strength-to-Young's-modulus ratios and resilience for common materials (e.g. polypropylene modulus 1.4 GPa) to choose flexible materials; Chapters 3-5 and the appendix give pseudo-rigid-body model formulas for flexural pivots, cantilevers and fixed-guided beams (stiffness and deflection) and bistable beam equations; library entries themselves have no dimensions
- **generator_or_parametric**: no
- **terminology_value**: high for compliant mechanisms: the standard source for names such as pseudo-rigid-body model, LET joint, CORE, lamina emergent mechanism, cross-axis flexural pivot, ortho-planar spring and bistable mechanism; English only, no translations
- **decision_support**: moderate: the functional category tree plus Chapter 2 ('Locating Ideas and Concepts in the Library', 'Summary of Design Approaches') and Chapter 13's worked example show how to go from a needed function to candidate designs; no interactive selector, flow chart or comparison tables

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - cost
  - covers
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - file_formats
  - item_count
  - special_behaviours_covered

## 33 Flexure and exact-constraint references (Smith's Flexures, Hopkins' FACT, Blanding's Exact Constraint)

*Book / classification system.* Books and a classification method for flexures, constraint patterns and kinematic mounts

### Basics

- **name**: Flexure and exact-constraint references: Stuart T. Smith, 'Flexures: Elements of Elastic Mechanisms' (book); Jonathan B. Hopkins' Freedom and Constraint Topologies (FACT, a classification chart and design method); Douglass L. Blanding, 'Exact Constraint: Machine Design Using Kinematic Principles' (book)
- **url**: Smith (publisher page): https://www.routledge.com/Flexures-Elements-of-Elastic-Mechanisms/Smith/p/book/9789056992613 ; Blanding (Google Books record): https://books.google.com/books/about/Exact_Constraint.html?id=DtxSAAAAMAAJ ; FACT overview: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_and_constraint_topologies ; FACT library chart (CC BY-SA): https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Freedom_and_constraint_topologies_(FACT)_library_poster.pdf ; FACT paper (author copy on an MIT course page): https://web.mit.edu/2.70/Reading%20Materials/FACT%20flexure%20design%20Hopkings,%20Culpepper.pdf ; Hopkins' lecture videos: https://www.youtube.com/@TheFACTsofMechanicalDesign/videos ; Hackaday review of Blanding: https://hackaday.com/2019/09/11/books-you-should-read-exact-constraint-machine-design-using-kinematic-principles/
- **resource_type**: book (Smith; Blanding) plus classification system (FACT: a chart of 50 freedom and constraint space 'types', published in peer-reviewed papers, with a free Wikipedia article, a CC-licensed chart and a YouTube lecture channel)
- **license**: Both books: all rights reserved (link only; do not copy figures). FACT journal papers: (c) Elsevier, all rights reserved. Wikipedia FACT article text: CC BY-SA 4.0. The redrawn FACT library chart on Wikimedia Commons (by Kuba Fober, 2022) is CC BY-SA 4.0, so it can be reused with credit and share-alike
- **language**: English
- **year**: Smith: 2000 (CRC Press; Google Books lists 8 Aug 2000, 430 pages; the Routledge page says copyright 2000 and also lists a 444-page format); no later edition found. Blanding: 1999 (ASME Press; Google Books 170 pages, Goodreads 186-page hardcover, first published 1 Oct 1999); no later edition found. FACT: created 2005 by Hopkins as an MIT master's student, first published at ASPE 2006, MS thesis 2007, PhD thesis and 'Part I: Principles' / 'Part II: Practice' papers in Precision Engineering 2010, serial-flexure paper 2011; still taught (ASPE tutorial Nov 2024; YouTube channel active)
- **access_barrier**: paywall for both books and for the FACT journal papers (academic licence via ScienceDirect); none for the Wikipedia article, Commons chart, YouTube lectures, the MIT-hosted author copy and the Hackaday review
- **authority_level**: academic peer-reviewed (FACT papers in Precision Engineering by Hopkins and Culpepper, MIT / LLNL / UCLA) plus professional reference books from established precision-engineering publishers (CRC Press; ASME Press). Blanding's book is widely cited as the classic on exact-constraint design (e.g. quoted in Layton Hale's 1999 MIT thesis chapter used as an MIT course reading)

### Coverage

- **scope_match**: medium: these are the authoritative sources for a family that is squarely in scope (flexure / compliant joints, kinematic mounts, couplings that tolerate misalignment) and FACT is a real classification of flexure arrangements, but they are precision-engineering theory, not a catalogue of named connection types, and they say nothing about fasteners, latches, clutches, gears or adhesives
- **taxonomy_used**: FACT: 50 'types' of freedom and constraint space pairs (a classification of screw systems suited to flexure design), grouped in 7 columns by degrees of freedom (0-6 DOF), symmetric about the 3-DOF column; a 'parallel pyramid' line separates types reachable by parallel flexures from those needing serial stacking; further split into parallel, serial and hybrid (incl. interconnected hybrid) systems. Blanding: constraint pattern analysis (constraints as lines in space, degrees of freedom as rotation lines; complementary patterns; Maxwell's rule that constraints plus degrees of freedom = 6 for an exactly constrained body). Smith: element vs system, planar vs rotationally symmetric flexures
- **example_types**: parallel leaf-spring flexure (blade flexure stage); notch hinge (circular/elliptical flexure hinge); cross-strip pivot (crossed-spring pivot); two-axis flexure hinge; four-bar flexure linkage; disc coupling (flexible disc shaft coupling); bellows flexure (bellows coupling); flexure lever (motion reducer/amplifier); wire flexure; sheet flexure; ball-and-socket joint as a 3-constraint connection; gimbal; edge guide with nesting force; lead-screw-to-carriage flexure coupling (FACT 2011 case study); serial flexure stage from stacked parallel modules; Hexblade 6-DOF compliant positioner; bistable binary-stiffness compliant mechanism; zero-stiffness compliant mechanism; compliant rolling-contact mechanism (flexure straps guiding rolling cams)
- **making_methods**: Precision engineering and instrument design (machined metal flexures, optics mounts, motion stages, MEMS); Blanding's rules apply to any machine and are popular with hobbyist 3D-printer builders (Hackaday); Hopkins' group also uses additive fabrication for flexure-based metamaterials
- **entry_structure**: Book chapters with line diagrams, derivations and worked hardware examples (Blanding adds chapter summaries); FACT chart cells: freedom space + constraint space pair, with rules for choosing flexure elements inside the constraint space and a case study
- **audience_level**: Smith: advanced undergraduate, graduate and practising engineers (publisher's description). Blanding: professional designers, but readable by hobbyists with little prior knowledge (Hackaday). FACT: graduate/research, with free lecture videos

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: Blanding medium-high (step-by-step, well-diagrammed examples, about 140-170 pages, little prior knowledge assumed, per Hackaday). FACT low-medium (the visual chart is approachable, but it rests on screw theory and projective geometry). Smith low (equation-heavy textbook)
- **has_animations**: no in the books; yes on Hopkins' YouTube channel 'The FACTs of Mechanical Design' (127 videos: FACT demonstrations, compliant mechanism demos, and a 'Traditional Mechanisms and Mechanical Systems' lecture series)
- **gaps**: No plain-English list of connection types; no guidance for 3D-printed flexures (layer direction, PLA/PETG fatigue, print-in-place); no fasteners, snap fits, latches, clutches or adhesives; books are paywalled and their figures cannot be reused; FACT needs screw theory to use beyond looking up the chart
- **good_for_entry_links**: Wikipedia FACT article is a stable link for a 'flexure design by degrees of freedom' entry (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_and_constraint_topologies). The books have stable publisher/Google Books pages but no per-type pages; individual YouTube videos can be linked from specific compliant-mechanism entries
- **reusable_data**: FACT chart: CC BY-SA 4.0 on Commons, reusable with credit and share-alike; the list of 50 types can be rebuilt from the papers (facts are not copyrightable) but no dataset exists. Books: not reusable
- **maintained**: Books: static (no new editions found since 1999/2000; Smith is still sold as print and eBook). FACT: actively taught and extended (UCLA Flexible Research Group research page, ASPE tutorial 4-5 Nov 2024, YouTube channel)
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Start the flow chart from the motion you want ('which directions should it move or turn?'), as FACT does, then show the matching joint layouts. (2) Blanding's simple counting rule in plain words: every direction you block is one constraint; blocking a direction twice makes it bind or wobble (overconstraint), so 6 minus the number of free motions = number of constraints needed. (3) 'Nesting force': say what holds the part against its locating points. (4) Hopkins' build-your-own flexure kits and short demo videos as hands-on learning. (5) Group flexures by element (blade, wire, notch hinge, cross-strip pivot, bellows, disc) like Smith
- **generator_or_parametric**: None found: no CAD generator or parametric models; FACT is a paper/chart method (the Commons chart is a redrawn PDF/JPG)
- **file_formats**: Print books and eBook (VitalSource for Smith); PDF journal papers; Commons chart as PDF and JPG; YouTube video
- **terminology_value**: high for flexure and constraint terms: degree of freedom, constraint line, overconstraint/underconstraint, exact constraint, kinematic design, nesting force, notch hinge, cross-strip pivot, blade/wire/sheet flexure, freedom space, constraint space, twist, wrench. No translations
- **decision_support**: FACT works as a lookup selector: pick the wanted degrees of freedom, find that freedom space in the chart, read off its constraint space, then place flexures inside it (with Maxwell's rule to check). Blanding's constraint pattern analysis is a manual check method. No interactive tool or filters
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Exact-constraint/kinematic design goes back to Maxwell (1890) and instrument makers; FACT builds on exact-constraint design and screw theory, and a FACT chapter appears in the Handbook of Compliant Mechanisms (Wiley 2013, pp. 79-92, per Wikipedia), overlapping the BYU Compliant Mechanisms item. Layton Hale's 1999 MIT thesis chapter on exact-constraint design (an MIT OCW 2.76 reading, https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/2-76-multi-scale-system-design-fall-2004/3aa5862a1724b75c3e4aa7a6fee6c511_reading_l3.pdf) quotes Blanding and overlaps the Slocum FUNdaMENTALs item. Roth's Konstruktionskataloge Band 2 has a parallel German treatment of blocking motion with support points (Stuetzpunkte)

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - cost
  - covers
  - design_rules_given
  - has_images
  - image_license
  - item_count
  - organisation
  - special_behaviours_covered

## 34 Roth design catalogues / VDI 2222 (Konstruieren mit Konstruktionskatalogen)

*Classification system.* German systematic design catalogues, including catalogues of connections and joints organised by principle

### Basics

- **name**: Roth design catalogues: Karlheinz Roth, 'Konstruieren mit Konstruktionskatalogen' (Designing with design catalogues, Springer, 3 volumes) and the guideline VDI 2222 Blatt 2 'Konstruktionsmethodik; Erstellung und Anwendung von Konstruktionskatalogen' (setting up and using design catalogues)
- **url**: Band 2 table of contents (3rd ed., German National Library/dandelon scan): https://external.dandelon.com/download/attachments/dandelon/ids/DE0048A7CC70CD50E5D1BC1257B48003FFDAB.pdf ; 1st edition (1982) contents: https://d-nb.info/810346869/04 ; Band 2 on Google Books: https://books.google.com/books/about/Konstruieren_mit_Konstruktionskatalogen.html?id=aCckBgAAQBAJ ; Band 3 (Verbindungen und Verschluesse): https://www.barnesandnoble.com/w/konstruieren-mit-konstruktionskatalogen-karlheinz-roth/1119170482 and https://openlibrary.org/books/OL12777361M.json ; VDI 2222 Blatt 2 at DIN Media: https://www.dinmedia.de/en/technical-rule/vdi-2222-blatt-2/549878
- **resource_type**: classification system and book (a multi-volume German handbook of systematic design catalogues, plus a VDI guideline on how such catalogues are built)
- **license**: All rights reserved (Springer; VDI/DIN Media). Link only; catalogue sheets and figures cannot be copied
- **language**: German
- **access_barrier**: paywall (books and guideline); the tables of contents are free as scanned PDFs from the German National Library / dandelon
- **image_license**: All rights reserved (Springer figures and catalogue sheets)

### Coverage

- **covers**: Band 2 (3rd ed.) holds 41 design catalogues, 210 figures with about 2,400 individual drawings and 516 term definitions. Catalogues relevant to connections and mechanisms: types of 'closure' (Schlussarten: form, force and material closure; element and combined closures), one-way and two-way stops, friction systems incl. self-clamping ones, variation of friction systems, logical pairing of solid bodies, mechanical logic gears (negation, AND/OR), mechanical flip-flops incl. push-push switches (T-flip-flops), generating force, single-stage force multipliers, mechanical backstops (one-way locks by friction clamping or positive contact, switchable backstops), chains and mechanisms up to four links, one-stage friction systems (wedge, lever and wrap-band systems), uniform-ratio gears with special tooth forms, fixed connections (rivets incl. blind rivets, shaft-hub connections), moving connections (linear guides incl. rolling-element guides, plain and rolling bearings), shape variation operations, forming processes, adhesive bonding of plates and cylinders, tolerance chains, plus a chapter on blocking motion with support points (Stuetzpunkte) and a directory of catalogues published by others. Band 3 covers connections and closures: classification of connections, catalogues of fixed connections, moving connections, function integration in connections and closing elements, systematics of combining connections, making fixed connections, and catalogues and a systematics of closures (Verschluesse, e.g. lids and latches)
- **organisation**: Each catalogue follows the uniform structure set out by Roth and VDI 2222 Blatt 2: a classifying part (Gliederungsteil: rows/columns by physical principle, closure type, motion or geometry), a main part (Hauptteil: sketches of each solution) and an access part (Zugriffsteil: properties to filter and compare), with a 'Prinzipblatt' explaining each catalogue; catalogues are either overview or detail catalogues, and object, operation, solution or relationship catalogues. Chapters follow the design process (task formulation, function, principle, embodiment). Very systematic, but all in German engineering jargon and organised by abstract principle, so a beginner who does not know the terms would find it hard
- **scope_match**: high for principle-level connection and mechanism types (closure types, backstops, stops, clamping friction systems, force multipliers, mechanical flip-flops, closures, shaft-hub joints), which match the project's 'clever types' scope; medium overall, because much is general design method, tolerancing and standard machine elements (rivets, bearings), and it is in German and paywalled
- **taxonomy_used**: Classification by physical principle and by 'closure' (Schluss): form closure (Formschluss, positive locking), force closure (Kraftschluss, friction/force locking) and material closure (Stoffschluss, bonding), plus combined closures; connections split into fixed (feste) and moving (bewegliche) connections, and closures (Verschluesse) as a separate group; mechanisms by number of links and joint types (turning, sliding, two-way joints); a 'logical closure matrix' (Logische Schluss-Matrix) that records which directions of motion each body pairing blocks. Band 2 arranges its 41 catalogues in 14 topic sections (11.1-11.14), with support-point motion blocking as a separate chapter (13)
- **making_methods**: General mechanical engineering and precision/fine-mechanism design (machining, forming, riveting, bonding); computer-aided and automatic design; nothing specific to 3D printing
- **entry_structure**: A catalogue sheet: classifying columns/rows, a sketch of each solution principle, and access columns listing properties (e.g. direction of blocking, force transmission, can be undone), preceded by a 'Prinzipblatt' explaining how the catalogue is built; supporting text chapters with case examples (e.g. bolt-setting tool, hand riveting machine, corkscrew)
- **audience_level**: professional and university (design engineers, mechanical engineering students and researchers)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low: German, dense technical terms (Schlussart, Elementenpaarung, Stuetzvektor), abstract principle drawings and matrix notation; valuable to an expert translating it, not to a beginner reading it directly
- **has_images**: yes: many small line sketches (about 2,400 in Band 2), schematic quality
- **has_animations**: no
- **gaps**: No English version; no photos, animations or plain explanations; no 3D-printing or maker guidance; standard machine elements dominate some catalogues; paywalled and not linkable per type; no compliant, print-in-place or powered connectors
- **good_for_entry_links**: poor: no per-catalogue web pages; only book-level records (Springer, Google Books, Open Library) and scanned tables of contents
- **reusable_data**: not reusable: print/eBook only, all rights reserved; the classification ideas (closure types, access-part properties) can be re-expressed in our own words and cited
- **maintained**: static: latest edition 3rd (2000-2001) with reprints around 2012; VDI 2222 Blatt 2 (1982) is marked withdrawn by DIN Media but still sold
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Uniform entry structure: a classifying part, a main part with one sketch per solution and an 'access part' of comparable properties; our finder fields can play the role of the access part. (2) Classify every connection by how it holds: shape (form-fit), friction/force (force-fit) or bonding (material-bond), which gives a simple first flow-chart question. (3) Split fixed connections, moving connections and closures (latches, lids) into separate branches. (4) Catalogue families of clever mechanisms by function, e.g. 'stop motion in one direction', 'multiply force', 'lock against reverse motion', 'toggle on/off with a push'. (5) Overview catalogue leading to detail catalogues. (6) A table of which directions of motion a joint blocks (Roth's closure matrix, similar to degrees of freedom tables)
- **generator_or_parametric**: No generator; Band 2 describes computer-aided and automatic design of connections using closure matrices and catalogues as software modules (research concepts, no downloadable tool)
- **terminology_value**: high for German design-methodology and machine-element terms (Formschluss, Kraftschluss, Stoffschluss, Ruecklaufsperre, Klemmgesperre, Schlingband, Welle-Nabe-Verbindung, Geradfuehrung, Verschluss), useful for German translations of our entries
- **decision_support**: Yes, the whole method is selection: access-part property columns let you filter solutions by required properties, plus evaluation and selection methods (technical-economic evaluation, utility analysis, scales) in Band 2 chapter 12; the 'algorithmic selection procedure' design flow chart in the 1st edition

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - authority_level
  - cost
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - design_rules_given
  - example_types
  - file_formats
  - item_count
  - special_behaviours_covered
  - year

## 35 DIN 8593 joining process classification

*Classification system.* German standard that groups every joining method (assembling, joining by mechanical means, forming, welding, bonding and so on)

### Basics

- **name**: DIN 8593 'Fertigungsverfahren Fügen' (Manufacturing processes: joining), parts 0 to 8, plus the withdrawn 1984 draft part 9 (textile joining)
- **url**: https://www.dinmedia.de/en/standard/din-8593-0/65031206 (DIN Media page for Part 0, the overview part; opened). Free German summary of the whole tree: https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%BCgen_(Fertigungstechnik)
- **resource_type**: classification system (German national standard in 9 parts; a process taxonomy that sits under main group 4 'Fügen' of the DIN 8580 manufacturing-process system)
- **license**: All rights reserved (DIN standards are copyrighted and sold under licence; reproduction is not allowed). The group names, numbers and short definitions can be cited as facts. The free German Wikipedia summaries are CC BY-SA 4.0 (licence link in the page footer), so they can be quoted or adapted with attribution and share-alike
- **access_barrier**: paywall (each part must be bought; no free official text). Free overviews on German Wikipedia and German teaching sites
- **authority_level**: official standard (DIN, German Institute for Standardization; committee NA 152-06-10 AA 'Terminologie der Fertigungsverfahren')

### Coverage

- **covers**: Every way of permanently or detachably joining solid parts (the 'Fügen' main group of manufacturing processes). There are 9 groups: 4.1 assembling (Zusammensetzen): lay on, insert, slide into each other, hook in, twist-lock (bayonet) and spring in (snap fits, clips, retaining rings). 4.2 filling (Füllen): e.g. impregnating, and filling gaps with lead, casting resin or foam. 4.3 pressing on and pressing in (Anpressen, Einpressen): screwing, clamping, clipping with staples, press fits and crimping, nailing and powder-actuated stud setting, wedging, bracing. 4.4 joining by primary forming (Fügen durch Urformen): casting in, embedding, overmoulding, potting, vulcanising in, electroforming in, sheathing, cementing with putty (Kitten). 4.5 joining by forming (Fügen durch Umformen): notching, centre-punching, collar joining, expanding, spreading, narrowing, necking, flanging, seaming, bending, tab joining (Verlappen), riveting and clinching, plus wire methods (twisting, stranding, splicing, knotting, weaving, stitching). 4.6 welding. 4.7 soldering and brazing. 4.8 adhesive bonding (Kleben). 4.9 textile joining (sewing, weaving, knitting, braiding, felting). DIN says the series defines almost 100 individual joining processes, 'from A for Auflegen via N for Nageln to Z for Zwischenzapfennieten'
- **organisation**: A strict numbered hierarchy by manufacturing process: main group 4 (joining) → group 4.x → process 4.x.y, going down to a fifth level in places. Grouping is by how the joint is made (by putting parts together, by elastic pressure, by casting, by deforming, by melting, with solder, with glue, with textiles), not by what the joint has to do. For beginners it's mixed: the group 4.1 verbs are plain and easy to picture (lay on, insert, slide in, hook in, twist in, spring in), but the rest assumes you already know the process names, and everything is in German
- **scope_match**: low: it's a manufacturing-process taxonomy, not a catalogue of clever connection or mechanism TYPES. It has no mechanisms, gear trains, powered connectors or special behaviours, and much of it (welding, soldering, riveting, screwing) is standard joining. It's still useful as an authoritative backbone for the joining side of the project, and group 4.1 names several in-scope types (bayonet/twist-lock, snap fit, clip, retaining ring, spring hook-in)
- **taxonomy_used**: DIN 8580 ordinal system: main group 4 'Fügen' split into 9 groups (4.1 Zusammensetzen, 4.2 Füllen, 4.3 Anpressen/Einpressen, 4.4 Fügen durch Urformen, 4.5 Fügen durch Umformen, 4.6 Fügen durch Schweißen, 4.7 Fügen durch Löten, 4.8 Kleben, 4.9 Textiles Fügen), each subdivided in its own part (DIN 8593-1 to -8). For example, 4.1 has 6 processes (4.1.1 lay on/set on; 4.1.2 lay in/insert; 4.1.3 slide into each other; 4.1.4 hook in; 4.1.5 twist-lock (Einrenken); 4.1.6 spring in (federnd Einspreizen)), and 4.3 has 7 (screwing, clamping, stapling/clipping, press fitting, nailing/driving in, wedging, bracing). The German Wikipedia article also gives two cross-cutting schemes from general joining practice (not from DIN 8593 itself): by how it holds (shape: Formschluss, friction: Kraftschluss, material bond: Stoffschluss) and by whether it comes apart (detachable, conditionally detachable, permanent)
- **example_types**: bayonet / twist-lock joint (Einrenken: push then turn, held by shape, released by overcoming friction or a spring, passing a detent step or opening a lock); snap-fit joint (Schnappverbindung, under 'federnd Einspreizen'); clip joint (Clipverbindung); retaining ring / circlip and expanding ring (Sicherungsring, Spreizring); leaf-spring clip (Blechfeder); hooking into a tension spring (Einhängen, e.g. tailgate hinges); sliding or telescoping fit (Ineinanderschieben, with clearance or transition fit); wedge joint (Verkeilen, self-locking); clamping joint (Klemmen); press / interference fit (Pressverbindung) and crimp joint (Crimpverbindung); powder-actuated stud setting (Bolzensetzen); clinching (Durchsetzfügen); sheet-metal seaming (Falzen) and flanging (Bördeln); tab bending / lanced tabs (Verlappen); collar joining (Kragenfügen); wire twisting, splicing and knotting; overmoulding and insert moulding (Umspritzen, Einbetten); potting and casting in (Vergießen, Eingießen); adhesive bonding (Kleben); sewing (textile joining)
- **making_methods**: Industrial manufacturing and assembly in general: metalworking, sheet metal, casting, plastics processing, electronics and textiles. Not aimed at 3D printing, and 3D printing is not a joining group (DIN 8580:2022 added additive manufacturing as group 1.10 under primary forming, not under joining)
- **item_count**: 9 groups (8 current parts plus the withdrawn textile draft); almost 100 individual joining processes defined, down to a fifth level in places (DIN news page). The Wikipedia summaries list 6 processes in 4.1, 7 in 4.3, 9 in 4.4, and 13 sheet/solid processes plus about 7 wire processes in 4.5
- **audience_level**: professional (manufacturing and design engineers, standards users); widely taught in German vocational training and undergraduate manufacturing courses

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low: German only, paywalled, and terse definitions for specialists. The free Wikipedia and teaching-site summaries (e.g. technikermathe.de) are much friendlier and give everyday examples, but they are still in German
- **has_animations**: no
- **gaps**: No mechanisms, motion or adjustable connections. No special behaviours (torque limiting, one-way, over-centre, hand-release). Nothing for 3D printing or print-in-place. No design rules, pictures or selection help. Adhesives are classified as a process, not by chemistry and material. Everything is in German and behind a paywall. The 2003 terms predate modern maker techniques (heat-set inserts, printed snap fits, magnets) and powered latches
- **good_for_entry_links**: Partly. DIN Media has stable per-part product URLs (e.g. https://www.dinmedia.de/en/standard/din-8593-1/65031218 for Part 1, assembling), but they are sales pages. German Wikipedia has one article per group (e.g. https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%BCgen_(Fertigungstechnik) with links to Zusammensetzen, An- und Einpressen, Fügen durch Umformen, etc.). These are fine as 'official classification' links for the German name, but useless for most English readers
- **reusable_data**: No dataset. The tree (numbers and names) is short enough to rebuild by hand from the Wikipedia summaries, and names and numbers are facts that can be cited. The standard's definitions are copyrighted, so don't copy them; the Wikipedia wording is CC BY-SA 4.0 (attribution and share-alike)
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) The plain assembly-motion verbs of group 4.1 (lay on, insert, slide in, hook in, twist-lock, spring in) make an excellent beginner flow-chart question: 'How do the parts go together?'. (2) The three holding principles, held by shape (Formschluss), friction (Kraftschluss) or material bond (Stoffschluss), as a top-level filter. (3) The detachable / conditionally detachable / permanent split as a filter (it maps to the project's removability field). (4) Stable numbered codes (4.1.5 etc.) as a cross-reference column so entries can cite the official German process name. (5) The idea of 'auxiliary joining parts' (Hilfsfügeteile: screws, rivets, staples, wedges) vs joining the parts directly, to separate hardware-based from integral connections
- **design_rules_given**: No. It gives terminology and classification only, with no dimensions, clearances or strengths
- **generator_or_parametric**: No
- **file_formats**: PDF download or printed copy (each part is 4 to 6 pages)
- **terminology_value**: high for German names and their English equivalents: Einrenken = bayonet/twist-lock joining, federnd Einspreizen = snap-in/spring-in joining, Einhängen = hooking in, Ineinanderschieben = sliding together, Verkeilen = wedging, Klemmen = clamping, Pressverbindung = press/interference fit, Durchsetzfügen = clinching, Falzen = seaming, Bördeln = flanging, Verlappen = tab joining, Kleben = adhesive bonding, Fügen durch Urformen = joining by primary forming (casting/moulding in). DIN Media's official English part titles give English group names (assembling; filling; joining by mechanical means; joining by processing of amorphous materials; joining by forming; welding; soldering or brazing; adhesives)
- **decision_support**: None. It's a classification only, with no selector, comparison tables or flow chart. Its hierarchy could serve as the skeleton of a 'how is it joined' branch

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - cost
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - entry_structure
  - has_images
  - image_license
  - language
  - maintained
  - special_behaviours_covered
  - year

## 36 Snap-fit and integral attachment books (Bonenberger's The First Snap-Fit Handbook, Messler's Integral Mechanical Attachment)

*Book / classification system.* Books that classify snap fits and integral locks, with design rules

### Basics

- **name**: Snap-fit and integral attachment books: 'The First Snap-Fit Handbook: Creating and Managing Attachments for Plastics Parts' (Paul R. Bonenberger, Hanser) and 'Integral Mechanical Attachment: A Resurgence of the Oldest Method of Joining' (Robert W. Messler, Butterworth-Heinemann/Elsevier)
- **url**: Bonenberger: https://www.hanserpublications.com/The-First-Snap-Fit-Handbook/978-1-56990-595-1 (publisher page, 3rd edition; opened). Messler: https://shop.elsevier.com/books/integral-mechanical-attachment/messler/978-0-7506-7965-7 (publisher page with full table of contents; opened)
- **resource_type**: book (two technical reference books, each built around its own classification system for snap fits and integral locks)
- **license**: All rights reserved (commercial copyright by Hanser and Elsevier). Cite and link only; don't reproduce text or figures. Hanser lets the cover image be used free of charge to promote the book
- **language**: English
- **access_barrier**: paywall (buy the book or eBook) or academic licence (ScienceDirect / Hanser eLibrary). Google Books has a limited 'Preview this book' for Messler
- **image_license**: All rights reserved, the same as the text. Both books are illustrated mainly with line drawings of features and joints (Hanser mentions 'many new figures with expert explanations'; Elsevier says Messler will 'fully illustrate the major classes of integral mechanical fasteners')
- **authority_level**: Bonenberger: industry expert (37 years at General Motors as Staff Project Engineer specialising in mechanical attachments; President of FasteningSmart, Inc. since 2006). Messler: academic (Professor and Associate Dean of Engineering, Rensselaer Polytechnic Institute). Both rest on 1990s peer-reviewed research into snap-fit classification (Messler, Genç and Gabriele's five-part 'Integral attachment using snap-fit features: a key to assembly automation', Assembly Automation, 1997). The books themselves are published technical references, not peer-reviewed

### Coverage

- **covers**: Bonenberger: snap fits in (mainly injection-moulded) plastic parts, treated as a whole attachment rather than one hook. Covers the key requirements (strength, constraint, compatibility, robustness); the elements of a snap fit (function, basic shapes, engage direction, assembly motion, constraint features, enhancements); 13 locator styles; lock styles (cantilever beam locks, planar locks, trap locks, torsional locks, annular locks) and lock pairs; enhancements for assembly, use, performance and manufacture; constraint rules and a constraint worksheet; lock 'decoupling' levels 0 to 4; material data and design points; cantilever-hook rules of thumb and full calculations (tapered beams, effective angles, assembly and separation forces); a step-by-step development process; diagnosis of problems; and organisational advice. Messler: all integral mechanical attachment, meaning joints where features built into the parts do the holding, across materials. It covers rigid interlocks (joint elements, locking and attachment features, couplings and connectors, embedded fasteners, locators); elastic snap fits (cantilever hooks and cantilevered holes, compressive traps and beams, annular, leaf-spring and window snaps, post-and-socket snaps, bayonet-finger snaps, combined assembly motions, hooks-and-loops); plastic (formed-in) attachments (formed tabs, co-formed features, crimping and hemming, staking/setting incl. thermal staking, Tog-L-Loc and Lance-N-Loc); then chapters by material (metals: sheet, cast, extruded, forged, machined; polymers: moulded-in features and inserts, thermally bonded stakes, rubber connections; wood: corner, T- and splice joints; concrete and masonry; glass and ceramics incl. glass-to-metal seals); mechanical electrical connections (incl. microelectronics and high-current power); and future directions (MEMS self-assembly, biomedical)
- **organisation**: Bonenberger: by physical element (locators, locks, enhancements), then by development process (concept → feature design → calculations → diagnosis). Each chapter ends with 'Important Points' and 'Design Rules Introduced'. Messler: by how the attachment behaves during assembly (rigid, elastic or plastic), then chapter by chapter by material, with a 'Classification Revisited' chapter that compares methods and gives a single taxonomy. Both are readable for a newcomer who knows what a snap fit is. Messler's rigid/elastic/plastic split is especially intuitive, but neither has a lookup by 'what do I need it to do'
- **scope_match**: high: snap fits, integral locks, bayonet-type snaps, living hinges, formed tabs, staking and interlocking joints are core in-scope connection TYPES for the project's first priority (3D-printed and plastic parts), and both books classify them rather than list catalogue hardware. They do not cover mechanisms, gear trains, powered connectors or adhesive chemistries
- **taxonomy_used**: Bonenberger's 'Attachment Level Construct' (called 'Key Requirements' and 'Physical Elements' in the 3rd edition). A snap fit is described by function (action, attachment type, retention, lock type), basic shapes of the mating and base parts, engage direction, assembly motion, constraint features (locators plus locks) and enhancements. That gives 3 families of physical element: locators (13 styles: lug, tab, wedge, cone, pin, catch, surface, land, edge, hole, slot, cutout, living hinge), locks (5 styles: cantilever, planar, trap, torsional, annular) and enhancements (guides, clearance, pilots, operator feedback, visuals, assists, user feel, guards, retainers, compliance, back-up locks, process-friendly, fine-tuning). Messler: 3 top-level classes by feature behaviour (rigid, elastic/snap-fit, plastic/formed-in), an alternative scheme by how the feature or joint is created, sub-classification schemes within each class, and a placement of integral attachment within the wider taxonomy of joining methods (mechanical, adhesive, welding/soldering/brazing)
- **item_count**: Bonenberger 3rd edition: 14 chapters, 412 pages; 5 lock styles, 13 locator styles, about 13 enhancement types, 5 decoupling levels, 8-step development process (Steps 0 to 7). Messler: 13 chapters, 407 pages; 3 main classes, about 9 named elastic snap sub-types, about 7 formed-in (plastic) types, plus rigid-interlock and material-specific examples
- **entry_structure**: Book chapters, not entries. Explanatory text with line drawings for each feature style, worked calculations, product examples and case histories, a symptom-based diagnosis list (Bonenberger: difficult assembly, distorted parts, feature damage, loose parts), and end-of-chapter lists of important points and design rules
- **audience_level**: professional (product, plastics and design-for-assembly engineers); Messler also targets upper-undergraduate and first-year graduate students. Bonenberger's 1.5 'Using this Book' has separate advice for snap-fit novices, experienced designers, DFA practitioners and managers

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: Bonenberger was written for beginners as well as experts (the publisher says the 3rd edition was 'extensively rewritten for clarity and user-friendliness'), and its locator/lock/enhancement vocabulary is simple to grasp. But it's long, expensive and full of engineering terms (constraint, degrees of motion, secant modulus). Messler is more academic and broad but explains why each type works
- **has_animations**: no (print and eBook only)
- **gaps**: Neither is free or online, so a beginner can't browse them. No 3D-printing tolerances, layer-direction or FDM strain guidance. No CAD files or generators. Bonenberger covers plastic snap fits only; Messler is broad but light on numbers. No mechanisms, adjustable or powered connections, or adhesive selection. Figures can't be reused
- **good_for_entry_links**: no: there are no per-type URLs. Entries can cite the book and section (e.g. 'Bonenberger 2016, ch. 5 Physical Elements: Locks' or 'Messler 2006, section 4.7 Bayonet-finger Snaps'). The publisher pages are stable enough to link as a 'further reading' reference
- **reusable_data**: not reusable: all rights reserved. The taxonomy terms themselves (e.g. the 5 lock styles, the rigid/elastic/plastic classes) can be used as facts with citation, but the text and figures can't be copied
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Messler's three-way split, rigid vs elastic (snap) vs plastic (formed-in, one-time), as a beginner-friendly top-level question: 'Does it just fit, does it flex to click in, or do you bend or melt it to lock?'. (2) Bonenberger's 'locator + lock + enhancement' pattern: every entry could show which features locate the part, which lock it, and which make it nicer to use. (3) Assembly motion and engage direction as flow-chart filters. (4) Step 0, 'Is the application appropriate for a snap-fit?', as a gatekeeping question before recommending a snap. (5) Decoupling levels as a way to explain stronger snap designs. (6) A symptom-based troubleshooting table (hard to assemble, parts distorted, feature damaged, parts loose). (7) Messler's by-material chapters as a filter (metal, plastic, wood, glass/ceramic, masonry)
- **generator_or_parametric**: No
- **file_formats**: print; PDF and EPUB eBooks (Hanser offers PDF, Print, EPUB; Messler is available as an eBook via Elsevier/ScienceDirect)
- **terminology_value**: high for snap-fit and integral-attachment vocabulary: locator, lock, enhancement, lug, catch, trap, planar lock, torsional lock, annular snap, back-up lock, decoupling, attachment level (Bonenberger); rigid / elastic / plastic interlocks, post-and-socket snap, bayonet-finger snap, window snap, cantilevered hole, formed tab, staking, Tog-L-Loc, Lance-N-Loc (Messler). Bonenberger's terms are the de facto standard names in snap-fit design literature. No translations
- **decision_support**: Bonenberger: a staged snap-fit development process (Step 0 'is a snap fit appropriate?', define the application, benchmark, generate concepts by choosing engage directions, assembly motions and constraint pairs, add enhancements, pick the best concept, analyse features, confirm with parts, fine-tune), a constraint worksheet, 'Selecting a Locking Feature' guidance and 'Lock Alternatives', and diagnosis lists by symptom. Messler: a comparison of relative advantages and disadvantages of methods (6.1) and a single taxonomy (6.2). No interactive tools

## 37 Japanese joinery catalogues and tools (Sato and Nakahara, The Joinery animations, Tsugite)

*Book / classification system.* Books cataloguing Japanese wood joints, the widely shared The Joinery 3D animations, and the Tsugite research tool

### Basics

- **name**: Japanese joinery catalogues and tools: The Complete Japanese Joinery (Hideo Sato and Yasuo Nakahara, trans. Koichi Paul Nii), The Art of Japanese Joinery (Kiyosi Seike), The Joinery 3D GIF animations (@TheJoinery_jp on X/Twitter) and Tsugite (Larsson, Yoshida, Umetani and Igarashi, University of Tokyo, UIST 2020)
- **url**: No single home page. Sato and Nakahara book record: https://archive.org/details/completejapanese0000unse (Open Library edition OL22474514M, with table of contents); Seike book record: https://archive.org/details/artofjapanesejoi0000seik (Open Library edition OL4542211M); The Joinery animations: https://x.com/thejoinery_jp; Tsugite code: https://github.com/marialarsson/tsugite (paper DOI 10.1145/3379337.3415899, metadata and abstract checked at https://api.semanticscholar.org/graph/v1/paper/DOI:10.1145/3379337.3415899?fields=title,year,abstract,citationCount,openAccessPdf,venue,authors)
- **resource_type**: book (two illustrated catalogues of traditional Japanese wood joints) + video/animation collection (The Joinery GIFs on X) + interactive tool (Tsugite, a research prototype desktop app on GitHub)
- **cost**: Sato and Nakahara: paid, paperback about USD 29.95 new (Lake Forest Book Store listing), cheaper used; free to borrow from Internet Archive's lending library. Seike: paid, about USD 29.95 new (William Stout Architectural Books listing), also borrowable on Internet Archive. The Joinery GIFs: free to view on X (a free X login may be needed to scroll the full history). Tsugite: free for personal use and non-commercial research; commercial use needs a licence from the University of Tokyo TLO (README)
- **language**: English (both books are English translations of Japanese originals; Seike's is a translation of 'Kigumi'). The Joinery posts give each joint's Japanese name in kanji plus a romanised (romaji) name. Tsugite: English interface and README
- **access_barrier**: Books: paywall (buy) or free Internet Archive login to borrow (the scans are access-restricted lending copies, not open downloads). The Joinery: none to view the profile; X may ask for a free login to scroll or view older posts. Tsugite: desktop install (Python 3.8 with packages from requirements.txt, then run tsugite_app.py) plus a 3-axis CNC mill to make the joints

### Coverage

- **organisation**: Sato and Nakahara: by function, then by building part (end joints, right-angle joints, fasteners, framing, floors, roofs); uses Japanese category terms with English glosses (e.g. 'Tsugite—End Joints', 'Shiguchi—Right Angle Joints'). Seike: a curated gallery of 48 joints with history. The Joinery: a chronological social media feed; each post is titled only with the kanji and romanised Japanese name, so it is hard to browse for someone who does not know the terms (names end in -tsugi for splices and -shikuchi for corner joints, which is the only built-in grouping). Tsugite: interactive, with a manual edit mode and a gallery mode filtered by validity
- **scope_match**: low to medium: these are clever self-locking interlocking joints, which fit the 'clever connection types' idea, but they are woodwork/CNC joinery, which the project ranks lower priority; useful mainly for the short list of interlocking joints and as a visual presentation model
- **making_methods**: Hand-tool woodwork and timber framing (both books); CAD animation (The Joinery used Autodesk Fusion 360); 3-axis CNC milling (Tsugite, with corner rounding so milled parts still slide together)
- **entry_structure**: Books: joint name (Japanese and English), isometric exploded drawing, photographs, use in a building, cutting and layout method (Sato and Nakahara add step-by-step layout and tool technique). The Joinery: one looping animated GIF per post with the kanji and romaji name only, no text explanation or dimensions. Tsugite: an editable 3D voxel joint with live feedback (slidability, fabricability, durability with regard to fibre direction), up to four suggested fixes, a .tsu save file and CNC milling path export
- **audience_level**: Books: hobbyist to professional woodworker and carpenter (Seike's publisher says even weekend carpenters can copy the joints); The Joinery: general public and hobbyists; Tsugite: research / advanced maker with a CNC mill

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: The Joinery animations are the most beginner-friendly way to see how an interlocking joint slides together, but the posts use only Japanese names; Seike is a gentle illustrated introduction; Sato and Nakahara is dense and uses Japanese carpentry terms; Tsugite needs Python setup and CNC knowledge
- **has_images**: yes: both books have clear isometric line drawings (Seike adds 64 pages of photographs); The Joinery has clean shaded 3D renders; Tsugite README has interface screenshots
- **gaps**: No plain-English names or grouping by what the joint does; no strength numbers, clearances or fits for the maker's materials; nothing on 3D printing (tolerances for printed dovetails, for example); The Joinery gives no text, dimensions or source book for each joint and has not posted since 2017; books are paid and not openly online; Tsugite is a research prototype with no support and needs a CNC mill
- **good_for_entry_links**: partly: individual X posts have stable per-post URLs (e.g. https://x.com/thejoinery_jp as the profile; individual status URLs exist but none were opened), though X may require login and posts can vanish. Book records are stable (https://archive.org/details/completejapanese0000unse) but are whole-book, not per-joint. Tsugite repo link (https://github.com/marialarsson/tsugite) is fine as a single 'design your own joint' link
- **reusable_data**: not reusable as data: the books and GIFs are all rights reserved and scrape-only; joint names can be reused as facts. The Open Library record gives a machine-readable table of contents. Tsugite code is available but only for personal and non-commercial research use
- **ideas_to_borrow**: One short looping animation per joint showing the parts sliding together and locking (The Joinery's format is the single most shared way these joints have been explained). Compositional naming: break a name into shape + locking device + group, and teach those few root words (ari = dovetail, kama = gooseneck, sen = peg, tsugi = splice, shikuchi = corner). Split joints into 'lengthening' (end-to-end) and 'meeting at an angle' groups. Tsugite's live checks (can it slide together? can it be made with a round cutter? is the grain strong here?) could become flow-chart questions
- **generator_or_parametric**: yes for Tsugite: a voxel-grid joint editor with a gallery of pre-computed valid joints and automatic 3-axis CNC toolpath generation; the books and GIFs are not parametric
- **terminology_value**: high for Japanese joint names: the books and The Joinery give standard kanji and romanised names (tsugite, shiguchi, ari, kama, sen, shachi) that make good aliases next to English terms such as dovetail, gooseneck and draw-bore peg
- **decision_support**: None in the books or The Joinery beyond grouping by joint location. Tsugite gives live pass/fail feedback and suggestions for valid joints, which is a kind of design check but not a selector
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: The Joinery's animations were redrawn from the creator's collection of Japanese woodwork books (sources not named per joint), so they overlap with Sato and Nakahara and Seike. Seike is a translation of 'Kigumi'. Jochen Gros's 50 Digital Wood Joints (separate item) includes CNC versions modelled on Japanese joints (e.g. 'Ryo-men-ari-tsugi'). Tsugite overlaps with other HCI joint tools in the 'HCI fabrication research on joints' item (Zheng's Joinery tool) and with MiGumi (2025)

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - authority_level
  - design_rules_given
  - example_types
  - file_formats
  - has_animations
  - image_license
  - item_count
  - license
  - maintained

  - year

## 38 Digital joinery catalogues (Jochen Gros 50 Digital Wood Joints, WikiHouse, IBOIS EPFL tools)

*Book / classification system.* Catalogues and open systems of CNC-cut interlocking joints

### Basics

- **name**: Digital joinery catalogues and open systems: 50 Digital Wood Joints (Jochen Gros and Friedrich Sulzer, C-Lab, HfG Offenbach), WikiHouse Skylark (Open Systems Lab) and IBOIS EPFL timber joinery tools (Manis plate joinery solver, compas_wood, Robeller's integral plate joints)
- **url**: No single home page. 50 Digital Wood Joints: https://winterdienst.info/50-digital-wood-joints-by-jochen-gros/ (download https://jochen-gros.de/A/C-Lab_files/Digital_Wood_Joints_Complete.zip, poster https://jochen-gros.de/A/C-Lab_files/50%20digital%20joints.pdf, project page https://jochen-gros.de/A/C-Lab.html, mirror https://openup.design/we-learn/50-digital-wood-joints/). WikiHouse: https://www.wikihouse.cc/blocks and https://www.wikihouse.cc/engineering/bow-ties (repo https://github.com/wikihouseproject/Skylark). IBOIS: https://www.epfl.ch/labs/ibois/index-html/tools/ (Manis https://github.com/ibois-epfl/Manis-timber-plate-joinery-solver, compas_wood docs https://petrasvestartas.github.io/compas_wood/latest/)
- **resource_type**: classification system + CAD library (Gros's 50 joints with CAD/CAM files and PDF guide) + open building system (WikiHouse block library) + interactive tools / GitHub repos (IBOIS Grasshopper and Python joinery generators)
- **license**: Gros joints: CC BY-NC-SA 3.0 on winterdienst.info; Open Up! lists CC BY-NC-SA 4.0; the re-organised cutting files on GitHub (ayseesindurmaz) say CC BY-SA 4.0 but credit Gros and Sulzer and reserve commercial credit to them. So: free to share and adapt with credit, non-commercial, share-alike. WikiHouse Skylark: CC BY-SA 4.0 (commercial use allowed, improvements must be re-shared; the name 'WikiHouse' is a trademark). Manis: MIT (Copyright 2021 IBOIS, EPFL). compas_wood: MIT (Petras Vestartas)
- **language**: 50 Digital Wood Joints: English and German (the CD package has parallel ENGLISH and DEUTSCH folders; the C-Lab project page is in German). WikiHouse and IBOIS tools: English
- **year**: Gros joints: C-Lab founded 1993, published as a CD-ROM in 1997; zip file last modified September 2016; winterdienst page April/May 2018; poster laid out by Meredith Scheff-King (Make: article December 2014); Open Up! mirror October 2023. WikiHouse: started summer 2011 (Alastair Parvin and Nick Ierodiaconou of 00); Skylark system about 2020, Skylark v1.0 repo release current in 2026. IBOIS: Robeller's integral joints PhD 2011 to 2015; Manis 2021 (Automation in Construction vol. 130); compas_wood docs updated in 2026 (unreleased changelog entries)
- **access_barrier**: none for the Gros download and WikiHouse files; desktop install for IBOIS tools (Rhino 7/8 with Grasshopper on Windows, plus Robots plugin, COMPAS and Abaqus for the full Manis workflow); the Gros CAD files are in 1990s formats (DGN, IGES, DXF R10 and CAM formats) that need a CAD program to open
- **authority_level**: Gros: educator (design school research lab, HfG Offenbach). WikiHouse: community/open-source with engineering testing (University of Strathclyde bow-tie tests; peer-reviewed Materials 2025 paper on TIE joints). IBOIS: academic peer-reviewed (EPFL timber construction lab, Prof. Yves Weinand)

### Coverage

- **covers**: CNC-cut interlocking wood joints only. Gros: about 50 joints (51 entries in the English guide, as some appear twice) for solid wood frames and for board materials such as plywood, including knock-down (tool-free, detachable) furniture joints and joints with loose keys. WikiHouse: a complete building chassis made of CNC-cut plywood/OSB blocks joined with plywood bow-tie (TIE) connectors and slot-together parts; it is a system rather than a catalogue of joint types. IBOIS: generators for timber-plate joints (through tenons, chamfered tenons, finger joints, half-laps, dowels, tab-and-slot and dovetail plate joints) and 5-axis CNC and robot assembly
- **scope_match**: low to medium: clever interlocking and knock-down joints fit the 'clever connection types' idea and can be laser-cut or CNC-cut by makers, but this is CNC woodwork joinery, which the project ranks lower priority; the Gros list is the best source for the wanted short list of interlocking CNC joints
- **taxonomy_used**: Gros: 3 top-level groups (frame joints, board joints, carcass joints), each split by joint position (lengthening, corner, cross, T, slotting). compas_wood: 7 contact-type families with code ranges (1-9 side-to-side in-plane, 10-19 out-of-plane, 20-29 top-to-side, 30-39 cross, 40-49 top-to-top, 50-59 rotated, 60-69 boundary). Manis: joint functions (dowels, tenons, chamfered tenons, 'sunrise', custom finger/slot, fingers, half-lap). Robeller: multiple tab-and-slot joints with one degree of freedom (1DOF), assembled along a single insertion direction
- **example_types**: From Gros: Shouldered Dovetail; Double Dovetail; Symmetrical Double Dovetail (modelled on the Japanese Ryo-men-ari-tsugi); Double Jigsaw (puzzle-shaped splice); Plain Scarf with Dovetail Keys (butterfly/bow-tie key); Gooseneck Mortise and Tenon with Stub Tenons; Ginkgo Scarf; Halving with Elliptical Tenon; Jigsaw Cross Halving; Hooked Jigsaw Halving; Stop Lap with Jigsaw Key; Secret Fingertip Tenons; Hammer Tenons; Clip Tenons; Catch Tenon (detachable corner); Slotting Girder Joint (tool-free shelf joint); Hooked Slotting Joint; Lengthening with Meander Key; Detachable Lengthening with Key; Locked Leg Framing (removable); 3-Dimensional Finger Tenon; Tenon Joint with Star-Shaped Mortise. From WikiHouse: plywood bow-tie (TIE) connector. From IBOIS: multiple tab-and-slot plate joint (through tenon), chamfered tenon, dogbone and T-bone corner reliefs for CNC cutting
- **making_methods**: CNC routing (3-axis for Gros and WikiHouse, 5-axis for IBOIS), laser cutting (Gros joints per the GitHub repo), robotic assembly (IBOIS); materials are solid wood, plywood, OSB, LVL and cross-laminated timber
- **entry_structure**: Gros: joint name, rendered drawing, a paragraph on history, use and strength, an 'example of application' (e.g. Simplizissimus Table, Meander Shelf), and a file code linking to CAD/CAM files in several formats plus a machining tutorial. WikiHouse: block page with model, cutting files and metadata; engineering pages give design values. IBOIS: Grasshopper components and Python API docs with example scripts, a 3D viewer and videos
- **audience_level**: Gros: hobbyist to professional furniture maker with a CNC router. WikiHouse: self-builders, architects, structural engineers and fabricators. IBOIS: research / professional computational designers

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: Gros's joints have plain descriptive English names (Jigsaw Cross Halving, Hammer Tenons) and short explanations, and the poster gives an at-a-glance overview, but the data files are in dated formats; WikiHouse is well documented but aimed at building a house; IBOIS tools are low (Rhino/Grasshopper scripting)
- **has_images**: yes: Gros has rendered drawings of each joint and a one-page poster of all 50; WikiHouse has photos and diagrams; IBOIS has renders and research images
- **gaps**: No 3D-printing versions or tolerances for printers; Gros gives few numbers beyond the 8 mm router bit; no strengths for most joints; the files need conversion to modern formats (no STEP, STL or SVG); no selector by load or removability; WikiHouse and IBOIS are about buildings and large plates, not small maker parts; IBOIS tools need paid Rhino
- **good_for_entry_links**: medium: there are no per-joint web pages for Gros's joints (all inside one zip and one poster), so link to the winterdienst or Open Up! page. WikiHouse engineering pages have stable per-topic URLs (e.g. https://www.wikihouse.cc/engineering/bow-ties). compas_wood has per-example doc pages (e.g. https://petrasvestartas.github.io/compas_wood/examples/joinery_parameters/)
- **reusable_data**: structured: Gros's joint list and CAD files can be reused non-commercially with credit and share-alike (CC BY-NC-SA); a GitHub re-packaging of the cutting files exists. WikiHouse block library is on GitHub and Airtable under CC BY-SA 4.0. IBOIS code is MIT (freely reusable, including the joint-family code scheme)
- **ideas_to_borrow**: Gros's grid: first ask 'what are you joining (frame, board or carcass)?' then 'how do they meet (end-to-end, corner, cross, T, slot)?' This is a ready-made flow-chart branch for joints. Give every joint an 'example of application' photo. Offer one printable poster of all joints. compas_wood's contact-type families (side-to-side, top-to-side, cross and so on) and 'insertion direction' idea are good plain-language filters. WikiHouse shows how to publish tested design values next to a joint
- **design_rules_given**: Gros: designed for an 8 mm router bit; cut 1 mm per pass on the FBZ training machine; slotting joints are cut 2 mm narrower than the board thickness with a 2 mm groove extension so they fit despite thickness variation. WikiHouse bow ties: shear area 1008 mm², shear stiffness 3 kN/mm, tensile area 1008 mm², tensile stiffness 1.5 kN/mm, contact area at failure about 767 mm², with a capacity spreadsheet; the Materials 2025 study measured TIE joints in 18 mm plywood at about 7.7 kN in tension and 8.2 kN in shear (best variants). IBOIS: tool-radius offsets and dogbone/T-bone notch generation
- **generator_or_parametric**: yes for IBOIS: Manis and compas_wood generate joint geometry, CNC toolpaths and robot paths from plate models (Grasshopper/Python). WikiHouse: fixed blocks now, with parametric block models on its roadmap. Gros: fixed drawings only
- **terminology_value**: medium: Gros gives clear English and German names for CNC-adapted joints and links some to their Japanese originals; compas_wood gives a systematic contact-type vocabulary; WikiHouse terms (bow tie, TIE joint, block) are system-specific
- **decision_support**: Gros's frame/board/carcass and lengthening/corner/cross/T/slot grid acts as a simple two-step selector. WikiHouse gives rules of thumb (e.g. minimum lengths of solid bracing wall per 6 m) and a bow-tie capacity spreadsheet. IBOIS tools pick joints by contact type automatically. No comparison tables across joint types
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Gros's joints are CNC reinterpretations of traditional European and Japanese joints (e.g. Symmetrical Double Dovetail from Ryo-men-ari-tsugi), so they overlap with the Japanese joinery catalogues item; the ayseesindurmaz GitHub repo and the Meredith Scheff-King poster (Instructables, Make:) are derived from Gros. Robeller's IBOIS joints were inspired by cabinetmaking joints; Manis and compas_wood overlap with each other. Overlaps with the HCI fabrication joints item (Zheng's Joinery, kyub) and Boxes.py for laser-cut finger joints

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - cost
  - file_formats
  - has_animations
  - image_license

## 39 Making Things Move (Dustyn Roberts)

*Book.* Beginner book on mechanisms, couplings, gears and motors for non-engineers

### Basics

- **name**: Making Things Move: DIY Mechanisms for Inventors, Hobbyists, and Artists (Dustyn Roberts), McGraw-Hill / TAB, with the author's companion web page
- **url**: https://dustynrobots.com/making-things-move/
- **resource_type**: book (printed paperback and eBook), plus an author companion page with CAD files, photos and videos for each chapter's projects
- **cost**: Paid. In September 2026 Barnes & Noble listed the paperback at $39.00 and the eBook at $35.00; Apple Books sells the eBook at $22.99; Adafruit used to sell it at $30.00 (now 'no longer stocked'). Used copies are cheap on second-hand sites. Chapter 1 is a free PDF download from Make: (offered with the author's permission), and the author's page says Chapter 6 was also posted as a free excerpt
- **language**: English (original). A German translation 'Making Things Move - Die Welt bewegen' (first published 30 November 2011, ebook about 360 pages) and a Polish translation 'Wpraw to w ruch. Proste mechanizmy dla wynalazcow, majsterkowiczow i artystow' (2015) also exist
- **year**: 2010 (only one English edition: Kindle edition dated 15 November 2010, paperback dated December 2010); no second edition found. The companion page is still online in 2026
- **access_barrier**: paywall (buy the book or eBook; also on subscription services such as McGraw-Hill AccessEngineering and O'Reilly, which were not opened). None for the free Chapter 1 PDF and the companion page
- **authority_level**: educator (Dustyn Roberts is a mechanical engineer who wrote the book from her NYU ITP course on mechanisms for artists and designers; later an assistant professor of mechanical engineering at the University of Delaware). Trade-published, not peer reviewed

### Coverage

- **organisation**: As a course, by topic in teaching order (vocabulary, materials, joining, forces, power, motors, components, mechanisms, making, projects). Each chapter ends with small projects and the last chapter has bigger projects that combine topics. This works well for a beginner reading cover to cover because terms are explained as they appear, but it is not a lookup reference: there is no grouping by what a connection does and you need the index to find a given part
- **scope_match**: low to medium: it explains the basic building blocks a beginner needs (fasteners, shaft couplers, set screws, gears, worm drives, springs, cams, linkages) very clearly, but it covers few unusual or clever connection types (no torque limiters, ratchets, detents, over-centre latches, compliant or print-in-place joints) and leans towards standard off-the-shelf parts from suppliers like McMaster-Carr
- **taxonomy_used**: No formal mechanism classification. Components are grouped by role in a machine: the six simple machines as the basic vocabulary; five gear types (spur, rack and pinion, bevel, worm, planetary); joints split into non-permanent (screws, pins) and permanent (welds, rivets, glue); motion makers (motors of several kinds, solenoids, muscle wire); 'guts' (bearings, couplers, gears, screws, springs); and mechanisms that convert rotary to linear motion (cams and followers, cranks, linkages). About 10 chapter-level groups
- **example_types**: Six simple machines (lever classes 1-3, pulley and block and tackle, wheel and axle, inclined plane and wedge, screw, gear); spur gears; rack and pinion; bevel gears; worm gear (explained as not back-drivable, so it holds position without slipping); planetary (epicyclic) gears; power screw / screw jack; friction drive pulley; timing belt; set screw and shaft collar; shaft coupler (attaching things to a motor shaft); thrust bearing; torsion spring; cam and follower; crank and linkage (automata); minimum constraint design (three-point mounting); solenoid; muscle wire (shape-memory alloy); glue, pins, rivets and tapped threads
- **making_methods**: Hobby and art prototyping: off-the-shelf parts, hand tools and a drill press, laser cutting (Ponoko), 3D printing (services and home printers such as Shapeways), aluminium extrusion (80/20), moulding and casting (Smooth-On), paper models, plus Arduino electronics. Examples were designed in free or low-cost CAD (Alibre Design, Inkscape gear generator)
- **entry_structure**: Chapter sections in plain conversational English with photos and diagrams, worked number examples (for example seesaw balancing with the dog 'Fido' and the cat 'Fluffy'), shopping lists with supplier part numbers, and step-by-step project instructions with figures. No per-part data sheets, degrees-of-freedom tables or CAD per entry, but the companion page links CAD and templates for the projects
- **audience_level**: hobbyist (artists, designers, inventors and makers with no engineering background)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: high: written for non-engineers, explains every term the first time it appears, uses everyday examples (seesaws, can openers, bicycles, wine-bottle openers), and pairs each idea with a small project. The maths is simple and worked through step by step
- **has_images**: yes: many black-and-white photos, clear line drawings, screenshots and renders of 3D models throughout (every project is illustrated)
- **has_animations**: no in the book; the companion page links Vimeo videos of some projects (Rube Goldberg machine, DIY motor, DC motor control)
- **gaps**: Very few unusual or clever connection types (no torque limiters, ratchets, detents, snap fits, over-centre latches, compliant or print-in-place joints, magnetic couplings). No design rules for 3D-printed parts (clearances, printed thread and snap-fit sizes). Published in 2010, so the 3D-printing, CAD software and supplier advice is dated (Alibre, RadioShack, Ponoko). Organised as a course, not a lookup reference or selector. Many companion links on the author page point to old Make: blog, Ponoko and yfrog addresses that no longer work as written
- **good_for_entry_links**: low: the book has no per-topic web pages. The companion page is one long page with a section per chapter (https://dustynrobots.com/making-things-move/) and the free Chapter 1 PDF (https://cdn.makezine.com/make/pdf/MTM_chap01.pdf) is useful for linking basic entries (simple machines, gear types, worm gear self-locking, minimum constraint design). Otherwise cite by chapter and page
- **maintained**: Static book from 2010 with no new edition; still in print and sold as an eBook in 2026. The author's companion page is still online (opened September 2026) and includes one correction (AC voltage on page 101), but many of its outbound links are old and some are broken
- **ideas_to_borrow**: Start with a short 'vocabulary' chapter (simple machines, degrees of freedom) before the catalogue, so beginners can read later entries; explain each term with an everyday object ('a can opener is a pair of gears'); put a tiny build-it project or test beside each concept; add shopping lists with the proper search terms for suppliers; a dedicated section on 'how do I attach something to a motor shaft' (set screws, shaft collars, couplers), which the author says is one of the biggest problems her students have; clearly separate permanent from removable joints; friendly chapter titles ('Screw it or glue it') that say what the reader wants to do
- **generator_or_parametric**: no in the book; one project uses the Inkscape gear generator to make laser-cut spur gears
- **terminology_value**: medium: a good plain-English authority for basic terms (classes of lever, rack and pinion, back-driving, minimum constraint design, shaft collar, set screw, diametral pitch), but covers few specialist mechanism names and no translations (apart from the German and Polish editions of the whole book)
- **decision_support**: limited: advice on choosing materials and choosing a motor type 'by process of elimination' from the torque and power needed, with comparisons of motor types; no flow chart, selector or comparison tables for connections
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Grew out of the course the author developed for NYU's Interactive Telecommunications Program (ITP); chapter 1 cites the US Navy 'Basic Machines and How They Work', David Macaulay's 'The Way Things Work' and Kamm and Skakoon on minimum (exact) constraint design. Overlaps with the free US Navy Basic Machines text (already in the prior-art list) and with maker-oriented mechanism guides; much less mechanism coverage than '507 Mechanical Movements' or Sclater and Chironis
