## 40 Robot Mechanisms and Mechanical Devices Illustrated (Sandin)

*Book.* Illustrated book of robot mechanisms: drivetrains, arms, grippers and sensors

### Basics

- **name**: Robot Mechanisms and Mechanical Devices Illustrated (Paul E. Sandin), McGraw-Hill / TAB Electronics
- **url**: https://books.google.com/books/about/Robot_Mechanisms_and_Mechanical_Devices.html?id=ybfH64fP3ZsC
- **resource_type**: book (paperback and eBook; an illustrated sourcebook of mobile-robot mechanisms)
- **license**: All rights reserved (McGraw-Hill, 2003). Fine to link to and cite; the text and drawings cannot be copied. Several full PDFs circulate on unofficial sites; these are pirated copies and must not be used or linked
- **language**: English
- **year**: 2003 (eBook dated 21 May 2003; paperback June-July 2003). Only one edition found; no later update
- **access_barrier**: paywall (buy the book or eBook; a Google Books preview with selected pages and the table of contents is free; also on McGraw-Hill AccessEngineering, which could not be opened here)
- **authority_level**: educator / practitioner (written by a working robot designer: Paul E. Sandin, mechanical engineer at iRobot, previously RedZone Robotics, where he designed suspensions for large toxic-waste clean-up robots). Trade-published, not peer reviewed

### Coverage

- **organisation**: By robot subsystem: power (motors), transferring power (indirect, then direct), getting around (wheels, tracks, steering, legs, special crawlers, comparison), handling things (arms and grippers), and sensing. Grouping by subsystem is easy for someone building a robot, but a reader looking for a connection by what it does (for example 'slips when overloaded') has to know it lives in 'Direct Power Transfer Devices'. A 2016 reader review describes the organisation as poor: chapter introductions do not say what is coming, and several sections are batches of captioned drawings ('ten mechanisms which couple shafts or limit torque') that the main text never discusses
- **scope_match**: medium: strong on robot drivetrains, gear reducers, shaft couplings, torque limiters, suspensions and grippers, which are in scope as mechanism types, but much of the book is whole-vehicle layout (wheel counts, tracks, walkers) and motor control, which is beyond single connections, and it is aimed at metal industrial and research robots, not 3D printing
- **taxonomy_used**: By robot subsystem and function, not by kinematic pair: 11 top-level chapters (motor and motion control; indirect power transfer; direct power transfer; wheeled suspensions and drivetrains; tracked suspensions and drivetrains; steering; walkers; pipe crawlers and special cases; comparing locomotion; manipulator geometries; proprioceptive and environmental sensing). Within the wheeled chapter, drivetrains are grouped by number of wheels (1-3, 4, 6, 8)
- **making_methods**: Robotics (mobile, research and consumer robots); general engineering with metal parts, bought-in gearmotors, belts and chains. Includes a section on rapid prototyping processes (photopolymer 3D printing, urethane moulding) but not design for hobby 3D printing
- **entry_structure**: Chapter text explaining each subsystem with comparisons, plus many line drawings, some with only a one- or two-sentence caption (for example the entire description of one torque limiter is two sentences). No dimensions, degrees-of-freedom tables, CAD files or build instructions
- **audience_level**: hobbyist (advanced) to professional: mobile-robot builders, competition robotics teams and engineers; the motor-control section is written for non-electrical-engineers

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low to medium: it uses robot-engineering vocabulary without much explanation, many drawings have terse captions that are hard to decode, and the structure can be confusing; but it is visual, and the subsystem chapters (wheels, tracks, steering, grippers) match how robot builders think
- **has_images**: yes: many black-and-white line drawings and diagrams of mechanisms, drivetrains and grippers; readers praise the effort in the illustrations but find some hard to understand because parts are unlabelled or hidden
- **has_animations**: no
- **gaps**: No 3D-printing design rules, clearances or print-in-place ideas; little on latches, ratchets, detents, snap fits or adhesives; terse captions on borrowed drawings; no plain-English aliases; examples are 2003-era research and industrial robots; no selector beyond the locomotion comparison chapter; no companion website, CAD or videos found
- **good_for_entry_links**: low: no per-mechanism web pages. The Google Books page (https://books.google.com/books/about/Robot_Mechanisms_and_Mechanical_Devices.html?id=ybfH64fP3ZsC) shows the table of contents with chapter page numbers, so entries can cite 'Sandin 2003, chapter 3' with a page number
- **reusable_data**: not reusable: all rights reserved; only the chapter structure and the ideas can be used
- **maintained**: Static 2003 book with no new edition; the eBook is still sold in 2026, the paperback appears out of print
- **ideas_to_borrow**: A 'comparing locomotion methods' chapter that sets options side by side, which could become a comparison table in the flow chart; grouping power transmission into 'indirect' (belts, chains, gears) and 'direct' (couplings, splines, torque limiters), a clear first question for 'how do I get motion from the motor to the part'; organising drive and suspension options by a simple countable property (number of wheels); a lesson from the reader criticism: every mechanism drawing needs labelled parts and a plain explanation of how it works, not just a caption
- **generator_or_parametric**: no
- **file_formats**: Printed paperback, eBook (Apple Books, Kindle, McGraw-Hill digital); no downloadable files
- **terminology_value**: medium: a useful source for robot drivetrain and suspension names (skid steer, rocker suspension, tracked road wheels, pipe crawler, gearhead, harmonic drive, spline) and gripper types; no translations or alias lists
- **decision_support**: limited: a chapter comparing locomotion methods (wheels, tracks, legs and others) and comparisons within chapters; no flow chart, filters or selector for connections

## 41 LEGO Technic references (Isogawa's Technic Idea Books, Sariel's Unofficial LEGO Technic Builder's Guide)

*Book.* Picture-led catalogues of gear, linkage, differential and steering mechanisms built from LEGO

### Basics

- **name**: LEGO Technic references: Yoshihito Isogawa's 'The LEGO Technic Idea Book' series (Simple Machines, Wheeled Wonders, Fantastic Contraptions) and Pawel 'Sariel' Kmiec's 'The Unofficial LEGO Technic Builder's Guide' (1st and 2nd editions)
- **url**: https://sariel.pl/guide2/ (the author's page for the Builder's Guide, 2nd edition, with the full chapter list). No single web home exists for the Isogawa series; its catalogue records are at https://archive.org/details/simplemachines0000isog, https://archive.org/details/wheeledwonders0000isog and https://archive.org/details/legotechnicideab00isog (Internet Archive lending library). The publisher's pages (nostarch.com) could not be opened from this environment (HTTP 403)
- **resource_type**: book (two picture-led book series from the same publisher, No Starch Press)
- **cost**: Paid. The Isogawa Idea Books were $19.95 each when launched in 2010 (per the ROBOTMAK3RS review). The Builder's Guide 2nd edition is 'around $35/£25' (The Lego Car Blog review). All four printed books can also be borrowed free, one reader at a time, from the Internet Archive lending library with a free account
- **license**: All rights reserved (commercial books, copyright of the authors and No Starch Press; LEGO is a trademark of the LEGO Group and the books are unofficial). What this means for the encyclopedia: cite and link to them as further reading, but do not copy their photos, drawings or building instructions
- **language**: English (the No Starch editions). The Isogawa books began as Japanese books (the 'Red, Blue and Green books', mostly in Japanese text) and the English editions use very little text. The Builder's Guide is also translated: 1st edition into Finnish, German, Korean, Polish and Simplified Chinese; 2nd edition into German and Polish (list on sariel.pl/books)
- **year**: Isogawa Technic Idea Books: English editions October 2010 (library records give 2010 or 2011). Builder's Guide: 1st edition June 2012 (353 pages, 20 chapters); 2nd edition October 2016 (424 pages by the author's count, 25 chapters; Open Library records 403 pages, dated 2017). Related later Isogawa titles: The LEGO Power Functions Idea Book vols 1-2 (2015/2016) and LEGO Technic Non-Electric Models: Simple Machines (July 2021, 180 pages). No newer edition of either original series was found
- **access_barrier**: paywall (buy the book), or a free login to borrow the scanned copy for a limited time from the Internet Archive lending library (the items are marked access-restricted, i.e. controlled lending)
- **image_license**: All rights reserved, same as the text. Isogawa's photos and Sariel's rendered diagrams belong to the authors/publisher. Sariel's own website photos are also his copyright (his site warns about scam shops using stolen photos)
- **authority_level**: community or unvetted, but expert-level: both authors are well-known LEGO builders, not engineers writing to a standard. Sariel's 1st edition had a technical review by Eric Albrecht (Blakbird) and a foreword by Fernando Correia (TechnicBRICKs editor), per sariel.pl. Commercially published by No Starch Press, not peer-reviewed

### Coverage

- **covers**: LEGO Technic building mechanisms, mostly transmission of motion and small machines. Isogawa vol. 1 'Simple Machines' (x + 157 pages) covers gears and power transmission: 22 sections from gears, shafts and connectors, gear combinations, gears in a turntable, gears in series, angled gears, worm drives, chains and treads, rubber bands and rack-and-pinion gears to 'moving to and fro', pulley systems, rubber-band power and 'making music'. Vol. 2 'Wheeled Wonders' (ix + 131 pages) covers vehicles that drive, turn, move things and reverse. Vol. 3 'Fantastic Contraptions' (161 pages) covers walkers (4-, 6- and 2-legged), wind, weights, vibration, springs, magnets, lights, pneumatics, solar panels, off-centre axis of rotation, intermittent movement and changeover mechanisms. Each book holds 'hundreds' of small working models. Sariel's Builder's Guide (2nd edition) has 25 chapters in 5 parts: Basics (concepts, pieces, studless vs studded, axles/bushes/joints, wheels), Mechanics (gears, chains and pulleys, levers and linkages, custom mechanical solutions, pneumatics, building strong), Motors (LEGO motors, Power Functions, RC), Advanced mechanics (steering, suspension, tracked vehicles, transmissions, adders and subtractors, planetary gearing, 3D-printing custom pieces) and Models (form vs function, scaling, modelling). The 1st edition had over 500 figures and 40 building instructions; the 2nd added 13 more instructions
- **organisation**: Isogawa: by what the mechanism does or what powers it (e.g. 'Worm drives', 'Moving to and fro', 'Using magnets', 'Intermittent movement', 'Changeover mechanisms'), with pictograms instead of words; even the table of contents uses icons (ROBOTMAK3RS review). This works very well for someone who doesn't know the terms: you browse pictures by the motion you want. Sariel: a textbook order from basics to advanced, by mechanical topic (gears, linkages, pneumatics, steering, suspension, transmissions), with an index. It uses correct engineering names, so it suits readers who can name what they want
- **scope_match**: medium. Both are catalogues of mechanism TYPES (gear trains, worm drives, differentials, planetary gearing, gearboxes, steering and suspension linkages, intermittent motion, walkers), which fits the in-scope gear trains and semi-complex mechanisms. But everything is built from one proprietary toy system, so the connection methods themselves (pins, axles, bushes) are LEGO-specific, and there is little on connections for printed, machined or woodworking parts, adhesives or special locking behaviours
- **taxonomy_used**: No formal taxonomy. Isogawa groups models into themed sections (22 in Simple Machines, 17 in Fantastic Contraptions) by motion or power source. Sariel uses 5 parts and 25 chapters by mechanical topic (Basics, Mechanics, Motors, Advanced mechanics, Models)
- **example_types**: Worm drive (worm gear); rack and pinion; gears at an angle (bevel gears); gears in a turntable (slewing ring drive); chain and sprocket drive; rubber-band (belt) drive; pulley system (block and tackle); differential; adders and subtractors (differential used to add or subtract two motions); planetary gearing (epicyclic gear train); sliding-gear and driving-ring gearboxes (manual transmission); virtual-pivot steering (steering linkage whose pivot sits in the tyre centre line); wheeled and tracked suspension; levers and linkages; walker linkages (four-, six- and two-legged walking mechanisms); intermittent movement (intermittent-motion mechanism); changeover mechanism (direction-dependent output switching, e.g. by reversing a motor); off-centre axis of rotation (eccentric/crank); pneumatic devices (pneumatic cylinders and valves); Sariel's 'Infinite Stepper Mechanism' (web post)
- **making_methods**: LEGO Technic building (toy construction system), educational robotics (LEGO Mindstorms), and in Sariel's 2nd edition a chapter on 3D printing custom LEGO-compatible pieces. Principles carry over to general mechanism design, but no CNC, woodwork or general 3D-printing design rules
- **entry_structure**: Isogawa: a short title and pictograms, then close-up colour photos of each small model from several angles, with parts colour-coded so you can rebuild it from the pictures alone; no step-by-step instructions and almost no text. Sariel: textbook chapters with explanation, computer-rendered diagrams, tables (e.g. a gear ratio table) and, for selected mechanisms, step-by-step building instructions
- **audience_level**: hobbyist (children to adult LEGO fans, schools and educational robotics). Sariel's book 'starts simple for first-time builders, but quickly move[s] to advanced material'

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: high. Isogawa's books need no reading at all: you learn each mechanism from photos and icons. Sariel's book is written plainly, 'doesn't have the feel of a school science textbook' (The Lego Car Blog) and explains gear ratios and linkages from first principles, but assumes you are building with LEGO parts
- **has_images**: yes, full colour throughout. Isogawa: large, close-range photos from several angles with colour-coded parts. Sariel: clear rendered diagrams (the author is a graphic designer; over 100 figures were redrawn for the 2nd edition)
- **gaps**: Everything assumes LEGO parts, so dimensions, tolerances and joints do not carry over directly to 3D printing or machining; no plain-language index of 'what do I need this for'; nothing on snap fits, flexure design, adhesives, quick-release or hand-release locking, over-centre latches or torque limiters (as far as the tables of contents show); Isogawa has no explanations of why a mechanism works; paid and copyrighted, so pictures cannot be reused
- **reusable_data**: not reusable: printed books with no dataset or API. Tables of contents are available as library metadata (Open Library, Internet Archive), which can be read but not the content itself. The list of mechanism names is only a set of facts that can be referenced
- **maintained**: Books in print as of the last editions (Idea Books 2010; Builder's Guide 2nd edition 2016), with no newer editions found. LEGO parts have changed since (e.g. Power Functions and RC are now discontinued). Sariel's website is active, with new mechanism posts in May and July 2026. Isogawa has continued with newer No Starch titles up to 2021 (Non-Electric Models: Simple Machines, MINDSTORMS Robot Inventor Idea Book)
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Pictogram-led navigation: an icon for each motion ('to and fro', 'turns 90 degrees', 'stops and starts') that lets non-readers browse, a good model for flow-chart answer buttons. (2) One idea per photo, shown from several angles with colour-coded parts, so the working principle is obvious without text. (3) Grouping by power source or effect ('using springs', 'using magnets', 'using weights', 'using vibration'). (4) Sariel's split of 'basics -> mechanics -> motors -> advanced mechanics', which suits a learning path. (5) Tables of ready-made combinations (gear-ratio table, compatible transmission parts). (6) A 'form vs function' trade-off discussion that could become a 'what to watch out for' field
- **generator_or_parametric**: No generator in the books. Sariel offers separate web tools and Android apps (Gear Ratio Calculator, LEGO Units Converter, Model Scaler, LEGO Wheels table, LEGO Motors specs chart), linked from sariel.pl, and a July 2026 post says the source code of his tools for adult LEGO fans is now available
- **terminology_value**: low to medium. Sariel uses correct engineering names (differential, planetary gearing, steering and suspension systems, transmissions) and defines technical terms, but mixes in LEGO jargon (studless, liftarm, driving ring). Isogawa is almost wordless. Not an authority for proper names or translations
- **decision_support**: None as selector logic. Isogawa's icon-coded contents let you pick by motion visually; Sariel has comparison tables (gear ratios, motor characteristics, compatible parts) and discusses pros and cons of options (e.g. planetary gearing pros and cons, studless vs studded building), but no flow chart
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: The Isogawa English editions are translations/re-editions of his earlier Japanese 'Red, Blue and Green' books, and his later Power Functions and Non-Electric Models books reuse the same photo-only format. Sariel's 2nd edition updates and extends his 2012 1st edition (30-50% rewritten, per the author). Both overlap with other LEGO mechanism collections (e.g. Technicopedia, linked from sariel.pl) and, for principles, with general mechanism books such as 507 Mechanical Movements

### Other Info

- **sources**: 
  - https://sariel.pl/guide2/ (author's page for the 2nd edition: 25-chapter list, 424 pages, new chapters, October 2016)
  - https://sariel.pl/2012/06/my-book-available-now/ (1st edition: over 350 pages, 500+ figures, 40 building instructions, 20 chapters, reviewer and foreword)
  - https://sariel.pl/books/ (Sariel's books and translation languages; sidebar lists his tools and recent 2026 mechanism posts)
  - https://thelegocarblog.com/2016/11/12/review-the-unofficial-lego-technic-builders-guide-2nd-edition/ (review: style, virtual pivots, transmission rings, planetary gears, 3D printing chapter, price)
  - https://blog.robotmak3rs.com/2010/10/lego-technic-idea-books-review.html (review of the three Isogawa Idea Books: icons, photos, $19.95 each, Japanese originals)
  - https://archive.org/metadata/simplemachines0000isog (Internet Archive record for archive.org/details/simplemachines0000isog; metadata: Simple Machines, x + 157 pages, controlled lending)
  - https://archive.org/metadata/wheeledwonders0000isog (Internet Archive record for archive.org/details/wheeledwonders0000isog; metadata: Wheeled Wonders, ix + 131 pages)
  - https://archive.org/metadata/legotechnicideab00isog (Internet Archive record for archive.org/details/legotechnicideab00isog; metadata and contents of Fantastic Contraptions)
  - https://archive.org/metadata/legopowerfunctio0000isog_f2e0 (Internet Archive record for archive.org/details/legopowerfunctio0000isog_f2e0; metadata: LEGO Power Functions Idea Book vol. 1)
  - https://openlibrary.org/books/OL24902615M.json (Simple Machines table of contents, 22 sections)
  - https://openlibrary.org/books/OL27219054M.json (Builder's Guide 2nd edition record and table of contents)
  - https://openlibrary.org/books/OL32434774M.json (LEGO Technic Non-Electric Models: Simple Machines, 2021, 180 pages)

## 42 IFToMM terminology for mechanism and machine science

*Classification system.* The international standard vocabulary of mechanism terms, with multilingual equivalents

### Basics

- **name**: IFToMM terminology for mechanism and machine science (IFToMM Dictionary / 'Terminology for the Mechanism and Machine Science', by the IFToMM Permanent Commission for the Standardization of Terminology, formerly Commission A)
- **url**: https://iftomm-terminology.antonkb.nl/ (the 'IFToMM dictionaries online', Version 3.1, May 2014; the English dictionary is at https://iftomm-terminology.antonkb.nl/2057/frames.html). Linked from the commission's page https://iftomm-world.org/commissions/permanent-commission-for-the-standardization-of-terminology/
- **resource_type**: classification system (a standard terminology: a numbered, subject-ordered dictionary of terms with definitions in four languages, published in print and as an online dictionary)
- **cost**: Free online. The printed editions in the journal Mechanism and Machine Theory (Elsevier) are paywalled (e.g. the 2003 edition is marked closed access in OpenAlex; ScienceDirect articles are sold individually)
- **language**: Four official IFToMM languages: English, French, German and Russian. The site has monolingual dictionaries and bilingual side-by-side views for every pair (12 pairs). Coverage differs: chapter 14 (Transportation) is in English and Russian only, and chapter 16 (Compliant Mechanisms) is in English only. National translations have been made in Japan, China, Romania, the Czech Republic and Poland (Segla 2014), but they are not on the site
- **year**: The commission was founded in 1969. Printed editions in Mechanism and Machine Theory: 1983 (vol. 18 no. 6, English master version), 1991 (vol. 26 no. 5, four languages, 763 terms), 1997 (vol. 32 no. 6, abbreviations/symbols and graphical symbols for mechanisms), and 2003 (vol. 38 nos. 7-10, 3rd edition, special issue edited by T. Ionescu). The online dictionary started about 2004; its latest version is 3.1 (May 2014). In 2019 a new online editor (THEDI) inside DMG-Lib was introduced for future releases
- **access_barrier**: none for the online dictionary (no login). Academic licence or paywall for the printed journal versions
- **image_license**: Not applicable: the dictionary has no figures, animations or videos (only flag and fold/unfold icons were found in the chapter pages)
- **authority_level**: official standard (international scientific federation). IFToMM is the international federation for mechanism and machine science; its terminology commission of nearly 60 specialists from 25 countries (to 2014) wrote the definitions under agreed rules for defining terms, and the printed editions appeared in IFToMM's own journal

### Coverage

- **covers**: The whole vocabulary of mechanism and machine science, not only mechanism types: structure of machines and mechanisms (components such as crank, rocker, cam, pawl, latch, ratchet, stop; sub-assemblies such as kinematic pairs, couplings and clutches; mechanism types such as step, Geneva, differential and self-locking mechanisms), kinematics, dynamics, machine control and measurement, robotics, general terms, rotor dynamics, vibrations and non-linear oscillations, stability, biomechanics, gearing, mechatronics, transportation machinery and logistics, and compliant mechanisms. The English online dictionary has about 1,795 term entries across 16 chapters (my count of headwords: e.g. chapter 1 Structure 125, chapter 3 Dynamics 352, chapter 12 Gearing 226, chapter 16 Compliant Mechanisms 30). The 2003 printed edition had a core of 771 terms in four languages plus a supplement of 823 English terms
- **organisation**: Classified (systematic) order: numbered chapters and sub-chapters by subject (e.g. 1 Structure > 1.1 Components > 1.1.49 PAWL), with an alphabetical index and a free-text search box. Each term links to the terms used in its definition. For someone who doesn't know the terms this works poorly: you must already know a word to search for it, the chapters are arranged by academic discipline rather than by what you want to do, and there are no pictures
- **scope_match**: medium. It is the authority for the proper names of mechanism types and elements (pawl, latch, ratchet, self-locking mechanism, step mechanism, Geneva mechanism, differential, harmonic drive, living hinge, bistable compliant mechanism), which the encyclopedia needs for its 'engineering term in brackets'. But most of its roughly 1,800 terms are theory (kinematics, dynamics, vibration, control), not connection types, and it gives no pictures, uses or design advice
- **taxonomy_used**: Subject chapters, 16 top-level groups in the online version: 0 General; 1 Structure of Machines and Mechanisms (1.1 Components, 1.2 Sub-assemblies, 1.3 Mechanisms); 2 Kinematics; 3 Dynamics; 4 Machine Controls and Measurement; 5 Robotics; 6 General Terms; 7 Dynamics, Supplement; 8 Rotor Dynamics and Measurement; 9 Vibrations and Non-linear Oscillations; 10 Stability; 11 Biomechanics; 12 Gearing (12.1-12.6, including 12.6 Geared Mechanisms); 13 Mechatronics; 14 Transportation Machinery and Logistics; 16 Compliant Mechanisms (listed as 15 in Segla 2014). Within 'Structure', mechanisms are named by kinematic structure (kinematic pairs: lower/higher, revolute, prismatic, helical, cylindrical, spherical, planar; closure: force-closed vs form-closed) and by function (guidance, function-generating, path-generating, step, dwell, differential, self-locking, adjustable), following the Reuleaux / German VDI tradition
- **example_types**: Pawl [click, detent]; latch; ratchet; stop; self-locking mechanism; step mechanism; pilgrim-step mechanism; dwell mechanism; Geneva mechanism [Geneva drive]; geared Geneva mechanism; differential mechanism; adjustable mechanism; universal [Cardan, Hooke's] joint; clutch; coupling; Scotch-yoke mechanism; wedge mechanism; screw mechanism; epicyclic [planetary] gear train; wave gear train [harmonic drive]; enveloping [hourglass, globoidal] worm; spiroid gear; contrate [face] gear; lantern gear [cage gear]; force-closed and form-closed kinematic pairs; bistable compliant mechanism; living hinge; cross-axis flexural pivot; cartwheel hinge; notch flexure hinge
- **making_methods**: General mechanical engineering and mechanism/machine science (research and teaching). Nothing specific to 3D printing, CNC, woodwork or hobby making
- **item_count**: About 1,795 English term entries online (headwords counted across the 16 chapter pages, September 2026); German about 1,614, French about 1,600, Russian about 1,766 (chapters 14 and 16 missing in some languages). Printed 2003 edition: 771 core terms in four languages plus 823 English supplement terms (1,594). 1991 edition: 763 terms
- **special_behaviours_covered**: torque-limiting: no (only generic 'clutch' and 'coupling'); self-locking: yes, 'self-locking mechanism' is defined (cannot transmit motion and power from output back to input); hand-release only: no; one-way/ratchet: yes, 'pawl [click, detent]' (prevents motion in one direction) and 'ratchet'; indexing/detent: yes, 'step mechanism' (one-way output with periodic dwells), 'Geneva mechanism', 'dwell mechanism' and 'detent' as a synonym of pawl; over-centre: only indirectly ('dead point', 'limit position of a mechanism'), no toggle entry; compliant: yes, a whole chapter (30 terms: compliant joint, flexure hinge, living hinge, bistable and monolithic compliant mechanisms, pseudo-rigid-body model); print-in-place: no; gear trains/gearboxes: yes, strongly (226 gearing terms, including epicyclic, differential and harmonic-drive gear trains); powered connectors: only 'actuator', 'intelligent actuator' and 'compliant actuator', no electromagnets or solenoid latches; adhesive types: no
- **entry_structure**: Number (e.g. 1.1.49), term in capitals with synonyms in square brackets (e.g. 'PAWL [CLICK, DETENT]', 'UNIVERSAL [CARDAN, HOOKE'S] JOINT'), and a one-sentence definition with hyperlinks to other defined terms. An 'unfold' button shows the definitions of the linked terms inline. Bilingual views put the entry in two languages side by side under the same number. No figures, examples of use, degrees of freedom tables, design rules or CAD
- **audience_level**: research / professional / undergraduate (mechanism and machine science specialists, lecturers and translators)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low. Definitions are short and exact but written in the field's own jargon (e.g. 'link', 'kinematic pair', 'frame'), there are no pictures, and you can only find a term if you already know its name. Its value to beginners is indirect: it gives the proper name and aliases to put in brackets
- **has_animations**: no
- **gaps**: No pictures, examples or 'what is it used for'; no plain-English or beginner names (many everyday names such as 'toggle clamp', 'freewheel', 'snap fit', 'detent ball', 'quick-release' are missing); nothing on fasteners, adhesives, powered latches, torque limiters or hand-release locks; no route from a need to a term (index and search only); French and German lack the two newest chapters; the online version has not been updated since 2014 and some links on the site are broken
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Headword plus synonyms in brackets ('PAWL [CLICK, DETENT]'), which is exactly the project's 'plain term (engineering term)' pattern in reverse, so aliases should be a field. (2) Stable hierarchical IDs (1.1.49) that survive reordering. (3) Side-by-side language view for translations. (4) Inline 'unfold' of the terms used inside a definition, a handy way to explain jargon without leaving the page. (5) The commission's rules for writing definitions (Segla 2014): no circular definitions, no negative definitions, simplest possible language, don't invent terms just to pair with others, and 'when in doubt, leave it out'. (6) Separating structure (components, sub-assemblies, mechanisms) from function (guidance, step, dwell, self-locking, adjustable) as two classification axes
- **design_rules_given**: No. It is a vocabulary; it defines quantities (backlash, pressure angle, module, contact ratio) but gives no numbers for design
- **generator_or_parametric**: No
- **file_formats**: HTML web pages (frame-based, one page per chapter per language or language pair). The underlying work was compiled from Word files via XML into compiled HTML help (CHM-style) files, per Segla 2014. Printed/PDF journal articles from Elsevier
- **terminology_value**: high, the top authority for this field: official definitions and standard names for mechanism and machine science terms, with synonyms and equivalents in English, French, German and Russian. Best source for the bracketed engineering term and its translations in those four languages; weaker for everyday maker names and for other languages
- **decision_support**: None: no selector, flow chart, filters or comparison tables. Only a search box, an alphabetical index and a subject table of contents
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Builds on earlier national terminologies such as Levitskii et al.'s Russian 'Teoriya mekhanizmov: Terminologiya' (1964) and the German VDI guideline 2127 on mechanism terms (as cited in Ionescu, Bogelsack and Leinonen 2004). It feeds and overlaps the DMG-Lib multilingual thesaurus (the IFToMM commission page links to DMG-Lib, and the THEDI editor for the dictionary is part of DMG-Lib). Cite the IFToMM dictionary or the 2003 Mechanism and Machine Theory edition for definitions rather than DMG-Lib

### Other Info

- **sources**: 
  - https://iftomm-world.org/commissions/permanent-commission-for-the-standardization-of-terminology/ (commission mission, members, links to the IFToMM dictionary and DMG-Lib)
  - https://iftomm-terminology.antonkb.nl/ (IFToMM dictionaries online, Version 3.1 May 2014, language choice)
  - https://iftomm-terminology.antonkb.nl/2057/frames.html and https://iftomm-terminology.antonkb.nl/2057/toc.html (English dictionary and table of contents)

## 43 Functional Basis and the Oregon State Design Repository

*Classification system.* Function-based taxonomy of what components do (couple, guide, transmit, lock) and a repository of products classified with it

### Basics

- **name**: Functional Basis (Stone and Wood 2000; reconciled with the NIST function taxonomy by Hirtz, Stone, McAdams, Szykman and Wood 2002) plus the related Component Basis (Kurtoglu et al. 2005), and the Oregon State Design Repository (OSDR, originally the UMR Design Repository) of products described with them
- **url**: Reconciled Functional Basis, NIST Technical Note 1447 (free PDF): https://tsapps.nist.gov/publication/get_pdf.cfm?pub_id=821676 ; NIST record: https://www.nist.gov/publications/functional-basis-engineering-design-reconciling-and-evolving-previous-efforts ; Component Basis (ICED 05 paper, free PDF): https://www.designsociety.org/download-publication/22867/deriving_a_component_basis_for_computational_functional_synthesis ; Design Repository project page: https://design.engr.oregonstate.edu/repo ; its 'alternate link' http://ftest.mime.oregonstate.edu/repo/browse/ did not resolve when tried on 2026-09-25 and has no Wayback copy (see maintained) ; AutoFunc data-mining tool built on the repository: https://github.com/AlexMikes/AutoFunc
- **resource_type**: classification system (a controlled vocabulary of functions and flows, plus a component-name taxonomy) and a digital library / research database of products described with it
- **cost**: Free: the NIST technical note, the ICED 05 component-basis paper and the repository web interface (when online) cost nothing. The journal versions (Research in Engineering Design 13, pp. 65-82, 2002; Journal of Mechanical Design 122(4), p. 359, 2000) are paywalled at the publishers
- **language**: English
- **year**: Functional basis first proposed by Little et al. 1997 and published by Stone and Wood (1999/2000, J. Mech. Des. 122(4)); reconciled version NIST TN 1447 dated February 2002 and in Research in Engineering Design 13 (2002); Component Basis 2005 (ICED 05, Melbourne). Repository: NSF grant DMI-9988817 (around 2000) at the University of Missouri-Rolla, schema paper Bohm et al. 2008 (Computer-Aided Design 40(7)), moved to Oregon State and still used in papers up to at least 2020 (IDETC 2020)
- **access_barrier**: none for the vocabulary (free PDF); the repository web interface is currently unreachable (host does not resolve), so in practice the product data cannot be browsed today
- **image_license**: Figures in the NIST note and the ICED paper are part of the publications (no separate licence); there are no images of mechanisms as such, only function diagrams and screenshots
- **authority_level**: academic peer-reviewed (NIST, University of Missouri-Rolla, University of Texas at Austin, Oregon State University Design Engineering Lab); the functional basis is the most widely cited function vocabulary in US engineering-design research

### Coverage

- **covers**: Not a catalogue of connection types but a vocabulary of what parts DO. The reconciled function set has 8 classes (Branch, Channel, Connect, Control Magnitude, Convert, Provision, Signal, Support) with 53 function terms in total, each with a definition and an everyday example (e.g. 'A ratchet joins a socket on its square shaft interface', 'A turnbuckle links two ends of a steering cable', 'A four bar linkage allows a rotational DOF for the trunk lid', 'The gear train of a power screwdriver decrements the flow of rotational energy', 'On a bicycling glove, a Velcro strap secures the human hand'). The flow set has 3 classes (Material, Signal, Energy) with 42 terms. The Component Basis adds 92 standard component names with synonyms and definitions (e.g. Latch = release catch, pawl, lock; Hinge; Key; Magnet incl. electromagnet; Spring; Gear; Cam; Rotational Coupler; Stop = snubber, travel limiter). The repository holds products taken apart and described by component, function, flow and assembly connection: a 2020 study drew 234 products with 7,245 components from it, another used 139 consumer products
- **organisation**: Hierarchical by function: class (primary), secondary and tertiary terms, plus a 'correspondents' column that maps everyday synonyms onto basis terms (e.g. fasten and assemble map to Join; attach maps to Link; unlock and unfasten map to Allow DOF; hold and fix map to Secure; align and orient map to Position). This synonym table is quite friendly for someone who knows what they want a part to do but not its name. The repository is organised per product (bill of materials of dissected artifacts) with browse and search modes; each component carries a common name, component-basis name, material, functions and connections to other components
- **scope_match**: medium: it gives a strong, well-defined way to describe connections by purpose (couple, join, link, guide, allow DOF, secure, stop, position, transmit, change/decrement) which suits the flow chart, but it names functions rather than clever connection or mechanism types, and its product data are mostly everyday consumer products, not a list of unusual mechanisms
- **taxonomy_used**: Functional basis: verb-noun pairs (function + flow) in a 3-level hierarchy. 8 function classes: Branch (separate, divide, extract, remove, distribute), Channel (import, export, transfer, transport, transmit, guide, translate, rotate, allow DOF), Connect (couple, join, link, mix), Control Magnitude (actuate, regulate, increase, decrease, change, increment, decrement, shape, condition, stop, prevent, inhibit), Convert, Provision (store, contain, collect, supply), Signal (sense, detect, measure, indicate, track, display, process), Support (stabilize, secure, position). 3 flow classes: Material (human, gas, liquid, solid, plasma, mixture), Signal (status, control) and Energy (13 secondary types such as mechanical, electrical, magnetic, hydraulic, pneumatic, human). Component Basis: 92 flat component names. Built on Pahl and Beitz function structures and Value Analysis
- **example_types**: Component Basis names that fit the scope: latch (release catch, pawl, lock); hinge (pivot, pin, dowel); key (half-moon key, cotter key, shear key); rotational coupler (compression coupling, clamping coupling); stop (snubber, travel limiter, bumper); spring (cantilever, coil, leaf, torsion); cam (eccentric, cam plate, camshaft); gear (rack, pinion, ring, sun, planet); pulley and belt; sprocket (chain drive); magnet (incl. electromagnet); screw (lead screw, power screw); link (connecting rod, pawl); lever; guide (v-guide, track, locating hole, jig pin); inclined plane (wedge); rivet (pop rivet); insert (grommet, eyelet, bushing); seal (gasket, o-ring); switch (toggle, flip-flop). Function terms useful as flow-chart labels: couple, join, link, allow DOF, guide, translate, rotate, secure, stabilize, position, stop/prevent/inhibit, transmit, increment/decrement
- **making_methods**: General product design of electromechanical consumer products (reverse engineering and product teardown); not aimed at 3D printing or any specific making process
- **special_behaviours_covered**: Only indirectly. Self-locking, hand-release and one-way behaviours appear only through component names (latch/pawl/lock, stop/travel limiter) and function words (secure, prevent, inhibit, allow DOF); there are no terms for torque-limiting, over-centre, indexing/detent, compliant or print-in-place behaviour. Gear trains: yes as a component (gear with sun/planet/ring synonyms) and as the function 'change/decrement mechanical energy'. Powered connectors: magnet (incl. electromagnet), electric motor, switch and solenoid-like 'actuate' functions, and the flow 'magnetic energy' with the example of a magnetic lock. Adhesives: not covered as types (bonding is only implicit in 'couple'/'join')
- **audience_level**: research and undergraduate (engineering design courses and design-theory researchers)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium for the vocabulary: short plain definitions with household examples (pencil eraser sleeve, turnbuckle, trunk lid linkage, Velcro glove strap), and the correspondents list maps everyday words to the proper term; low for the repository and the research papers, which assume design-theory jargon (flows, function structures, data schema)
- **has_animations**: no
- **gaps**: Says what a part does, not how to build it: no geometry, drawings, design rules, CAD or making advice; no names for clever mechanism types (no ratchet, freewheel, cam cleat, over-centre latch, bayonet or snap fit as distinct entries); nothing on 3D printing, compliant or print-in-place parts; the repository is offline, and its data were never released under an open licence
- **good_for_entry_links**: poor: the vocabulary is one PDF with no per-term pages; the repository had per-product pages but the host no longer resolves. Best cited as a whole (e.g. the NIST PDF)
- **maintained**: Vocabulary: static since 2002 (NIST record updated 2021, still downloadable). Repository: the Oregon State Design Engineering Lab page (copyright 2026) still describes it and gives the alternate link http://ftest.mime.oregonstate.edu/repo/browse/, but that host did not resolve on 2026-09-25 and the Wayback Machine had no snapshot; the older UMR address http://function.basiceng.umr.edu/repository/ is given in the 2005 paper (not opened). Last research use found: IDETC 2020 papers by Oregon State
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Use a short, fixed list of plain verbs for the first flow-chart question 'what should the connection do?': join/couple, link through a middle part, guide or allow a motion (slide, turn), hold in place (secure, position), stop or limit, pass on force or turning (transmit), change speed or force (increment/decrement). (2) Copy the 'correspondents' idea: a synonym table that maps the words beginners type (fasten, attach, clip, lock, unlock) onto our category names. (3) Give every term a one-line definition plus an everyday example product. (4) Keep the two-part description function + what flows through it (force, turning, fluid, signal, electricity). (5) The component basis shows a way to give every entry one standard name plus a synonyms list. (6) Describing each part by what it touches (assembly connections) is a model for a 'connects X to Y' field
- **design_rules_given**: no numbers; it is a vocabulary and data schema only
- **generator_or_parametric**: No geometry generator. Research tools on the repository: a prototype concept generator (function-to-component matrices), and AutoFunc (Python, MIT) which predicts the functions of components from repository data
- **file_formats**: PDF (vocabulary papers); repository stored in a database with XML export viewed as web pages (2005 paper); CSV component-function-flow data used by AutoFunc
- **terminology_value**: high for function words and standard component names with synonyms (e.g. latch = release catch, pawl, lock; stop = snubber, travel limiter; rotational coupler = union, compression coupling, clamping coupling); low for names of specific clever mechanisms
- **decision_support**: Indirect: the function vocabulary supports function-based concept generation (the repository's prototype concept generator maps required functions to components that have performed them before) and later data-mining tools (decision trees, association rules) that suggest components or functions. No beginner-facing selector, filters or comparison tables
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Builds on Pahl and Beitz function structures, Value Analysis and the NIST Design Repository function/flow taxonomy (Szykman et al. 1999); the Component Basis adopts elements from Greer et al.'s list of 114 components. Overlaps in spirit with Roth's German design catalogues (function-first classification) and with TRIZ/TIPS function lists. Descendants: Repository of Innovative Products (Oman et al. 2014), biologically inspired design keyword maps, AutoFunc

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - entry_structure
  - has_images
  - item_count
  - license
  - reusable_data

## 44 Plastics makers' snap-fit design guides (Covestro/Bayer, BASF, DuPont)

*PDF design guide.* Manufacturer manuals on cantilever, torsional and annular snap fits, with design equations

### Basics

- **name**: Plastics makers' snap-fit design guides: Covestro (formerly Bayer) 'Snap-Fit Joints for Plastics - A Design Guide'; BASF 'Snap-Fit Design Manual' (Technical Expertise series); DuPont 'General Design Principles for DuPont Engineering Polymers - Design Guide Module I' (chapter 10 on snap-fits and press fits)
- **url**: Covestro guide (26 pages, official PDF): https://solutions.covestro.com/-/media/covestro/solution-center/brands/downloads/imported/1557218421.pdf (identical copies at https://solutions.covestro.com/-/media/covestro/solution-center/brands/downloads/imported/1557216102.pdf and https://www.covestro.com/-/media/covestro/solution-center/brands/downloads/imported/1556891135.pdf?rev=a81fb62f7f9948afb36e7602d237e912) ; Covestro companion 'Engineering Polymers Joining Techniques Design Guide': https://solutions.covestro.com/-/media/covestro/solution-center/brands/downloads/imported/1556890495.pdf?rev=6035fb2043ef4fd8b536fdb54abf6939 ; BASF manual: no BASF-hosted copy found; full copy filed as a public exhibit at the US Patent Trial and Appeal Board: https://ptacts.uspto.gov/ptacts/public-informations/petitions/1529702/download-documents?artifactId=qS3ulmCY5CWcGMJ3BonZ4Z1aucAGL4RFnzzCy4zxr4ZvIGobt8dq-u4 ; BASF online Snapfit Calculator (registration): https://plastics-rubber.basf.com/northamerica/en/performance_polymers/services/service_calculation_programmes ; DuPont Module I (136-page PDF hosted by distributor Distrupol): https://www.distrupol.com/General_Design_Principles_for_Engineering_Polymers.pdf
- **resource_type**: PDF design guide (manufacturer design manuals)
- **cost**: Free (PDF downloads); BASF's Snapfit Calculator is free with registration on its TechXcellence portal
- **license**: All rights reserved: '(c)2015 Covestro LLC. All rights reserved' (COV-033 10/2015); '(c) 2007 BASF Corporation'; 'Copyright (c) 2000 E.I. du Pont de Nemours and Company. All rights reserved'. Link to the PDFs and cite the equations and numbers; do not copy figures
- **access_barrier**: none for Covestro and DuPont (direct PDFs); BASF manual not on BASF's own site any more (only on third-party and USPTO copies); BASF calculator needs a free login
- **image_license**: All rights reserved (manufacturer photos, line drawings and graphs)
- **authority_level**: manufacturer (material suppliers' application engineering departments); the BASF short-beam correction cites its own SPE ANTEC 1987 paper and was checked by FEA and part tests; these guides are the standard references cited by Wikipedia, textbooks and 3D-printing snap-fit studies

### Coverage

- **covers**: Snap-fit joints (clip joints) in thermoplastics. Covestro: general principles, types (cantilever, U-shaped, torsion, annular), cantilever design with 4 beam-shape designs and 4 cross-sections, permissible undercut, deflection and mating forces, torsion snap joints, annular snap joints, the case where both mating parts flex, and worked examples (e.g. a traffic light held together only by snaps, incl. a bayonet-style sun visor and a push-to-release hook). BASF: why use snaps, types (cantilever, U- and L-shaped cantilevers, cylindrical/annular mentioned), classical beam theory, an improved method for short beams using a 'deflection magnification factor' Q, formulas for L- and U-shaped snaps, and failure causes (stress concentration, creep/stress relaxation, fatigue). DuPont Module I: cylindrical (annular) undercut snap-fits and cantilever lug snap-fits inside a wider guide that also covers press fits, self-tapping screws, springs, plastic bearings, plastic gears (spur, helical, worm, bevel), combined-function gears (ratchet plus gear, torque-limiting gear, slip coupling, backlash-free flexible gear), spin, ultrasonic, vibration and hot-plate welding, riveting and design for disassembly. About 4 to 6 snap-fit types in total
- **organisation**: By snap-fit type (by how the hook bends: bending, twisting, hoop stretching), then by calculation step (allowed strain, deflection, force to push together, force to pull apart), with worked examples. DuPont is organised by design topic and assembly technique. Clear once you know the words 'undercut', 'strain' and 'secant modulus'; a beginner can follow the pictures and the worked examples but not the equations without help
- **scope_match**: high for the snap-fit family, which is a top priority for 3D printing (cantilever, U/L-shaped, torsion and annular snaps, separable vs permanent, push-to-release), and DuPont adds in-scope plastic mechanisms (ratchet gears, torque-limiting gears, slip couplings, integral springs); low for breadth, as they cover only plastics joining and are written for injection moulding
- **taxonomy_used**: By loading mode of the flexing feature (Covestro's 4 basic shapes): cantilever snap joints (bending), U-shaped snap joints (a cantilever variant), torsion snap joints (shear in a torsion bar), annular snap joints (rotationally symmetric, multiaxial hoop strain). BASF: cantilever, 'U' shaped cantilever, 'L' shaped cantilever, plus cylindrical. DuPont: 2 categories, cylindrical undercut snap-fits and cantilever lug snap-fits. All also split joints by the return angle into separable (removable) and inseparable (permanent)
- **example_types**: cantilever snap-fit hook (tapered-thickness and tapered-width versions); U-shaped snap arm; L-shaped snap (slot in base wall); torsion snap joint / snap-fitting rocker arm on a torsion bar (press one end to release); continuous annular snap joint (lamp cover, semi-hermetic); discontinuous (slotted) annular snap joint; cylindrical snap-fit of a steel shaft into a plastic hub; positive-locking segmented undercut with 90-degree return; push-to-release snap (press tongues to free the arms); rigid lug plus snap hook on the opposite side; bayonet-style sun-visor catch; snap-fit ball bearing retention; snap-together split worm gear; molded-in spring on a ratchet wheel (DuPont); torque-limiting gear with pivoting springs (DuPont); gear with sliding (slip) coupling on a split hub (DuPont); backlash-free flexible (ovalised) gear that slips at end stops (DuPont); press fit / interference fit
- **making_methods**: Injection-moulded thermoplastics (PC, PC/ABS, PBT, nylon, acetal, PET, PEI, ABS and glass-filled grades); mould-tooling notes (slides, slots to avoid side cores). The equations transfer to 3D-printed and machined plastic parts but the guides say nothing about printing
- **item_count**: Covestro snap-fit guide: 26 pages, sections A to F, 29 figures, 3 tables, 3 worked examples. BASF: 24 pages, 6 parts, about 5 worked examples. DuPont Module I: 136 pages (snap-fits about 4 pages, pp. 72-76, plus about 2 pages of combined-function gear examples, 6 figures)
- **entry_structure**: Per snap-fit type: description with application photos, a sketch with symbols, calculation equations (deflection, strain, deflection force, mating and separation force), material data tables (allowable strain, friction coefficient, secant modulus curves), worked numerical examples and design hints
- **audience_level**: professional (plastic part designers and engineers); usable by undergraduates and advanced hobbyists

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: short plain-English introductions and many real-product photos (fuse box, wheel cover, lamp housing, traffic light), but the core is equations with engineering symbols (strain, secant modulus, section modulus), and imperial/metric mixed units
- **has_animations**: no
- **gaps**: No guidance for 3D printing (layer direction, lower printed strain limits, PLA/PETG values, clearances); materials limited to each maker's own resins; no snap-fit families such as ball-and-socket clips, living-hinge latches or print-in-place clips; torsion and annular sections are short; no interactive calculator except BASF's behind login; equations need unit care; no selector comparing snap-fit with other joints except DuPont's short table
- **good_for_entry_links**: fair: each guide is a stable-looking single PDF (e.g. https://solutions.covestro.com/-/media/covestro/solution-center/brands/downloads/imported/1557218421.pdf) that can be linked from snap-fit entries with a page number; no per-type web pages; company media URLs have changed before (Bayer to Covestro) and BASF's own copy has vanished, so add a note of title and edition
- **reusable_data**: not reusable as data (all rights reserved PDFs); the equations, allowable-strain values and friction coefficients are facts that can be restated in our own words with a citation
- **maintained**: Static documents: Covestro PDF is the 10/2015 edition, still online in 2026; BASF manual (2007) is no longer found on BASF's site, but BASF offers an online Snapfit Calculator; DuPont Module I (2000) survives on distributor sites, not on an official DuPont or Celanese page that we opened
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Classify snap-fits by what flexes: bending hook (cantilever), longer bending path (U or L shape), twisting bar (torsion), stretching ring (annular); a clear first split for the snap-fit family. (2) Explain 'removable or permanent' by the back angle of the hook (return angle), which maps directly onto our removability filter. (3) Show one fully worked example per type, in plain numbers. (4) A 'both parts flex' note and 'use 60% of the strain for joints opened often' rule are good one-line tips. (5) A small comparison table of assembly methods (screw, snap-fit, press-fit, welding, bonding, overmolding) against material mix, recyclability and disassembly (DuPont Table 11.01) is a model for our flow-chart comparison pages. (6) Real product photos (battery lid, fuse box, wheel cover) help beginners recognise each type
- **design_rules_given**: Yes, many numbers: Covestro allowable short-term strain at 23 C (Makrolon PC 4%, Bayblend PC/ABS 2.5%, Makroblend 3.5%, Apec 4%, 10% glass PC 2.2%, 20% glass PC 2.0%); use about 60% of these for frequent separation; partly crystalline plastics up to near yield, amorphous up to about 70% of yield strain, glass-filled about half the elongation at break; tapering thickness to half at the tip allows over 60% more deflection; root radius not less than 0.015 in (about 0.4 mm), R/h about 0.6 limit; friction coefficients for 13 plastics on steel; mating force W = P(mu + tan a)/(1 - mu tan a); annular joint remote from the tube end needs about 3 times the force. BASF: fillet radius at least 0.5 t; beam formulas valid above L/t about 10, correction factor Q below that; allowable strains (e.g. PC 4-9.2%, acetal 7%, nylon 6 8% dry, PBT 8.8%, ABS 6-7%, 30% glass grades about 2%); friction coefficients for 8 plastics; 90-degree return angle to resist relaxation. DuPont: allowable strains for lug snap-fits used once vs frequently (e.g. Delrin 100: 8% / 2-4%; Zytel 101 dry 4% / 2%; glass-filled Zytel 0.8-1.2% / 0.5-0.7%; Hytrel 20% / 10%); cylindrical snap-fit undercut table for shaft diameters 2-35 mm; 45-degree return angle for removable, 90 degrees for permanent, 30-degree lead-in
- **generator_or_parametric**: No generator in the PDFs; BASF offers an online Snapfit Calculator (registration required, not tested)
- **file_formats**: PDF
- **terminology_value**: high for snap-fit terms and their aliases: snap joint / snap-fit, cantilever snap (hook, lug, finger), U-shaped and L-shaped snaps, torsion snap joint (rocker arm), annular or cylindrical snap joint (bead and groove), undercut, overhang, lead (entrance) angle, return (retraction) angle, mating force, separation / pull-off force, permissible strain, secant modulus, deflection magnification factor
- **decision_support**: Limited: rules of thumb for choosing a type (U or L shapes when a straight cantilever would be over-strained; annular when high-elongation unfilled plastic is used; cantilever lugs for non-round parts); separable vs inseparable guidance by return angle; DuPont's comparison table of assembly techniques (screw, snap-fit, press-fit, welding, bonding, overmolding) by material combination, recyclability and disassembly. No flow chart

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - has_images
  - language
  - special_behaviours_covered
  - year

## 45 3D printing service design guides (Hubs, Protolabs, Xometry, Formlabs, Prusa)

*PDF design guide.* Web guides on snap fits, living hinges, print-in-place joints, threads and inserts for 3D printing

### Basics

- **name**: 3D printing service design guides (Hubs / Protolabs Network, Protolabs, Xometry, Formlabs, Prusa)
- **resource_type**: PDF design guide (in practice a set of free web design-guide articles and blog posts from 3D printing services and printer makers; a few downloadable white papers)
- **language**: English (Hubs also in German, French and Dutch; Prusa Knowledge Base has several translations, e.g. Japanese; Protolabs has regional sites)
- **image_license**: Same as text: all rights reserved company photos and diagrams (Hubs uses its own drawings of cantilever and annular snap fits and hinge cross-sections; Protolabs has L = pi R hinge diagrams; Formlabs has photos and CAD screenshots)
- **authority_level**: manufacturer (3D-printing services and printer makers writing design-for-manufacture guidance for their customers; not peer-reviewed, and partly marketing)

### Coverage

- **covers**: Joining and moving features for 3D-printed parts, spread across roughly 10-15 relevant articles: snap fits (Hubs: cantilever and annular; Formlabs: cantilever, U-shaped, torsion and annular), living hinges (Hubs design rules for FDM, SLS and material jetting; Protolabs MJF/SLS hinge-length formula), interlocking joints (Formlabs: puzzle/jigsaw/tongue, dovetail, key, finger/comb, tenon, scarf joints and print-in-place chain links; Hubs: finger, dovetail and puzzle joints), print-in-place parts (Formlabs chain mail and print-in-place vise), threaded connections (Hubs: heat-set and tap-in inserts, embedded (captive) nuts, self-tapping screws, cut threads, printed threads; Formlabs: screw-to-expand inserts, heat-set inserts, captive nuts, tapping, thread-forming screws, printed threads; Protolabs: heat-stake and press-fit inserts, tapping, chasing), fits and tolerances (clearance, transition, interference), and adhesives (Prusa: cyanoacrylate, solvent cement, two-part epoxy, plus brief notes on PVA, contact cement and methacrylate, with a tensile test). Prusa adds embedding nuts and magnets with a mid-print pause
- **organisation**: Each company has a blog or knowledge base of standalone articles, grouped loosely by process (FDM, SLA, SLS, MJF) or by topic ('Design for 3D printing', 'Design tips'). Each article is organised as: what it is, types, which process and material, tolerances, best practices, FAQ. This works well for a beginner who already knows the name ('snap fit', 'living hinge'), but there is no cross-company index and no way to start from a need such as 'I want a lid that clicks shut'
- **scope_match**: high for the 3D-printing priority: they are the main practical source for snap fits, living hinges, print-in-place and interlocking joints, heat-set inserts and captive nuts, with real clearance numbers. Low for everything else (no gear trains, no powered connectors, few clever mechanisms)
- **taxonomy_used**: No formal taxonomy. Snap fits are split by shape of the flexing part (cantilever, U-shaped, torsion, annular) and by permanent vs releasable; threaded joining is split by method (insert, embedded nut, self-tapping screw, tapped thread, printed thread); interlocking joints by shape (puzzle, dovetail, key, finger, tenon, scarf, chain link); adhesives by chemistry (cyanoacrylate, solvent, two-part epoxy, other). About 4-6 families of joining method in total
- **example_types**: cantilever snap fit; U-shaped snap fit; torsion snap fit; annular snap fit (bottle-cap / pen-cap style); living hinge (FDM single-strand, SLS/MJF nylon, multi-material TPU hinge); print-in-place chain links (chain mail); print-in-place vise; puzzle / jigsaw joint; dovetail joint; key joint; finger (comb) joint; tenon joint; scarf joint; locating lugs; heat-set threaded insert; tap-in (press-fit) insert; screw-to-expand insert; captive (embedded) hex nut in a nut pocket; thread-forming (self-tapping) screw for plastics; printed semicircular-profile thread; magnets embedded with a mid-print pause; cyanoacrylate (superglue), solvent cement (acetone for ABS/ASA), two-part epoxy
- **making_methods**: 3D printing: FDM/FFF (Prusa, Hubs), SLA resin (Formlabs), SLS, MJF and material jetting (Hubs, Protolabs, Formlabs); some links to injection-moulding practice
- **item_count**: About 10-15 relevant articles across the five companies (Hubs design-for-3D-printing index lists 49 knowledge-base links, of which about 5 are about joining; Formlabs about 3-4 relevant posts; Protolabs about 3-4; Prusa 2-3). Roughly 20-25 distinct joint or fastening types in total
- **special_behaviours_covered**: Compliant: yes (snap fits and living hinges, with strain and thickness guidance). Print-in-place: partly (Formlabs chain links and print-in-place vise; Prusa suggests at least 0.3 mm gap for moving parts). Hand-release only: partly (releasable vs permanent snap fits set by undercut shape). Adhesive types: yes in the Prusa guide (cyanoacrylate incl. gel, rubber-filled, odourless and thin grades; solvent cements matched to plastics: acetone for ABS/ASA, limonene for HIPS, isopropyl alcohol for PVB, PETG inert; two-part epoxy; PVA and contact cement mentioned). Torque-limiting, self-locking, one-way/ratchet, indexing/detent, over-centre, gear trains, powered connectors: not covered in the articles opened
- **entry_structure**: Article with a definition, photos and simple diagrams, a table of process suitability, numeric design rules (thickness, clearance, fillet ratio), best-practice bullets, FAQ, and a call to get a quote; Formlabs adds step-by-step CAD tutorials and a video; Hubs embeds a YouTube tutorial
- **audience_level**: hobbyist to professional (written for engineers ordering parts, but plain enough for hobbyists; Prusa is aimed at home FDM users)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: high: short plain-language explanations, photos of real printed parts, clear rules of thumb (for example 0.5 mm clearance for FDM), little maths except the Protolabs hinge formula and the Formlabs strain discussion
- **has_images**: yes: good-quality photos of printed parts and clean diagrams (snap-fit types, hinge cross-sections with dimensions, interlocking joint shapes, insert installation), all company-owned
- **has_animations**: some video: Hubs embeds a YouTube snap-fit tutorial and has a 'Watch and learn' video section; Formlabs threads article includes an application video; no interactive models
- **good_for_entry_links**: yes: stable, descriptive per-topic URLs, e.g. https://www.hubs.com/knowledge-base/how-design-snap-fit-joints-3d-printing/ for snap fits, https://www.hubs.com/knowledge-base/how-design-living-hinges-3d-printing/ for living hinges, https://formlabs.com/blog/how-to-3d-print-interlocking-joints/ for dovetail and puzzle joints, https://www.hubs.com/knowledge-base/how-assemble-3d-printed-parts-threaded-fasteners/ for heat-set inserts and captive nuts, https://blog.prusa3d.com/the-great-guide-to-gluing-and-assembling-3d-prints_44908/ for glue types. Hubs changed brand to Protolabs Network but kept hubs.com URLs
- **reusable_data**: not reusable beyond linking and citing facts: all rights reserved, and the Hubs Terms of Use forbid copying or building a competing site from its content. Scrape-only HTML (Hubs has an XML sitemap at https://www.hubs.com/sitemap.xml)
- **maintained**: yes: all sites are live and commercial; Protolabs threading article modified Sep 2026; Hubs sitemap updated Sep 2026; Prusa gluing guide last modified 2021 but still online; Hubs pages carry the 2026 copyright
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) One clear number per process (clearance 0.5 mm FDM, 0.3 mm SLS/SLA/MJF) shown as a small table on each entry. (2) Hubs' 'best practices' mini-illustrations: taper the beam, fillet the root, widen the clip, only deflect during assembly, avoid Z-direction flexing, add lugs. (3) Protolabs' hinge-length rule L = (bend angle / 180) x pi x R, with a picture of too-short vs too-long hinges. (4) Formlabs' 'which joint type' gallery with a one-line verdict per type ('good for 3D printing' / 'not ideal'). (5) Prusa's glue guide structure: rule of thumb, which glue for which plastic, warnings, then a simple strength test table; also the 'reversible?' question (debonder for CA). (6) Comparing threaded options in one table (insert vs captive nut vs self-tapping vs printed thread) by strength and reusability
- **design_rules_given**: Yes, many numbers: Hubs snap fits: clearance 0.5 mm FDM, 0.3 mm SLS/SLA/MJF/material jetting; cantilever base at least 1 mm thick with at least 1 mm overhang (MJF); root fillet at least 0.5 x base thickness; width at least 5 mm. Hubs living hinges: FDM 0.4-0.6 mm thick (at least two layers), SLS 0.3-0.8 mm thick and at least 5 mm long (30-50 cycles), material jetting 0.4-0.8 mm; anneal in boiling water. Protolabs hinges: thickness equal to minimum feature size (0.02 in MJF, 0.03 in SLS), L = pi R; split one wide hinge into several narrow ones. Hubs interlocking joints: 0.5 mm FDM, 0.2 mm SLA/SLS, 0.1 mm material jetting. Formlabs: 0.2 mm gap for small parts, 0.4 mm for chunky ones; printed threads M6 or 1/4-20 and larger, semicircular thread profile with 0.1 mm offset for small threads. Hubs threads: wall around a thread at least the screw diameter (M5 needs 5 mm); avoid FDM printed threads below M5; captive nut offset 0.2 mm total; pilot hole for 75-80% thread engagement. Protolabs: 0.25 in (6.35 mm) clearance around heat-stake inserts; screw-to-expand inserts with epoxy for SLA. Prusa: printer accurate to 0.2 mm; at least 0.3 mm for moving parts; CA keeps about 50% strength at 80 C
- **generator_or_parametric**: No generators; Formlabs links a downloadable STL enclosure on Pinshape and CAD step-by-steps, nothing parametric
- **file_formats**: HTML articles with images, embedded YouTube video; downloadable PDF white papers (Formlabs, Hubs reports) and occasional STL example files
- **terminology_value**: medium: good for maker-level names and aliases (snap fit = connector = clip; hook = bead = head; undercut; lugs; heat-set vs tap-in vs screw-to-expand inserts; thread-forming = self-tapping; captive nut = nut boss; capillary gluing; CA frosting (blooming), debonder), but not a formal standard vocabulary and no translations beyond Hubs' four languages
- **decision_support**: Process-suitability tables (FDM vs SLA vs SLS vs material jetting for snap fits and interlocking joints, star ratings in Formlabs), the Hubs threaded-fastener method table with one-line pros and cons, the Prusa glue-to-plastic compatibility notes and tensile test table. No flow chart or interactive selector

## 46 Gear type references (KHK gear technical reference, SDP/SI Elements of Metric Gear Technology)

*PDF design guide.* Gear makers' guides that classify gear types and gear trains

### Basics

- **name**: Gear type references: KHK (Kohara Gear Industry) Gear Technical Reference and Gear Knowledge pages, and SDP/SI 'Elements of Metric Gear Technology'
- **resource_type**: PDF design guide (manufacturers' gear engineering handbooks, published as web pages and PDFs)
- **cost**: Free
- **license**: All rights reserved. KHK pages: 'Copyright (c) 2021 Kohara Gear Industry Co.,Ltd. All Rights Reserved'; KHK's reducer-types article is itself reproduced with permission from a Japanese book (Japan Institute of Plant Maintenance, Shinpan Gensokuki No Hon, 2008). SDP/SI: 'No part of this publication may be reproduced in any form or by any means without prior written permission of the company.' Link and cite only; do not copy figures
- **image_license**: Same as text: all rights reserved. KHK uses clean rendered gear images (webp) for each gear type and line drawings for gear trains and planetary systems; SDP/SI uses line drawings and tables
- **authority_level**: manufacturer (two established gear makers; KHK is a Japanese gear maker, SDP/SI is Stock Drive Products / Sterling Instrument, part of Designatronics). SDP/SI credits KHK for the material and Dr George Michalec (Stevens Institute of Technology) as a contributor

### Coverage

- **covers**: Gear types and gear trains in depth. Types: spur gear, gear rack, internal gear, helical gear, helical rack, double helical (herringbone), straight / spiral / Zerol bevel gears, miter gears, worm gear pair, screw (crossed helical) gear, face gear, enveloping (hourglass) worm, hypoid gear (about 15 types). Gear trains: single-stage (with rotation direction for external, internal, bevel and worm meshes), rack and pinion, two-stage, idler, planetary (2K-H) with planetary / solar / star arrangements, hypocycloid (small tooth-difference internal gearing, e.g. 30:31 for 30:1), constrained (closed-loop) gear systems. KHK's reducer article adds gearbox families: parallel, orthogonal (bevel), skew (worm, hypoid) and coaxial (simple planetary, differential planetary, eccentric planetary, elastic planetary), with the Cyclo (cycloidal) reducer explained in detail. Plus involute geometry, profile shift, tooth thickness, backlash and anti-backlash methods, accuracy grades, materials and heat treatment, strength (bending and pitting), plastic gear design, forces, lubrication, damage and noise. KHK also has an 86-term gear nomenclature glossary and pages on magnetic gears and couplings. SDP/SI has 21 sections of about 230 pages (T-7 to T-232)
- **organisation**: Textbook order by engineering topic (types and terms, trains, involute profile, dimension calculations, tooth thickness, backlash, accuracy, mounting, materials, strength, plastic gears, forces, lubrication, damage, noise, gear systems). Gear types are classified by how the two shafts sit: parallel, intersecting, or neither (skew). That shaft-arrangement question is a good plain-language starting point, but the rest assumes you want to calculate gear dimensions, so beginners will get lost after the first two chapters
- **scope_match**: medium: the best authority for gear types and gear-train types (which the project wants), with clear shaft-arrangement classification and planetary, hypocycloid and cycloidal reducers, but most of the content is detailed calculation for standard involute gears; it does not cover strain-wave (harmonic) drives, differentials as a type, belts and chains, or non-gear connections
- **taxonomy_used**: By axis orientation, 3 groups plus extras: parallel axes gears, intersecting axes gears, nonparallel-and-nonintersecting axes gears, plus 'other special gears' (face gear, enveloping worm, hypoid). Gear trains by stages (single, two-stage) and by speed ratio (increasing, equal, reducing). Gear reducers by shaft position (parallel, orthogonal, skew, coaxial = 4 groups), with coaxial split into simple, differential, eccentric and elastic planetary
- **example_types**: spur gear; gear rack (rack and pinion); internal (ring) gear; helical gear; double helical (herringbone) gear; straight bevel gear; spiral bevel gear; Zerol bevel gear; miter gear; face (crown) gear; worm gear pair (self-locking at low lead angle); enveloping (hourglass) worm; screw (crossed helical) gear; hypoid gear; idler gear; planetary (2K-H epicyclic) gear system; hypocycloid mechanism (one-tooth-difference internal gearing); constrained (closed-loop) gear system; Cyclo (cycloidal) speed reducer; eccentric / differential / elastic planetary reducers; anti-backlash split gear (spring-loaded); magnetic gear and magnetic coupling (non-contact, slips under overload)
- **making_methods**: General mechanical engineering and gearbox design; machined and moulded gears; KHK and SDP/SI both cover plastic gears (nylon, Duracon/acetal, moulded vs cut), which is useful for 3D-printed gears, but neither mentions 3D printing
- **item_count**: About 15 gear types and about 8 gear-train or reducer types; KHK Gear Technical Reference has 17 chapters plus extra pages (anti-backlash gears, gear design, gear pump); KHK glossary has 86 terms; SDP/SI has 21 sections, about 230 pages
- **special_behaviours_covered**: Gear trains/gearboxes: yes, strong (single and multi-stage trains, idlers, planetary with three fixed-member cases and worked ratios, hypocycloid, cycloidal reducer, worm reducers up to 1:100 per stage, bevel and hypoid reducers). Self-locking: yes (worm gears at small lead angle; KHK notes true self-locking is hard to guarantee; SDP/SI gives the conditions for worm self-locking). Torque-limiting: only indirectly, via KHK's magnetic gears and couplings that slip under overload. Backlash removal with springs (dynamic methods: split gear halves forced apart, spring-closed centre distance). One-way/ratchet, indexing/detent (no Geneva or intermittent gears seen), over-centre, hand-release, compliant, print-in-place, powered connectors, adhesives: not covered
- **entry_structure**: Per gear type: name, rendered image, figure number, 2-4 sentence description with pros and cons, efficiency range. Per chapter: definitions, formulas, tables of symbols and worked numerical examples (e.g. za = 16, zb = 16, zc = 48 gives ratio 4)
- **audience_level**: professional and undergraduate (gear designers); KHK's 'ABCs of Gears - A', 'First step of mechanism design using gears' and 'Gear Tips' pages are aimed at beginners

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: the gear-type pages are short, well illustrated and sorted by a simple question (how are the shafts arranged?), and KHK has explicit beginner primers, but the technical reference quickly becomes formula-heavy with ISO/JIS symbols and no plain-English layer
- **has_images**: yes: KHK has clear rendered images of every gear type and neat line drawings of gear trains, planetary and cycloidal reducers; SDP/SI has black-and-white line drawings and many tables
- **has_animations**: no animations or videos seen on the pages opened
- **reusable_data**: not reusable beyond linking and citing facts (all rights reserved; SDP/SI forbids reproduction without written permission). Scrape-only HTML and PDF
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Use the shaft-arrangement question as a flow-chart branch: 'Are the input and output shafts parallel, crossing at an angle, or skewed / offset?' then show the gear types for each. (2) Give each gear type an efficiency range (spur/helical 98-99.5%, bevel 98-99%, screw 70-95%, worm 30-90%) so beginners see the cost of worm drives. (3) Show rotation direction per mesh type (external reverses, internal keeps direction, worm depends on helix hand). (4) Use KHK's three planetary cases (ring fixed, sun fixed, carrier fixed) with one worked ratio each. (5) Typical ratio ranges per reducer family (parallel 1:1-1:7 per stage, worm 1:5-1:100, Cyclo up to 1:119 per stage) as quick guidance. (6) A short linked glossary like KHK's gear nomenclature
- **design_rules_given**: Yes: efficiency ranges per gear type; single-stage ratio ranges per reducer family (parallel 1/1-1/7 one stage, 1/10-1/30 two stage; straight bevel 1:1 and 1:2 under 1000 rpm; spiral bevel up to 1:6; worm 1:5-1:100, and worm efficiency 60-95% for lead angles 5-40 degrees; Cyclo about 98% per stage, up to 1:119 one stage); worm housing temperature limit about 90 C with mineral oil; planetary tooth-count conditions (coaxial, even spacing, adjacent-planet clearance); full strength, backlash, accuracy (ISO/JIS/AGMA) and plastic-gear strength calculations
- **file_formats**: HTML pages and PDF handbooks (KHK 'ABCs of Gears' PDFs; SDP/SI PDF)
- **terminology_value**: high: authoritative, ISO/JIS-aligned English names and symbols for gear types and gear geometry (module, profile shift, Zerol, hypoid, screw gear = crossed helical gear, enveloping worm, 2K-H planetary, sun gear, planet carrier, idler), with an 86-term glossary; no translations in the English pages
- **decision_support**: Tables that compare gear types by axis arrangement and efficiency, and reducer families by ratio and characteristics; KHK's separate 'Selecting gears' page and step-by-step design procedure series; no interactive selector or flow chart

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - access_barrier
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - gaps
  - generator_or_parametric
  - good_for_entry_links
  - language
