## 47 Supplier catalogues as a vocabulary source (McMaster-Carr, MISUMI, igus)

*Supplier catalogue.* Catalogues that name clever off-the-shelf mechanisms (torque limiters, index plungers, toggle clamps, solenoid latches) and their variants

### Basics

- **name**: Supplier catalogues as a vocabulary source: McMaster-Carr, MISUMI and igus (plus the 2017 McMaster-Carr print catalogue scanned on the Internet Archive)
- **url**: McMaster-Carr: https://www.mcmaster.com/ (example category URL opened: https://www.mcmaster.com/products/torque-limiters/); McMaster-Carr Catalog 123 (2017) full scan: https://archive.org/details/mcmastercarr123; MISUMI Americas: https://www.misumi.com/ and its engineering blog https://blog.misumiusa.com/ (the e-catalogue itself, us.misumi-ec.com, refused automated access, so no catalogue page could be opened); igus: https://www.igus.com/ (e.g. https://www.igus.com/custom-parts-services/3d-printing/3d-printed-gear) and its Engineer's Toolbox https://toolbox.igus.com/category/design-guides
- **resource_type**: Supplier catalogue (three online industrial parts catalogues, used here as a naming/vocabulary reference rather than as a shop), plus one scanned print catalogue on the Internet Archive
- **language**: English (McMaster-Carr is US English and inch-first with metric ranges; MISUMI and igus run many country sites in other languages, e.g. MISUMI Japan/Germany/India and igus Germany/UK, but only the English sites were checked)
- **access_barrier**: For a person in a normal browser: none (free browsing, no login needed to view categories, though McMaster's site needs JavaScript; its plain HTML only shows the 26 top-level department names and 'Our website requires JavaScript'). For automated tools: high. McMaster served a 'To continue browsing, please log in' wall to a scripted browser, and MISUMI's e-catalogue (us/uk/de/in/jp.misumi-ec.com) returned Akamai 'Access Denied' to every scripted request. igus pages load normally
- **authority_level**: Manufacturer / distributor. Very reliable for the trade names, variants and specs of off-the-shelf parts; not neutral (each names things its own way, e.g. McMaster's 'instant-bonding adhesives' for cyanoacrylate glue, igus's brand names like iglide/iglidur, drylin, igubal)

### Coverage

- **scope_match**: medium: the catalogues are mostly standard hardware (out of scope), but their clever-mechanism families (torque limiters, index/spring plungers, toggle clamps, latches, one-way clutches, position hinges, gas springs, solenoids, electromagnetic locks, strain-wave and worm gear joints, adhesives by type) give the correct trade names and variant lists for many in-scope entries
- **example_types**: torque limiter (friction torque limiter, double-spring torque limiter, ball-detent torque limiter); adjustable-torque threaded knob (slips at a set torque); torque-limiting coupling; magnetic transmission drive (non-contact magnetic gear that skips poles when overloaded, MISUMI TM series); one-way locking needle-roller bearing clutch (drawn-cup roller clutch); spring plunger (ball-nose, long-nose) used as a detent catch; retractable/indexing plunger (pull-ring, L-handle, with or without locking/rest position); push-button quick-release pin and expanding-diameter quick-release pin; toggle clamp (hold-down, push/pull, right-angle, squeeze-action plier, auto-adjusting); draw latch (snap-down, turn-to-open, tight-hold); cam latch and lift-and-turn cam latch; push-to-close and push-to-open latch; position hinge, lever-lock hinge, friction hinge and incremental-angle position hinge; detent turntable (one-, four- and eight-position); remote-release gas spring; constant-force spring and constant-force retractor; rotary and linear speed limiter (damper); linear solenoid (push/pull); electromagnetic door and gate lock (magnetic lock); surface-contact electromagnet; strain-wave gear and modular worm gear joint (igus); planetary gear and right-angle gearbox; turn-lock / twist-lock (bayonet) connector; instant-bonding adhesive (cyanoacrylate), two-part epoxy, contact adhesive, hot-melt adhesive, threadlocker and retaining compound
- **making_methods**: General engineering, machine building and factory automation (all three); robotics (igus robot joints and strain-wave/worm gearboxes, MISUMI automation); 3D printing (igus sells wear-resistant 3D-printing filament and a 3D-printed gear service with an online gear configurator; McMaster and MISUMI CAD models are handy for designing around bought parts); woodwork/DIY only incidentally (hinges, latches, adhesives)
- **audience_level**: professional (mechanical and manufacturing engineers, machine builders, maintenance staff); usable by hobbyists, and McMaster's explainers are readable by beginners

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: McMaster's picture tiles, plain one-line variant names and 'About' boxes make it the most beginner-friendly of the three, but everything is written as buying specs, not 'which one should I use'; MISUMI assumes you already know the part name; igus hides generic names behind brand names (iglide, drylin, igubal, e-chain)
- **has_images**: yes: McMaster uses consistent grey line drawings and photos (very clear, and deliberately plain); MISUMI and igus use product renders and photos; all three offer CAD models
- **gaps**: No 'how it works' animations or motion diagrams; no comparisons across families (e.g. torque limiter vs slip clutch vs shear pin); nothing on making the mechanism yourself (3D printing, print-in-place, compliant versions); brand/trade names instead of neutral terms in places; inch-first sizes (McMaster); heavy scripting and bot-blocking make them hard to cite or scrape; MISUMI's catalogue refused automated access entirely; content changes over time and old category URLs may move
- **ideas_to_borrow**: 1) McMaster's 'About ...' box at the top of each family: a short plain explanation plus one simple drawing before any list; copy that pattern for each encyclopedia family. 2) Name variants by how they behave ('push-button', 'expanding-diameter', 'incremental-angle', 'remote-release', 'adjustable-torque') so the name itself teaches. 3) Picture-tile navigation: let beginners pick by look, not by term. 4) Filters with tiny schematic drawings (e.g. a drawing that shows what 'offset' means for a latch). 5) McMaster's Adhesives Bonding Guide matrix (adhesive type x material to bond, rated excellent/good) is a ready-made template for the adhesive part of the flow chart. 6) MISUMI's inCAD Library idea: show each component inside a small working example mechanism. 7) igus-style 'how long will it last' calculators and a gear configurator as optional links for printed gears. 8) A 'buy it' link per entry pointing at the McMaster family page, so makers can learn the trade name to search for
- **design_rules_given**: Yes, as ratings rather than rules: torque ratings and bore sizes for torque limiters, holding force for toggle clamps and latches, spring end forces for plungers, force ratings for gas springs, pull force for electromagnets, stroke and force for solenoids, gear module/teeth ranges (igus spur gears module 0.5-3, 12-150 teeth; bevel gear ratio 1.1-1.5), igus test data (a laser-sintered iglide i3 gear lasted about five times as long as a machined POM gear in a gear-on-rack test). Adhesive bonding suitability by material (McMaster). Few general design rules (clearances, fits) for making your own
- **decision_support**: Faceted filters on every family (size, torque/force, material, mounting, actuation) with small explanatory drawings; McMaster 'A Guide to Choosing the Correct Gas Springs', 'About Push-to-Close Latches' (face-mount vs edge-mount by door offset) and the Adhesives Bonding Guide matrix; igus gear project checklist and service-life calculator. No cross-family selector (nothing asks 'what do you want to happen?')
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Original sources for their own ranges, but many clever parts are resold from specialist makers: MISUMI resells JW Winco/Ganter (GN-numbered) indexing plungers, so Ganter/JW Winco and Kipp catalogues overlap and may be the original naming source (JW Winco site checked, it lists technical guides such as 'Choosing the Correct Standard Part'). McMaster overlaps with Grainger and MSC (Wikipedia 'See also' also lists MISUMI USA). igus overlaps with other plastic bearing makers. The engineering blogs (MISUMI Mech Lab, igus Engineer's Toolbox) overlap with the textbooks already in this list

## 48 Mechanism design apps (MotionGen, Linkage, PMKS+, geargenerator.com)

*Interactive tool.* Free apps for designing and animating linkages and gear trains

### Basics

- **name**: Mechanism design apps: MotionGen (Pro), Linkage (Mechanism Designer and Simulator), PMKS+ (Planar Mechanism Kinematic Simulator Plus) and Gear Generator 3 (geargenerator.com)
- **url**: MotionGen: https://motiongen.io/ (web app) with info pages at https://www.stonybrook.edu/commcms/motiongen/; Linkage: https://blog.rectorsquid.com/linkage-mechanism-designer-and-simulator/ (download https://blog.rectorsquid.com/download-linkage/, samples https://blog.rectorsquid.com/sample-linkage-mechanisms-2/, help https://www.rectorsquid.com/Linkage/index.html); PMKS+: https://pmksplus.com/ (app https://app.pmksplus.com/, source https://github.com/PMKS-Web/PMKSWeb); Gear Generator: https://geargenerator.com/ (manual https://geargenerator.com/help.html)
- **resource_type**: interactive tool (four free design-and-animate apps: three planar linkage simulators and one gear-train designer)
- **language**: English (all four). Gear Generator's author is Hungarian (iparigrafika.hu) but the app and manual are in English
- **authority_level**: MotionGen: academic peer-reviewed (Stony Brook University mechanical engineering, Prof. Anurag Purwar and Prof. Jeff Ge, NSF STTR funding; algorithms published in ASME journals) with a university spin-off company (Mechanismic Inc.). PMKS+: academic/educator (Worcester Polytechnic Institute students and faculty, verified with a MATLAB six-bar check). Linkage: single hobbyist/professional software developer (David Rector), community-trusted but unvetted. Gear Generator: single developer (Abel Vincze), unvetted but its manual cites ISO 606, DIN 6885 and ISO 4032 and explains its geometry openly

### Coverage

- **covers**: MotionGen: planar N-bar linkages with revolute (pin) and prismatic (slider) joints, one or more degrees of freedom; four-bar motion synthesis from 3-5 poses; AI-driven path synthesis; library of four-bar (RRRR, RRRP, RRPR), six-bar (Watt I, Watt II, Stephenson I, II, III) and eight-bar linkages; rotary and linear actuators; slotted links; 2D and 3D views. Linkage: any configuration of links, pivoting and sliding connectors, rotary and linear (hydraulic-style) inputs, circular gears and chains (gears on moving links allowed), spline cams; about 50 downloadable sample mechanisms, with samples and screenshots showing (for example) Paddington rolling bridge, Hoberman circle, grasshopper beam engine, Theo Jansen leg, Klann linkage, Watt linkage, Whitworth quick-return speed-changing oscillator, level-moving table, link and sliding apertures, front-end loader, backhoe). PMKS+: planar linkages with revolute and prismatic joints (one slider at a time), welded links, kinematic analysis (position, velocity, acceleration graphs) and static/dynamic force analysis, four-bar synthesis from 3 coupler poses; example library (four-bar, slider-crank, Watt I, Watt II, Stephenson III). Gear Generator 3: involute spur, helical, double-helical and herringbone gears, internal (ring) gears, racks, planetary sets (with input/fixed/drive/output roles, so it can model a differential), ISO 606 chain sprockets and timing-belt pulleys (GT2 2 mm and 3 mm, HTD 3M/5M/8M, T5, T10), multi-stage gear trains on shared axles, profile shift, backlash and lost-motion simulation, tooth forces
- **organisation**: Tools, not catalogues: each opens on a blank canvas plus an example library. MotionGen organises its library by linkage family (four-, six-, eight-bar, then Watt/Stephenson types), which needs vocabulary; its Stony Brook pages and video playlist explain the controls. Linkage has a sample page (one entry per mechanism with a file and a video) and a help site with a glossary. PMKS+ has a short 'Linkage Library (Example Linkages)'. Gear Generator is organised as a tree (each gear hangs off a parent by 'mesh' or 'axle') with a thorough manual (quick start, recipes such as 'a two-stage reducer', warnings table, reference card), the most beginner-readable of the four
- **scope_match**: medium: they are tools rather than catalogues, but they cover in-scope families (linkages, motion converters like slider-cranks, quick-return and cams, and especially gear trains: planetary, rack, chain and belt drives, multi-stage reducers) and let a beginner see and test a mechanism before making it. They do not cover connections, latches, torque limiting or adhesives
- **taxonomy_used**: MotionGen: kinematic chain types (four-bar with RRRR/RRRP/RRPR joint strings; six-bar Watt I/II and Stephenson I/II/III; eight-bar) and dyad types (RR, PR, RP). PMKS+: same Watt/Stephenson naming for its examples, joints split into revolute, prismatic and welded. Linkage: element types (links, pivot connectors, sliding connectors, gears, chains, splines/cams, motors/actuators). Gear Generator: object types (gear, internal gear, rack, planetary set, chain wheel set, belt wheel set) and connection types (mesh, axle). No single top-level classification of mechanisms
- **example_types**: four-bar linkage (crank-rocker); slider-crank; Watt six-bar (Watt I, Watt II); Stephenson six-bar (I, II, III); eight-bar linkage; Theo Jansen leg linkage; Klann walking linkage; Watt straight-line linkage; Whitworth quick-return mechanism; Hoberman circle (expanding linkage); Paddington rolling bridge (curling bridge linkage); linkage aperture (iris); level-keeping parallel linkage (level-moving table); spline cam and follower; involute spur gear pair; helical, double-helical and herringbone gears; internal (ring) gear; rack and pinion; planetary (epicyclic) gear set and planetary differential; multi-stage (compound) gear reducer; roller-chain sprocket drive; timing-belt pulley drive (GT2/HTD)
- **making_methods**: 3D printing and laser cutting (MotionGen exports STL/SVG part geometry and pairs with SnappyXO kits; Gear Generator exports STL/OBJ for printing and SVG/DXF for laser/CNC, with a 0.1-0.3 mm fit allowance tip for printed bores; Linkage exports DXF used by makers on laser cutters); robotics (MotionGen for walking robots, drawing bots and grippers); kinetic sculpture and automata (Linkage's rolling-ball sculpture lift, Keith Newstead owl-and-pussycat automaton sample); undergraduate engineering analysis (PMKS+)
- **entry_structure**: Not entries but interactive models: each example is an editable, animated mechanism. Linkage sample page entries have a name, a one-line description, a .linkage2 file and a video. Gear Generator shows for each gear its diameters, contact ratio, working pressure angle and tooth forces in an info box with warnings. PMKS+ shows joint/link tables and graphs of position, velocity, acceleration and joint forces over time. MotionGen shows the linkage, coupler path and dimensions, with export to PDF
- **audience_level**: MotionGen: school (K-12 student/teacher account types) through undergraduate and makers ('school going children, college students, makers and hobbyists, and industry practitioners'). PMKS+: undergraduate. Linkage: hobbyist and kinetic-sculpture makers, also used by engineers. Gear Generator: hobbyist to professional

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: all four are free and visual, and animation makes motion easy to grasp, but you must already know roughly which mechanism you want; MotionGen and PMKS+ use kinematics jargon (dyads, RRPR, Watt/Stephenson, synthesis, poses); Linkage is simple but Windows-only; Gear Generator's manual is excellent and plain but assumes basic gear terms (module, pressure angle, profile shift), which it then explains
- **has_images**: yes (the apps draw clean 2D diagrams; Gear Generator's manual has labelled figures; Linkage pages have screenshots and a book-like drawing style)
- **has_animations**: yes, that is their main purpose: live, editable simulations. Gear Generator animates whole trains (turn by hand, lost-motion display) and has a 3D view; Linkage runs at 30 frames per second and exports HD video; MotionGen exports GIFs and has 2D/3D views; PMKS+ animates and graphs motion. Linkage and MotionGen also have YouTube tutorial videos
- **gaps**: Tools, not a reference: no explanation of when or why to choose a mechanism, no connection/latch/fastening types, no torque-limiting, ratchet, detent or compliant elements, no Geneva drive or escapement components, no springs or gravity (Linkage, and MotionGen/PMKS+ are kinematic), no 3D (spatial) linkages; Linkage lacks rack gears and runs only on Windows; Gear Generator exports cost money; MotionGen library names need jargon; link-sharing formats are app-specific
- **reusable_data**: PMKS+ is MIT-licensed and on GitHub (code and example linkage URLs can be reused and forked). Linkage files are XML (.linkage2) and the samples are free downloads under the author's WTFPL-plus terms (allowed to use, not to resell). Gear Generator .gg3 files are JSON, but the app is proprietary. MotionGen .motiongen files and its library are proprietary. None provides a list or dataset of mechanism types
- **ideas_to_borrow**: 1) Embed or link a live, editable model for each mechanism entry (share-URL designs from Gear Generator and PMKS+). 2) Gear Generator's 'warnings' style: plain messages that say what is wrong and what to change (e.g. 'Ring teeth must equal sun + 2 x planet teeth', 'Internal gear needs at least 8 more teeth than the pinion'). 3) Gear Generator's recipes ('a two-stage reducer') as worked examples in entries. 4) Linkage's sample page: one line of description, one file, one video per mechanism, a good minimal entry format. 5) Tracer/path drawing (Linkage, PMKS+, MotionGen) to show the path a point follows. 6) MotionGen's flow of 'draw the motion you want, get a linkage' (motion/path synthesis) as an advanced flow-chart endpoint. 7) Use the Watt/Stephenson naming as aliases, with plain names first
- **design_rules_given**: Gear Generator gives many: standard rack geometry (addendum 1 x module, dedendum 1.25 x module, root clearance 0.25 x module), pressure angles (20 degrees standard, 14.5 degrees older, 25 degrees for strength), teeth 4-400, ring must have at least 1 more (hard limit) and 8 more (practical) teeth than the pinion, planetary rules RN = SN + 2PN and (SN + RN)/n a whole number, ISO 606 sprocket tooth width 0.93-0.95 x inner width, DIN 6885 keyways, ISO 4032 nut traps, and a 0.1-0.3 mm bore allowance for printed parts. PMKS+ gives computed forces and torques. MotionGen and Linkage give dimensions only
- **generator_or_parametric**: Yes: all four generate custom geometry. Gear Generator is a parametric gear generator (teeth, module, pressure angle, helix, profile shift, holes, hubs). MotionGen synthesises linkage dimensions from target poses or paths and exports part shapes. PMKS+ synthesises a four-bar from 3 coupler poses. Linkage produces dimensioned parts lists and DXF outlines
- **terminology_value**: medium: good for kinematic names (four-bar, slider-crank, Watt and Stephenson six-bars, RRRP-style joint strings, dyads, coupler, crank, rocker) and gear terms (module, diametral pitch, pressure angle, profile shift, undercut, contact ratio, backlash, lost motion, sun/planet/ring/carrier); Gear Generator's manual is a clear plain-English source for gear vocabulary; no translations
- **decision_support**: No selector or flow chart. Decision help is by trial: change a number and watch the result. Gear Generator shows live warnings and ratios; PMKS+ graphs forces; MotionGen solves 'which linkage gives this motion' (motion and path synthesis), which is a kind of design-time selector for linkages
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: PMKS+ is built on PMKS (Prof. Matthew I. Campbell, Oregon State University). MotionGen builds on Purwar and Ge's published synthesis research and is part of the SnappyXO Design suite (Stony Brook University and Mechanismic Inc.). Linkage users describe it as a replacement for Autodesk ForceEffect Motion (FAQ comment). Gear Generator 3 replaces Gear Generator 2 (imports its files). Overlaps: SAM, Working Model and SolidWorks (compared on the PMKS+ site), the Wolfram Demonstrations and MechSimulator sites already in this list, and many other gear generators (geardxf.com and others found by search, not checked)

## 49 Joining and selection tools (TWI, Swift and Booker, Granta EduPack)

*Interactive tool.* Selectors and decision aids for choosing joining methods; models for the project's finder

### Basics

- **name**: Joining-method selectors and decision aids: TWI (JoinIT and the Adhesives Design Toolkit, plus TWI's free 'choosing a joining method' FAQs), Swift and Booker (joining process selection matrix and PRIMA process maps, in 'Process Selection' 1997/2003 and the 'Manufacturing Process Selection Handbook' 2013), and Ansys Granta EduPack (formerly CES EduPack; the 'ProcessUniverse: Joining' data table and selector, based on Esawi and Ashby's joining-selection method)
- **url**: No single URL. Opened: Swift and Booker handbook page https://shop.elsevier.com/books/manufacturing-process-selection-handbook/swift/978-0-08-099360-7 ; Granta EduPack product page https://ansys.synopsys.com/products/materials/granta-edupack ; TWI free guide https://www.twi-global.com/technical-knowledge/faqs/what-are-the-most-common-material-joining-methods ; review of all three approaches (Gutzler and Kirchner, DESIGN 2026): https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/2D1B1A5DC771973D3E23C32EE2E8EE4B/S2732527X26105549a.pdf/novel_selection_framework_of_joining_technologies_for_metal_additive_manufacturing.pdf
- **resource_type**: interactive tool (decision aids and selector software) plus book (the Swift and Booker handbook, which is a paper-based selector)
- **license**: All rights reserved for all three (TWI copyright; Elsevier/Butterworth-Heinemann copyright; Ansys proprietary software and data under a licence agreement that forbids unauthorised use, distribution or copying). The selection criteria and names of methods can be cited as facts; tables, figures and datasheet text cannot be copied
- **language**: English
- **year**: Booker and Swift 'Process Selection: From Design to Manufacture' 1997 (Arnold); 2nd edition 2003 (Butterworth-Heinemann). Brown, Swift and Booker's joining-selection method paper 2002 (Proc. IMechE Part B 216:1311-1324). Swift and Booker 'Manufacturing Process Selection Handbook' 15 Feb 2013, the latest edition. TWI JoinIT won a Vision 100 award in 2001 (Anti-Corrosion Methods and Materials 48(5), Oct 2001), and a JoinIT address (joinit.grantapark.com) was cited in 2004. TWI and the UK DTI's Adhesives Design Toolkit (adhesivestoolkit.com): first phase finished 2003, testing module 2006. LeBacq et al. joining selector 2002. Esawi and Ashby 'Computer-based selection of joining processes' 2004 (Materials and Design 25(7):555-564). Granta EduPack: 'Getting Started' guide for release 2024 R1 (Jan 2024). The Ansys forum mentions 2025 R2, and the product is sold today under Synopsys/Ansys (2026)
- **access_barrier**: Varies: TWI FAQs none; TWI Digital Library and member content membership; the Swift and Booker book paywall; Granta EduPack desktop install plus academic licence
- **image_license**: All rights reserved: TWI figures, the book's process diagrams and PRIMA charts, and EduPack's datasheet images and charts

### Coverage

- **organisation**: By process family, with selection logic on top. All three use the same two-stage logic: (1) screen out processes that can't meet hard requirements, then (2) compare the survivors using a datasheet or ranking. Swift and Booker: a paper matrix with material across one axis and thickness on the other, then permanence and production quantity, which narrows the field to a shortlist, and then one standard-format PRIMA (process information map) per process to check in detail. EduPack: a tree of joining processes plus screening 'stages' (a Tree stage by material, Limit stages on attributes, then ranking charts). For a beginner the questions are good, but the answers are industrial process names (MIG, TIG, EBW) that a hobbyist rarely needs
- **scope_match**: low for content, high for method: they catalogue industrial joining processes (mostly welding), not unusual connection or mechanism types. There are no snap fits, bayonets, ratchets, gear trains or powered latches, and mechanical fastening is one lumped entry. But their selection logic (questions about material, thickness, permanence, loading, environment and quantity, with screening followed by ranking and a datasheet) is the most mature model for the project's beginner flow chart
- **making_methods**: General industrial manufacture and assembly (metals first, then plastics and composites). Swift and Booker's handbook also covers rapid prototyping processes (SLA, 3DP, FDM, LOM) as shaping processes, not as joining. Nothing aimed at hobby 3D printing. A 2026 paper (Gutzler and Kirchner) adapts these selectors to metal additive manufacturing
- **audience_level**: undergraduate (EduPack; the Swift and Booker book is also used by advanced students) and professional (TWI members; design and manufacturing engineers)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low to medium: the questions (what materials? how thick? permanent or removable? how many? what loads? what environment?) are easy for a beginner to answer, and TWI's FAQ is plain English. But the answers are industrial process names, the tools are paid or institutional, and EduPack assumes an engineering course
- **gaps**: No clever or unusual connection types (snap fit, bayonet, cam lock, magnets, ratchet, detents, flexures). Mechanical fastening is lumped into one entry. No mechanisms, gear trains or powered connectors. Nothing for 3D printing, print-in-place or hobby tools. Welding dominates, even though most makers can't weld. Adhesive choice by material is only covered in TWI articles and the (possibly defunct) Adhesives Design Toolkit. Everything useful is paywalled or institution-only, and the free public web tools (JoinIT, adhesivestoolkit.com) are gone or unreachable
- **good_for_entry_links**: low: none of them has stable, free per-method pages except TWI's FAQ and Job Knowledge articles (e.g. https://www.twi-global.com/technical-knowledge/faqs/what-are-the-most-common-material-joining-methods), which are good for linking from adhesive or welding baseline entries. EduPack datasheets live inside desktop software, and the book has no per-process URLs
- **reusable_data**: not reusable: EduPack data is proprietary and licensed for teaching only, the book is copyrighted, and JoinIT was member-only. What can be reused is the published selection criteria and logic (from the peer-reviewed papers), rebuilt in the project's own words
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Screen first, then compare: ask a few elimination questions, show the survivors, then give each survivor a standard one-page card. This is exactly Swift and Booker's matrix-then-PRIMA and Ashby's screen-then-rank-then-datasheet pattern. (2) The core question set that all three share, translated for beginners: What are the two parts made of? (same or different materials); How thick or big are they?; Must it come apart again? (permanent / take apart occasionally / take apart often); How many will you make?; Which way will it be loaded? (pulled apart, sheared, peeled, twisted); What environment? (heat, water, chemicals); How should it look?; What tools do you have? (LeBacq's 'company competence' check becomes 'can you do this with a 3D printer, hand tools, a soldering iron?'). (3) A standard card format per entry, like the PRIMA fields: how it works with a diagram, suitable materials, variants, cost and effort, typical uses, design tips, and common failures. (4) Explain why an option was excluded (LeBacq et al. list rejected methods with the reason), which teaches beginners. (5) If nothing fits, loop back and relax a requirement, as Gutzler and Kirchner's flow chart does. (6) The EduPack 'Tree stage' idea: pick a material and see every joining method linked to it. The project could offer 'I'm joining PETG to aluminium' and get all compatible connection types. (7) For adhesives, borrow TWI's rules: design joints for shear and compression, not peel (roughly 100:100:1), and match glue chemistry to the plastic, flagging hard-to-bond plastics (PP, PE, PTFE, silicone)
- **generator_or_parametric**: No (EduPack has a part-cost estimator and a 'Synthesizer' for modelling, but nothing generates geometry)
- **file_formats**: Book (print and ebook), desktop software (EduPack, Windows), web pages (TWI), PDFs (papers and guides)
- **terminology_value**: medium: authoritative for standard English names of joining processes (e.g. tungsten inert-gas welding (TIG), resistance welding, thermoplastic welding, anaerobic adhesive) and of the selection criteria (permanence, joint geometry, mode of loading). Nothing for mechanism or clever-connector names
