## 50 TRIZ effects database (Oxford Creativity)

*Interactive tool.* Free function-based lookup ("how do I hold / move / join ...") built from patents

### Basics

- **name**: TRIZ Effects Database (Oxford Creativity), written and maintained by Andrew Martin
- **url**: https://wbam2244.dns-systems.net/EDB/index.php (the database itself; opened over https, since the plain-http address the Oxford Creativity page links to was blocked here). Landing page: https://www.triz.co.uk/triz-effects-database (opened). Help page: https://wbam2244.dns-systems.net/EDB/EDBHelpGeneral.html (opened)
- **resource_type**: interactive tool (free web lookup that answers 'how do I <do something> to <something>?' questions with a list of physical effects and devices)
- **cost**: Free. No sign-up, no login, no adverts. The author says it has 'no barriers to use (no signing up, signing in, adverts or invoices)'
- **language**: English only
- **access_barrier**: none (runs in any browser; queries are sent as form POSTs)
- **image_license**: Not applicable: the results are text only, with no images, diagrams or animations. Images on the linked Wikipedia pages carry their own Commons licences
- **authority_level**: educator (a TRIZ consultancy and training firm; one expert's curated list, which he says is based on analysis of patents; not peer-reviewed or an official standard). The design process behind the database is described in a peer-reviewed conference paper (Procedia Engineering, 2015)

### Coverage

- **covers**: Physical phenomena ('effects') and simple devices that can perform a given function, such as holding, joining, moving, rotating, constraining, heating or separating a solid, divided solid (powder or granules), liquid, gas or field. A full sweep of all 175 Function queries (35 verbs x 5 object types) on 2026-09-25 returned 881 distinct effects, 867 of them linked to Wikipedia. There are also 5 x 39 Parameter queries and 9 x 9 Transform (energy-conversion) queries. For connections the relevant queries are large: 'Join Solid' returns 132 suggestions, 'Hold Solid' 123, 'Constrain Solid' 101, 'Rotate Solid' 122, 'Move Solid' 237, 'Stabilise Position' 99 and 'Increase Friction' 76. The results mix everyday connection types (adhesive, cyanoacrylate, screw, pin, hook, knot, Velcro, wedge, welding, soldering, brazing, mechanical fastener) with mechanisms (ratchet, escapement, cam, worm drive, epicyclic gearing, rack and pinion, sun and planet gear, swashplate, Peaucellier-Lipkin linkage, Hart's inversor, hinge, gimbal), powered and smart-material effects (electromagnet, electropermanent magnet, solenoid, shape memory alloy and polymer, magnetorheological and electrorheological fluids, electroactive polymer, Johnsen-Rahbek effect) and exotic physics (Casimir effect, Meissner effect, optical tweezers, acoustic levitation)
- **organisation**: By function, as a two-part question. First you choose one of 3 query types: Function (e.g. 'Move Liquid'), Parameter (e.g. 'Increase Temperature') or Transform (e.g. 'Transform Mechanical Energy to Thermal Energy'). Then you pick one button from each of two lists, a verb and an object (e.g. Join + Solid), and optionally filter to pure Effects, Applications or both. The result is an alphabetical list of effect names, each followed by a short description and sometimes an application note that is specific to the query. This works well for someone who doesn't know the terms, because the questions are plain verbs ('Hold', 'Join', 'Rotate', 'Constrain'). The weakness is the output: 100 to 240 unranked suggestions with no grouping, so a beginner can't tell a practical fastener from an exotic lab effect
- **scope_match**: medium: it isn't a catalogue of connection types, and most of its entries are physics rather than hardware. But its function-first questions ('How do I hold / join / constrain / rotate a solid?') are exactly the kind of entry point the project's flow chart needs. Its answers also name many in-scope types (ratchet, escapement, worm drive, epicyclic gearing, wedge, electropermanent magnet, solenoid, shape-memory alloy, gecko-type dry adhesive, hook-and-loop, knot), as well as a few odd, clever ideas worth mining (Johnsen-Rahbek electroadhesion, magnetorheological fluid clamping, tensegrity, origami)
- **taxonomy_used**: TRIZ function-object-effect scheme. 3 top-level query modes: Function (35 verbs: Absorb, Accumulate, Bend, Break Down, Change Phase, Clean, Compress, Concentrate, Condense, Constrain, Cool, Deposit, Destroy, Detect, Dilute, Dry, Evaporate, Expand, Extract, Freeze, Heat, Hold, Join, Melt, Mix, Move, Orient, Produce, Protect, Purify, Remove, Resist, Rotate, Separate, Vibrate, each applied to 5 objects: Solid, Divided Solid, Liquid, Gas, Field); Parameter (5 verbs: Change, Decrease, Increase, Measure, Stabilise, applied to 39 parameters such as Force, Friction, Position, Orientation, Rigidity, Shape, Strength, Vibration); and Transform (9 x 9 energy types: acoustic, chemical, electrical, electromagnetic, kinetic, magnetic, mechanical, optical, thermal). Results are also tagged as pure Effects or Applications (the Effects / Applications / both filter)
- **example_types**: ratchet; escapement; worm drive (self-locking gear); epicyclic (planetary) gearing; sun and planet gear; rack and pinion; cam; swashplate; Peaucellier-Lipkin linkage and Hart's inversor (straight-line linkages); gimbal; wedge; hook; knot; hook-and-loop (Velcro) and nano-Velcro; gecko-foot bristle array (dry adhesive); cyanoacrylate (superglue); electromagnet; electropermanent magnet (switchable magnet that holds with no power); solenoid; shape memory alloy; shape memory polymer; magnetorheological fluid and elastomer (field-controlled stiffening); Johnsen-Rahbek effect (electrostatic clamping); Halbach array; suction; tensegrity; rigid origami; fusible alloy (melts to release)
- **making_methods**: None in particular. It's a general engineering and problem-solving aid based on patents, so it covers everything from mechanisms to chemistry and physics. There's nothing specific to 3D printing apart from '3D Printing' and 'Selective Laser Sintering' appearing as effects
- **special_behaviours_covered**: Partly, as bare names with a one- or two-sentence description and no design detail. One-way/ratchet: yes (Ratchet appears under Move, Rotate, Stabilise Position and Increase Friction). Indexing/detent: only indirectly (Escapement, Groove). Self-locking: yes, as Wedge, Worm Drive and Static Friction, although the entries don't explain self-locking. Hand-release only, torque-limiting (no clutch or slip entry) and over-centre (no toggle entry): no. Compliant: partly (Elasticity, Spring, Torsion Spring, Hinge, Rigid Origami, Auxetic Structures), but no flexure or compliant-mechanism entry. Print-in-place: no. Gear trains/gearboxes: partly (Gear, Epicyclic Gearing, Sun and Planet Gear, Worm Drive, Rack and Pinion, Pulley, Block and Tackle, Swashplate). Powered connectors: strong (Electromagnet, Electropermanent Magnet, Solenoid, Shape Memory Alloy, Magnetic Shape Memory, Electroactive Polymer, Electrorheological and Magnetorheological fluids, Johnsen-Rahbek electroadhesion, Electrostatics). Adhesive types: weak. Only generic 'Adhesive', 'Binder' and 'Cyanoacrylate' appear, plus processes (welding, soldering, brazing, sintering, photopolymerisation), and there's no guidance on which glue suits which material
- **entry_structure**: Effect name (which links to Wikipedia or another web page for most effects), a 1-3 sentence plain description of the phenomenon and, for some query and effect pairs, a one-line application note (e.g. under Anisotropy: 'Self-assembling systems use anisotropic joints to facilitate correct selection and orientation of components'). No figures, no degrees of freedom, no design rules, no sources for individual entries
- **audience_level**: professional (TRIZ practitioners, product engineers and people learning TRIZ); usable by a curious hobbyist

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium: the questions are very easy (pick a verb and a thing), and the descriptions are short and mostly plain. But the answers are long, unranked alphabetical lists that mix everyday hardware with advanced physics (e.g. Casimir effect next to Chain under 'Join Solid'), with no guidance on practicality, cost or difficulty. The author's own paper notes users now ask 'can they be filtered in some way?'
- **has_images**: no (text only)
- **has_animations**: no
- **gaps**: No pictures. No ranking or practicality rating (everyday, maker-feasible or lab-only). No distinction between permanent, removable and adjustable joints. No design rules or sizes. No materials or making-method filter. No special-behaviour vocabulary (torque-limiting, over-centre, hand-release, detent). Adhesives aren't broken down by chemistry or material. Results can't be bookmarked (POST form, no per-query or per-effect URL). Many important connection types are missing entirely: a name search of all 881 Function results found no snap fit, bayonet, press fit, threaded insert, circlip, dovetail, flexure, latch, clutch, brake, detent, toggle/over-centre, buckle or rivet (only generic 'Mechanical Fastener', 'Screw' and 'Pin')
- **good_for_entry_links**: low: queries are POST-only, so there's no stable URL for a query result or a single effect. Only the start page (https://wbam2244.dns-systems.net/EDB/index.php) and help page can be linked. For single entries, link to the Wikipedia page that the database itself points to instead
- **reusable_data**: scrape-only: there's no API, download or dataset, but the whole Function set can be collected with 175 simple form POSTs (as done for the counts here). The list of effects is Oxford Creativity's own curation, and the site terms forbid republishing, so use it as a source of ideas and cross-check names, not as a dataset to copy. The function verbs and object classes are generic TRIZ vocabulary and free to reuse
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Verb + object question as the first step of the flow chart: 'What do you want it to do?' (Hold / Join / Constrain / Rotate / Move / Orient / Stabilise position / Increase friction) x 'to what?' (a solid part, a powder, a liquid, a gas). This maps directly onto a beginner's own words. (2) Three entry modes: by function, by changing a parameter (make it stiffer, stop it moving, increase grip) and by energy conversion (turn electrical into mechanical, which suits powered connectors and actuators). (3) An Effects vs Applications filter, which is like the project splitting 'principles' (friction, magnetism, elasticity) from 'devices' (cam cleat, ratchet). (4) Query-specific one-line application notes that explain why an effect answers this particular question. (5) Linking each result out to Wikipedia for depth instead of rewriting it. (6) Learning from its weakness: rank or group results (everyday / maker-friendly / specialist), because long flat lists overwhelm users
- **design_rules_given**: No. No numbers, clearances, strengths or torques
- **generator_or_parametric**: No
- **file_formats**: HTML web pages only
- **terminology_value**: medium: useful for finding the proper physics or device name behind a vague idea (e.g. electropermanent magnet, Johnsen-Rahbek effect, magnetorheological elastomer, Peaucellier-Lipkin linkage, Stockbridge damper), and each name comes with a Wikipedia link. No aliases, translations or maker slang. A few typos and encoding glitches (e.g. 'Govenor', the dash in 'Peaucellier-Lipkin' shown as a bad character)
- **decision_support**: Yes, a simple two-step selector with no scoring. Step 1: choose a query type (Function, Parameter or Transform). Step 2: choose one verb and one object from two sets of radio buttons (e.g. Join + Solid, or Stabilise + Position), plus an optional Effects / Applications / both filter, then submit. The output is an unranked alphabetical list of candidate effects, with counts shown (e.g. '132 suggestions for Join Solid'), each with a description and link. The user decides which suggestions are useful ('You will have to decide for yourself which (if any) of the suggested effects is useful'). There's no further filtering by material, size, cost, reversibility or feasibility, no comparison table and no follow-up questions

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - item_count
  - license
  - maintained
  - year

## 51 thisTOthat adhesive selector

*Interactive tool.* Beginner "glue this to that" selector: pick two materials, get recommended adhesive types; a model decision tool

### Basics

- **name**: thisTOthat (This to That: Glue Advice), the 'glue this to that' material-pair glue selector
- **url**: https://www.thistothat.com/ (home page with the two drop-down menus; opened). Example result page: https://www.thistothat.com/cgi-bin/glue.cgi?this=Wood&that=Plastic (opened). Selection philosophy: https://www.thistothat.com/phil.shtml (opened)
- **resource_type**: interactive tool (a web 'glue calculator': pick material A and material B from two drop-down menus, get a results page of recommended glues), plus short glue-type explainer pages
- **cost**: Free, no account. Paid for by advertising (Google AdSense code is in every page); the site has a Statement of Impartiality saying advertisers don't influence its picks
- **license**: All rights reserved ('Copyright © 1999-2024 thistothat.com, All rights reserved' in the page footer). No open licence, so link to it but don't copy its text, tables or icons. The pairing facts (e.g. 'for wood to plastic they suggest a silicone-type glue, then 5-minute epoxy, then contact cement') can be summarised in our own words with a citation
- **year**: Launched 1999 (copyright line '1999-...'; the 'Glue of the Month' archive runs from the early 2000s to August 2003). Restyled for mobile phones on 2 November 2018 (News page). Latest news item seen: 20 March 2025. Home page footer reads 1999-2024; results pages read 1999-2023
- **access_barrier**: none
- **image_license**: All rights reserved. The site has almost no pictures: small icons (tip, toxicity, time, link) only, and no photos or diagrams of joints
- **authority_level**: community or unvetted: an independent hobby/consumer advice site run by a small team (the Links page cross-links other sites 'also by us', e.g. Hogdex, a Toronto portal). Recommendations come from the authors' own household tests plus manufacturers' safety data sheets (MSDS) for toxicity ratings; no named experts or references

### Coverage

- **covers**: Adhesive bonding only, for common household materials. 11 materials in each menu: ceramic, fabric, glass, leather, metal, paper, plastic, rubber, Styrofoam (expanded polystyrene foam), vinyl and wood, which gives 66 different material pairs (121 ordered choices; 'A to B' and 'B to A' give the same advice). All 66 pairs were opened and all return a recommendation. Across them the results link about 45 glue pages: roughly 10 generic glue-type pages (cyanoacrylate, PVA, contact adhesive, spray adhesive, hot glue, silicone-based glue, metal epoxy, PVC adhesive, plus 'reactivity' and 'clamping' tip pages) and about 35 named consumer products (mostly Canadian/US brands). The FAQ says the site deliberately sticks to 'common household items' and has no concrete, stone or skin advice
- **organisation**: A two-question decision tool: 'Attach (This) to (That)'. The answer page lists glues in ranked or conditional groups: 'For the strongest bond…', 'For a faster bond, but not as foolproof…', 'For a less toxic alternative…', 'If appearance isn't much of a concern…', 'If you want the joint to stay flexible…', 'If your vinyl has a woven back…', 'If you are gluing PVC plumbing pipes…'. Tip notes follow (clean metal with steel wool, glass must be oil-free, test plastics in a hidden spot, sand glossy plastic with 120 grit, never put solvent glue or hot glue straight onto Styrofoam). Each product links to a page naming its glue family and giving toxicity icons and setting time. This works very well for someone with no vocabulary: you only need to know the names of the two materials
- **scope_match**: medium: it's exactly the beginner 'which glue for these two materials' question the project wants for its adhesive family, and it's a model for the flow-chart UX. But it answers with brand products rather than adhesive types, covers only 11 coarse materials (all plastics are one bucket), and covers nothing else in the project's scope (no mechanisms or mechanical connections)
- **taxonomy_used**: A material × material grid (11 × 11, symmetric), not a chemistry taxonomy. Each recommended product is tagged with a glue family on its own page (e.g. 'This is a contact adhesive', 'This is a flexible PVA', 'This is a metal epoxy', 'This is a cyanoacrylate', 'This is a silicone-based glue', 'This is a spray adhesive'). Material groups are the everyday names: 11 top-level materials with no sub-types (e.g. no ABS vs PLA vs polypropylene split)
- **making_methods**: Household repairs, crafts, sewing and upholstery, bookbinding and paper craft, theatre props and costumes, light woodwork. Not aimed at 3D printing, CNC or robotics, and doesn't mention printed plastics such as PLA, PETG or ABS
- **item_count**: 11 materials; 66 unique material pairs (all with advice); about 45 linked glue pages (about 10 generic type or tip pages plus about 35 products); about 30 archived 'Glue of the Month' reviews
- **special_behaviours_covered**: Adhesive types only. It covers cyanoacrylate, two-part epoxy, metal epoxy/epoxy putty, contact cement, spray adhesive, PVA (rigid and flexible), hot melt, silicone-type flexible glue, UV-curing glass glue, PVC solvent cement, starch paste and polyurethane, and matches them to ceramic, fabric, glass, leather, metal, paper, plastic, rubber, polystyrene foam, vinyl and wood. The other special behaviours (torque-limiting, self-locking, hand-release, one-way/ratchet, indexing, over-centre, compliant, print-in-place, gear trains, powered connectors) are not covered. It does touch on 'flexible vs stiff joint' as a selection factor (flexible PVA or silicone-type glue when the joint must bend)
- **entry_structure**: Results page: heading 'Material A to Material B', 1 to 5 ranked or conditional recommendation groups, each with 1 to 4 product links, then tip notes. Product page: glue family ('This is a contact adhesive'), a short comment, toxicity icons for inhalation, ingestion and skin absorption (3 levels each, taken from MSDS), time to adhere, where to buy, related products and a link to the maker. Generic type page (e.g. cyanoacrylate, PVA, contact, hot glue): a paragraph plus a bulleted list of how-to tips
- **audience_level**: hobbyist (general public, crafters, DIY home repair)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: high: two plain drop-downs, everyday material names, humorous plain-English writing, clear 'strongest / fastest / least toxic / most invisible' trade-off labels, and practical tips (clamp it, sand it, clean it, test in a hidden spot). Downsides: answers are brand names that may not be sold outside Canada or the US, and some chemistry explanations are wrong (see gaps)
- **has_images**: Barely: only small icons (tip, toxicity level, time). No photos or diagrams of joints, glue application or failure modes
- **has_animations**: no
- **good_for_entry_links**: Yes. Result pages have stable query URLs per material pair, e.g. https://www.thistothat.com/cgi-bin/glue.cgi?this=Wood&that=Plastic and https://www.thistothat.com/cgi-bin/glue.cgi?this=Metal&that=Glass, and glue-type pages have stable .shtml URLs (e.g. https://www.thistothat.com/glue/cyan.shtml for cyanoacrylate, https://www.thistothat.com/glue/pva.shtml for PVA, https://www.thistothat.com/glue/contact.shtml for contact adhesive; all opened). Best used as a 'try it yourself' link from adhesive entries
- **reusable_data**: Scrape-only (plain server-rendered HTML from a simple CGI script; there's no API or download). The 11 × 11 grid is small enough to summarise by hand. All rights reserved, so the grid and wording can't be copied or republished; only facts restated in our own words with a citation
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) The two-drop-down 'Attach THIS to THAT' entry point is the ideal first step for the adhesive branch of the flow chart: people know their materials, not chemistry names. (2) A symmetric material × material grid (every pair has an answer) as the adhesive data model, but with adhesive TYPES as answers and finer material classes (e.g. split plastics into 'easy to glue' vs 'slippery/low surface energy: PE, PP'). (3) Ranked answer groups labelled by the trade-off: strongest / fastest / least toxic / most invisible / stays flexible / fills gaps. (4) Conditional follow-up questions inside a result ('Does your vinyl have a woven back?', 'Is it PVC plumbing pipe?', 'Is the glass clear enough for UV light?'). (5) Per-material 'gotcha' tips (e.g. solvent glue melts polystyrene foam; put hot glue on the other part first and let it cool a few seconds; sand glossy plastic; clean metal). (6) A simple 3-level toxicity rating for breathing, swallowing and skin contact. (7) The philosophy page's beginner rules: no glue is universal, the glue must suit the material, the bond is only as strong as the weakest material, more surface area means a stronger bond, and add a mechanical fastener where possible
- **design_rules_given**: Almost none. Only qualitative rules (more area = stronger, clamp PVA, use two coats of contact cement on porous materials, bond contact cement within about 2 hours, sand plastic with 120 grit). A few 'time to adhere' figures (e.g. hot glue fast, PVC adhesive 3 hours, metal epoxy 4 to 6 hours). No gap sizes, strengths or temperature limits
- **generator_or_parametric**: No
- **file_formats**: HTML pages only
- **terminology_value**: low to medium: good for everyday names (superglue, white glue, contact cement, hot glue, spray adhesive, epoxy putty) and a few terms (adherend, contact adhesive, cyanoacrylate, PVA). Not an authority, because some chemistry descriptions are wrong
- **decision_support**: Yes, and it's the core of the site: a two-input selector (material A × material B from 11 choices each) returning ranked and conditional recommendations with trade-off labels (strength, speed, toxicity, visibility, flexibility, large-area use) and per-material tips. The logic is a hand-written lookup table (one CGI script, one answer per pair), with no weighting or filtering by environment (heat, water, outdoor)
- **derived_from_or_overlaps**: Original content based on the authors' own tests and manufacturers' MSDS sheets. Similar 'glue this to that' tools exist (e.g. manufacturers' consumer glue advisers such as Loctite's consumer 'Product Advisor' and gluing guides, which recommend only their own brand). Overlaps in purpose with the manufacturers' technical selection tables (Henkel Loctite, 3M, Permabond: chemistry × substrate ratings), which are more authoritative but less beginner-friendly. Reviewed approvingly by Kevin Kelly's Cool Tools (2004: 'functions as a glue calculator… pretty reliable') and Improvised Life (2022: 'may not be up-to-date with the latest in glue technology')

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - example_types
  - gaps
  - language
  - maintained

## 52 Adhesive manufacturers' selection and design guides (Henkel/Loctite, 3M, Permabond)

*PDF design guide.* Guides that classify adhesive chemistries (cyanoacrylate, epoxy, acrylic, polyurethane, silicone, anaerobic, hot melt, contact) by substrate and use, e.g. the Loctite Design Guide for Bonding Plastics

### Basics

- **name**: Adhesive manufacturers' selection and design guides: Henkel LOCTITE Design Guides (Bonding Plastics Vol. 6, Bonding Metals Vol. 6, Bonding Rubber and TPEs Vol. 3) and the LOCTITE Adhesive Sourcebook; 3M Scotch-Weld Structural Adhesives Product Selection Guide and white paper 'Choosing and Using a Structural Adhesive'; Permabond Adhesive Guide and '5 Steps to choosing the best adhesive'
- **url**: Main example: LOCTITE Design Guide for Bonding Plastics, Vol. 6 (LT-2197, 2011), distributor-hosted PDF https://www.ellsworth.com/globalassets/literature-library/manufacturer/henkel-loctite/henkel-loctite-design-guide-plastic-bonding.pdf (opened). Also opened: Henkel-hosted Metals guide https://dm.henkel-dam.com/is/content/henkel/Metal_Bonding_Guide.pdf; Henkel-hosted Rubber/TPE guide https://dm.henkel-dam.com/is/content/henkel/lt-2662a-brochure-design-guide-for-bonding-rubber-and-thermoplastic-elastomers-volume-3-2011; LOCTITE Adhesive Sourcebook 2017 Vol. 20 https://www.ellsworth.com/globalassets/literature-library/manufacturer/henkel-loctite/henkel-loctite-adhesive-sourcebook-2015.pdf; 3M Product Selection Guide (June 2024) https://multimedia.3m.com/mws/media/328280O/product-selection-guide.pdf; 3M white paper https://multimedia.3m.com/mws/media/795693O/choosing-and-using-a-structural-adhesive-white-paper.pdf; Permabond Adhesive Guide (2023, distributor copy) https://tewipack.de/fileadmin/website/service/downloads/produktinfos/permabond/permabond-adhesive-guide-2023-en.pdf; Permabond article https://permabond.com/adhesive-selection-5-steps-to-choosing-the-best-adhesive-for-your-situation/
- **resource_type**: PDF design guide (manufacturer technical literature: design guides, selector guides and catalogues with comparison tables; 3M also runs an online interactive selector)
- **cost**: Free. The PDFs download without login from manufacturer or distributor sites. Printed copies were historically free on request
- **license**: All rights reserved (e.g. '© Henkel Corporation, 2011. All rights reserved'; 3M and Permabond material is copyrighted and trademarked). Link to them and cite facts (e.g. 'cyanoacrylates rate Poor on glass'), but don't copy the tables, figures or text. Product names are trademarks and are out of scope for the encyclopedia anyway
- **access_barrier**: none (free PDF download). Some manufacturer sites block automated access or need a region choice, but distributor mirrors (e.g. Ellsworth) are open. 3M's online selector is a web app
- **image_license**: All rights reserved (manufacturer photos, joint-design and stress-distribution diagrams, bar charts)
- **authority_level**: manufacturer (the technical departments of Henkel, 3M and Permabond, based on their own lab testing, e.g. Henkel's block-shear tests on 30 plastics and 26 rubbers/TPEs; commercially biased toward their own products)

### Coverage

- **organisation**: By substrate, then by chemistry. Henkel design guides: an adhesive-family chapter (description, chemistry and cure mechanism, advantages and disadvantages), then one 2-page chapter per substrate (properties, trade names, typical uses, a shear-strength table for about 15 to 20 of their products against unfilled and filled grades, an 'Adhesive Performance' summary, the effect of surface treatments, compatibility notes). 3M: 'Step 1: select the most difficult substrate → Step 2: key attribute (e.g. general purpose, high temperature, low surface energy, oily metal) → Step 3: product family'. Permabond: 5 steps (certifications → technology → application requirements (substrate, gap, temperature, chemicals) → process → test) plus tables. The Henkel and Permabond grids work for beginners only once they know the plastic's proper name (e.g. polypropylene vs ABS). The 'most difficult substrate first' rule and the one-page chemistry × substrate ratings are the most beginner-usable parts
- **scope_match**: medium: this is the authoritative source for how adhesive TYPES (chemistries) differ by material and use, which the project's adhesive family needs, and it gives real selection logic. But it's industrial, focused on selling products (tables list product numbers), and covers nothing else in the project's scope. Hobby glues are mostly missing (PVA wood glue, contact cement, solvent cement and starch paste appear only in passing)
- **taxonomy_used**: By chemistry and cure mechanism. Henkel groups families as 'instant bonding' (cyanoacrylate, hot melt, light cure), 'flexible bonding and sealing' (elastomeric, silicone) and 'structural bonding' (two-step acrylic, two-part acrylic, epoxy, polyurethane), about 9 top-level categories in the Sourcebook. 3M splits 'structural adhesives' into rigid, flexible, toughened and heat-cured epoxy; MMA, low-odour and LSE/nylon-bonding acrylic; semi-rigid and flexible urethane; and instant adhesives (plastic-and-rubber vs surface-insensitive cyanoacrylate), ranked by load-bearing ability (pressure-sensitive tapes < hot melts < … < epoxy, the strongest). Permabond uses 8 product chemistries (AA, CA, ES, ET, MS/MT, PT, TA, UV). Substrates are grouped as metals / plastics / rubbers and elastomers / glass and ceramics / wood, with plastics further split by polymer and by surface energy (low-surface-energy polyolefins and fluoropolymers need primers or special acrylics)
- **example_types**: cyanoacrylate (instant adhesive, superglue); rubber-toughened cyanoacrylate; surface-insensitive cyanoacrylate (for acidic surfaces like wood, paper, leather); cyanoacrylate with polyolefin primer (for polypropylene/polyethylene); UV/light-curing cyanoacrylate; two-part epoxy (rigid, flexible and toughened); one-part heat-cure epoxy; two-part structural acrylic (MMA, methyl methacrylate); two-step (no-mix, surface-activated) acrylic; low-surface-energy (LSE) bonding acrylic for PE/PP; light-curing (UV) acrylic; two-part polyurethane (urethane); reactive polyurethane hot melt (PUR); hot melt (EVA, polyamide, polyolefin types); silicone adhesive/sealant (RTV); MS polymer / silane-modified hybrid (flexible bonder-sealant); anaerobic adhesive (threadlocker, retaining compound; metal-only, cures without air); elastomeric/contact adhesive (solvent-based rubber); plus brief mentions of phenolic, polyimide, plastisol, PVA and pressure-sensitive adhesive (tapes)
- **making_methods**: Industrial assembly and design engineering (automotive, aerospace, electronics, medical devices, appliances, signage, general metal and plastic fabrication, maintenance and repair). Not aimed at 3D printing, though the plastics data (ABS, ASA, acrylic, nylon, polycarbonate, PETG's relative PET, polypropylene) apply directly to printed parts
- **special_behaviours_covered**: Adhesive types only: cyanoacrylate, epoxy (rigid, flexible, toughened, heat-cure), structural acrylic (two-part MMA, two-step/no-mix, LSE-bonding), light/UV-cure acrylic, polyurethane (two-part and PUR hot melt), hot melt, silicone, MS polymer hybrid, anaerobic and elastomeric/contact adhesives. Materials: metals (aluminium, steel, stainless, copper, zinc/galvanised), about 30 plastics including hard-to-bond polyolefins and PTFE, rubbers and TPEs, glass, ceramics, wood, composites and concrete, with ratings and surface-prep advice for each. Uses: structural load-bearing, flexible or impact joints, sealing, gap filling, high temperature, solvent resistance. Anaerobic threadlockers and retaining compounds do touch the project's other families (thread locking against vibration loosening, retaining bearings: a kind of self-locking), but the other special behaviours (torque-limiting, one-way, indexing, over-centre, compliant, print-in-place, gear trains, powered connectors) are not covered
- **entry_structure**: Adhesive-family entry: general description, chemistry and cure mechanism, advantages and disadvantages list, typical uses. Substrate entry (Henkel): typical property table, trade names, general description, typical applications, adhesive shear-strength table (psi/MPa; shading shows statistically significant effects of fillers or roughening), adhesive performance summary, surface treatment notes, compatibility notes (e.g. 'ABS is incompatible with anaerobic adhesives'). Selector chart (3M, Permabond): rows of substrates or attributes, columns of chemistries, cells rated Poor to Excellent or '-' for not suitable
- **audience_level**: professional (design and manufacturing engineers); some sections (the Sourcebook comparison table, 3M's Q&A page, Permabond's viscosity-vs-everyday-liquids list) are readable by an advanced hobbyist

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: low to medium: dense tables of product codes, polymer acronyms (ASA, PBT, PEI, H-NBR) and lab data. But parts are very clear: one-page chemistry × substrate ratings (Poor to Excellent), plain advantages and disadvantages lists, joint-stress diagrams (shear vs peel vs cleavage), and Permabond's lovely viscosity scale (water 1 mPa·s, honey about 10,000, ketchup about 50,000, peanut butter about 250,000)
- **has_images**: yes: good-quality technical diagrams (joint types: lap, offset, butt, scarf, strap, cylindrical; stress-distribution curves; bond width vs overlap), bar charts (temperature performance, adhesive vs weld vs rivet vs nut-and-bolt shear strength and cost) and product photos
- **gaps**: (1) Brand-product answers: the tables recommend product numbers, not generic types, and each maker covers only its own chemistries. (2) Hobby adhesives are missing or barely covered: PVA wood glue, contact cement, solvent cement for PVC/acrylic/polystyrene, starch and paper glues, spray adhesive, foam-safe glues, and 3D-printing materials by name (PLA, PETG, TPU). (3) Needs the proper polymer name ('polypropylene', 'EPDM'), which beginners often don't know; there's no 'I don't know what plastic this is' route (e.g. recycling codes are shown in the Henkel guide, but there's no burn or float test). (4) Industrial surface treatments (chromic acid, sodium etch, plasma, corona) are beyond a hobbyist. (5) Some data are old (Henkel plastics prices quoted from 2004; volume 6 is 2011). (6) Health and safety for home users (fumes, skin) isn't a focus. (7) No mechanical-joint context beyond adhesive joint design
- **good_for_entry_links**: Partly. The PDFs have stable-ish URLs but are single large documents (link to the guide plus a page number, e.g. the Henkel Plastics guide p. 62 for polypropylene). Distributor mirrors (Ellsworth, tewipack) may move; Henkel's own DAM links (dm.henkel-dam.com) are official. Permabond web articles have stable URLs, e.g. https://permabond.com/adhesive-selection-5-steps-to-choosing-the-best-adhesive-for-your-situation/
- **reusable_data**: Not reusable as data: PDF tables only, all rights reserved. The ratings (e.g. cyanoacrylate: glass Poor, rubber Very Good) are facts that can be restated in our own words and cited, and the chemistry × substrate grid is small enough to rebuild by hand from several sources
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) 'Choose for the most difficult material first' (3M Step 1): a strong top branch for the adhesive flow chart (e.g. if either part is polyethylene, polypropylene, PTFE or silicone rubber, go straight to the 'slippery plastics' branch: special primer plus superglue, or low-surface-energy acrylic). (2) The one-page chemistry × material rating table (Poor/Fair/Good/Very Good/Excellent for metals, plastics, glass, rubber, wood) as a compact comparison graphic for adhesive entries. (3) Per-chemistry trade-off rows: shear vs peel strength, flexibility, gap filling, temperature range, water/solvent resistance, cure time, one-part vs two-part. (4) Joint-design basics as a beginner panel: glue joints are strong in shear and compression and weak in peel and cleavage; making the joint wider helps more than making the overlap longer. (5) Viscosity explained with everyday liquids (water → honey → ketchup → peanut butter) to explain gap filling. (6) A 'will it stress-crack or melt this plastic?' compatibility check (Henkel stress-cracking table: cyanoacrylates and acrylics 'should be tested' on ABS, acrylic, polycarbonate, PVC, etc.). (7) 'Always test before trusting' as a final flow-chart step (Permabond step 5)
- **design_rules_given**: Yes, some numbers. 3M: optimum bond line (glue layer) thickness 0.005 to 0.020 in (about 0.13 to 0.5 mm); all structural adhesives give at least 1,000 psi overlap shear on aluminium; work life of 5 minutes or less for small, fast parts vs 20 minutes or more for large clamped assemblies; heat-cure epoxy at about 120 to 180 °C for 40 to 60 minutes. Permabond: cyanoacrylate gap fill up to about 0.5 mm (per grade); temperature ranges per chemistry (e.g. cyanoacrylate typical -54 to +82 °C, 1-part epoxy -40 to +150 °C). Henkel: shear strengths in psi/MPa per adhesive per plastic, stress-concentration tables vs bond-line gap and adhesive stiffness (modulus), temperature ranges per category (e.g. silicones up to about 204 °C typical)
- **generator_or_parametric**: No (3M offers an online filter-based product selector, not geometry)
- **file_formats**: PDF (plus web pages; 3M's interactive selector is a web app)
- **terminology_value**: high: authoritative for chemistry names and aliases (cyanoacrylate = instant adhesive; two-step/no-mix/surface-activated acrylic; MMA; PUR = reactive polyurethane hot melt; anaerobic; RTV silicone; MS polymer; low surface energy (LSE) plastics), stress terms (shear, peel, cleavage, tensile, compressive), joint types (lap, offset, butt, scarf, strap, cylindrical), and surface treatments (abrading, flame, corona, plasma, primer, etching). Good source for the 'proper engineering term in brackets'
- **decision_support**: Yes, several kinds. (a) Chemistry × substrate rating tables (Henkel Sourcebook p. 6; Permabond 5-steps article; Permabond surface-prep table with '-' not suitable / Poor / OK / Good / Very Good per chemistry for each of about 70 substrates). (b) 3M's 3-step selector chart (most difficult substrate → key attribute → product family) and a separate substrate selector. (c) Henkel measured shear-strength tables per plastic and rubber, with a written 'best performers' summary (e.g. polypropylene: primed cyanoacrylate or polyolefin-bonding acrylic give the strongest bonds). (d) Henkel Sourcebook decision trees per family (e.g. which cyanoacrylate or which epoxy). (e) Permabond's 5 steps: certifications → technology → substrate/gap/temperature/chemicals → process → test. (f) Stress-cracking compatibility table (Henkel)

### Other Info

- **uncertain** (fields left out above):
  - covers
  - derived_from_or_overlaps
  - has_animations
  - item_count
  - language
  - maintained
  - year

## 53 Adhesive reference works and classifications (Petrie's Handbook of Adhesives and Sealants, Adhesive and Sealant Council, Wikipedia adhesive types)

*Book / classification system.* Reference books, industry bodies and wiki pages that classify adhesive types by chemistry and curing (reactive, evaporative, pressure-sensitive, hot melt)

### Basics

- **name**: Adhesive reference works and classifications: Edward M. Petrie's 'Handbook of Adhesives and Sealants' (McGraw Hill), the Adhesive and Sealant Council's adhesives.org portal plus the ASC-CATIA-FEICA 'Adhesives & Sealants Classification Manual', and Wikipedia's 'Adhesive' and 'List of glues' articles
- **url**: Three main pages, all opened. (1) ASC adhesive technologies page: https://adhesives.org/adhesives-sealants/adhesives-sealants-overview/adhesive-technologies/ (2) Wikipedia: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adhesive and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_glues (3) Petrie, publisher page for the 3rd edition: https://www.mheducation.com/highered/mhp/product/handbook-adhesives-sealants-third-edition.html . Also opened: the free classification manual PDF https://adhesives.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/10/ASC-CATIA-FEICA-Adhesives-and-Sealants-Classification-Manual-2021.pdf , the ASC consumer/DIY page https://adhesives.org/markets/consumer-do-it-yourself/ , and Wikipedia https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Adhesives
- **resource_type**: Mixed: book (Petrie's handbook), classification system (ASC-CATIA-FEICA manual), industry portal (adhesives.org, run by a trade association) and encyclopedia/wiki (Wikipedia)
- **license**: Petrie: all rights reserved (commercial book), so link to it and cite it, but don't copy text, tables or figures. adhesives.org: '© Adhesives.org' footer with no open licence, so treat it as all rights reserved: link, and paraphrase with a citation. The classification manual PDF shows no licence statement; it is free to download, and its category names and numbers can be cited as facts. Wikipedia text is CC BY-SA 4.0 (licence link on the page), so it can be adapted with attribution and share-alike
- **language**: English for all three. The Wikipedia 'Adhesive' article has many language versions (e.g. German 'Klebstoff', Spanish 'Adhesivo', Czech 'Lepidlo'), which is useful for translations. FEICA's first (1996) European manual was printed in three languages, according to the 2021 manual's introduction
- **access_barrier**: Wikipedia, adhesives.org and the manual: none. Petrie: paywall (buy it, borrow from a library, or use an institutional AccessEngineering licence)
- **authority_level**: Petrie: professional reference by an expert author (about 50 years in the adhesives industry, formerly at Westinghouse and ABB, technical adviser to SpecialChem), from a major engineering publisher. ASC: the US industry trade association (founded 1958; 110 member companies, 'more than 75% of the U.S. industry'), so manufacturer-backed. Classification manual: the joint industry classification of ASC (North America), FEICA (Europe) and CATIA (China), which together cover more than 70% of the world market. Wikipedia: community or unvetted, with citations

### Coverage

- **organisation**: Petrie: by topic, from theory to joint design, testing, adhesive families (by polymer type), selection, substrates and applications. It's a handbook read by chapter and works if you already know the family names. ASC: website menu (Adhesives 101 → overview → technologies; science of adhesion; fastening and bonding; markets). Families are grouped by how they harden, which suits beginners, but only the physically hardening group is written up on the technologies page. Manual: numbered codes (e.g. 2.5.4 Acrylates, including cyanoacrylate and anaerobics), grouped partly by chemistry and partly by physical form (dispersion, hot melt, solvent, reactive, water-soluble). It's built for market statistics, so it's hard for beginners. Wikipedia: nested headings (By reactiveness → Non-reactive: drying, pressure-sensitive, contact, hot; Reactive: anaerobic, multi-part, pre-mixed and frozen, one-part), then By origin (natural / synthetic). The question 'how does it harden?' is easy to follow without knowing chemistry names
- **scope_match**: medium: adhesive TYPES are in scope, and these are the authoritative sources for the family names and how each family hardens. But they are industrial and general, with nothing specific on 3D-printing plastics (PLA, PETG, resin) and no mechanisms. They are best used as the naming and classification backbone for the adhesive branch, with maker test data from other sources
- **taxonomy_used**: Four overlapping schemes. (1) By how it hardens (Wikipedia; ASC): non-reactive or 'physically hardening' (it dries as water or solvent evaporates, cools as with hot melt, or stays permanently tacky as with pressure-sensitive adhesives) versus reactive or 'chemically curing' (two-part mix; one-part cured by moisture, heat, UV light or no air (anaerobic)). Wikipedia has 2 top groups with 4 + 4 subtypes; ASC lists 4 physically hardening families. (2) By chemistry and form (ASC-CATIA-FEICA manual): 7 top-level product groups: 2.1 natural polymers (starch/dextrin, protein such as casein and soy, animal glue); 2.2 polymer dispersion/emulsion (PVAc, EVA, acrylic, SBR, other latex including polychloroprene, natural rubber latex, polyurethane dispersion); 2.3 hot melt (polyolefin, EVA, polyamide, polyester, styrene block copolymers, polyurethane including moisture-cure, acrylic); 2.4 solvent-based (polychloroprene, polyurethane, rubbers, acrylic, silicone, PVC copolymers); 2.5 reactive (epoxy; polyurethane; unsaturated polyester; acrylates including cyanoacrylate, anaerobic/dimethacrylate and second-generation structural acrylics; silicone; formaldehyde condensates such as phenolic, urea, melamine and resorcinol; silane-modified polymers); 2.6 water-soluble polymers (polyvinyl alcohol, cellulose ethers, methylcellulose, CMC, PVP); 2.7 other. (3) By origin: natural versus synthetic (Wikipedia; the manual's 2.1). (4) Petrie: by polymer behaviour (thermosetting, elastomeric, thermoplastic, naturally occurring, specialty), which gives 5 type chapters
- **example_types**: cyanoacrylate / superglue (moisture-curing one-part); two-part epoxy (multi-part reactive); polyvinyl acetate (PVAc) white glue and yellow 'aliphatic resin' wood glue (polymer dispersion, drying); contact adhesive / contact cement (polychloroprene or natural rubber, coat both sides then press); solvent cement / solvent welding (acetone for ABS, MEK for polystyrene, dichloromethane for polycarbonate and acrylic/PMMA); hot-melt adhesive (EVA glue-gun sticks; also reactive polyurethane hot melts); pressure-sensitive adhesive (tapes, labels, removable and repositionable adhesives); anaerobic adhesive (thread-locking fluid); UV / light-curing adhesive (usually acrylic); heat-curing one-part epoxy; moisture-curing polyurethane (foaming 'multipurpose' glue); second-generation structural acrylic (SGA / methacrylate); silicone adhesive and sealant; silane-modified polymer (MS polymer) adhesive/sealant; construction adhesive; plastisol (PVC paste cured by heat); animal / hide glue (reversible with heat and water); casein and starch/dextrin glues; resorcinol- and urea-formaldehyde glues (exterior wood, plywood); electrically debondable adhesive (releases when a current is applied); pre-mixed and frozen (PMF) adhesives
- **making_methods**: General engineering and manufacturing (automotive, aerospace, construction, packaging, electronics, footwear, woodworking and joinery). There is a little on consumer/DIY use (ASC consumer page; the manual's segment 1.4.1). Nothing is aimed at 3D printing
- **item_count**: Manual: 7 adhesive product categories with about 45 chemistry subcategories, plus 9 sealant categories. Wikipedia 'Adhesive': 2 reactiveness groups with 8 named subtypes, plus 2 origin groups. 'List of glues': about 50 glue types in 4 tables. Category:Adhesives: about 98 articles. Petrie 2nd edition: 18 chapters (about 800 to 1,048 pages)
- **entry_structure**: Wikipedia: each type has a short plain paragraph with examples and typical uses, some with a photo, and links to a main article (e.g. Hot-melt adhesive, Pressure-sensitive adhesive). The 'List of glues' tables have columns for type, subtypes, sources, manufacture, chemistry, fumes (volatiles), safety, properties, shelf life, working time, setting, strength, solvents, uses and date, but many cells are empty. ASC: an essay-style paragraph per family (how it works, pros and cons, industrial uses, forms supplied, e.g. hot-melt sticks for glue guns). Manual: a numbered code, a name and a short note of what's included and excluded. Petrie: long chapters with formulations, property tables, test methods and design guidance (from the publisher and Google Books descriptions)
- **audience_level**: Petrie: professional (end users, formulators, marketers, design engineers), written so that 'those new to the field' get a fundamental knowledge base. ASC: designers and industry, plus one consumer/DIY page. Manual: industry and market analysts. Wikipedia: general public or undergraduate

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: medium overall. Wikipedia 'Adhesive' is high: plain wording, the reactive/non-reactive split and everyday examples (glue gun, Post-it notes, superglue). The ASC consumer page is high, with four household glue types and what they stick. ASC's technologies page is medium: clear, but written for industry. The manual is low: a market-code list with chemical names. Petrie is low to medium: thorough, but long, technical and paywalled
- **has_animations**: adhesives.org has a videos section and embeds a James May video explaining how glue works on its homepage. Wikipedia and Petrie: no
- **gaps**: Nothing on 3D-printing plastics (PLA, PETG, ASA, resin) or which glue suits which printed material. No simple 'this material to that material' matrix; Wikipedia only links out to the thistothat.com glue selector. The ASC technologies page describes only the physically hardening families; reactive types (CA, epoxy, anaerobic, UV) are covered only on the DIY page and in the manual. The manual is market segmentation, not selection help. 'List of glues' has been flagged incomplete since August 2008, with many empty cells. Few beginner numbers (strengths, gap sizes, cure times) except the DIY page's cure times. Petrie is paywalled and pitched at industry. None explains solvent welding of printed parts, surface prep for low-energy plastics in beginner terms, or glue-plus-mechanical hybrid joints
- **good_for_entry_links**: Yes for Wikipedia, which has stable per-type articles, e.g. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adhesive (overview; section anchors such as #Contact and #Hot); related articles titled Cyanoacrylate, Hot-melt adhesive, Pressure-sensitive adhesive, Polyvinyl acetate, Epoxy, Solvent bonding, Thread-locking compound and Construction adhesive appear in Category:Adhesives. ASC: one long page for all technologies (no per-type URLs), but good as a 'how glues harden' citation. The manual PDF has a stable URL and is good for citing the official category code of each family. Petrie: cite by chapter; there are no free per-chapter pages
- **reusable_data**: Wikipedia: yes. It's CC BY-SA 4.0, and the category tree and articles are available through the MediaWiki API; the 'List of glues' tables can be scraped (with attribution and share-alike). Manual: not a dataset, but the numbered list is short enough to rebuild by hand, and names and codes are citable facts (no licence stated). ASC and Petrie text: not reusable, so link and paraphrase
- **maintained**: Wikipedia 'Adhesive' is actively edited (28 August 2026). adhesives.org is live with news posted September 2026, though its technologies page was last modified in September 2023. The manual's current edition is 2021 (updated about every 9 years: 2012, then 2021). Petrie's 3rd edition (December 2020) is still sold in 2026
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) Use 'How does it harden?' as the first adhesive question, in plain words with the term in brackets: dries (evaporative / drying), cools (hot melt), stays sticky (pressure-sensitive), or reacts: two-part mix (multi-part reactive), air moisture (moisture-cure, e.g. CA), no air plus metal (anaerobic), UV light (light-cure), heat (heat-cure). (2) ASC's wet-bond versus contact-bond method (coat both sides, let it dry, then press) as a 'how you apply it' tag. (3) ASC's selection triad as flow-chart questions: What are you bonding (same or different, porous or smooth)? How will you apply it? How will it be used (strength, heat, water)? (4) A 'can it be undone?' filter: hot melt reheats, CA dissolves with debonder or acetone, hide glue with heat and water, PSA peels, epoxy and solvent welds are permanent. (5) Wikipedia's failure types (adhesive failure versus cohesive failure versus the part breaking) to explain test results. (6) The manual's numeric codes as a cross-reference column so each entry can cite its official family. (7) Link to the 'porous substrate needed' rule for water-based glues
- **generator_or_parametric**: No
- **file_formats**: HTML (Wikipedia, adhesives.org); PDF (classification manual, 18 pages); print and eBook for Petrie (Adobe Digital Editions eBook from McGraw Hill; online chapters on AccessEngineering)
- **terminology_value**: High. Proper names and aliases: glue = adhesive = cement = mucilage = paste (the manual; Wikipedia); superglue / instant glue = cyanoacrylate; white glue = PVAc; yellow carpenter's glue = aliphatic resin (a PVAc variant); contact cement = contact adhesive (also 'impact glue'); hot glue = hot-melt adhesive; threadlocker = anaerobic adhesive; tape adhesive = pressure-sensitive adhesive (PSA); MS polymer = silane-modified polymer; SGA = second-generation (structural) acrylic; 'physically hardening' versus 'chemically curing'; wet bonding versus contact bonding; adherend or substrate (the part being glued); cohesive versus adhesive failure. Interlanguage links give translations

## 54 Maker adhesive guides and tests for 3D prints and hobby materials (Prusa knowledge base, CNC Kitchen glue tests, woodworking glue guides)

*Encyclopedia / wiki.* Hobbyist-level comparisons of glues for PLA, PETG, ABS, resin, wood and mixed materials, with test results

### Basics

- **name**: Maker adhesive guides and tests for 3D prints and hobby materials: Prusa's 'The Great Guide to Gluing and Assembling 3D Prints' (with tensile tests) and the Prusa Knowledge Base material pages; CNC Kitchen's glue and solvent-bonding tests; and woodworking glue guides (Woodcraft glue chart, Rockler 'Understanding Wood Glue'), plus smaller hobby tests (MakerBuildIt) and the thistothat.com glue picker
- **url**: Main page: https://blog.prusa3d.com/the-great-guide-to-gluing-and-assembling-3d-prints_44908/ (opened). Also opened: Prusa KB https://help.prusa3d.com/article/pla_2062 , https://help.prusa3d.com/article/petg_2059 and https://help.prusa3d.com/article/asa_1809 ; CNC Kitchen https://www.cnckitchen.com/blog/can-acetone-glue-layers-of-3d-prints-together ; Hackaday write-up of CNC Kitchen's glue video https://hackaday.com/2019/12/28/stronger-3d-prints-glue-or-carbon-fiber/ (video https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wN1AFG08P28 , title confirmed via YouTube oEmbed); Woodcraft https://www.woodcraft.com/blogs/sanding-finishing/complete-guide-to-woodworking-glues ; Rockler https://www.rockler.com/learn/understanding-wood-glue ; MakerBuildIt https://makerbuildit.com/blogs/3d-printing/strongest-glue-for-3d-printed-parts ; Formlabs https://formlabs.com/blog/advanced-3dp-techniques/ ; This to That https://www.thistothat.com/
- **resource_type**: Mixed maker guides: manufacturer blog and knowledge base (Prusa, Formlabs), video channel with a test blog (CNC Kitchen), retailer guides (Woodcraft, Rockler), a hobby test blog (MakerBuildIt) and a small interactive tool (This to That)
- **cost**: Free (no login needed for any page opened)
- **language**: English. The Prusa gluing guide also exists in Czech, German, Spanish, French, Italian and Polish (hreflang versions). The Prusa Knowledge Base is multilingual. thistothat.com has a French version
- **access_barrier**: none
- **image_license**: All rights reserved (the authors' own photos of glued samples, test rigs and glue products; CNC Kitchen videos on YouTube under the standard YouTube licence). No openly licensed images found
- **authority_level**: Manufacturer (Prusa Research, Formlabs) and educator/tester (CNC Kitchen, an engineer who runs repeatable strength tests on a DIY universal test machine). Woodworking retailers (Woodcraft, Rockler) are community or trade level; MakerBuildIt and thistothat.com are community or unvetted. For peer-reviewed data on bonded prints, see Polymers 2025, 17(6), 768 (doi:10.3390/polym17060768), a CC BY single-lap-joint study of epoxy-bonded 3D-printed ABS and PLA (abstract read via Europe PMC)

### Coverage

- **covers**: Hobby adhesive TYPES for 3D-printed plastics, resin and wood, with test results. PRUSA GUIDE: 3 main types in depth (cyanoacrylate/CA, including gel, rubber-filled, odourless/foam-safe and extra-thin capillary grades, plus debonders; solvent glues, both extra-thin and thick modeller's cements, and capillary gluing; two-part epoxy). It briefly lists PVA wood glue, contact cement, hot glue, silicone, 'resin + UV light', and construction ('mounting') adhesive for very large prints. It also covers non-permanent options (screws, magnets, pausing the print to embed nuts or magnets), and ends with a tensile test of 12 glues on PLA, PETG and ASA. PRUSA KB material pages: which solvent glues or welds which filament (ASA and ABS with acetone; some PLA partly with acetone, or superglue; PETG dissolves only in lab chemicals such as dichloromethane). CNC KITCHEN: glue and carbon-fibre reinforcement of printed hooks (thin and thick CA, epoxy), and acetone/IPA solvent bonding of ABS and PVB. WOODWORKING: Woodcraft's chart compares 10 wood glues (simple PVA, three compound PVAs (Titebond Original, II and III style), dry/hot hide glue, liquid hide glue, polyurethane, epoxy, urea-formaldehyde, CA) on pot life, open time, clamp/cure time, colour, cleanup, water resistance, shelf life and cost, plus advice on creep, toxicity, speed, temperature, water and gap filling. Rockler describes 6 types (PVA, aliphatic resin, epoxy, polyurethane, CA, hide glue). EXTRAS: MakerBuildIt snap and twist tests on PLA (hot glue, CA, '3D Gloop', a plastics epoxy, polyurethane glue, construction adhesive); Formlabs' 'resin plus 405 nm laser' as a glue for resin prints; thistothat.com's 11-by-11 material-pair glue picker
- **organisation**: Prusa guide: by glue type (CA → solvent → epoxy → others → non-permanent → test table), each with how it works, which product to choose, and warnings and tips, plus a table of contents. Prusa KB: one page per filament, with gluing and smoothing notes inside. CNC Kitchen: one experiment per video or blog post, with a TL;DR summary, method, results and chart. Woodcraft: a side-by-side comparison chart, then 'Avoid / Try' advice by situation (creep, health, speed, cold, water, gaps), which works well for beginners. thistothat.com: pick 'this' material and 'that' material from two drop-downs. All are easy for beginners because they start from the material or job, not the chemistry
- **scope_match**: high: exactly the project's adhesive scope (types compared by material and use, especially PLA, PETG, ABS/ASA, resin and wood), with real test numbers. The only drawbacks are product and brand focus (the Prusa test names products, not just types), and that the sources are scattered
- **taxonomy_used**: Informal, by how the glue works. Prusa: 3 main groups (cyanoacrylate, solvent-based, two-part epoxy) plus an 'other' list (PVA, contact cement, hot glue, silicone, UV resin, construction adhesive) and non-permanent assembly. Woodcraft: 10 columns grouped by chemistry (PVA simple and compound, hide dry and liquid, polyurethane, epoxy, urea-formaldehyde, CA). Rockler: 6 types. thistothat.com: by material pair (11 material classes: ceramic, fabric, glass, leather, metal, paper, plastic, rubber, styrofoam, vinyl, wood)
- **example_types**: cyanoacrylate / superglue (thin, medium, gel, rubber-toughened, odourless/foam-safe, extra-thin capillary); CA debonder (solvent that undoes CA); solvent welding / solvent cement (acetone for ABS and ASA, also some PLA; limonene for HIPS; isopropyl alcohol for PVB; polystyrene modeller's cement); 'ABS juice' (plastic dissolved in solvent as a thick glue); capillary gluing with extra-thin cement or CA; two-part epoxy (5-minute and slow-cure, as a coating or filler); CA tack points plus epoxy fill (hybrid); PVA white glue; aliphatic resin (yellow) wood glue, water-resistant Type II and waterproof Type I; hot hide glue and liquid hide glue (reversible); polyurethane foaming glue (moisture-cure); urea-formaldehyde (resin) glue for veneer and bent laminations; contact cement (suggested for flexible TPU); hot-melt glue; silicone adhesive; construction (mounting) adhesive for large prints; UV-cured resin as glue for resin prints (405 nm laser); methacrylate 'plastic welder' (reader suggestion for PETG and ABS, not tested)
- **making_methods**: 3D printing (FDM/FFF, with some SLA/resin) is the main focus; also woodworking; scale modelling and RC hobby practice feeds into the Prusa guide
- **item_count**: Prusa guide: 3 main types, 6 'other' types, 12 products tensile-tested on 3 materials. Prusa KB: 1 page per filament (dozens of materials). CNC Kitchen: 2 relevant glue/solvent test pieces found (the site has about 125 blog posts in total). Woodcraft: 10 glue columns by 9 property rows. Rockler: 6 types. MakerBuildIt: 7 glues on PLA. thistothat.com: 11 × 11 material pairs (about 66 unique combinations)
- **special_behaviours_covered**: Adhesive types by material, the main subject. PLA: CA was best in Prusa's test (about 9.5 to 10.2 MPa for the three best CAs (other CAs 5.1 to 8.0), versus 5.0 to 6.3 MPa for 5-minute epoxies and 0.9 to 4.9 MPa for solvent or 'all plastics' glues). Some PLA can be glued with acetone (Prusa guide and KB). MakerBuildIt: CA, 3D Gloop and a plastics epoxy all broke the PLA before the bond failed; hot glue, a construction adhesive and 'clear Gorilla Glue' (chemistry not stated) failed, and foaming polyurethane glue held when twisted but snapped cleanly and pushed parts out of line; hot glue can deform PLA. CNC Kitchen: thin CA reached 58 kg on a PLA-blend hook versus about 53 kg untreated; epoxy cracked first, so no gain. PETG: CA was clearly best (13.1 and 12.6 MPa for the top two), and there's no hobby solvent (the Prusa guide says PETG is 'inert to all chemicals normally available to a hobbyist'; the KB says it dissolves only in dichloromethane in a lab). ABS/ASA: acetone solvent-welds (Prusa KB). CNC Kitchen found an acetone repair of ABS reached 17 MPa, as strong as the part's own layer bond (19 MPa), and 2 of 3 samples broke beside the joint. But brushing acetone on every layer while printing gave only 6 MPa (about 15% of flat-printed strength). Prusa's ASA test: extra-thin CA 11.3 MPa, standard CA 9.4, polystyrene poly cement 7.4, Tamiya extra-thin 6.1; one modeller's cement didn't bond ASA at all. HIPS: limonene. PVB: isopropyl alcohol. Resin (SLA): CA works (gel CA is recommended because the bond is almost instant), or liquid resin cured with a 405 nm laser (Formlabs). TPU: CA works if the mating faces are fairly stiff, and contact cement is suggested. Styrofoam: ordinary CA melts it, so use odourless/foam-safe CA. Wood: PVA for indoor joinery, Type II/III for damp, epoxy for gaps and constant water, polyurethane for wet wood or wood to non-wood, hide glue for reversible repairs, urea-formaldehyde for creep-free laminations, CA for quick fixes but brittle. Heat: ordinary CA keeps about 50% of its strength at 80 °C and about 20% at 100 °C; heat-resistant CA keeps 80% at 120 °C; CA goes brittle when frozen. Hand-release / reversible: CA debonders or acetone (test first); freezing snaps CA joints; hide glue with heat and water; screws and magnets as the non-permanent option. Not covered: torque-limiting, ratchet, detent, over-centre, gears, powered connectors
- **entry_structure**: Prusa guide sections: how the glue type works → pros and cons (set time, strength in tension versus shear or peel, sanding, blooming or frosting, shelf life, storage) → product examples with one-line verdicts → warnings, tips and fun facts. Then a results table (MPa per glue for PLA, PETG and ASA). CNC Kitchen: TL;DR, method, reference samples, results chart, discussion of failure, and a video. Woodcraft: chart rows (pot life, open time, clamp/cure time, colour, cleanup, water resistance, shelf life, cost, notes) plus Avoid/Try blocks
- **audience_level**: hobbyist (FDM and resin printer owners, scale modellers, cosplay, DIY woodworkers)

### Usefulness

- **beginner_friendliness**: high: plain language, product photos, clear 'do this, not that' advice, and practical tips (breathe on CA to speed it up, clean off and restart if the fit is wrong after a few seconds, keep CA fumes away to avoid white frosting). Some brand names are local to the Czech Republic, and the numbers need context (single samples)
- **has_images**: yes: good-quality photos of products, glued joints, CA frosting and test samples (Prusa); test rigs, fracture surfaces and result charts (CNC Kitchen); the Woodcraft chart. No diagrams of joint geometry
- **has_animations**: Video: CNC Kitchen YouTube tests ('STRONGER 3D Prints with GLUE? (and CARBON FIBERS)', and the acetone layer-bonding video); the Prusa guide embeds a video of large multi-part prints; MakerBuildIt is a video-based test. No interactive models
- **gaps**: No single place with all types × all materials. Prusa's test has one quick sample per cell and names products, not grades. No shear or peel data, only tension (and the guide itself notes CA is weak in shear, peel and torsion). Thin coverage of PETG alternatives (methacrylate structural adhesives are only a reader comment), of nylon, polycarbonate and TPU, and of bonding prints to metal, wood or fabric. Almost no joint design advice (overlap area, keys, pins, dovetails to help the glue). CNC Kitchen has no dedicated multi-glue comparison blog post (the glue data is from one 2019 video summarised by Hackaday). Woodworking guides don't mention printed plastics. thistothat.com gives brand names, and lumps all plastics into one class
- **reusable_data**: Scrape-only. The Prusa tensile table (12 glues × 3 materials, MPa) and the Woodcraft chart (10 glues × 9 properties) are small enough to cite as facts with attribution, but not to copy wholesale (all rights reserved). No API or dataset. thistothat.com results come from a simple query URL (cgi-bin/glue.cgi?this=Plastic&that=Wood), but the content is copyrighted
- **maintained**: All online in September 2026. The Prusa guide hasn't been updated since April 2021 (it promised more alternatives later; a reader's methacrylate suggestion was acknowledged). The Prusa KB is maintained. CNC Kitchen is active (sitemap updated September 2026). Woodcraft and Rockler pages are live. thistothat.com shows a 1999–2024 copyright
- **ideas_to_borrow**: (1) A material-by-glue matrix with test numbers, like Prusa's MPa table, but by TYPE rather than brand, with the source per cell. (2) Woodcraft's 'Avoid / Try' blocks per situation (long-term load or creep, outdoor/water, gaps, cold shop, health, need for speed): great flow-chart branches. (3) Split the property columns: open time, clamp time, full cure time, gap filling, cleanup, water resistance, reversibility. (4) thistothat.com's 'this material + that material' two-drop-down picker as a model for the adhesive part of the flow chart. (5) Show failure location (glue failed versus the part broke next to the glue) as the real measure of a good joint (CNC Kitchen, MakerBuildIt). (6) 'Can I undo it?' notes (debonder, freezer, heat). (7) Hybrid tips: CA tack points plus epoxy fill; glue plus screws, inserts or magnets. (8) Warn about 'look-alike product names' (e.g. one product line with both CA and solvent versions) to push readers to think in types
- **design_rules_given**: Yes, some numbers: Prusa tensile strengths per glue for PLA, PETG and ASA (about 0.9 to 13.1 MPa); CA hardness develops over 8 to 24 hours; CA heat derating (50% at 80 °C, 20% at 100 °C; heat-resistant CA 80% at 120 °C); epoxy cure times from under 90 seconds to over 12 hours. CNC Kitchen: ABS 41 MPa flat versus 19 MPa across layers, acetone-bonded 17 MPa; hook loads (untreated about 53 kg versus thin CA 58 kg). Woodcraft: open time, clamp time and cure time per glue (e.g. PVA 5 to 10 minutes open, 30 to 60 minutes clamp, 24 hours cure; epoxy 4 to 50 minutes pot life); PVAs need above about 50 °F (10 °C), Titebond III-type down to 45 °F, some epoxies down to 35 °F; epoxy and UF stable to about 200 °F and 180 °F; at least 50% of the joint faces should be in contact. Peer-reviewed extra: epoxy single-lap joints on printed PLA reached 2.7 to 3.4 kN and ABS 1.4 to 1.7 kN, strongest when cured at 80 °C for 1 hour (Polymers 2025)
- **generator_or_parametric**: No
- **file_formats**: HTML articles, YouTube video, tables in HTML (no downloadable data)
- **terminology_value**: medium: good for the hobby names and their aliases (CA = superglue = instant glue; accelerator/activator ('kicker'); debonder; extra-thin cement; capillary gluing; frosting/blooming; gel CA; foam-safe CA; yellow glue = aliphatic resin; Type I (waterproof) versus Type II (water-resistant); open time, pot life, clamp time, creep; chalk temperature (the temperature below which PVA dries white and fails)). Not an authority for chemistry names, so use the adhesive reference works item for those
- **decision_support**: Prusa: a comparison table of test results plus pros-and-cons prose, but no selector. Woodcraft: a side-by-side comparison chart plus situation-based Avoid/Try rules (effectively a decision list). thistothat.com: a two-drop-down material-pair selector that returns recommended glue products, 'faster' alternatives, 'less toxic' alternatives and tips (e.g. plastic to wood: silicone-based glue for strongest, 5-minute epoxy for faster, contact cement as the less toxic option, clamp, and sand smooth plastic with 120 grit)
